### [1] [ISEE: Interactive Semantic Enrichment for Database Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02604)
*Yuan Tian,Yiru Chen,Rakesh R. Menon,Zifan Liu,Ting Cai,Fei Wu,Anudeep Chimakurthi,Prashanthi Ramamurthy,Sridevi Aishwariya Ganesan,Kun Qian,Yunyao Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ISEE is an interactive system that improves data field descriptions by assessing their quality, gathering domain knowledge from users, and collaboratively enriching semantics to enhance LLM agent performance in data-related tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents struggle with data tasks due to ambiguous or incomplete field descriptions, where essential context (like customized field meanings) comes from users' undocumented domain knowledge, limiting performance in downstream tasks like entity-linking.

Method: ISEE is an Interactive SEmantic Enrichment system that: 1) measures field description quality through a scoring system, 2) gathers domain knowledge from users, and 3) collaboratively enriches semantics with users in an interactive process.

Result: Through user studies, automated user simulations, quantitative evaluations, and case studies, ISEE significantly reduces cognitive load, improves description quality, and enhances downstream task performance.

Conclusion: Interactive semantic enrichment with ISEE effectively bridges the gap between user domain knowledge and formal data semantics, enabling better LLM agent performance on data-related tasks by addressing the ambiguity and incompleteness of field descriptions.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly being deployed for data-related tasks, including data sense-making, exploration, and retrieval. However, their performance heavily depends on the clarity and completeness of data semantics. In practice, many field descriptions remain ambiguous or incomplete, as much of the essential context (e.g., the meaning of a customized field) originates from users' domain knowledge and is rarely documented publicly. This gap restricts the agents' task performance in downstream tasks, such as entity-linking. To bridge this gap, we introduce a novel and comprehensive Interactive SEmantic Enrichment system (ISEE). Given a data field description, ISEE measures its quality through a scoring system, gathers domain knowledge, and collaboratively enriches the semantics with users. Through a user study, automated user simulation, quantitative evaluation, and case study, we demonstrate that ISEE significantly reduces cognitive load, improves description quality, and enhances downstream task performance.

</details>


### [2] [Self-Organising Digital Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02606)
*Marcello Barylli,Gabriel Béna,Alexander Mordvintsev,Eleni Nisioti,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-organising digital circuits using transformers to configure Boolean gates enable adaptive fault tolerance, achieving near-perfect recovery from hardware faults and scaling beyond training sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional fault tolerance uses static strategies like hardware redundancy, while biological systems show adaptive plasticity by dynamically reorganizing around damage. The paper aims to bridge biological self-organisation principles with digital hardware to create adaptive, fault-tolerant circuits.

Method: Introduces Self-Organising Digital Circuits using a topology-masked Transformer that configures Lookup Tables (LUT) of Boolean gates. Extends Neural Cellular Automata pattern-generation paradigm to navigate Boolean search space for computational tasks rather than fixed target states. Treats functional logic generation as meta-learning on graphs.

Result: Can self-assemble functional circuits from scratch and rapidly re-route logic around permanent hardware faults. Achieves >99.99% accuracy recovery from soft errors with damage sizes exceeding training conditions. Shows generalization across circuit scales with improved accuracy on graphs substantially wider than training data.

Conclusion: Successfully bridges biological self-organisation principles with digital hardware, demonstrating adaptive fault tolerance that surpasses traditional static approaches through dynamic reconfiguration and generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Fault tolerance in classical computing has traditionally relied on static strategies like hardware redundancy and error-correcting codes. Biological systems, in contrast, exhibit adaptive plasticity, maintaining function through dynamic re-organisation around damage. Inspired by this principle, we introduce Self-Organising Digital Circuits, framing functional logic generation and maintenance as a meta-learning problem on graphs. Our architecture employs a topology-masked Transformer that configures the Lookup Tables (LUT) of a circuit's Boolean gates. Extending the pattern-generation paradigm of Neural Cellular Automata (NCA), it navigates the degenerate Boolean search space to satisfy a computational task, rather than regenerating a fixed target state. We demonstrate that it can self-assemble functional circuits from scratch and rapidly re-route logic around permanent, previously unseen hardware faults. For soft errors, the policy achieves near-perfect recovery (>99.99\% accuracy) from damage sizes far exceeding training conditions. We further observe generalisation across circuit scales: accuracy improves on graphs substantially wider than those seen during training. This work bridges the principles of biological self-organisation with the practical domain of digital hardware.

</details>


### [3] [Beyond the Hivemind: Escaping LLM Homogeneity via Meta-Persona Anchoring and Sequential Temperature Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02618)
*Tairan Fu,Javier Conde,Carlos Arriaga,Gonzalo Martínez,Pedro Reviriego,Javier Coronado-Blázquez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Meta-Persona Anchoring with Filtered Temperature Scaling to mitigate "Artificial Hivemind" effect in LLMs, reducing semantic convergence and increasing response diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent studies identified an "Artificial Hivemind" effect where LLMs converge on narrow, homogenized consensus even for open questions, limiting AI diversity with high inter-response similarity (≈0.80-0.90).

Method: Two-stage generation: 1) Meta-Persona Anchoring - model self-selects unique persona to anchor starting point; 2) Filtered Temperature Scaling (FTS) - dual-stage sampling with Top-p filtering for grammatical validity followed by extreme temperature scaling (T ≥ 4.0) on surviving candidates.

Result: Significant reduction in semantic convergence: average pairwise cosine similarity drops from ≈0.85 to ≈0.65, with majority of questions below 0.7 threshold, reducing gap between AI mode collapse and human-level diversity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively mitigates "Artificial Hivemind" effect, enabling more diverse and creative AI deployments; implementation provided as open-source.

Abstract: Recent studies have identified an ``Artificial Hivemind'' effect in Large Language Models (LLMs) causing models to converge on a narrow, homogenized consensus even for open questions. This semantic collapse limits the diversity of AI, resulting in high inter-response similarity ($\approx 0.80-0.90$) even under high-temperature sampling. In this paper, we propose a novel mitigation framework to increase diversity: Meta-Persona Anchoring combined with Filtered Temperature Scaling (FTS). Our approach utilizes a two-stage generation process: first, the model is prompted to self-select a unique, idiosyncratic persona to anchor its starting point; second, we apply a dual-stage sampling sieve, utilizing Top-$p$ filtering to preserve grammatical validity followed by extreme temperature scaling ($T \ge 4.0$) on the surviving candidates to explore the broadened probability distribution. We evaluate our method using the INFINITY-CHAT dataset on state-of-the-art open weight models under $\sim$20B parameters. Our results demonstrate a significant reduction in semantic convergence, with average pairwise cosine similarity dropping from ($\approx 0.85$) to ($\approx 0.65$). Our scheme achieves a majority of questions below the 0.7 threshold, effectively reducing the gap between artificial mode collapse and human-level typological diversity. We provide our implementation as an open-source framework to enable more diverse and creative AI deployments.

</details>


### [4] [PULSE: An Executable Contract Language for Spatiotemporal Knowledge Graph Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02630)
*Dongxu Yang,Ziyi Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PULSE is a novel knowledge graph engineering language that localizes operational roles and write effects in a single typed runtime, providing safety guarantees and trace parity for tested fragments while keeping superior usability outside its evaluation scope.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current knowledge graph engineering distributes state, observations, constraints, processes, and scenarios across artifacts with external combined execution contracts, creating coordination challenges and safety concerns.

Method: Developed PULSE, an Object-Process-Methodology-inspired language that localizes four operational roles and write effects in one typed runtime, implemented with core calculus for effect confinement and safety properties verified using Lean 4 formal verification.

Result: PULSE successfully matches separate workflows, distinguishes mutants, agrees with GEOS on hurricane data, maintains trace parity across 37,440 temporal traces, and achieves contract localization with safety arguments for the tested fragment.

Conclusion: PULSE supports contract localization, safety arguments, and trace parity for the tested knowledge graph engineering fragment, though language superiority and usability aspects remain outside the current evaluation scope.

Abstract: Knowledge graph engineering often distributes accepted state, observations, constraints, processes, and hypothetical scenarios across artifacts whose combined execution contract remains external. We present PULSE, an Object-Process-Methodology-inspired language that localizes four operational roles and their write effects in one typed runtime. Here, modes denote operational roles rather than modal or deontic logic. The implemented contract fixes evidence non-overwrite, branch isolation, grounded multi-subject timers, guarded state change, and declaration-ranked event ordering over time and space; an external runner still decides whether evidence becomes an authoritative move. GeoSPARQL, SOSA, and SHACL remain generated views. A core calculus gives an effect-confinement lemma and six safety properties. Lean 4 checks kernel analogues for positions, evidence, clocks, monitors, atomicity, and branch source retention; 88 tests, 3,534 bounded checks, and 32 Lean/Python runtime-kernel cases bound the implementation claim to the checked cases. First-author implementations of a standards composition and a separate Sismic statechart reproduce the tested cold-chain trace. Across 37,440 generated temporal traces, PULSE matches a separate workflow and distinguishes ten single-field mutants. On the complete NOAA IBTrACS since1980 subset it agrees with GEOS and an event sweep on 1,476,290 transition-zone pairs, including 4,800 sampled and 12,831 duration-qualified events. Project-specific GeoSPARQL probes measure interface coverage. Overall, the results support contract localization, safety arguments, and trace parity for the tested fragment; language superiority and usability remain outside the evaluation.

</details>


### [5] [HyperAgent: Planning and Acting over Tool-Schema Hypergraphs for Tool-Use LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02650)
*Zian Zhai,Xingyu Tan,Gaowang Zou,Xiaoyang Wang,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperAgent: A Tool-Schema Hypergraph-guided framework for dynamic planning and execution that improves LLM agent performance on complex real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents struggle with reliable tool-use planning due to implicit reasoning limitations and evolving real-world environments. Current approaches rely on LLMs inferring tool compositions from text, leading to inefficient exploration and unreliable execution in complex tasks.

Method: 1. Model tool relations at schema level using directed Tool-Schema Hypergraph (tools as hyperedges from input-schema nodes to output-schema nodes). 2. Extract task-relevant tool context graph to guide schema-aware Task DAG construction. 3. Dynamically realize subtasks by constructing state-conditioned tool support graphs through deficit-oriented expansion, identifying unresolved requirements and retrieving supporting producer tools.

Result: Experiments on AppWorld show HyperAgent improves task completion performance while reducing redundant API calls, LLM interactions, and token consumption compared to existing agent baselines.

Conclusion: HyperAgent provides an effective framework for reliable tool-use planning by leveraging schema-level tool relations and dynamic execution strategies, addressing key limitations of current LLM-based agent approaches.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external tools to complete complex real-world tasks. However, reliable tool-use planning remains challenging due to the limitations of implicit reasoning and the evolving nature of real-world execution environments. Existing tool-use agents typically rely on LLMs to infer tool compositions from textual descriptions, which can lead to inefficient exploration and unreliable execution in complex tasks. To address these challenges, we model tool relations at the schema level and construct a directed Tool--Schema Hypergraph, in which tools are represented as hyperedges from their required input-schema nodes to their output-schema nodes. Furthermore, we propose HyperAgent, a Tool--Schema Hypergraph-guided framework for dynamic planning and execution. Given a task, HyperAgent first extracts a task-relevant tool context graph and uses it to guide the construction of a schema-aware Task DAG. During execution, HyperAgent dynamically realizes each subtask by constructing a state-conditioned tool support graph through deficit-oriented expansion, which identifies unresolved requirements and retrieves supporting producer tools according to the current agent state. Experiments on AppWorld demonstrate that HyperAgent improves task completion performance while reducing redundant API calls, LLM interactions, and token consumption compared with existing agent baselines.

</details>


### [6] [Hybrid LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agents for Complex Sequential Decision Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03502)
*Christophe D. Hounwanou,John Emeka Eze,Yaé Ulrich Gaba*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agent that combines LLM planning with RL action optimization for improved performance in sequential decision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents struggle with long-horizon sequential decision tasks requiring precise action optimization, while RL lacks high-level abstraction and task decomposition abilities for complex scenarios. A hybrid approach is needed.

Method: Integrates LLM-driven planning with RL-based action optimization. LLM generates subgoals, structured plans, and contextual guidance; RL agent refines low-level actions through environment interaction.

Result: Experiments on sequential decision tasks show improved sample efficiency, higher success rates, and more coherent action trajectories compared to RL-only and LLM-only baselines.

Conclusion: This hybrid paradigm of LLM-augmented RL represents a promising direction for building more capable autonomous systems that combine high-level planning with precise low-level control.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown strong capabilities in reasoning, planning, and tool-use, enabling new forms of autonomous agents. However, LLM-based agents struggle with long-horizon sequential decision tasks that require precise action optimization and environment interaction. Reinforcement Learning (RL), while effective for sequential control, often lacks the high-level abstraction and task decomposition abilities needed for complex scenarios. This paper introduces an LLM-Augmented Reinforcement Learning Agent that integrates LLM-driven planning with RL-based action optimization. The proposed architecture leverages the LLM to generate subgoals, structured plans, and contextual guidance, while the RL agent refines low-level actions through interaction with the environment. Experiments on sequential decision tasks demonstrate improved sample efficiency, higher success rates, and more coherent action trajectories compared to RL-only and LLM-only baselines. This hybrid paradigm highlights a promising direction for building more capable autonomous systems.

</details>


### [7] [Explainable AI for the EU Right to Explanation: A Systematic Review of the Law-XAI Translation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02699)
*Benjamin Fresz,Elena Dubovitskaya,Marco F. Huber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic literature review reveals that current Explainable AI (XAI) research fails to adequately operationalize the EU Right to Explanation under GDPR and AI Act due to insufficient integration of legal and technical perspectives, problematic conceptual patterns, and lack of practical implementation frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EU grants individuals a Right to Explanation for algorithmic decisions affecting their lives (loan eligibility, hiring, healthcare), but it remains unclear whether and how XAI can practically satisfy this legal right, affecting individuals' ability to contest automated decisions.

Method: Systematic literature review of XAI papers (2024 onwards, after AI Act finalization) examining 57 full texts from 2643 initial records, with focus on GDPR Art. 15(1)(h) and AI Act Art. 86, identifying papers with substantive legal-technical integration.

Result: Only 19 of 57 papers showed substantive integration of legal and technical perspectives. The review identified three problematic patterns: misidentification of GDPR legal basis, lack of engagement with relevant CJEU judgments, and conflation of explanation form vs. content distinctions.

Conclusion: The Right to Explanation risks remaining formal without technically realizable compliance. The paper proposes an Addressee/Purpose Framework, a four-phase operationalization blueprint, and six open research questions to bridge the legal-technical gap.

Abstract: When algorithms make or influence consequential decisions---about loan eligibility, hiring, or healthcare---EU law grants affected individuals a Right to Explanation. Yet whether (and how) Explainable AI (XAI) can satisfy this right in practice remains poorly understood, with direct implications for individuals' ability to contest automated decisions that affect their lives. This paper presents a systematic literature review of XAI in the context of the EU Right to Explanation, with particular focus on Art. 15(1)(h) GDPR, Art. 86 AI Act (AIA), and related instruments. We consider papers published from 2024 onwards, as the final version of the AIA was published in July 2024---with Art. 86 being added late. From 2643 initial records identified by a deliberately broad search, we review 57 full texts, of which only 19 papers demonstrate substantive integration of both legal and technical perspectives, showing gaps in the interdisciplinary synthesis of the current regulatory framework. We document three problematic patterns across the corpus: Most misidentify the GDPR legal basis; few engage with the CJEU's Dun & Bradstreet judgment (likely due to publication timing); and the distinction between explanation form (governed by addressee) and content (governed by legal purpose) is often conflated. We conceptualize this as the Addressee/Purpose Framework, propose a four-phase blueprint for operationalization, and identify six concrete open research questions. Without further progress, the Right to Explanation risks remaining a formal obligation without a technically realizable path to compliance.

</details>


### [8] [Adversarial Fast-Moving Real-World Domains as Test Beds for Benchmarking AI Scientist Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03569)
*William Bolton,Philip Torr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new benchmarking framework using real-world competitive domains (Formula 1 and Magic: The Gathering) to evaluate AI scientists' ability to generate novel ideas, showing models can produce plausible outputs but struggle to match expert solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for evaluating AI scientists' ability to generate novel ideas rely on synthetic tasks or retrospective targets that may be confounded by prior exposure, making it difficult to properly assess true scientific creativity and reasoning.

Method: The authors propose using complex, adversarial, fast-moving real-world domains where experts independently generate observable outputs. They implement this framework in two structurally different domains: 1) Formula 1 (2026 season car design concepts), and 2) Magic: The Gathering (deck building with recently updated card pools). Models are evaluated against real-world expert solutions.

Result: In Formula 1, GPT-5.2 matched 10 of 40 real innovations with 166 ideas proposed. In Magic: The Gathering, the best deck recovered 5 of 7 new-set cards from a third-place Pro Tour deck, and card selection patterns correlated strongly with real expert choices (Spearman ρ=0.74, p=0.0003).

Conclusion: The key capability gap for AI scientists is not idea generation (models can produce plausible outputs) but rather filtering, prioritization, and coherent novelty - the ability to identify and refine the most promising ideas that align with expert solutions.

Abstract: Benchmarking the ability of AI scientists to generate novel ideas is notoriously difficult. Existing benchmarks in this field have made progress in evaluating scientific reasoning and research replication, but often rely on synthetic tasks or retrospective targets, which may be confounded by prior exposure. We hypothesize that complex, adversarial, fast-moving real-world domains where expert practitioners independently generate observable outputs can provide a practical solution to fill this gap and evaluate the capabilities needed for AI scientists, including reasoning, novelty, and hypothesis formulation. We instantiate this framework in two structurally different domains, Formula 1 (F1), where models ideate around car design concepts for the 2026 season, and real pre-season innovations provide a ground truth, and Magic: The Gathering (MTG), where models propose decks from a recently updated card pool and are evaluated against 19 Pro Tour (PT) decklists. Across both domains, models produce plausible outputs, but few align with real-world expert solutions. In F1, the best model, GPT-5.2 matched 10 of 40 real innovations with 166 ideas proposed across runs. In MTG, the best deck from Gemini 3 Flash recovered 5 of 7 new-set cards from the third-place PT deck, and across all 108 decks, the cards models selected most often were also the cards most widely adopted by PT decks (Spearman $ρ= 0.74$, $p = 0.0003$). These results suggest that a key capability gap for AI scientists is not idea generation, but filtering, prioritization, and coherent novelty.

</details>


### [9] [Predictive Set Theory: A Generative Framework for Cognitive Architecture with Operationalized Core Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02704)
*Yiyang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Predictive Set Theory (PST) is a formal generative framework that reconstructs cognitive architecture from first principles using set theory, addressing gaps in predictive processing and Bayesian cognitive science theories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing predictive processing theories lack operational definitions for predictions and prediction errors, while Bayesian cognitive science presupposes closed hypothesis spaces without explaining how discrete cognitive objects emerge. There's a need for a foundational framework that addresses internal consistency maintenance under uncertainty and irreversible risk.

Method: PST establishes a minimal set of operations: a sensor as identity function, set-theoretic state refresh, and three fundamental reference chains (reference, counter-reference, semi-reference). From these first principles, it derives core cognitive functions including state sequences, demand, comparison, efficiency, and finite-horizon probabilistic planning.

Result: The framework provides a design specification for systems needing internal consistency under incomplete information, resolves classical problems like Russell's paradox and Gödelian incompleteness, and addresses practical applications like understanding film editing and grounding negative feedback.

Conclusion: PST offers a complete, original generative framework that establishes cognition on rigorous mathematical foundations, providing a blueprint for any system that must maintain coherence while acting under uncertainty and irreversible risk.

Abstract: Predictive processing theories portray the brain as a hierarchical prediction engine that minimizes prediction error, yet they lack operational definitions for the structure of a "prediction," the standardized response to a prediction error, and the mechanism that maintains consistency across successive updates. Bayesian cognitive science attempts to subsume all uncertainty under probabilistic belief updating, but it presupposes a closed hypothesis space and provides no generative account of how the objects over which probabilities are distributed become discrete, identifiable referents in the first place. This paper introduces Predictive Set Theory (PST), a formal generative framework that reconstructs cognitive architecture from first principles. PST anchors cognition in a minimal set of operations---a sensor formalized as an identity function, set-theoretic state refresh, and three fundamental forms of reference chains (reference, counter-reference, and semi-reference)---and rigorously derives core cognitive functions including state sequences, demand, comparison, efficiency, and finite-horizon probabilistic planning. Rather than modeling neural mechanisms, PST constitutes a design specification for any system that must maintain internal consistency while acting under incomplete information and irreversible risk. The framework offers novel resolutions to classical problems such as Russell's paradox, the cognitive status of Gödelian incompleteness, the grounding of negative feedback, and the comprehension of film editing. The primary purpose of this paper is to establish, through the public academic record, the originality and completeness of the Predictive Set Theory framework.

</details>


### [10] [Is Inter-Seed Cross-Play Enough? Evaluating the Robustness of Zero-Shot Coordination Algorithms to Implementation Details](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03644)
*Maksymilian Wolski,Nicholas Hoernle,Johannes Forkel,Jakob Foerster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation shows that standard zero-shot coordination evaluation methods are surprisingly robust proxies for more thorough cross-implementation testing when applied to the Other-Play algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current zero-shot coordination (ZSC) algorithms are typically evaluated using only a single implementation across random seeds, leaving uncertainties about their robustness to specification ambiguities and implementation variations that would naturally arise when independent parties implement the same algorithm.

Method: The authors introduce a new evaluation scheme called "cross-implementation cross-play" that systematically varies implementation details known to affect multi-agent reinforcement learning performance, and apply this scheme to evaluate the popular Other-Play algorithm.

Result: The findings suggest that for Other-Play, standard ZSC evaluation methods (single implementation across random seeds) actually serve as reasonable proxies for the more thorough cross-implementation evaluation, indicating the algorithm's robustness to implementation variations.

Conclusion: While standard evaluation methods for ZSC algorithms appear to work well for Other-Play, the introduced cross-implementation cross-play scheme provides a more rigorous evaluation framework that should be adopted for future ZSC algorithm development to ensure robustness to real-world implementation variations.

Abstract: AI agents deployed in real-world settings must be capable of coordinating with humans and other AI agents they have not encountered before. Zero-shot coordination (ZSC) algorithms aim to achieve this by specifying high-level learning rules such that independently engineered agents can coordinate with each other at test time. Rigorous evaluation of ZSC algorithms remains difficult: ideally, multiple independent implementations of each proposed algorithm must be used, reflecting the variation that arises when independent parties interpret and implement the same specification. In practice, however, ZSC algorithms have almost exclusively been evaluated using a single implementation trained across different random seeds, with only a handful of works additionally varying the neural network architecture. This leaves open questions about robustness to specification ambiguities and implementation details. In this work, we provide the first systematic evaluation of this robustness. We introduce a new evaluation scheme, cross-implementation cross-play, varying implementation details that prior work has shown to affect the performance of multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms, and we evaluate Other-Play, a popular ZSC algorithm, with this scheme. Our findings are encouraging and suggest that, for Other-Play, the standard ZSC evaluation is, in fact, a reasonable proxy for this more thorough cross-implementation evaluation.

</details>


### [11] [Towards a new paradigm of scientific discovery with socialized artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02775)
*Xinjie Yao,Xingxin Xu,Xiyuan Gao,Zhoupeng Guo,Kunlong Yang,Dengyu Zhao,Siqi Zhao,Zhihe Fan,Yichen Dong,Xin Li,Jiekang Feng,Jiahe Wu,Sen Wang,Beiming Yu,Kejia Zhao,Ruipu Zhao,Jiaqi Zhou,Heyang Li,Jianjun Chen,Anbo Dai,Xin Liu,Zhengtao Yu,Qinghua Hu,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BLAZE is a new paradigm that organizes AI as infrastructure for scientific discovery, connecting knowledge, reasoning, experiments, and human judgment into a continuous, cumulative research lifecycle rather than just assisting isolated tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Science faces a new challenge: organizing expanding knowledge, reasoning, and evidence into coherent discovery processes rather than just producing more information. Current fragmented approaches lack systematic integration of knowledge, hypotheses, experiments, and verification.

Method: Introduces Bridging Literature, Agents, and Zero-gap Experimentation (BLAZE) as a paradigm of socialized scientific intelligence. BLAZE organizes AI as infrastructure connecting persistent knowledge, collective reasoning, empirical validation, and human judgment within a continuous research lifecycle.

Result: BLAZE transforms fragmented scientific activities into a cumulative process of inquiry, criticism, and revision, making discovery more traceable, reproducible, and cumulative while preserving human creativity and responsibility.

Conclusion: Socialized scientific intelligence provides a foundation for next-era science, extending collective scientific inquiry's scale, depth, and continuity without replacing human discovery.

Abstract: Scientific discovery has advanced through successive transformations in the organization of knowledge. Observation and experimentation established the empirical foundations of science. Theory made it possible to derive general principles from particular phenomena. Computation extended inquiry into systems beyond direct observation, while data-intensive methods opened new spaces of pattern and prediction. Science now confronts a different frontier. The central challenge is no longer simply to produce more information, but to organize expanding knowledge, reasoning, and evidence into a coherent process of discovery. Here, we introduce Bridging Literature, Agents, and Zero-gap Experimentation (BLAZE), a paradigm of socialized scientific intelligence. BLAZE conceives AI not as an assistant for isolated research tasks, but as an organizational infrastructure for scientific discovery. It connects persistent knowledge, collective reasoning, empirical validation, and human judgment within a continuous research lifecycle, transforming fragmented activities into a cumulative process of inquiry, criticism, and revision. The central premise of BLAZE is that scientific intelligence does not arise from computation alone. It emerges from the sustained interaction among knowledge, hypotheses, experiments, and collective verification. By organizing humans and machines within a shared scientific process, BLAZE makes discovery more traceable, reproducible, and cumulative while preserving human creativity, judgment, and responsibility. Socialized scientific intelligence may provide a foundation for the next era of science. Its purpose is not to replace human discovery, but to extend the scale, depth, and continuity of collective scientific inquiry.

</details>


### [12] [BAP-SQL: Budget-Aware Observation Planning for Agentic Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02876)
*Chong Peng,Pin Qian,Su Wang,Yihang Chen,Varun Sah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BAP-SQL is a new approach for agentic text-to-SQL that treats observation formation as a budget-control problem, using risk estimation, SQL rewriting, and runtime shielding to improve performance under tight token budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tool-using agents in text-to-SQL waste context and database work on broad queries before useful evidence appears. Post-hoc compression cannot recover omitted rows or expended work, leading to inefficient resource usage under constrained budgets.

Method: BAP-SQL introduces observation formation as a budget-control stage with three components: 1) estimating query risk, 2) rewriting SQL when useful, and 3) delegating hard limits to an independent runtime shield. The approach integrates budget-aware planning directly into the agent's policy.

Result: Across 4B and 7B model backbones, BAP-SQL improves tight-budget success rates. On the primary BIRD-derived benchmark, it gains 3.4-3.6 percentage points over standard supervised fine-tuning while using 4.5-5.0% fewer tokens. Benefits come from policy-visible planning and budget-sensitive rescue mechanisms.

Conclusion: BAP-SQL effectively addresses budget constraints in agentic text-to-SQL through proactive observation control. However, benefits attenuate with higher model capabilities and larger budgets, and the approach doesn't reduce database work itself, suggesting it's primarily valuable for token-limited scenarios.

Abstract: Tool-using agents do not merely consume observations: their actions determine what arrives next. In agentic text-to-SQL, a broad query can spend context and database work before useful evidence appears, while post-hoc compression cannot recover omitted rows or expended work. We present BAP-SQL, which treats observation formation as a budget-control stage: it estimates query risk, rewrites SQL when useful, and delegates hard limits to an independent runtime shield. Across general 4B, specialized FINER-SQL 4B, and 7B backbones, BAP-SQL improves tight-budget success. On the primary BIRD-derived setting, it gains 3.4/3.6 percentage points over matched SFT while using 4.5/5.0% fewer tokens. Matched retraining and task-level transfer associate the gain with policy-visible planning and budget-sensitive rescue. The benefit attenuates as model capability and budget increase, reverses at the loosest setting, and does not reduce database work.

</details>


### [13] [VeriTrace: Human-Like Temporal Exploration Completes Agentic Action Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02878)
*Yu-Tung Liu,Cunxi Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeriTrace is a multi-agent system with Agentic Temporal Exploration that achieves 100% Pass@1 on VerilogEval-V2 by enabling hypothesis-driven root-cause debugging through complete signal and time-window control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems for Verilog RTL generation plateau at ~95% accuracy due to incomplete debugging action spaces that restrict signal inspection and time-window queries, limiting debugging to pattern matching rather than proper root-cause analysis.

Method: VeriTrace introduces a multi-agent system with an Inspector agent operating over a complete debugging action space, featuring independent control over signal selection, time-window bounds, and iteration depth. This Agentic Temporal Exploration enables hypothesis formation, evidence querying, and iterative refinement like human verification engineers.

Result: VeriTrace achieves 100% Pass@1 on VerilogEval-V2, the first system to attain perfect functional correctness on this benchmark. On a shared Claude Sonnet 4.0 backbone, it outperforms the strongest reproduced baseline by +5.1%.

Conclusion: Debugging agency through complete action space control (Agentic Temporal Exploration) closes the final accuracy gap in automated Verilog RTL generation, enabling human-like exploratory debugging and achieving state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: Large language models have shown promise for automated Verilog RTL generation, yet state-of-the-art multi-agent systems plateau at ~95% accuracy on standard benchmarks. We trace this ceiling to an incomplete debugging action space: existing systems restrict which signals the agent can inspect, which time windows it can query, or both, reducing debugging to pattern matching on a narrow, predetermined view of circuit behavior rather than hypothesis-driven root-cause analysis. We present VeriTrace, a multi-agent system whose Inspector agent operates over a complete debugging action space, with independent control over signal selection, time-window bounds, and iteration depth. This capability, which we term Agentic Temporal Exploration, enables the agent to form hypotheses about failure causes, query the waveform for evidence, and refine its understanding iteratively, mirroring the exploratory process of human verification engineers. VeriTrace achieves 100\% Pass@1 on VerilogEval-V2, the first system to attain perfect functional correctness on this benchmark. On a shared Claude Sonnet 4.0 backbone, VeriTrace outperforms the strongest reproduced baseline by +5.1%, demonstrating that debugging agency closes the final accuracy gap.

</details>


### [14] [Interpreting Black-Box Large Language Models with Sentence-Level Energy Landscapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02879)
*Maryam Rezaee,Pooriya Safaei,Maryam Asgarinezhad,Fatemeh Seyyedsalehi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a model-agnostic sentence-level attribution interpreter for closed API LLMs using Energy-Based Models to identify influential prompt sentences without further API queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The widespread use of proprietary LLMs through closed APIs creates a critical challenge for responsible deployment due to lack of interpretability and transparency in how these models generate outputs.

Method: Uses Energy-Based Model as a surrogate to capture the LLM's internal conceptual consistency between prompts and responses. The energy landscape guides training of a lightweight interpreter network that operates as standalone tool without needing additional API queries after training.

Result: Experiments show the EBM accurately simulates target LLMs, and the interpreter effectively identifies the most influential prompt sentences for generating specific target outputs. The framework captures broader generation patterns and mitigates instance-specific biases.

Conclusion: The proposed model-agnostic, post-hoc attribution interpreter provides an effective solution for interpretability of closed API LLMs, enabling users to understand which prompt sentences influence specific outputs without ongoing access to the LLM API.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of proprietary Large Language Models (LLMs) accessed strictly through closed APIs has created a critical challenge for responsible deployment: a fundamental lack of interpretability. To address this, we propose a model-agnostic, post-hoc attribution interpreter operating at the sentence level. Our approach trains an Energy-Based Model (EBM) as a surrogate to capture the LLM's internal conceptual consistency between prompts and responses. This energy landscape guides the training of a lightweight interpreter network. Uniquely, our interpreter operates as a standalone tool; once trained, it quantifies the influence of prompt sentences on a user-specified target output without requiring further API queries to the LLM. By globally training a local interpreter across diverse inputs, our framework captures broader generation patterns and mitigates instance-specific biases. Experiments demonstrate that our EBM accurately simulates the target LLM, allowing the interpreter to effectively identify the prompt sentences most influential in generating specific target outputs.

</details>


### [15] [Hypercubes, Hyperplanes, and Constraint-Induced Complexity Collapse in Atomic Concept Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02930)
*Irene Tsapara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Atomic concept learning complexity is localized to specific hyperplanes in high-dimensional instance spaces, not uniformly distributed. Full diagonals show unbounded complexity while other hyperplanes collapse into finite equivalence classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand where complexity is actually concentrated in higher-arity atomic concept learning, moving beyond the assumption that complexity is uniformly distributed throughout the instance space.

Method: Geometric analysis using hypercubes and hyperplanes of ground instances, with canonical simple concepts, minimal orderings, and representative reductions. Includes case studies of binary and ternary hypercubes to illustrate the reduction machinery.

Result: Identifies an asymmetry in the hypercube structure where most hyperplanes collapse into finitely many elementary-equivalence classes (bounded complexity), while the full diagonal exhibits unbounded growth in class count. Complexity is localized rather than spread uniformly.

Conclusion: The geometric-logical perspective reveals that complexity in atomic concept learning is concentrated in specific structures (particularly full diagonals), providing insights for constrained hypothesis spaces and structured classification approaches.

Abstract: We revisit higher-arity atomic concept learning through the geometry of hypercubes and hyperplanes of ground instances. Our starting point is the observation that the ambient r-dimensional hypercube of ground atoms is not structurally uniform. Its logical complexity is organized by hyperplanes: every hyperplane other than the full diagonal collapses into finitely many elementary-equivalence classes, with a bound independent of the term depth, while the full diagonal is exceptional and its class count grows without bound. This asymmetry is not merely geometric. It reflects the reduction-theoretic structure of the concepts themselves. Building on a higher-dimensional framework developed in the author's earlier work, we reinterpret these results through canonical simple concepts, minimal orderings, and representative reductions. This yields a taxonomy of hyperplane behavior in higher dimensions and shows that complexity is localized rather than spread uniformly through the instance space. The paper includes a fully worked binary case, an explicit treatment of the ternary hypercube, and an unpacked account of the reduction machinery that drives the collapse. The three-dimensional case already exhibits the essential phenomenon of orthogonal families, partial diagonals, and the exceptional full diagonal. This geometric-logical perspective clarifies where complexity is concentrated in atomic concept learning and suggests a modern interpretation in terms of constrained hypothesis spaces and structured classification.

</details>


### [16] [When Compression Scores Cannot Decide: Information Boundaries for Group-Robust LLM Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02940)
*Andrew Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a framework for model compression and pruning where even reliable statistics can select suboptimal endpoints, proposing information interfaces and group-aware allocation methods that improve performance over standard approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the problem where seemingly reliable compression statistics can still lead to selecting wrong candidates or suboptimal model endpoints, despite having high split-half reliability scores. This gap between statistical reliability and actual performance needs to be understood and bridged.

Method: The paper introduces information interfaces to model the gap between statistics and performance, develops mathematical models including a conic law for pooling prices with positive linear fixed-candidate damage, uses three two-world constructions and observation-fiber radius analysis, implements group-resolved diagonal approaches, and applies model-specific complete-mask endpoint selection with local measurements for candidate construction.

Result: Results show that dense pruning scores with 0.906 split-half reliability predicted 16.1% gain but selected endpoints were 6.0-7.7% worse than controls. Group-resolved diagonal methods recovered broad damage order (Spearman 0.9239) while fine order remained weak. Coarse depth allocation reduced worst-group perplexity inflation by 12.6-20.9% across three dense LLMs, and model-specific endpoint selection improved by 2.7-8.0% over references. In OLMoE, router traces predicted singleton direction (114/192 vs 81/192), and finite-menu decisions on one layer yielded 13.7% and 7.2% held-out worst-group KL reductions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that selection should be licensed by complete candidate endpoints or validated uniform guarantees, with uncertainty calibrated to every comparison. The proposed information interface framework and group-aware allocation methods provide better approaches for model compression and pruning than relying solely on reliable statistics, leading to improved performance across various LLM architectures.

Abstract: A reproducible compression statistic can still select the wrong candidate. A dense pruning score with 0.906 split-half reliability predicted a 16.1% gain. Its selected endpoint was 6.0% and 7.7% worse than two controls. We model the gap through information interfaces that delimit which distinctions each statistic supports. For equal-weight groups, a conic law gives the exact pooling price for positive linear fixed-candidate damage, including diagonal and full PSD second moments. Three two-world constructions and an exact observation-fiber radius characterize what pooled moments, group-local moments, and reference-path curvature leave unresolved. A group-resolved diagonal recovers broad damage order (Spearman 0.9239) while fine order remains weak. Relative to balanced uniform allocation, a coarse depth allocation cuts worst-group perplexity inflation by 12.6--20.9% across three dense LLMs. Model-specific complete-mask endpoint selection improves over those references by 2.7--8.0%. In OLMoE, router traces predict singleton direction (114/192 versus 81/192 under the strongest relabeling). Finite-menu decisions on one layer yield held-out worst-group KL reductions of 13.7% and 7.2%. Local measurements construct candidates. Selection is licensed by complete candidate endpoints or a validated uniform guarantee, with uncertainty calibrated to every comparison.

</details>


### [17] [On the missing data layer and a potential solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02949)
*Francis F Daniel,Mauro Ibañez,Francis Perelman,Marian Basti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes DataHub, a task-first data infrastructure to address Latin America's missing AI dataset layer by organizing datasets through task/domain/language ontology with discovery, metadata, and contribution mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latin America lacks foundational AI infrastructure layers: dataset and benchmark layers. Existing AI datasets are scattered across platforms without shared indexing, and even with perfect indexing, the total volume is insufficient for frontier AI development requirements.

Method: Proposes DataHub: a task-first data infrastructure organized through ontology structure of /<task?>/<domain?>/<language?>, with mechanisms for dataset discovery, metadata management, contribution, licensing, and reuse.

Result: The paper presents the DataHub infrastructure design to specifically address the dataset layer challenges in Latin America, focusing on solving discovery and supply problems through systematic organization and contribution mechanisms.

Conclusion: A specialized infrastructure like DataHub is needed to address Latin America's AI dataset gap by creating organized, discoverable, and scalable dataset repositories through task-oriented ontology and contribution mechanisms.

Abstract: Latin America is missing two foundational layers of AI infrastructure: the dataset layer and the benchmark layer. This paper targets the dataset layer. The dataset layer faces two compounding problems: discovery and supply. Latin American AI datasets exist but are scattered across platforms with no shared index. Even with perfect indexing, the total volume would remain far below what frontier AI development requires. We propose DataHub: a task-first data infrastructure organized through the ontology /<task?>/<domain?>/<language?>, with mechanisms for dataset discovery, metadata, contribution, licensing, and reuse.

</details>


### [18] [Neurosymbolic Reasoning with Incremental Knowledge for Sample Efficient Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02993)
*Subrat Prasad Panda,Blaise Genest,Arvind Easwaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes neurosymbolic hierarchical RL with incremental knowledge (InK) where symbolic components plan on updatable knowledge and neural modules learn motion primitives, improving sample efficiency in sparse-reward navigation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat RL agents struggle with sparse rewards requiring long-horizon reasoning. Standard hierarchical RL encodes knowledge in fixed forms that can't be updated during learning, making incremental knowledge reasoning impractical and leading to poor sample efficiency.

Method: Neurosymbolic hierarchical RL with Incremental Knowledge (InK): symbolic high-level components perform symbolic planning (e.g., using D*) on updatable knowledge representations, while low-level goal-conditioned neural modules learn motion primitives using reward shaping. Also develops Belief World Tree Search for optimal symbolic planning with prior knowledge.

Result: Experiments on navigation tasks show that incorporating incremental knowledge substantially improves sample efficiency compared to standard approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed neurosymbolic HRL framework with incremental knowledge effectively combines symbolic planning with neural learning, overcoming limitations of fixed hierarchical RL and improving performance in sparse-reward environments.

Abstract: (Flat) Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents face significant challenges in environments with sparse rewards that require long-horizon reasoning. A compelling approach to improve sample efficiency is to incorporate knowledge into learning and decision-making. In standard Hierarchical RL (HRL), knowledge is encoded in a fixed, non-updatable form, such as architectural choices, and remains unchanged throughout learning. With fixed HRL, reasoning with incremental knowledge learned during exploration is impractical before sufficient environmental knowledge is acquired, leading to poor sample efficiency. In this work, we propose neurosymbolic HRL with {\em Incremental Knowledge (InK)}: symbolic high-level components perform {\em symbolic planning} (e.g. using $D^*$) on an updatable representation of current InK, while low-level goal-conditioned neural modules learn motion primitives through experience using reward shaping. Experiments on navigation tasks demonstrate that incorporating InK substantially improves sample efficiency. Additionally, to perform {\em optimal} symbolic planning given {\em prior} knowledge about the world, we develop Belief World Tree Search. The code is available at https://github.com/CPS-research-group/ink_bwts.

</details>


### [19] [On the missing benchmarks layer and a potential solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02996)
*Francis F Daniel,Mauro Ibañez,Francis Perelman,Marian Basti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Latin America lacks AI benchmarking infrastructure, so researchers propose creating LatamBoard as the first regional instance of an EvalsHub platform to enable local AI evaluation and optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latin America lacks a foundational benchmark layer for native AI development, which prevents public institutions from independently evaluating foreign AI systems and companies from optimizing AI for local problems with state-of-the-art performance.

Method: Propose creating an EvalsHub platform with LatamBoard as its first regional instance - an open, task-first benchmark infrastructure where various stakeholders can publish, execute, compare, and maintain evaluations across AI systems.

Result: The paper introduces a conceptual framework for an open benchmark infrastructure designed to be used by universities, public institutions, professional communities, and companies to evaluate AI systems against regional requirements.

Conclusion: Establishing regional benchmark infrastructure like LatamBoard is essential for AI auditability and optimization direction in Latin America, representing critical infrastructure for the region's AI development.

Abstract: Latin America is missing a foundational layer for native AI development: the benchmark layer. The benchmark layer does two things no other layer can - it audits AI systems against regional social requirements and it directs AI optimization in economically relevant environments. Without it, public institutions cannot independently evaluate foreign AI systems, and companies cannot optimize AI systems to solve local problems with SOTA performance. The cost of the missing layer is dual: a loss of auditability and a loss of optimization direction over a technology that is increasingly critical infrastructure. We propose an EvalsHub, with LatamBoard as its first regional instance - an open, task-first benchmark infrastructure where universities, public institutions, professional communities, and companies can publish, execute, compare, and maintain evaluations across models, workflows, and agents. Built once, measured forever - re-run by institutions as new AI systems ship and by industry teams after every system change. Open by design and incentive-driven by construction.

</details>


### [20] [ProPRL: Property-Aware Prerequisite Relation Learning in Educational Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03006)
*Xinghe Cheng,Jiapu Wang,Chaobo He,Ruihai Dong,Quanlong Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProPRL is a framework for learning prerequisite relations in education that adaptively fuses complementary evidence from different sources while enforcing anti-symmetry constraints to avoid contradictory predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prerequisite relation learning methods treat it as conventional link prediction, which limits their ability to adaptively integrate complementary educational evidence for individual concept pairs and fails to prevent contradictory reverse predictions (predicting both A→B and B→A as prerequisites).

Method: ProPRL learns complementary concept representations from two graphs: a concept-resource hypergraph and a directed learning-behavior graph using direction-preserving personalized propagation. It then uses a Pair-conditioned Gate to adaptively weight and fuse the two views for each candidate concept pair. Finally, it applies an Irreversibility Constraint as an anti-symmetry regularizer that penalizes high confidence in both directions of the same concept pair.

Result: Experiments on multiple real-world educational datasets demonstrate that ProPRL achieves state-of-the-art performance on prerequisite relation learning tasks.

Conclusion: ProPRL effectively addresses limitations of existing methods by adaptively fusing complementary evidence and enforcing anti-symmetry constraints, making it a promising framework for adaptive instruction and prerequisite relation learning.

Abstract: Prerequisite relation learning is central to adaptive instruction, yet existing methods often formulate it as conventional link prediction, limiting their ability to adaptively integrate complementary educational evidence for individual candidate pairs and to discourage contradictory reverse predictions. We propose ProPRL, a Property-aware Prerequisite Relation Learning framework. ProPRL first learns complementary concept representations from a concept-resource hypergraph and a directed learning-behavior graph, where direction-preserving personalized propagation aggregates multi-hop behavioral evidence. It then employs a Pair-conditioned Gate to adaptively weight and fuse the two views for each candidate ordered concept pair. Finally, an \textit{Irreversibility Constraint} introduces an anti-symmetry regularizer that penalizes simultaneously high confidence in both directions of the same concept pair. Experiments on multiple real-world educational datasets show that ProPRL achieves state-of-the-art performance on prerequisite relation learning.

</details>


### [21] [UrbanAgent: A Tool-Augmented Agent for Cross-System Urban Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03018)
*Jiayu Cao,Xingyuan Zeng,feiyu Li,Zhijing Huang,Xujie Yuan,Rongxiang Chen,Shimin Di,Libin Zheng,Jian Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Urban-Agent is a tool-augmented agent framework that combines LLM reasoning with code execution, API calls, and Model Context Protocol to handle cross-system urban tasks through an adaptive closed loop, achieving 71% task success rate (10 points above baselines).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite increasing digital services in cities, residents still face difficulty meeting daily needs due to fragmented services with poor interoperability, creating heavy operational burdens. Existing solutions (digital platforms, urban foundation models, intelligent assistants) only address isolated aspects and struggle to reliably convert complex natural-language requests into executable cross-system workflows.

Method: Urban-Agent couples LLM cognitive/reasoning capabilities with a tool-set supporting code execution, API calls, and Model Context Protocol. It uses an adaptive closed loop that: 1) clarifies missing information before acting, 2) grounds tool use in live observations, and 3) aligns final responses with observed evidence and task constraints.

Result: Urban-Agent achieves 71% task success rate, 10 points above the strongest baseline. This performance lead is consistent across multiple LLMs (GPT-5-mini, Gemini-2.5-flash, DeepSeek-V4-flash, Qwen3-235B-A22B). The paper also introduces Urban-Eval benchmark for evaluating cross-system urban tasks, assessing both task results and execution quality including tool coverage, dependency validity, and evidence traceability.

Conclusion: Urban-Agent effectively addresses the challenge of converting complex natural-language urban requests into executable cross-system workflows, demonstrating significant performance improvements over existing approaches and providing a comprehensive evaluation framework through Urban-Eval benchmark.

Abstract: Modern cities rely on an increasing number of digital services to operate, but residents' daily needs are still difficult to meet. Services are fragmented and have little interoperability, placing a heavy operational burden on users. Existing digital platforms, urban foundation models, and intelligent assistants each address only isolated aspects of an urban task. But they struggle to reliably convert complex natural-language requests into executable cross-system workflows. We propose Urban-Agent, a tool-augmented agent framework for cross-system urban tasks. It couples the cognitive and reasoning capabilities of a large language model with a tool-set supporting code execution, API calls, and Model Context Protocol. Through one adaptive closed loop, it clarifies missing information before acting, grounds tool use in live observations, and aligns the final response with observed evidence and task constraints. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce Urban-Eval, a benchmark specifically designed for cross-system urban request. Unlike prior benchmarks that assess either general tool use or urban knowledge and reasoning, Urban-Eval evaluates both task results and execution quality, including required tool coverage, dependency validity, and evidence traceability. Experimental results indicate that Urban-Agent reaches a 71% task success rate, 10 points above the strongest baseline. This lead holds across GPT-5-mini, Gemini-2.5-flash, DeepSeek-V4-flash, and Qwen3-235B-A22B.

</details>


### [22] [LoCA: Forward-Only LLM Tuning after One-Shot Calibration with Local Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03020)
*Linhan Xia,Rui Liu,Zhaofeng Zhang,Yihao Wang,Binrui Shen,Shengxin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoCA replaces repeated backpropagation with one-time calibration for parameter-efficient adaptation, using local hidden-state corrections to fit low-rank adapters via closed-form ridge solves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parameter-efficient post-training methods like LoRA still require repeated end-to-end backpropagation through frozen backbones, needing backward-capable hardware and storing/recomputing activations for every adaptation step. This is impractical when repeated backpropagation is not feasible.

Method: Two-stage method: 1) One probe backward pass fits low-rank maps at each transformer block from final prediction error to local hidden-state corrections, 2) These maps are reused to form blockwise regression targets from forward activations and fit low-rank adapters using closed-form ridge solves without further backbone backward passes.

Result: LoCA outperforms LoRA in 16 of 25 task-scale comparisons on Qwen2.5 models (0.5B-14B), reduces GPU peak memory by 26-29%, reduces CPU steady-state memory by 36-52% after calibration, and reduces per-pass time by 43-48%. The approach works across different model sizes and architectures.

Conclusion: LoCA successfully amortizes global credit assignment into one calibration, enabling forward-only tuning when repeated backpropagation is impractical, making adaptation more resource-efficient while maintaining or improving performance compared to existing parameter-efficient methods.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient post-training reduces the number of trainable parameters, but still requires repeated end-to-end backpropagation through the frozen backbone. Every adaptation step therefore needs backward-capable hardware and must store or recompute activations. We ask whether this repeated backward chain can be replaced by a one-time calibration. We introduce Local Credit Assignment (LoCA), a two-stage method for small-shift adaptation. One probe backward pass fits a low-rank map at each transformer block from the final prediction error to a local hidden-state correction. LoCA then reuses these maps to form blockwise regression targets from forward activations and fits low-rank adapters with closed-form ridge solves. No further backbone backward pass is required. We evaluate LoCA on five discriminative benchmarks with Qwen2.5 models from 0.5B to 14B. In 16 of 25 reported task--scale comparisons, LoCA yields lower evaluation cross-entropy than the corresponding LoRA run. Its measured full-run GPU peak, including calibration, is 26--29\% lower than LoRA's. After calibration, its CPU steady-state memory is 36--52\% lower and its per-pass time is 43--48\% lower. A shared scale-normalized candidate set is reused across all tested Qwen2.5 sizes and on SmolLM2-1.7B. LoCA thus amortizes global credit assignment into one calibration and enables later forward-only tuning when repeated backpropagation is impractical. The code associated with this paper is available \href{https://github.com/Xia12121/LoCA}{here}.

</details>


### [23] [DiffImaginE: Imagine to Verify Entity Types with Diffusio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03025)
*Feng Zhang,Feiyu Han,Rongxin Yang,Yang Liu,Yancheng Chen,Rui Wang,Yingguang Yang,Tian Xueyun,Chongyang Zhang,Hao Zheng,Xu Kefu,Congjing Ran,Fuhai Chen,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiffImaginE improves multimodal named entity recognition by replacing deterministic visual verification with a diffusion-based probabilistic scorer that ranks entity types based on how well they explain observed visual evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal NER systems use deterministic "imagine-and-compare" verifiers that compress diverse visual realizations into single prototypes without explicit probabilistic semantics, limiting their ability to properly rank entity type hypotheses based on visual evidence.

Method: DiffImaginE formulates MNER type verification as conditional latent diffusion inference. It uses a type-conditioned denoiser to predict noise in standardized latent representations, with denoising error providing an ELBO-consistent surrogate for type-conditional negative log-likelihood. The approach retains standard multimodal encoders but replaces deterministic verifiers with classifier-free-guided diffusion scoring trained using Min-SNR weighting, with antithetic sampling to reduce variance.

Result: Experiments on Twitter-2015 and Twitter-2017 show consistent gains over matched deterministic ImaginE controls under identical encoder, auxiliary objectives, and evaluation protocols. The method demonstrates improved performance with statistical significance.

Conclusion: DiffImaginE provides a probabilistic alternative to deterministic verification for multimodal NER, enabling better ranking of entity type hypotheses through diffusion-based scoring that quantifies how well different types explain observed visual evidence.

Abstract: Multimodal named entity recognition (MNER) determines whether each candidate span and entity-type hypothesis is supported by joint textual and visual evidence. Existing imagine-and-compare verifiers map each (span, type) pair to one predicted visual feature, compressing diverse visual realisations into a single prototype and providing a compatibility score without explicit probabilistic semantics. We introduce DiffImaginE, which formulates MNER type verification as conditional latent diffusion inference. Given span-localised visual evidence, a type-conditioned denoiser predicts noise injected into its standardised latent. The resulting denoising error provides an ELBO-consistent surrogate for type-conditional negative log-likelihood, allowing competing type hypotheses to be ranked by how well they explain the observation. DiffImaginE retains a standard multimodal encoder stack and replaces the deterministic verifier with a classifier-free-guided diffusion scorer trained using Min-SNR weighting. We directly supervise per-type diffusion scores as classification logits, learn aggregation across noise levels, and use antithetic sampling to reduce Monte Carlo comparison variance. Our analysis shows that classifier-free guidance sharpens the induced type posterior and characterises when antithetic pairing reduces variance at equal denoiser cost. Experiments on Twitter-2015 and Twitter-2017 show consistent gains over a matched deterministic ImaginE control under the same encoder, auxiliary objectives, and evaluation protocol, supported by ablations and paired significance tests.

</details>


### [24] [Evaluating Counterfactual Sensitivity to Patient Information in Medication-Safety Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03028)
*Zhitian Hou,Yuhang Liu,Pengkai Wang,Zeyu Liu,Guanghao Zhu,Zheng Liu,Shuo Cai,Congkai Xie,Zhijie Sang,Kun Zeng,Hongxia Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedPIC-Bench is a new benchmark that evaluates LLMs on patient-specific medication-safety reasoning by testing their ability to adjust recommendations when patient conditions change, revealing models' limitations in applying conditional safety rules correctly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical evaluations use isolated, fixed scenarios that allow models to answer correctly by simply recalling drug-risk associations without demonstrating they actually use patient information to determine whether safety rules apply. This creates a gap in assessing whether models can perform true patient-specific medication-safety reasoning.

Method: The researchers created MedPIC-Bench, a benchmark containing 467 expert-validated questions annotated along six clinical and reasoning dimensions. The key innovation is pairing guideline-following questions with counterfactual questions where a controlled change in patient information changes whether a safety rule should apply, allowing measurement of conditional rule application.

Result: Across 28 medical-specific, general, and proprietary LLMs, every model performed worse on counterfactual questions, with mean accuracy dropping from 63.6% to 45.1%. Models performed well only when explicit patient attributes directly signaled familiar contraindications, but struggled when patient information needed to narrow or withdraw safety warnings. Model rationales often acknowledged changed patient information but final answers still retained previous safety judgments, and medical-specific LLMs actually performed worse than general LLMs on counterfactual questions.

Conclusion: MedPIC-Bench makes conditional rule application measurable and highlights that static medication-safety accuracy assessments are insufficient for evaluating patient-specific reliability. The benchmark reveals persistent vulnerabilities in LLMs' ability to properly apply medication-safety rules based on changing patient conditions.

Abstract: Applying a valid medication-safety rule when its patient-specific conditions are not met can produce an incorrect decision. Existing medical evaluations largely use isolated and fixed scenarios. A model may therefore answer correctly by recalling a drug-risk association without showing that it used patient information to decide whether the rule applies. To address this gap, we introduce MedPIC-Bench, a benchmark of source-verifiable recommendations and expert-validated questions for patient-specific medication-safety reasoning. It combines guideline-following questions with paired counterfactual questions in which a controlled change in patient information changes whether a rule applies. The benchmark contains 467 questions annotated along six clinical and reasoning dimensions. Across 28 medical-specific, general, and proprietary LLMs, every model performs worse on counterfactual questions, with mean accuracy falling from 63.6\% to 45.1\%. Models perform well when an explicit patient attribute directly signals a familiar contraindication, but struggle when patient information must narrow or withdraw a safety warning. Model rationales often acknowledge the changed patient information, yet the final answers retain the previous safety judgment. This vulnerability persists among medical-specific LLMs, whose average CF performance trails that of general LLMs. MedPIC-Bench therefore makes conditional rule application measurable and highlights the limitations of static medication-safety accuracy for assessing patient-specific reliability.

</details>


### [25] [CastFSR: A Fast--Slow--Reflect Agentic Reasoning Framework for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03031)
*Xiaoyu Tao,Mingyue Cheng,Bokai Pan,Chuang Jiang,Huanjian Zhang,Tian Gao,Yaguo Liu,Qi Liu,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CastFSR is an agentic framework for context-aware time series forecasting using a Fast-Slow-Reflect workflow that leverages LLMs for reasoning about contextual features and their impact on future dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series forecasting approaches often lack explicit mechanisms to identify relevant contexts, reason about their impacts, and validate forecasts against temporal and domain constraints. While LLMs show promise for context-aware reasoning, existing methods don't effectively integrate contextual understanding with forecasting.

Method: Proposes CastFSR framework with three-phase workflow: 1) Fast thinking: profiles observations and selects lightweight forecasters to construct data-driven forecast prior; 2) Slow deliberation: retrieves contextual evidence, determines adaptive look-back windows, and reasons about how contexts reshape future dynamics; 3) Reflection: iteratively refines forecasts for temporal, contextual, and domain consistency. Supports both training-free inference with off-the-shelf LLMs and efficient deployment via two-stage SFT+RL.

Result: Extensive experiments on public datasets demonstrate that CastFSR consistently outperforms representative baselines. The framework shows strong performance in context-aware time series forecasting.

Conclusion: CastFSR successfully addresses the limitations of existing approaches by providing explicit mechanisms for context identification, impact reasoning, and forecast validation through its innovative Fast-Slow-Reflect workflow, enabling more accurate and reliable context-aware forecasting.

Abstract: Time series forecasting is fundamental to decision-making in complex systems, where future dynamics are influenced not only by historical observations but also by evolving contextual features. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have extended forecasting beyond numerical extrapolation toward context-aware reasoning. However, existing approaches often lack explicit mechanisms to identify relevant contexts, reason about their impacts, and validate forecasts against temporal and domain constraints. In this work, we propose CastFSR, an agentic framework that formulates context-aware forecasting as a Fast--Slow--Reflect workflow. In fast thinking, CastFSR profiles observations and selects lightweight forecasters to construct a data-driven forecast prior. In slow deliberation, it retrieves contextual evidence, adaptively determines informative look-back windows, and reasons about how contexts reshape future dynamics. In reflection, it iteratively refines forecasts to ensure temporal, contextual, and domain consistency. CastFSR supports both training-free inference with off-the-shelf LLMs and efficient deployment through a two-stage SFT and reinforcement learning strategy that transfers its orchestration capability to compact LLMs. Extensive experiments on public datasets demonstrate that CastFSR consistently outperforms representative baselines. Our code is available at https://github.com/Xiaoyu-Tao/CastFSR.

</details>


### [26] [TraceCAD: Trace-Guided Repair for Agentic CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03062)
*Fengxiao Fan,Jingzhe Ni,Fan Sang,Xiaolong Yin,Yu Liu,Ruofeng Tong,Min Tang,Peng Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TraceCAD is a recovery system for LLM-based CAD agents that maintains persistent state of design traces, enabling localized fault diagnosis, bounded repairs, and reusable skill memory for more reliable CAD model correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based CAD agents generate executable parametric programs, but their correction loops lose important evidence about satisfied requirements, faulty operations, and prior repair attempts, making recovery inefficient and unreliable.

Method: TraceCAD introduces a recovery layer that maintains persistent state linking requested features, modeling steps, failure evidence, and candidate outcomes. It diagnoses likely faulty operations, searches bounded edits in dependency regions, validates candidates through execution and preservation checks, and stores successful/failed repair outcomes in reusable skill memory.

Result: On DeepCAD-derived benchmarks with 200-model ablations and 1K-model comparison, TraceCAD achieves competitive geometric quality (IoU, Chamfer distance, Hausdorff distance). Removing persistent state halves recovery score; removing localized search more than doubles geometric regression and doubles code-agent invocations. Initializing skill store on training models further reduces retries, token cost, and latency.

Conclusion: Persistent, localized, and reusable recovery through TraceCAD significantly improves final CAD quality and repair reliability compared to standard LLM-based CAD correction approaches.

Abstract: LLM-based CAD agents produce executable parametric programs, but their correction loops may lose evidence about satisfied requirements, faulty operations, and prior repairs. We introduce TraceCAD, a recovery layer that links requested features, modeling steps, failure evidence, and candidate outcomes as persistent state. TraceCAD diagnoses likely faulty operations, searches bounded edits in their dependency regions, validates candidates through execution and preservation checks, and retains successful and failed repair outcomes in reusable skill memory. On DeepCAD-derived benchmarks with 200-model ablations and a 1K-model comparison, TraceCAD achieves competitive geometric quality in terms of IoU, Chamfer distance, and Hausdorff distance. Removing persistent state nearly halves recovery score; removing localized search more than doubles geometric regression and doubles code-agent invocations. Initializing the skill store on disjoint training models further reduces retries, token cost, and latency. These results demonstrate that persistent, localized, and reusable recovery improves final CAD quality and repair reliability.

</details>


### [27] [Getting the Parameters Right: A Difficulty-Graded Benchmark and Probe-Guided Training for LLM Tool Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03071)
