### [51] [Traceable Multi-Agent System for Knowledge-Based Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03339)
*Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Hyeokjun Choe,Soonyoung Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TraceMAS is an interactive demo system for traceable multi-agent forecasting that makes autonomous agent decisions inspectable through causal-loop diagrams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents in enterprise forecasting create adaptive pipelines but make it difficult for practitioners to inspect why forecasts changed, what evidence supported changes, and how data/modeling choices were revised.

Method: TraceMAS organizes agent outputs around two causal-loop representations: an Ideal CLD (key factors and causal relations from domain documents) and a Data-Grounded CLD (links factors to internal variables, external data, or proxies). The Data-Grounded CLD guides feature construction and model design while preserving connections between textual evidence, data choices, and model revisions.

Result: The system was demonstrated on crude oil price forecasting with an interface allowing users to compare forecasting iterations, inspect agent-level revisions, explore causal maps, review feature-data mappings and model architecture, and connect scenario forecasts to market narratives.

Conclusion: TraceMAS shows how autonomous forecasting agents can retain flexibility while making the evidence-to-forecast process inspectable, addressing the transparency problem in autonomous enterprise forecasting systems.

Abstract: Enterprise forecasting increasingly relies on autonomous agents that interpret documents, search for data, generate code, and revise models. While this autonomy helps build adaptive forecasting pipelines, it also makes it difficult for practitioners to inspect why a forecast changed, which evidence supported the change, and how data and modeling choices were revised. We present TraceMAS, an interactive demo system for traceable multi-agent forecasting. TraceMAS organizes agent outputs around two causal-loop representations: an Ideal Causal Loop Diagram (Ideal CLD), which captures key factors and their causal relations extracted from domain documents, and a Data-Grounded Causal Loop Diagram (Data-Grounded CLD), which links those factors to internal variables, external data, or documented proxies. The Data-Grounded CLD guides feature construction and model design while preserving the connection between textual evidence, data choices, and model revisions. We demonstrate TraceMAS on crude oil price forecasting. The demo interface allows users to compare forecasting iterations, inspect agent-level revisions, explore causal maps, review feature-data mappings and model architecture, and connect scenario forecasts to market narratives. This demonstration shows how autonomous forecasting agents can retain flexibility while making the evidence-to-forecast process inspectable.

</details>


### [52] [MMLongBench-Doc-V2: A Corrected-Annotation, Semantics-Aware Revision of MMLongBench-Doc](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03397)
*Mingtian Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MMLongBench-Doc-V2 is a corrected version of a long-document QA benchmark that fixes annotation errors and improves evaluation metrics, addressing issues with string comparison metrics and incorrect ground-truth annotations that skewed V1 scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original MMLongBench-Doc benchmark had two major issues: 1) The reference metric used string comparison which caused false negatives (e.g., "1,358,000" losing to "1358000"), and 2) Many ground-truth annotations were wrong, ambiguous, or incomplete, particularly affecting questions that capable systems could answer correctly.

Method: The authors created V2 by: 1) Correcting 106 annotations with documentation of page references and arithmetic justifications, 2) Replacing the string metric with a pinned LLM judge that assesses whether a response means the same as the reference, 3) Removing 10 questions with incorrect document filenames and 1 duplicated question, 4) Developing a decision procedure for handling empty set keys in annotations, and 5) Widening 14 of 208 rows based on this procedure.

Result: MMLongBench-Doc-V2 contains 1,071 questions over 134 documents (down from 1,082 questions over 135 documents in V1). The corrections ensure more accurate evaluation, though V2 scores are not directly comparable with published V1 results. The corrected corpus, detailed correction records, and evaluation harness are publicly available on GitHub.

Conclusion: The paper presents a significant improvement to long-document QA evaluation by addressing fundamental flaws in benchmark annotation and evaluation methodology, providing a more reliable tool for assessing model capabilities while cautioning against comparing results between the two versions.

Abstract: MMLongBench-Doc is a long-document QA benchmark of 1,082 questions over 135 PDFs. Two properties of it push measured scores away from the quantity they are meant to capture: the reference metric compares extracted answers, so 1,358,000 loses to 1358000; and a non-trivial share of ground-truth annotations are wrong, ambiguous, or incomplete --- concentrated, because of how they were found, in exactly the questions capable systems answer correctly. MMLongBench-Doc-V2 corrects 106 annotations, each published with the page and arithmetic that settle it, and replaces the string metric with a pinned LLM judge asked whether a response means the reference. Ten questions whose document ships under the wrong filename are removed rather than counted wrong, along with one duplicated question, leaving 1,071 questions over 134 documents. The most reusable contribution is a decision procedure for when an empty set key may be widened and when widening would destroy a deliberate negative sample; applied to all 208 rows, it widened 14. V2 scores are not comparable with published V1 numbers. The corrected corpus, the per-entry correction record and the evaluation harness are available at https://github.com/VectifyAI/MMLongBench-Doc-V2.

</details>


### [53] [Towards Robust Tool Use in Agents via Experience-Driven Adaptive Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03403)
*Can Wang,Haoran Chen,Li Yu,Ding Hao,Bohai Zhao,Zhaoyang Liu,Zhiying Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExpG is a mechanism that builds and refines adaptive guidance for robust tool use in AI agents by learning from past execution experiences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The performance bottleneck of agents is shifting from model capability to execution robustness. Tools are crucial for agent-environment interaction, but existing methods lack focus on ensuring robust tool use across diverse runtime conditions.

Method: ExpG consists of three phases: 1) Experience acquisition analyzing tool invocation quality from historical trajectories, 2) Experience distillation filtering unhelpful experiences and summarizing them into generalizable guidance, and 3) Experience reuse applying guidance adaptively during future task solving.

Result: ExpG brings consistent improvements across tool selection, tool calling, and response generation tasks, enabling smaller agents to outperform larger ones without ExpG. It achieves particularly strong gains in challenging settings.

Conclusion: ExpG provides a promising path toward more robust tool use by building adaptive guidance that captures tool capability boundaries and best practices, addressing the execution robustness bottleneck in AI agents.

Abstract: The performance bottleneck of agents is increasingly shifting from model capability to the robustness of their execution processes. Tools play a central role as the primary interface through which agents interact with external environments, yet existing methods rarely focus on ensuring robust tool use across diverse runtime conditions. To address this problem, we propose ExpG, a mechanism that builds and refines adaptive guidance capturing each tool's capability boundaries and best practices, thereby enabling agents to use tools more robustly and effectively. ExpG consists of three phases: (1) experience acquisition, which analyzes tool invocation quality from historical execution trajectories, producing structured learnable experiences through multi-aspect attribution; (2) experience distillation, which keeps the experience pool effective by filtering unhelpful experiences, selecting representative ones with an equivalence-class-based method, and summarizing them into generalizable guidance; and (3) experience reuse, which applies the guidance adaptively during future task solving. Extensive experiments show that ExpG brings consistent improvements across the tool selection, tool calling, and response generation tasks, enabling smaller agents to outperform larger ones that do not use ExpG. Moreover, ExpG achieves particularly strong gains in challenging settings, suggesting a promising path toward more robust tool use. Our code, experiments, and results are available.

</details>


### [54] [Enactive Artificial Intelligence: A Decision-Centric Architecture for Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03413)
*Zuojun Max Shen,Yuan Qu,Pujun Zhang,Anbang Liu,Yunhao Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Enactive AI as a conceptual framework for enterprise and industrial systems, focusing on integrating AI tools/agents into real-world complex operations while addressing reliability, feasibility, resilience, and responsibility requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI has evolved beyond just LLMs and generative tasks to include tools, agents, and harnesses for solving real business problems. However, there's a gap in verifying AI power in real-world complex systems due to requirements like reliability, feasibility, resilience, and responsibility in commercial and industrial operations.

Method: Proposes Enactive AI framework with four complementary roles: 1) Organizational World (strategic-institutional horizon for operations management), 2) Site World (operational-realization horizon for industrial optimization), 3) Schema Intelligence (coupling mechanism between world models), and 4) Enactive Decision Cycle (self-evolving dynamic update and audit process).

Result: Enactive AI expands AI frontier from model capability to system-aware action, enabling scalable, governable, and socially valuable AI deployment. The framework supports enterprise/industry reasoning, site-level decision support, and execution feedback in complex systems.

Conclusion: Enactive AI represents the next frontier of AI research for enterprise/industrial systems, shifting focus from what models can generate/automate to how reliably intelligent systems can support consequential action, responsible governance, and durable social value in complex real-world systems.

Abstract: As artificial intelligence (AI) continues to evolve and mature, recent AI practices have moved beyond large language models (LLMs) and text or image generation tasks, increasingly integrating tools, agents, and harnesses to solve real business and industrial problems. However, the power of AI is not verified under these real-world complex systems for various reasons, considering reliability, feasibility, resilience, and responsibility requirements in real commercial and industrial operations. This study synthesizes adjacent research and introduces Enactive AI as a conceptual framework for enterprise and industry reasoning, site-level decision support, and execution feedback. Four complementary roles organize the framework: an Organizational World defines operations management logic and an organizational behavior world model behind an enterprise from a strategic-institutional horizon; a Site World defines a physically bounded industrial optimization and execution world model from an operational-realization horizon; Schema Intelligence provides the coupling mechanism between two world models to weave various AI applications via two models; and Enactive Decision Cycle triggers the self-evolving dynamic process to update and audit the entire framework. By foregrounding decision intelligence in complex systems, Enactive AI expands the frontier of AI from model capability to system-aware action, opening new possibilities for scalable, governable, and socially valuable AI deployment. Enactive AI points toward a future in which AI progress is measured not only by what models can generate or automate, but by how reliably intelligent systems can support consequential action, responsible governance, and durable social value in the complex systems that shape modern life, which we believe will define the next frontier of AI research for enterprise-level and industrial complex systems.

</details>


### [55] [AI World Cup 2026: Benchmarking Large Language Models for End-to-End Football Tournament Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03416)
*Jonaid Shianifar,Iias Faiud*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark comparing 10 LLMs forecasting the 2026 FIFA World Cup under standardized conditions, revealing GPT-5.5 Thinking as top performer with tournament forecasting showing different skills than match-by-match prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current comparisons of LLM forecasting capabilities are difficult due to inconsistent information, tools, and evaluation rules across studies, creating a need for standardized benchmarking.

Method: Created AI World Cup benchmark with 10 LLM-based assistants making single pre-tournament forecasts using identical tournament snapshot, prompt, JSON schema, and scoring procedure covering group-stage scores, rankings, knockout bracket, and final placings.

Result: GPT-5.5 Thinking finished first with 744 points, followed by GPT-5.5 (717), Gemini (699), and Qwen 3.7 (687). GPT-5.5 Thinking was only model to correctly predict Spain as champion. Knockout performance strongly correlated with total score (r=0.986), while group-stage accuracy and confidence showed no meaningful relationships with overall performance.

Conclusion: Complete tournament forecasting tests different capabilities than match-by-match prediction, and bracket-based leaderboards depend heavily on scoring design, highlighting the importance of standardized evaluation frameworks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are now regularly asked to forecast real-world events, but comparisons are often difficult because models receive different information, use different tools, and are evaluated under different rules. This paper reports the completed \emph{AI World Cup} benchmark, in which ten LLM-based assistants made a single pre-tournament forecast of the entire 2026 FIFA World Cup. Every submission used the same tournament snapshot, prompt, JSON schema, and scoring procedure. The forecasts covered group-stage scores, group rankings, the knockout bracket, final placings, confidence values, and short explanations. After all 104 matches had been played, GPT-5.5 Thinking finished first with 744 points, followed by GPT-5.5 with 717, Gemini with 699, and Qwen 3.7 with 687. GPT-5.5 Thinking was also the only model to select Spain, which defeated Argentina 1--0 in the final, as champion. The final ranking was driven mainly by knockout performance: total score was strongly correlated with knockout points ($r=0.986$), but showed little relationship with group-stage match points ($r=0.055$), group-standing points ($r=-0.103$), or their combined pre-knockout score ($r=-0.054$). Match-level accuracy produced a different ordering. Claude Sonnet 4.6 correctly predicted the largest number of group-stage outcomes (63.89\%) but placed sixth overall. Average self-reported confidence was also unrelated to either outcome accuracy ($r=-0.060$) or total score ($r=-0.067$). The results suggest that forecasting a complete tournament tests something different from predicting matches one at a time, while also showing how strongly a bracket-based leaderboard can depend on scoring design. The benchmark materials, raw responses, and scoring code are released to support replication and future extensions.

</details>


### [56] [Towards Improving Sequential Decision-Making in LLM Agents via Experience Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03420)
*Jakub Rada,Viliam Lisý*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs underperform in sequential decision-making games like tic-tac-toe and Connect Four, losing to MCTS opponents, but post-game reflection and experience memory can improve performance without weight updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs excel at single-shot reasoning, their performance in sequential decision-making tasks is poorly understood. The researchers want to systematically evaluate LLMs' sequential reasoning capabilities using fully-observable two-player zero-sum games where ground-truth evaluation is available through game rules and optimal move calculations.

Method: Researchers tested LLMs across model tiers on simple games like tic-tac-toe and Connect Four, comparing against MCTS opponents. They used obfuscation techniques to test if performance gaps were due to memorization vs. reasoning. They then introduced an agentic framework with experience memory for sequential decision-making, addressing challenges like credit assignment through post-game reflection and rule extraction.

Result: LLMs consistently underperform in sequential games, losing to MCTS opponents. Obfuscation experiments show the performance gap persists even when game surfaces are rewritten, suggesting it's not just memorization failure. However, post-game reflection and rule extraction in their agentic framework yield measurable improvements in tic-tac-toe performance without modifying model weights.

Conclusion: LLMs have significant gaps in sequential decision-making compared to their single-shot reasoning capabilities. These gaps appear to be genuine reasoning limitations rather than memorization failures. The proposed agentic framework with experience memory shows promise for improving sequential decision-making without weight updates, suggesting future directions for enhancing LLMs' multi-step reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models have improved substantially on single-shot reasoning tasks, but their performance in sequential decision-making is less well understood. We study this on fully-observable two-player zero-sum games, which provide ground-truth evaluation: outcomes are determined by the rules, and optimality of individual moves can be computed or approximated, without relying on a judge model. Across model tiers, LLMs play suboptimally in simple games such as tic-tac-toe or Connect Four, and lose to MCTS opponents. Obfuscations that preserve the game tree but rewrite its surface form leave performance largely unchanged, indicating the gap is not fully explained by recall of memorized strategies. Motivated by this performance gap, we introduce an agentic framework enhanced with an experience memory designed for the sequential setting and addressing common challenges of sequential decision-making such as credit assignment. We show that post-game reflection and rule extraction yield measurable improvements on tic-tac-toe without modifying the model weights.

</details>


### [57] [State Propagation Also Satisfies: A Complex-Valued State-Space Model for Deterministic State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03425)
*Xiaohe Li,Yang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A minimalistic recurrent architecture called Complex State Propagator (CSP) achieves 100% accuracy on deterministic state tracking tasks using only state propagation without attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers with attention mechanisms dominate sequence modeling, but they may be unnecessarily complex for certain deterministic state tracking tasks like parity checking, modular counting, and parenthesis matching where state propagation alone could be sufficient.

Method: Proposes Complex State Propagator (CSP), a recurrent architecture that only propagates hidden states across layers without output projections at intermediate steps. Uses complex-valued vectors updated via input-dependent rotations in complex domain with block-level skip connections, element-wise complex normalization, and SiLU activation at sequence boundaries to prevent gradient issues.

Result: Achieves 100% accuracy with perfect F1 scores across canonical deterministic state tracking tasks when applied with Focal Loss.

Conclusion: For deterministic state tracking tasks, attention mechanisms may be overkill, and state propagation alone is sufficient, as demonstrated by the successful Complex State Propagator architecture.

Abstract: Transformer-based architectures have dominated sequence modeling, largely due to the expressive power of attention mechanisms. However, for a class of deterministic state tracking tasks---such as parity checking, modular counting, and parenthesis matching---attention may be overkill. In this paper, we show that \textbf{state propagation alone is sufficient}.
  We propose the \textbf{Complex State Propagator (CSP)}, a minimalistic recurrent architecture that \textbf{only propagates hidden states} across layers without output projections at intermediate steps. The state is represented as a complex-valued vector, updated via input-dependent rotations in the complex domain. To enable deep propagation without gradient vanishing or degradation, we introduce a \textbf{block-level skip connection} alongside element-wise complex normalization and SiLU activation at sequence boundaries. Applied with Focal Loss, CSP achieves \textbf{100\% accuracy} with perfect F1 scores across canonical tasks.

</details>


### [58] [DataSpace: Benchmarking Data Agents for Verifiable Analytics over Heterogeneous Workspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03451)
*Boyan Li,Zhuowen Liang,Yupeng Xie,Xiaotian Lin,Tianqi Luo,Xinyu Liu,Yizhang Zhu,Zhangyang Peng,Yuan Li,Zhengxuan Zhang,Jiayi Zhang,Nan Tang,Guoliang Li,Yuyu Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DataSpace is a benchmark for evaluating data agents on heterogeneous data analysis tasks, featuring 410 cross-language tasks across 7,439 artifacts totaling 15GB of multimodal data, with deterministic evaluation of tabular outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks isolate structured querying, retrieval, or open-ended analysis, leaving heterogeneous evidence discovery, complete tabular outputs, and deterministic evaluation insufficiently unified. There's a need for comprehensive evaluation of data agents working with real-world organizational workspaces containing scattered evidence across databases, structured files, documents, and multimedia.

Method: Created DataSpace benchmark with 410 cross-language tasks and 7,439 artifacts across CSV, JSON, SQLite, Markdown, PDF, and video. Built using DataSpace-Builder execution-grounded framework with cross-language transformation, constraint-aware relational sampling, modality routing, artifact rendering, and human review by 11 domain experts. Evaluation uses deterministic evaluator with header-invariant column alignment, type- and precision-aware normalization, and order-aware row comparison.

Result: Best accuracy across six frontier multimodal models and five agent harnesses reached 66.34%, with harness choice creating 15.36-point spread. Multimodal evidence integration and joins consistently reduced accuracy across all six backbones. DataSpace remains unsaturated, identifying key challenges for improving data-agent reliability.

Conclusion: DataSpace provides a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating data agents on heterogeneous data analysis tasks, revealing current limitations in multimodal evidence integration and join operations. The benchmark serves as an official evaluation for KDD Cup 2026 competition and identifies critical research directions for improving data-agent reliability.

Abstract: Data agents enable natural-language analytics over organizational workspaces, where relevant evidence may be scattered across databases, structured files, long documents, and multimedia. Existing benchmarks largely isolate structured querying, retrieval, or open-ended analysis, leaving heterogeneous evidence discovery, complete tabular outputs, and deterministic evaluation insufficiently unified. We introduce DataSpace, a benchmark in which data agents produce verifiable tabular results from task-local heterogeneous workspaces. It contains 410 cross-language tasks and 7,439 artifacts totaling 15.01 GB across CSV, JSON, SQLite, Markdown, PDF, and video. DataSpace also served as the official evaluation benchmark for the KDD Cup 2026 Data Agents for Complex Data Analysis competition. Each agent receives only a question and workspace and returns the complete requested tabular result. We construct DataSpace with DataSpace-Builder, an execution-grounded framework comprising cross-language transformation, constraint-aware relational sampling, modality routing and artifact rendering, and human review and task repair by 11 domain experts. A deterministic evaluator performs header-invariant column alignment, type- and precision-aware normalization, and order-aware row comparison. Across six recently released frontier multimodal models and five widely used agent harnesses, the best accuracy reaches 66.34%, while harness choice creates a 15.36-point spread with the backbone fixed. Multimodal evidence integration and joins consistently reduce accuracy across all six backbones. These results show that DataSpace remains unsaturated and identify key challenges for improving data-agent reliability.

</details>


### [59] [LLaDA MoE v2: Scaling Mixture-of-Experts Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03457)
*Fengqi Zhu,Shaoxuan Xu,Jingyang Ou,Zebin You,Yipeng Xing,Huabin Liu,Xiaolu Zhang,Jun Zhou,Zhenzhong Lan,Yankai Lin,Wayne Xin Zhao,Jianguo Li,Chongxuan Li,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scaling analysis reveals different trends for Mixture-of-Experts diffusion language models compared to autoregressive models, leading to LLaDA MoE v2 which achieves competitive performance with fewer tokens than Qwen3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While diffusion language models offer an alternative to autoregressive language modeling, the scaling behavior of Mixture-of-Experts dLLMs remains poorly understood compared to AR models, creating a knowledge gap in how to effectively scale these architectures.

Method: Systematic characterization of optimization hyperparameters, compute allocation, and architecture scaling for MoE dLLMs, including IsoFLOP analysis to study model-data allocation trade-offs, and training LLaDA MoE v2 (30B-A3B dLLM) from scratch on 23.5T tokens using identified principles.

Result: MoE dLLMs show different scaling trends: optimal batch size grows faster and learning rate decays more rapidly with compute; data-side tilt with optimal token budget growing faster than activated computation; larger scales favor larger expert pools; LLaDA MoE v2 trained with these principles approaches Qwen3 performance with 65% of its tokens and outperforms SDAR Chat on most reasoning/coding benchmarks.

Conclusion: The study establishes practical scaling laws and design principles for MoE diffusion language models, demonstrating that dLLMs can achieve competitive performance with proper scaling strategies, bridging the gap with autoregressive models through systematic architectural and optimization insights.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language modeling, yet the scaling behavior of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) dLLMs remains poorly understood. We systematically characterize how optimization hyperparameters, compute allocation, and architecture scale for MoE dLLMs, identifying quantitative differences from scaling trends previously reported for AR models. Specifically, for optimization, the optimal nominal batch size grows faster, while the optimal learning rate decays more rapidly with compute. For model--data allocation, IsoFLOP analysis reveals a slight data-side tilt: the optimal token budget grows faster than activated model-side computation. For MoE architecture, larger scales increasingly favor larger expert pools at fixed activated capacity, while moderate expert granularity remains consistently effective and the preferred fraction of activated capacity assigned to shared experts remains stable across scales. Guided by these findings, we train LLaDA MoE v2, a 30B-A3B dLLM, from scratch on 23.5T tokens. With approximately 65\% as many pretraining tokens as Qwen3, LLaDA MoE v2 approaches Qwen3 on several knowledge, reasoning, and coding benchmarks. After supervised fine-tuning alone, it outperforms SDAR Chat on seven of eight reasoning and coding benchmarks and remains close to Qwen3 on several tasks. These results establish practical scaling laws and design principles for MoE dLLMs.

</details>


### [60] [Solver-Aware Decompositions for Programming-by-Example: When Dividing Requires Knowing how to Conquer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03461)
*Janis Zenkner,Tobias Sesterhenn,Tim Grams,Christian Bartelt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Decomposition-based PBE systems often train decomposers to imitate ground-truth subgoals, but this doesn't ensure solver tractability. SAD optimizes decomposers using direct feedback from the solver itself, prioritizing decompositions the solver can actually handle over GT alignment, leading to better synthesis performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches treat ground-truth (GT) subgoals as optimal targets for decomposer training, but these GT decompositions reflect annotator choices rather than the solver's actual search dynamics and capabilities. A decomposer trained on GT may produce logically valid but intractable subgoals for the solver, creating a mismatch between decomposition quality and solver performance.

Method: Proposes Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD), which uses supervised training on GT subgoals as a structural scaffold, but additionally optimizes the decomposer via direct feedback from a frozen synthesizer. The decomposer is rewarded based on the synthesizer's loss on the target program, creating a signal of subtask difficulty that encourages decompositions the solver can effectively act upon.

Result: Experiments show an accuracy paradox: higher agreement with GT decompositions doesn't improve synthesis success. SAD learns decompositions that trade GT alignment for solver tractability, yielding consistent gains in synthesis and end-to-end task accuracy across two PBE domains. SAD even solves tasks that a GT decomposition oracle fails, demonstrating GT decompositions aren't universally optimal for bounded solvers.

Conclusion: Decomposition quality is solver-relative rather than intrinsic to the task. GT decompositions reflect annotator factorization choices rather than solver capabilities. SAD's solver-aware optimization approach produces more effective decompositions by considering the solver's actual limitations and search dynamics, leading to improved overall system performance.

Abstract: Decomposition-based Programming-by-example (PBE) scales performance by splitting tasks into subtasks that a learned synthesizer solves: a decomposer predicts intermediate subgoals, and a synthesizer generates programs conditioned on them. Current approaches train the decomposer to imitate ground-truth ( GT) subgoals, implicitly treating decomposition quality as intrinsic to the task. We challenge this assumption: for bounded solvers with fixed inductive biases, GT decompositions reflect the annotator's factorization choices - not the solver's search dynamics. A decomposer trained to match GT decompositions may therefore propose subgoals that are logically valid yet intractable for the solver. We propose Solver-Aware Decomposition (SAD), a training framework that retains supervised training on GT subgoals as a structural scaffold, while additionally optimizing the decomposer via direct feedback from a frozen synthesizer. Subgoals are rewarded based on the synthesizer's loss on the target program - a signal of subtask difficulty that encourages decompositions the solver can act on. Our experiments reveal an accuracy paradox: higher agreement with GT decompositions does not improve synthesis success - even though the synthesizer was trained on the very same GT data the decomposer is optimized to mimic. SAD instead learns decompositions that trade GT alignment for solver tractability, yielding consistent gains in synthesis and end-to-end task accuracy across two PBE domains. Moreover, SAD solves tasks that a GT decomposition oracle fails - empirical evidence that GT decompositions are not universally optimal for bounded solvers, and that decomposition quality is solver-relative, not intrinsic.

</details>


### [61] [LeanMem: Simple and Efficient Long-Term Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03463)
*Yuxin Liao,Le Wu,Min Hou,Hao Liu,Han Wu,Zishu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LeanMem is a lightweight long-term memory framework for LLM agents that adaptively processes dialogue content by filtering low-value information and storing different memory types (profile, event, record) based on information characteristics, with selective updates and dynamic retrieval allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory systems process heterogeneous dialogue content through uniform summarization/retrieval pipelines, causing either excessive token consumption or irreversible loss of fine-grained evidence. Historical dialogue content varies in compressibility, temporal dynamics, and fidelity requirements, suggesting the need for differentiated handling.

Method: LeanMem filters low-value content, then stores informative segments as: 1) compact profile memory (stable characteristics), 2) temporally structured event memory (dynamic sequences), or 3) source-grounded record memory (immutable facts). During maintenance, only event memories are selectively updated. During inference, it dynamically selects memory types and allocates retrieval budgets based on query-specific evidence demands.

Result: On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S benchmarks with GPT-4.1-mini and Qwen3-8B, LeanMem improves accuracy over strongest memory-based baselines in every setting by up to 15.1 points, while achieving lowest or near-lowest construction cost, inference tokens, and latency.

Conclusion: LeanMem demonstrates that differentiated handling of dialogue content based on information characteristics enables efficient long-term memory for LLM agents, balancing accuracy improvements with computational efficiency across multiple models and benchmarks.

Abstract: Long-term memory is essential for LLM-based agents to sustain interactions and reliably leverage distant history. However, existing memory systems typically process heterogeneous dialogue content through a uniform summarization and retrieval pipeline, leading to either excessive token consumption or irreversible loss of fine-grained evidence. We argue that historical dialogue content should be handled differently according to its compressibility, temporal dynamics, and fidelity requirements. Based on this insight, we propose LeanMem, a lightweight long-term memory framework. LeanMem first filters out low-value content, then stores informative segments as compact profile memory, temporally structured event memory, or source-grounded record memory, depending on the nature of the information. During maintenance, only dynamically evolving event memories are selectively updated, avoiding redundant consolidation of stable profiles and immutable records. During inference, LeanMem dynamically selects memory types and allocates retrieval budgets according to query-specific evidence demands, assembling relevant evidence on demand. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S with GPT-4.1-mini and Qwen3-8B, LeanMem improves accuracy over the strongest memory-based baseline in every setting, by up to 15.1 points, at the lowest or near-lowest construction cost, inference tokens, and latency. The code and datasets are included in the supplementary materials.

</details>


### [62] [ChartAnno: Evaluating MLLMs for Chart Annotation Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03464)
*Zhenghan Chen,Zekai Shao,Lidan Tan,Xin Lin,Xingchen Zeng,Yi Shan,Ziyue Lin,Xiaoliang Fu,Xinyuan Liu,Yuetong Guo,Fen Wang,Bongshin Lee,Siming Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A benchmark called ChartAnno is introduced to evaluate multimodal LLMs on chart annotation generation, revealing that while proprietary models lead, open-source models are catching up, and the task remains challenging due to semantic grounding requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal large language models have advanced in chart understanding and generation, but their ability to annotate existing charts—a common yet challenging communicative task requiring message inference, semantic interpretation, and appropriate element placement—remains underexplored.

Method: Created ChartAnno benchmark with 1,200 real-world charts, paired code, and annotation instructions across three specificity levels. Evaluated 10 representative MLLMs under two input settings: chart code alone, and both chart code plus chart image, with an image-only ablation study.

Result: Proprietary models perform best overall, though large-scale open-source models are closing the gap. More specific instructions improve annotation quality, but inferring abstract intent remains most difficult. Chart images provide limited overall gains, mainly improving design-related metrics.

Conclusion: Chart annotation generation is a challenging task requiring both semantic grounding and effective annotation design. The findings establish a foundation for future research in this underexplored area, with code and data to be released.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in chart understanding, generation, and editing, but their ability to annotate existing charts remains underexplored. Annotating charts is a common yet challenging communicative task, requiring models to infer intended messages, interpret chart semantics, and place appropriate textual or graphical elements. To address this gap, we introduce ChartAnno, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on chart annotation generation. It contains 1,200 real-world charts with paired code and annotation instructions across three levels of instruction specificity. We evaluate 10 representative MLLMs under two primary input settings: (1) chart code alone and (2) both chart code and chart image, and further include a chart image-only ablation study. Results show that proprietary models remain stronger overall, although large-scale open-source models narrow the gap. More specific instructions improve annotation quality, while inferring abstract intent remains most difficult for current MLLMs. Providing chart images brings limited overall gains, with improvements mainly appearing in design-related metrics. These findings highlight chart annotation generation as a challenging task requiring semantic grounding and effective annotation design. Code and data will be released in a future version.

</details>


### [63] [When Correct Solutions Repeat: Rarity-Aware Credit Redistribution for GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03467)
*Zhe Cao,Miaowen Wen,Fangjiong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cue-GRPO addresses credit distribution imbalance in reinforcement learning by redistributing positive advantages based on solution rarity, using Strategy Cues for efficient implementation without extra model inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies that in GRPO (RLVR methods), recurring correct solution forms receive disproportionate credit due to how often they're sampled, while rare correct forms receive limited credit. This creates structure-level skew where common solutions accumulate positive coefficient mass unfairly.

Method: Introduces a partition-conditioned rule that redistributes positive advantages according to cluster rarity. Cue-GRPO implements this using deterministic Strategy Cues to construct rollout-local partitions of verified-correct traces without needing auxiliary-model inference.

Result: Cue-GRPO improves AIME repeated-sampling performance across Qwen2.5-Math-7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct models, with largest gains at high sampling budgets. It adds only 6% wall-clock training overhead over GRPO. Credit Redistribution with Judge Partitions shows the mechanism can also work with judge-derived partitions.

Conclusion: Structure-level credit redistribution is a practical design axis for RLVR, with Strategy Cues providing a low-overhead implementation suitable for competition mathematics. The approach addresses multiplicity-induced credit concentration effectively while maintaining efficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) com- monly optimizes each correct completion as an independent learning signal. In GRPO, this completion-level uniformity creates structure-level skew: recurring correct solution forms accumulate positive coefficient mass in proportion to how often they are sampled, while rare forms receive limited credit. We formalize this behavior as multiplicity-induced structure-level credit concentration and introduce a partition- conditioned rule that redistributes positive advantages accord- ing to cluster rarity. Cue-GRPO instantiates this rule with- out auxiliary-model inference by using deterministic Strategy Cues to construct rollout-local partitions of verified-correct traces. Across Qwen2.5-Math-7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Cue-GRPO improves AIME repeated-sampling performance, with the largest gains at high sampling budgets. Credit Re- distribution (CR) under Judge Partitions (JP) further indi- cates that the proposed redistribution mechanism can oper- ate with judge-derived partitions. Cue-GRPO adds only 6% wall-clock training overhead over GRPO. These results sup- port structure-level credit redistribution as a practical design axis for RLVR, with Strategy Cues providing a low-overhead implementation for competition mathematics. Code is avail- able at https://github.com/CzZ12/When-Correct-Solutions- Repeat-Rarity-Aware-Credit-Redistribution-for-GRPO.

</details>


### [64] [ToolLIFT: Lifting Tool-Specific Trajectories into Function-Level Graphs for Generalizable Tool Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03468)
*Xiuhui You,Jiayi Luo,Zichao Shen,Qingyun Sun,Ziwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolLIFT lifts tool-specific trajectories to function-level workflow graphs for better generalization across different tool sets, using trajectory-lifting, decoupled workflow planning, and reinforcement learning for reliable dataflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches construct tool-level graphs from historical trajectories, but these graphs remain tied to specific tools and don't generalize well across different tool sets. The authors recognize that analogous tasks often share common function-level workflow structures, which could serve as a more transferable abstraction for tool planning.

Method: Proposes ToolLIFT framework with three key components: 1) trajectory-lifting mechanism that encodes workflow structures in function-level workflow graphs (FWG) and shares collaboration experience across tools; 2) decoupled workflow planning and tool selection based on FWG's global structure to align individual tool choices with overall workflow; 3) reinforcement learning with source-gated and skill-specific rewards to maintain reliable, source-traceable information flow across tool calls.

Result: Experiments on two in-distribution (ID) and three out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks show that ToolLIFT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, demonstrating strong generalization to unseen tool sets.

Conclusion: Function-level workflow abstraction provides a more transferable representation than tool-specific graphs, enabling better generalization across different tool sets for LLM agents, with ToolLIFT showing superior performance and generalization capabilities compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Historical tool-use trajectories provide valuable experience for large language model (LLM) agents to plan and coordinate tool usage. Existing approaches directly construct tool-level graphs from these trajectories, but the resulting graphs remain tied to specific tools and are hard to generalize across tool sets. To tackle this challenge, we find that despite differences in the tools involved, analogous tasks often share a common function-level workflow structure, which serves as a potentially more transferable abstraction for tool planning. Based on this insight, we propose ToolLIFT, a framework that lifts tool-specific trajectories into a function-level workflow graph (FWG) for generalizable tool planning. Specifically, we first propose a trajectory-lifting mechanism that encodes workflow structures in the FWG and shares collaboration experience across tools. Then, building on the global structure of the FWG, we introduce decoupled workflow planning and tool selection to align individual tool choices with the overall workflow. Lastly, to ensure reliable tool dataflow, we adopt Reinforcement Learning (RL) and propose source-gated and skill-specific rewards to maintain source-traceable information flow across tool calls. Experiments on two in-distribution (ID) and three out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks show that ToolLIFT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, demonstrating strong generalization to unseen tool sets.

</details>


### [65] [WeClawArena: An Auditable Sandbox and Benchmark for Cross-User Agents Collaboration and Security in Human-Centered Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03499)
*Prince Zizhuang Wang,Aojie Yuan,Haiyue Zhang,Xiyang Hu,Yue Zhao,Shuli Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WeClawArena is an auditable benchmark and sandbox for testing multi-party AI agent collaboration over personal workspaces, with focus on security threats and realistic user environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As persistent personal-agent frameworks enable human-centered agent networks, there's a need to test how these agents collaborate across personal workspaces with limited visibility. Current benchmarks lack end-to-end sandbox environments for verifiable cross-user collaboration with realistic workspaces or security testing of harmful action propagation through agent networks.

Method: Created WeClawArena benchmark with 124 base tasks across six cross-user domains, expanded to 620 scenario variants (1 benign control + 4 attack-vector variants per base task). Includes a runtime sandbox that records peer messages, tool calls, resource operations, governed decisions, and final workspace states for auditability.

Result: WeClawArena provides separate reporting for utility and attack success rates, with attack success auditing from bounded runtime evidence. Supports diagnosis of task breakdown, privacy leakage, poisoned evidence, and invalid authority paths in multi-agent collaboration scenarios.

Conclusion: WeClawArena addresses the critical need for testing security and collaboration in human-centered agent networks with personal workspaces, providing a comprehensive benchmark and sandbox environment for evaluating both utility and security aspects of multi-party owned-agent collaboration.

Abstract: Recent advances in persistent personal-agent frameworks are making human-centered agent networks realistic deployment targets: each user can be served by an AI agent that acts on the user's behalf, maintains state, and communicates with other agents through social and task relations. In these networks, everyday tool use becomes multi-party owned-agent collaboration over personal workspaces, where files, records, tools, and policies are not directly visible across owners. Existing agent benchmarks study tool use and collaboration, but they do not provide an end-to-end sandbox for verifiable cross-user agent collaboration with realistic user digital workspaces or test how harmful actions can travel through the human-centered agent network. We introduce WeClawArena, an auditable benchmark and runtime sandbox for multi-party owned-agent collaboration over personal workspaces. WeClawArena targets collaborative tool-use tasks in which personal workspaces serve as both operational tools and personal constraints. The benchmark contains 124 base tasks across six cross-user task domains and expands them into 620 scenario variants, with one benign control and four attack-vector variants per base task. The sandbox records peer messages, tool calls, resource operations, governed decisions, and final workspace states. WeClawArena reports utility and attack success rate separately and audits attack success from bounded runtime evidence, supporting diagnosis of task breakdown, privacy leakage, poisoned evidence, and invalid authority paths.

</details>


### [66] [Can LLM design high-quality experiments? A Comprehensive and Systematic Benchmark on Autonomous Experimental Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03501)
*Zejun Liu,Jian Wu,Ru Peng,Yuliang Ji,Dongyuan Li,Renhe Jiang,Yue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCOPE benchmark reveals LLMs struggle with scientific experiment design, especially low-level configurations; OptED workflow addresses these limitations through structured improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI4Research focuses on code implementation/execution while overlooking experimental design phase. No benchmark exists to evaluate AI's ability to conduct systematic experiment design, creating a critical gap in automated scientific workflows.

Method: Proposes SCOPE benchmark constructed from 300 recent papers across 19 research domains from top-tier venues (ICML, NeurIPS, ICLR). Evaluates LLMs on two dimensions: High-Level planning completeness (main/ablation/analysis experiments) and Low-Level configuration accuracy/rationality (datasets/baselines/metrics). Also introduces OptED, an agentic workflow with stage isolation, tool augmentation, and rule-based constraints to optimize LLM-based experimental design.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Most LLMs cannot directly design high-quality experiments; (2) All LLMs show performance bottleneck in low-level configuration; (3) Search mode does not improve design quality. OptED effectively alleviates the configuration bottleneck and enhances experimental planning.

Conclusion: There's significant room for improvement in AI-assisted experimental design. The SCOPE benchmark fills a critical gap in evaluating AI's experiment design capabilities, while OptED demonstrates that structured workflows with proper constraints can substantially improve LLM performance on this complex task.

Abstract: AI for Research (AI4Research) leverages AI to automate and improve scientific workflows. While experimental design is a critical stage of the research process, prior work has focused primarily on code implementation and execution, overlooking the importance of this stage, and no benchmark exists to evaluate AI's ability to conduct systematic experiment design. To bridge this gap, we propose SCOPE, a Scientific COmprehensive Planning Evaluation Benchmark constructed from 300 high-quality latest papers across 19 research domains from top-tier venues (e.g., ICML, NeurIPS, and ICLR),evaluating LLMs on two dimensions: High-Level planning completeness (main, ablation, and analysis experiments) and Low-Level configuration accuracy and rationality (datasets, baselines, and metrics). Benchmarking reveals three findings: (1) most LLMs cannot directly design high-quality experiments; (2) all LLMs exhibit a performance bottleneck in low-level configuration; and (3) search mode does not improve design quality. Furthermore, to address these challenges, we propose OptED, a novel agentic workflow to optimize LLM-based experimental design, that enhances LLM-based experimental planning through stage isolation, tool augmentation, and rule-based constraints, effectively alleviating the configuration bottleneck.

</details>


### [67] [When Many Answers Are Valid, Voting Fails: Symbolic Verification for Best-of-K Causal Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03506)
*Omatharv Bharat Vaidya,Connor Thomas Jerzak,Zayne Rea Sprague,Fangcong Yin,Nhat Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CALVER is a training-free symbolic verifier that scores reasoning traces against causal criteria instead of using frequency voting, outperforming traditional methods on causal reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-consistency often fails in causal reasoning because samples repeat the same confounding error, and votes fragment across multiple valid answers, allowing invalid answers to win despite valid minority traces.

Method: CALVER uses symbolic verification against Pearl's causal criteria (including d-separation, backdoor adjustment, and intervention) to score structured reasoning traces. It selects the highest-scoring candidate without consulting reference answers, operates training-free, and can use causal structures either supplied directly or built from text.

Result: CALVER achieves 42.1% accuracy on CLEAR find-one-valid queries vs. 30% for traditional methods. It selects different valid answers than benchmark references in 11 of 21 cases, scales well with sampling budget, generalizes across model families and Bayesian networks, and improves treatment effect decisions against exact ground truth.

Conclusion: CALVER demonstrates that aggregating reasoning via causal validity rather than frequency voting provides superior performance on causal reasoning tasks, offering a training-free approach that generalizes across domains and operates efficiently on CPU.

Abstract: Self-consistency assumes the most frequent answer among sampled reasoning traces is the most reliable, but this can fail in causal reasoning: samples often repeat the same confounding error, and votes fragment across multiple valid answers, letting an invalid answer win despite a valid minority trace. We introduce CALVER (Causal Axiom-Level VERification), a training-free symbolic verifier that scores structured traces against Pearl's causal criteria, including -separation, backdoor adjustment, and intervention, and selects the highest-scoring candidate without consulting a reference answer. On CLEAR find-one-valid queries that admit multiple graph-valid answers, CALVER reaches 42.1% where plurality, a reward model, an LLM judge, and model confidence remain near 30% on identical frozen pools. Scaling the judge to 72B does not close the gap. In an audited clean-core subset, 11 of 21 graph-valid CALVER selections differ from the benchmark's listed answer while still satisfying the requested predicate. The advantage widens with the sampling budget and reproduces across ten published Bayesian networks, a second model family, and settings where the model must build the graph from text. CALVER also improves thresholded average-treatment-effect decisions against exact ground truth, generalizes to logic under a truth-table checker, and scores each candidate in milliseconds on CPU. CALVER needs only a causal structure, supplied outright or built from the text; wherever that holds, selection can aggregate via causal validity.

</details>


### [68] [Reversing Arrows in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03512)
*Sefika Efeoglu,Adrian Paschke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show systematic asymmetries in accurately classifying inverse relations, with performance varying based on entity representations but not consistently improving with relation descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs perform well on text-to-KG tasks, it's unclear if they properly model the direction-dependent semantics of inverse relations (where argument order changes meaning, like "mother" vs "child").

Method: First systematic study of inverse relation directionality using a benchmark of 5,457 instances spanning 27 inverse relation labels. Evaluated 5 open-source LLMs with multiple-choice prompting, testing influence of relation descriptions and entity representations by substituting original entities with synthetic and masked entities.

Result: Findings reveal systematic asymmetries in inverse relation classification across LLMs, indicate relation descriptions don't consistently improve performance, and show model performance is sensitive to variations in entity representations.

Conclusion: LLMs have systematic limitations in modeling inverse relation semantics, suggesting current text-to-KG generation capabilities may be flawed in understanding direction-dependent relationships despite overall strong performance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance on text-to-knowledge graph generation and related tasks. Nevertheless, it is still unclear whether they accurately model the direction-dependent semantics of inverse relations, in which reversing the order of the arguments alters the meaning of a relation (e.g., \textit{mother} versus \textit{child}). To the best of our knowledge, this work presents the first systematic study of inverse relation directionality in LLMs, using a benchmark consisting of 5,457 instances spanning 27 distinct inverse relation labels. We evaluate five open-source LLMs under a multiple-choice prompting framework and further examine the influence of relation descriptions and entity representations by substituting the original entities with synthetic and masked entities. Our findings reveal systematic asymmetries in inverse relation classification across LLMs, indicate that relation descriptions do not consistently improve performance, and show that model performance can be sensitive to variations in entity representations.

</details>


### [69] [Dr. AGENTONOMICS: A Didactic Experiment of AGENTONOMICS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03524)
*Fengjunjie Pan,Alois Knoll*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGENTONOMICS is a framework treating AI agents as economic entities, with Dr. AGENTONOMICS as its first application - a web-based tutor that teaches the framework while demonstrating it, with plans to expand into avatar lecturer, design consultant, and meta-agent roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create an educational framework where AI agents are treated as economic entities that can be designed, managed, and governed, using a concrete teaching application (Dr. AGENTONOMICS) that serves both as the subject of study and the teaching medium.

Method: Developed a web-based, retrieval-grounded tutor prototype that explains AGENTONOMICS concepts and supports student questions. The system uses a shared interface, intelligence layer, tools, knowledge base, and ecosystem connection with an orchestrator that selects role-specific algorithms.

Result: Created a working prototype for the TUM AI agents course (introduced in summer 2026) that currently serves as a tutor. The system is designed to expand cumulatively into three additional roles: avatar lecturer, design consultant, and meta-agent for constructing specified agents.

Conclusion: The AGENTONOMICS framework demonstrates how agents can teach, apply, and potentially reproduce their own design frameworks, with implications for developing a polycentric AI economy and inviting further discussion on agent-based educational systems.

Abstract: AGENTONOMICS is a framework that treats AI agents as economic entities that can be designed, managed, and governed through an integrated management architecture. Dr. AGENTONOMICS is its first application: a lecture agent developed in the context of the TUM course on AI agents in business administration. Conceived during the winter semester 2025/26 and first introduced to students in the summer semester 2026, it serves as a didactic experiment in which the agent is both the object that students study and the medium through which they learn and apply the framework. The current prototype is a web-based, retrieval-grounded tutor that explains AGENTONOMICS concepts and supports student questions. This report argues that the same system can grow beyond tutoring into three additional cumulative roles: an avatar lecturer that delivers multimodal instruction, a design consultant that guides students through the AGENTONOMICS Design & Management Reference Framework (ADMRF), and a meta-agent that helps construct the agents students have specified. These roles are cumulative because they share the same interface, intelligence layer, tools, knowledge base, and ecosystem connection, while an orchestrator selects the role-specific algorithm required for each task. We present the architecture of the prototype, outline its development roadmap, and discuss its implications for a polycentric AI economy. This report is intended to invite further discussion on how agents can teach, apply, and eventually reproduce the frameworks by which they are designed.

</details>


### [70] [Behaviorally Adaptive Visual Diversion for Inclusive and Resilient Digital Assessment Delivery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03531)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sriram,Parali Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BAVD framework adaptively overlays non-semantic visual noise on assessment content based on candidate behavior to prevent cheating via screen capture while preserving accessibility for legitimate users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current assessment security measures (browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, behavioral analytics) are designed independently, overlook accessibility needs, and don't adequately address screen capture/sharing vulnerabilities in high-stakes digital assessments.

Method: Proposes Behaviorally-Adaptive Visual Diversion (BAVD) - a theoretical framework using coupled dynamical systems (Diversion Field Generator, Rendering Tensor, Behavior Tensor, Composite Integrity Functional, Multi-dimensional Entropy Model) that composites adaptive, non-semantic visual noise with assessment content based on observed behavior, while incorporating accessibility-aware attenuation.

Result: Establishes theoretical properties for content fidelity, rendering stability, entropy boundedness, integrity tracking, and closed-loop adaptation stability. The framework includes explicit threat modeling, deployment assumptions, and accessibility-security trade-off analysis.

Conclusion: BAVD provides a mathematically grounded foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware assessment security that can reduce usefulness of screen capture/sharing while minimizing intrusion for legitimate candidates, offering basis for future empirical validation.

Abstract: Institutions increasingly rely on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioral analytics to secure high-stakes digital assessments, yet these mechanisms are commonly designed and evaluated independently and often overlook learner accessibility. This paper introduces Behaviorally-Adaptive Visual Diversion (BAVD), a theoretical framework in which a synthetic, non-semantic visual field is composited with assessment content and adaptively modulated according to observed candidate behavior. The underlying assessment content is never altered; only its visual presentation is modified to reduce the usefulness of unauthorized screen capture or screen sharing while remaining minimally intrusive for legitimate candidates. The framework further incorporates an accessibility-aware attenuation mechanism that reduces or suppresses diversion intensity for candidates with approved visual-processing accommodations. We formulate the model using a coupled dynamical-systems representation comprising a Diversion Field Generator, Rendering Tensor, Behavior Tensor, Composite Integrity Functional, and Multi-dimensional Entropy Model, and establish theoretical properties for content fidelity, rendering stability, entropy boundedness, integrity tracking, and closed-loop adaptation stability. The framework explicitly states its threat model, identifies deployment assumptions and limitations, and discusses the trade-off between accessibility and capture resistance. This work provides a mathematically grounded foundation for behaviorally adaptive and accessibility-aware assessment delivery and offers a basis for future empirical validation in trusted digital assessment platforms.

</details>


### [71] [Soft Guidance Starts to Outperform CoT Prompting as LLMs Improve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03550)
*Denys Pushkin,Albert Q. Jiang,Aryo Lotfi,Colin Sandon,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Modern LLMs often perform better with simple zero-shot prompts than with traditional few-shot Chain-of-Thought prompting, as CoT can distract models from core reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To revisit the effectiveness of standard Chain-of-Thought prompting since many modern LLMs now produce CoT-style responses natively for reasoning tasks, questioning whether the original CoT technique is still optimal.

Method: Evaluated several modern mid-sized language models on math problem-solving tasks, comparing performance between zero-shot settings, zero-shot CoT prompts, and few-shot CoT examples.

Result: Models specialized for reasoning achieve better performance in simple zero-shot settings than with few-shot CoT examples (e.g., Mathstral on GSM8K improved from ~77% to ~84%). For general-purpose models, zero-shot CoT also outperforms few-shot CoT baselines.

Conclusion: Standard CoT prompting increasingly acts as a distraction as models grow stronger due to a 'guidance-distraction' tradeoff where CoT demands style adaptation and formatting compliance that can distract from core reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting remains the standard baseline for evaluating models' reasoning abilities. Originally, this technique was introduced to elicit step-by-step reasoning from large language models (LLMs), which would otherwise tend to directly output the final answer. However, many modern LLMs produce CoT-style responses \textit{natively} when presented with reasoning tasks, which made us revisit the effectiveness of standard CoT prompting.
  We evaluate several modern mid-sized language models on a math problem-solving task and find that models specialized for reasoning achieve better performance in a simple zero-shot setting than when using few-shot CoT examples - significantly surpassing officially reported results at no additional cost (e.g., from $\sim$77\% to $\sim$84\% for Mathstral on GSM8K). For the tested general-purpose model, a zero-shot CoT prompt is also sufficient to outperform a few-shot CoT baseline. We attribute this to a `guidance-distraction' tradeoff: standard CoT prompting also demands style adaptation, formatting compliance, and potentially undesired contextualization, which can distract models from the core reasoning task.
  Our findings suggest that using standard CoT prompting increasingly acts as a source of distraction as models grow stronger.

</details>


### [72] [Enhancing Tabular Learners with Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03565)
*Günther Schindler,Maximilian Schambach,Johannes Höhne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASE (Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings) improves tabular ML by using LLM semantics to better understand feature names and cell entries, creating context-aware embeddings that boost performance on semantic-rich datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tabular learners treat textual features as discrete symbols, ignoring the rich semantics in feature names and cell entries, creating a 'semantic vacuum' that limits performance on semantically rich datasets.

Method: Uses a contextualization strategy: pre-fills the KV cache of a custom-trained Gemma 3-based Tabular Language Model with a representative sample of rows to establish persistent semantic anchors, enabling dynamically contextualized row embeddings that resolve semantic ambiguities.

Result: Substantially improves tabular learner performance across several benchmarks (CARTE, TextTab, TabArena), particularly in low-data regimes, demonstrating better handling of semantically rich datasets.

Conclusion: CASE successfully bridges the gap between LLM semantic understanding and tabular learner statistical capabilities, providing an effective framework for leveraging semantics in tabular data analysis.

Abstract: While modern tabular learners excel at capturing statistical patterns, they frequently operate in a semantic vacuum, treating textual features as discrete symbols, ignoring the rich semantics inherent in feature names or cell entries. We propose CASE (Context-Aware Semantic Embeddings), a novel framework that bridges the gap between the semantic understanding of Large Language Models (LLMs) and the statistical capabilities of tabular learners. Unlike existing methods that embed rows in isolation, CASE utilizes a contextualization strategy: we pre-fill the KV cache of a custom-trained Gemma 3-based Tabular Language Model with a representative sample of rows to establish a persistent anchor of the dataset's semantics. This ensures that generated row embeddings are dynamically contextualized, resolving semantic ambiguities and anchoring representations in domain-specific context. Our experiments across several benchmarks (CARTE, TextTab, and TabArena) demonstrate that CASE substantially improves the performance of tabular learners on semantically rich datasets, particularly in low-data regimes.

</details>


### [73] [Policy Fragmentation or Institutional Alignment? Institutional Governance of AI in Universities and Business Schools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03584)
*Lydia Manikonda,Dominique Outlaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of AI policies across US higher education reveals divergent priorities: institutional policies focus on data security while school-level policies emphasize pedagogy, with business schools rarely having discipline-specific policies despite unique needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher education institutions struggle to balance teaching AI fundamentals with integrating emerging tools for workforce readiness, while grappling with how to incorporate AI into curricula and govern it through policies set at different institutional levels.

Method: Used natural language processing (NLP) to analyze institutional AI policies from higher education institutions across 34 US states, examining differences between university-level and school-level policies, with specific focus on business school policies.

Result: Found clear divergence: university-level policies emphasize data security and risk mitigation while school-level policies focus on pedagogical applications and tool usage. Few business schools maintain distinct AI policies, creating misalignment with discipline-specific learning objectives and posing challenges for faculty, students, and accreditation.

Conclusion: Guidelines should align with broader institutional policies while addressing discipline-specific learning objectives and evolving workforce demands to bridge the gap between institutional risk management and practical educational needs.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming high-skilled domains, requiring higher education institutions (HEI) to balance the teaching of foundational principles with the integration of emerging tools to ensure workforce readiness. While HEI are increasingly adopting AI, many continue to grapple with how it should be incorporated into curricula and governed through policy, especially when such policies are set at different levels of an institution. This research analyzes AI policies across HEI from 34 states in the United States to investigate what these policies entail and how policies set across institutions as well as within different levels at an institution differ. Using natural language processing (NLP) to analyze institutional AI policies, we find a clear divergence: university-level policies emphasize data security and risk mitigation whereas school-level policies, when present, focus on pedagogical applications and tool usage. When focusing on business school specific policies, relatively few business schools maintain AI policies distinct from university frameworks, creating misalignment with discipline-specific learning objectives. This gap poses challenges particularly for faculty and students as well as for accreditation purposes. Our insights suggest that guidelines should be aligned with broader institutional policies while addressing discipline-specific learning objectives and evolving workforce demands.

</details>


### [74] [From Social Coding to Agentic Coding: Productivity and Relational Reconfiguration in Open-Source Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03585)
*Mengying Zhou,Yongjie Yin,Yang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Coding agents increase software development productivity by 34-39% but reduce public knowledge sharing, concentrating gains among already active developers and shifting collaboration from public human interactions to private agent-mediated loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how generative coding agents (CAs) affect open-source software communities, specifically whether they shift development activities from public human collaboration to private human-agent interactions, and what impact this has on both productivity and knowledge generation within these digital public infrastructures.

Method: Used LLM-based multi-agent simulation initialized with real GitHub data from 1,084 active developers and their repository relationships. After warm-up with historical commits, branched same community state into parallel No-CA and CA conditions for 4-week simulations to compare outcomes.

Result: CA introduction increased planned and completed tasks by 34.0% and 39.0%, reduced median completion time from 45 to 20 minutes, but adoption only reached 26.0%. Gains concentrated among already active/well-connected developers. Direct human-human interaction declined from 32.4% to 11.6%, while CA-involved modes increased to 57.3%. Public knowledge under CA condition provided less support for later tasks, achieving only 22.3% knowledge coverage vs 81.1% in real-human corpus on retrieval benchmark.

Conclusion: There's a productivity-public knowledge tension: coding agents increase technical production efficiency but shift work to agent-mediated or private loops, leaving public records less useful for future contributors and potentially exacerbating inequality within developer communities.

Abstract: Open-source software communities are a form of digital public infrastructure that not only produces code, but also generates public knowledge and interpersonal relationships through visible collaboration. Generative coding agents (CAs) are an advanced tool to improve development efficiency while shifting part of activities from public human interaction to private human-agent loops. We study this shift using an LLM-based multi-agent simulation initialized with real GitHub data from 1,084 active developers and their repository relationships. After a warm-up with historical commits, we branch the same community state into parallel No-CA and CA conditions for 4-week simulations. CA introduction increases planned and completed tasks by 34.0% and 39.0%, respectively, and reduces median completion time from 45 to 20 minutes. However, adoption reaches only 26.0%, and the gains concentrate among developers who are already more active and well connected. CAs also restructure task execution pathways. Direct human-human interaction declines from 32.4% to 11.6%, while CA-involved modes increase to 57.3%, including 40.3% completed through CA-assisted self-loops. Public knowledge generated under CA condition also provides less support for later tasks. On a standardized retrieval benchmark, the CA corpus achieves 22.3% knowledge coverage, far below the 81.1% achieved by the real-human corpus, and requires more retrieval steps with a lower success rate. These results reveal a productivity-public knowledge tension: coding agents increase technical production, but more work shifts to agent-mediated or private loops, leaving public records less useful to future contributors.

</details>


### [75] [FOUND-AF: Benchmarking ECG Foundation Models for Atrial Fibrillation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03597)
*Amirhossein Taleshinosrati,Yangyang Wang,Atitaya Phoemsuk,Vahid Abolghasemi,Naser Hossein Motlagh,Sadasivan Puthusserypady,Daniel Teichmann,Abdolrahman Peimankar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FOUND-AF benchmarking framework evaluates ECG foundation models for atrial fibrillation detection, finding ECGFounder offers best performance with good computational efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atrial fibrillation is common and dangerous, but unclear which recent ECG foundation models work best due to inconsistent evaluation methods across studies.

Method: Created FOUND-AF framework with standardized evaluation: tested 9 foundation models as frozen feature extractors with consistent preprocessing, XGBoost classifier, and rigorous validation on 4 ECG datasets.

Result: ECGFounder consistently performed best across datasets with optimal balance of accuracy, model size, inference time, and memory usage.

Conclusion: FOUND-AF provides reproducible framework for model selection; compact clinically-pretrained encoders like ECGFounder enable robust, efficient AF detection across varied settings.

Abstract: Atrial fibrillation (AF) is the most common sustained cardiac arrhythmia and is associated with increased risks of stroke, heart failure, and mortality. Recent ECG foundation models offer transferable representations for automated AF detection. However, their relative effectiveness remains unclear because existing studies use different datasets, preprocessing procedures, classifiers, and validation protocols. This study presents FOUND-AF, a unified, leakage-controlled, and deployment-oriented benchmarking framework that evaluates the quality of pretrained ECG representations under identical experimental conditions. Nine publicly available foundation models from five families, including HuBERT-ECG, CLEF, ST-MEM, ECG-JEPA, and ECGFounder, were evaluated across four heterogeneous ECG datasets, namely AFDB, CinC2017, CPSC2021, and LTAFDB. All models were used as frozen feature extractors with standardized preprocessing, model-native resampling, a fixed XGBoost classifier, and recording-level grouped cross-validation. The evaluation included classification metrics, receiver operating characteristic analysis, paired recording-level bootstrap comparisons with Holm correction, embedding-space visualization, and computational efficiency profiling. The ECGFounder model consistently achieved the strongest overall performance across datasets while offering a favorable trade-off between accuracy, model size, inference time, and memory usage.
