FOUND-AF therefore provides a reproducible framework for selecting ECG foundation models and demonstrates that compact, clinically pretrained encoders can support robust and computationally efficient AF detection across heterogeneous acquisition settings.

</details>


### [76] [Large language models for partial differential equation workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03600)
*Han Wan,Rui Zhang,Hao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs are enabling end-to-end PDE workflows from model discovery to simulation feedback, but face data scarcity and real-world application gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PDEs require complete executable workflows (not just equations) connecting modeling, solvers, diagnostics, and decisions. LLMs offer potential to bridge natural language, symbolic math, code, and solver outputs across PDE research stages.

Method: Examine recent advances in LLM-assisted PDE research across three stages: 1) discovery/formulation of governing models, 2) generation/revision of numerical solvers, and 3) use of simulation feedback for control, design, and optimization.

Result: Current LLM systems primarily serve as workflow-level interfaces across PDE research stages. Key limitations include scarcity of high-quality datasets/benchmarks for knowledge discovery and real applications, plus persistent gaps between simulation results and real-world systems.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted PDE workflows represent a critical testbed for developing scientific AI systems that can integrate language, computation, physical constraints, and real-world decision-making, despite current limitations in data and application transfer.

Abstract: Partial differential equations (PDEs) become actionable in science and engineering not as isolated formulae, but as executable workflows that connect modelling assumptions, governing equations, numerical solvers, diagnostics, and decisions. Large language models (LLMs) are beginning to support such workflows by linking natural language, symbolic mathematics, code, solver outputs, and feedback. Here we examine recent advances in LLM-assisted PDE research across three stages: the discovery and formulation of governing models, the generation and revision of executable numerical solvers, and the use of simulation feedback to support control, design, and optimization. Across these stages, current systems act primarily as workflow-level interfaces. Despite this progress, the field remains limited by the scarcity of high-quality datasets and benchmarks, especially for knowledge discovery and real-world applications, where expert annotation, executable problem construction, and task-level feedback require substantial domain effort. A further challenge is the persistent gap between simulation-based results and real-world scientific and engineering systems, which limits the direct transfer of numerical simulations, control policies, and optimized designs to practical settings. These challenges make LLM-assisted PDE workflows a critical testbed for developing scientific AI systems that can connect language, computation, physical constraints, and real-world decision-making.

</details>


### [77] [FraQ: Efficient Coordinate-Space Recompression for Federated Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03605)
*Shenghui Li,Thiemo Voigt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FraQ is an efficient coordinate-space recompression method for federated LoRA that solves the aggregation mismatch problem while reducing communication and computation costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a fundamental aggregation mismatch in federated fine-tuning with LoRA: naively averaging client LoRA factors doesn't recover the average of their induced updates in full weight space. While exact aggregation in full weight space followed by recompression solves this, it's computationally expensive and memory-intensive due to dense matrix decomposition.

Method: FraQ starts from stacked LoRA factors representing the exact aggregate, factorizes it into an orthonormal basis and compact coordinate matrix. It then recovers the singular spectrum from a small Gram matrix, selects the smallest rank satisfying a prescribed energy threshold, and maps the selected coordinate subspace back through the basis to construct the global adapter.

Result: Experiments on text classification and commonsense reasoning benchmarks show that FraQ achieves accuracy close to uncompressed baselines while substantially reducing downlink communication with low server-side recompression overhead.

Conclusion: FraQ provides an efficient solution to the aggregation mismatch problem in federated LoRA, enabling accurate collaborative adaptation of LLMs while maintaining communication efficiency and low computational overhead.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient collaborative adaptation of Large Language Models (LLMs) without centralizing private data. However, LoRA's two-factor parameterization creates an aggregation mismatch across clients: naively averaging the factors does not recover the average of their induced updates. This mismatch can be avoided by forming the exact aggregate in the full weight space and then recompressing it, but decomposing the resulting dense matrix is computationally expensive and memory-intensive. We propose FraQ, an efficient coordinate-space recompression method for federated LoRA. Starting from stacked factors that exactly represent the aggregate, FraQ factorizes it into an orthonormal basis and a compact coordinate matrix. It then recovers the singular spectrum from a small Gram matrix, selects the smallest rank satisfying a prescribed energy threshold, and maps the selected coordinate subspace back through the basis to construct the global adapter. Experiments on text classification and commonsense reasoning benchmarks show that FraQ achieves accuracy close to uncompressed baselines while substantially reducing downlink communication with low server-side recompression overhead.

</details>


### [78] [Learning Clinical-Trial Strategy: Offline Policy Training for Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03606)
*William Bolton,Philip Torr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper frames oncology clinical development as an offline decision-making problem, creating a dataset from heterogeneous clinical data and comparing offline learning methods to plan future trial portfolios, with reward-weighted behavioral cloning performing best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical development involves sequential decision-making under uncertainty with heterogeneous evidence. There's a need for systematic approaches to plan optimal trial portfolios from diverse historical data sources.

Method: Constructed a temporal dataset combining 31.7k heterogeneous public data records into 881 offline decision episodes across 45 historical programs. Compared four offline objectives (behavioral cloning, reward-weighted behavioral cloning, learned-reward training, and value-based implicit Q-learning) against frontier LLM agents with date-gated retrieval scaffolds, evaluated across held-out splits.

Result: Models trained offline outperformed non-fine-tuned baselines, especially in post-August 2025 contamination-clean holdout. Reward-weighted behavioral cloning performed best with 46.2% indication F1 and 14.2% strict F1 vs 25.0% and 2.1% for best tool agent.

Conclusion: Structured offline learning can teach agents to effectively plan clinical experiments, with reward-weighted behavioral cloning emerging as the most promising approach for oncology clinical development portfolio planning.

Abstract: Clinical development is sequential decision-making under uncertainty, where a sponsor must plan a portfolio of experiments from heterogeneous evidence. We study this setting by framing oncology clinical development as an offline decision-making problem in which an agent predicts the next six-month trial portfolio of an oncology drug program from information available at the decision date. To support this, we construct a temporal dataset that combines 31.7k heterogeneous public data records, including trial registries, regulatory reviews, sponsor filings, utilization data, and epidemiology, into 881 offline decision episodes across 45 historical programs. We compare four offline objectives: behavioral cloning, reward-weighted behavioral cloning, learned-reward training, and value-based implicit Q-learning against four frontier LLM agents that share a common date-gated retrieval scaffold across held-out drug, sponsor, drug-class, and temporal splits. Models trained offline outperform the non-fine-tuned baselines, particularly in the post-August 2025 contamination-clean holdout. Reward-weighted behavioral cloning performs the best, obtaining 46.2% indication F1 and 14.2% strict F1 against 25.0% and 2.1%, respectively, for the best-performing tool agent on each metric. These results suggest that structured offline learning can teach agents to plan clinical experiments.

</details>


### [79] [Formal Verification of Agentic Systems over Operational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03609)
*Alejandro J. Mercado,Alessio Lomuscio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for verifying LLM-driven agentic systems against business requirements by modeling them as Stateful Tool-Enabled Agentic Deployments (STEADs), proving verification is generally undecidable but PSPACE-complete under finite-domain restrictions, and introducing a canonical deployment wrapper to ensure verification conditions are met.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems powered by LLMs are increasingly deployed in real-world workflows where they act on persistent operational data, but existing approaches fail to provide system-level guarantees for verifying these systems against business requirements that govern workflow execution and data evolution.

Method: Formalize LLM-driven agentic systems as Stateful Tool-Enabled Agentic Deployments (STEADs) with defined semantics, study verification against First-Order Computation Tree Logic (FO-CTL) specifications, identify sufficient conditions for exact preservation of FO-CTL specifications under finite-domain restriction, and introduce a canonical deployment wrapper that guarantees these conditions.

Result: Proved that verifying STEADs against FO-CTL specifications is undecidable in general, but PSPACE-complete under finite-domain restrictions with sufficient conditions. Showed that LLM-driven agents can violate these conditions, introduced a canonical deployment wrapper that guarantees condition satisfaction while preserving behavior, and proved computing canonical representations is graph-isomorphism-hard.

Conclusion: The paper provides a formal framework for verifying LLM-driven agentic systems, addressing a critical gap in system-level guarantees for real-world deployments. By modeling systems as STEADs and introducing a canonical deployment wrapper, it enables practical verification under certain conditions while revealing fundamental computational limits of the verification problem.

Abstract: Agentic systems driven by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world workflows where they act on persistent operational data. Before deployment, these systems need to be verified against business requirements that govern workflow execution and data evolution. However, existing approaches do not provide such system-level guarantees, as they mainly constrain or analyse behaviour at the agent's interface level. We study here the verification of agentic systems comprising a single LLM and a tool orchestration harness over relational operational data. We formalise them as Stateful Tool-Enabled Agentic Deployments (STEADs), give their semantics, define the problem of verifying them against First-Order Computation Tree Logic (FO-CTL) specifications, and show that it is undecidable. We identify sufficient conditions for exact preservation of FO-CTL specifications under a finite-domain restriction, over which verification is PSPACE-complete. The key requirement is that renaming opaque identifiers in the data must correspondingly rename the selected tool calls. We show that LLM-driven agents can violate this condition and introduce a canonical deployment wrapper that guarantees it for arbitrary base agents while preserving already-equivariant behaviour. We prove that computing canonical representations required by this construction is graph-isomorphism-hard. Finally, we illustrate our framework on an LLM agent orchestrating a case-management workflow.

</details>


### [80] [Rethinking Modality Reliability in Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Incomplete Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03611)
*Chunlei Meng,Jacqueline J. Pang,Pengbin Feng,Zhenyu Yu,Chun Ouyang,Zhongxue Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MRCF introduces explicit modality reliability modeling for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with incomplete observations, addressing reliability mismatch and propagation bias through reliability-aware branches and guided interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MSA methods for incomplete observations treat modality reliability only implicitly, leading to two key issues: reliability mismatch (varying affective evidence across samples) and reliability propagation bias (degraded modalities adversely affecting cross-modal interaction).

Method: MRCF proposes a Modality Reliability-Calibrated Framework with three components: 1) Reliability-Aware Branch that estimates sample-specific modality reliability from intramodal quality cues and cross-modal consistency, 2) Reliability-Guided Interaction Branch that uses reliability scores to modulate cross-modal information flow, and 3) Reliability-Calibrated Fusion Module that integrates reliability and semantic cues for final prediction.

Result: MRCF achieves strong performance on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS datasets under standard incomplete-observation protocols. Further analyses show that explicit reliability modeling helps mitigate reliability mismatch and reliability propagation bias during interaction and fusion.

Conclusion: Explicit modeling of modality reliability is crucial for incomplete-observation MSA. MRCF effectively addresses reliability mismatch and propagation bias, demonstrating that reliability-aware frameworks can improve multimodal sentiment analysis when some modalities are missing or degraded.

Abstract: Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA) integrates text, audio, and vision to infer human affect, yet real-world multimodal observations are often incomplete. Existing methods for incomplete-observation MSA mainly follow two paradigms. Reconstruction-based methods recover missing information from observed modalities, while joint-representation methods learn directly from incomplete inputs. Although effective, these methods usually treat modality reliability only implicitly within representation learning or fusion design rather than modeling it explicitly. We argue that modality reliability is a central variable in incomplete-observation settings. Failure to model it explicitly gives rise to two related issues. The first is reliability mismatch, in which the affective evidence retained by each modality varies across samples and missing rates. The second is reliability propagation bias, in which messages from degraded modalities may adversely affect cross-modal interaction and predictive performance. To address these issues, we propose MRCF, a Modality Reliability-Calibrated Framework for MSA with incomplete observations. MRCF contains a Reliability-Aware Branch that estimates sample-specific modality reliability from intramodal quality cues and cross-modal semantic consistency, a Reliability-Guided Interaction Branch that uses the estimated scores to modulate cross-modal information flow, and a Reliability-Calibrated Fusion Module that integrates reliability and semantic cues for final prediction. Experiments on CMU-MOSI, CMU-MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that MRCF achieves strong performance under standard incomplete-observation protocols. Further analyses provide evidence that explicit reliability modeling helps mitigate reliability mismatch and reliability propagation bias during interaction and fusion.

</details>


### [81] [Unequal Verdicts: Investigating Gender Bias in LLM-Based Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03627)
*Razieh Chalehchaleh,Reza Farahbakhsh,Noel Crespi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show significant gender bias in fake news detection, with inconsistent verdicts based on speaker gender and systematic male-skeptic patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gender bias in LLM-based fact-checking is underexplored, despite the increasing use of LLMs for automated fake news detection in real-world applications.

Method: Systematically investigated gender bias by augmenting LIAR benchmark with three gender variants of speaker job titles (Neutral, Male, Female). Evaluated six state-of-the-art LLMs across multiple bias and fairness metrics on this augmented dataset.

Result: All models exhibited gender sensitivity: 9.79%-35.13% of statements received inconsistent labels across gender variants, with 6.5%-23.6% flip rates. Five models showed statistically significant directional effects, with strongest effects displaying male-skeptic patterns.

Conclusion: Gender bias undermines both reliability and fairness in LLM-based fake news detection, highlighting the need for bias-aware evaluation and mitigation strategies. The augmented dataset is released to support future research.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for automated fact-checking, yet their susceptibility to gender bias in this context remains underexplored. This study presents the first systematic investigation of gender bias in LLM-based fake news detection using real-world data. We augment the LIAR benchmark with three gender variants of speaker job titles (Neutral, Male, Female) for each statement to test whether veracity judgments vary solely based on gender presentation. Six state-of-the-art LLMs are evaluated across multiple bias and fairness metrics. All models exhibit gender sensitivity: 9.79%-35.13% of statements receive inconsistent labels across the three variants, with Male-Female comparisons showing 6.5%-23.6% flip rates. Two primary bias manifestations are identified: instability (inconsistent judgments) and directionality (systematic favoritism). Five models show statistically significant directional effects, with the strongest effects displaying male-skeptic patterns. These findings demonstrate that gender bias undermines both reliability and fairness in LLM-based fake news detection, highlighting the need for bias-aware evaluation and mitigation strategies. The augmented dataset is publicly released to support future research.

</details>


### [82] [Cross-Layer Interaction under Weight-Space Ablation: A Closed-Form Attention Jacobian Bound and a Test on a Real Pretrained Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03629)
*Abdallah Khemais*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper extends activation patching theory from single-layer idealized models to multi-layer real transformers, derives bounds for cross-layer interactions, validates on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, and discovers emergent circuits for indirect object identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the companion paper's single-layer theoretical results about when activation patching and weight-space ablation agree to more realistic multi-layer transformers and validate on real models.

Method: 1) Mathematical decomposition of multi-layer interactions into same-block terms plus cross-layer remainder; 2) Derivation of Jacobian bound for attention sub-block in closed form; 3) Validation on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct weights; 4) Search for emergent circuits using activation patching on indirect object identification task.

Result: 1) Multi-layer interactions decompose exactly into per-layer terms plus cross-layer remainder; 2) Cross-layer remainder isolated as double integral of mixed second derivative; 3) Jacobian bound derived and verified on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct without violations; 4) Emergent circuit found for indirect object identification with mixed results: shared carrier emerges, collapse/dissociation mostly hold, measurable interaction on 3/5 instances outside same-block case.

Conclusion: The theoretical framework for understanding activation patching/ablation agreement extends beyond single layers, with cross-layer interactions posing additional challenges but being mathematically characterizable. Real transformers exhibit emergent computational structures that partially align with theoretical predictions but show complexity beyond idealized models.

Abstract: A companion paper studies when activation patching and weight-space ablation agree, inside an idealized model where a conditional computation is carried additively through a residual stream. For the one composition in that model where two carriers are architecturally dependent, an attention head and its own layer's normalization-MLP composition, it derives an exact first-order interaction formula, zero when only the MLP is ablated and second-order bounded when the head is also ablated. That result is confined to a single residual block and checked only on small transformers on a synthetic task.
  This paper extends the result past both limits. First, the interaction from ablating carriers spanning several layers decomposes exactly into same-block terms, one per touched layer, plus a cross-layer remainder on which the decomposition makes no claim of smallness. Second, we isolate that remainder exactly, for two layers, as a double integral of a mixed second derivative, and name the missing ingredient needed to bound it: a Jacobian bound for the attention sub-block. We derive this bound in closed form and verify it, without a single violation, against Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct's real weights, though we do not yet chain it across layers. We also give, in closed form, the curvature constant the companion paper's bound leaves unexhibited.
  Third, on that same model, we search for and find an emergent circuit for indirect object identification, never designed into it, using the original activation-patching method for this task, and test collapse, dissociation, and interaction on it. The result is mixed: a shared carrier emerges across all five tested instances, collapse and dissociation hold on most but not all, and a nonzero interaction is measurable on three of five, at layer pairs outside the same-block case the companion theorem covers.

</details>


### [83] [When Teachers Mislead: Spurious-Signal-Aware On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03632)
*Yinuo Jiang,Yongjie Ye,Zhou Tao,Xiang Zhuang,Qiang Zhang,Huajun Chen,Tiankai Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SA-OPD is a new distillation framework that filters spurious token-level supervision by assessing input-groundedness, preventing models from learning misleading patterns from teacher signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current selective OPD methods prioritize confident, informative, or learnable signals but overlook that language models' token-level judgments can be driven by input-agnostic language priors, formatting conventions, or stereotyped reasoning rather than task-specific evidence, leading to spurious signals that provide large gradients but little task improvement.

Method: SA-OPD uses a lightweight input-groundedness proxy to estimate whether token-level distillation signals truly depend on input, then filters tokens exhibiting both low input-groundedness and extreme distillation divergence to remove high-impact spurious updates.

Result: Extensive experiments on LLM and VLM settings show SA-OPD consistently outperforms Vanilla OPD and competitive selective methods, establishing input-groundedness as a key dimension for OPD supervision selection.

Conclusion: SA-OPD offers an effective strategy for mitigating spurious updates in OPD, demonstrating the importance of considering input-groundedness when selecting supervision signals for distillation.

Abstract: On-Policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher capabilities by supervising student-sampled trajectories with dense token-level teacher signals. Recent selective OPD methods improve this process by prioritizing signals that are confident, informative, or learnable. However, the assumptions overlook a fundamental failure mode of language models: their token-level judgments can be driven by input-agnostic language priors, formatting conventions, or stereotyped reasoning templates rather than task-specific evidence. We refer to such optimization-relevant but weakly input-grounded supervision as spurious signals in OPD, which may produce large gradients while contributing little task-improving direction. To mitigate this issue, we propose SA-OPD, a Spurious-Signal-Aware On-Policy Distillation framework that identifies and filters misleading token-level supervision based on input-groundedness and optimization impact. SA-OPD introduces a lightweight input-groundedness proxy estimating whether a token-level distillation signal truly depends on the input. It then filters only tokens that simultaneously exhibit low input-groundedness and extreme distillation divergence, thereby removing high-impact spurious updates and achieving fine-grained OPD optimization. Extensive experiments on both large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) settings demonstrate that SA-OPD consistently outperforms Vanilla OPD and competitive selective methods. These results establish input-groundedness as a key dimension for OPD supervision selection and offer a simple, effective strategy for mitigating spurious updates.

</details>


### [84] [AutoSND: From Execution Evidence to Structural Policies for Automated Network Dismantling Heuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03653)
*Zhijing Hu,Changjun Fan,Yufan Deng,Zhiguang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoSND is a three-stage tree search framework that uses LLMs to automatically design effective network dismantling heuristics by compiling execution evidence into structural policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based methods for automatic heuristic design in network dismantling struggle to transform candidate quality or failure states into structural guidance for subsequent generation, limiting their effectiveness.

Method: Three-stage tree search framework: Stage I explores simple heuristics and archives execution evidence; Stage II compiles records into structural policies about local signals, neighborhood access, and state update ranges; Stage III continues search conditioned on these policies to produce quality-prioritized and speed-prioritized candidates.

Result: Experiments on 12 real-world networks and 3 large networks show AutoSND achieves better search performance and stability, discovers more competitive and structurally interpretable network dismantling programs, with final candidates forming an interpretable structure using residual degree as backbone with local signal adjustments.

Conclusion: AutoSND successfully bridges the gap between candidate execution evidence and structural-level guidance generation, producing competitive and interpretable network dismantling heuristics through systematic policy-guided search.

Abstract: Network dismantling is fundamental to analyzing the robustness and vulnerability of complex systems, yet practical heuristics must balance effectiveness and computational efficiency, and are usually designed manually by researchers. Existing large language model based automatic heuristic design methods can generate and screen candidates, yet they have difficulty further transforming candidate quality or failure states during execution into structural-level guid- ance for subsequent generation. We propose AutoSND, a three stage tree search framework for complete network dismantling pro- grams. Stage I broadly explores from simple heuristics and archives execution evidence. Stage II compiles candidate records into struc- tural policies concerning local signals, neighborhood access, and state update ranges. Stage III continues tree search conditioned on these policies and obtains the final quality prioritized and speed prioritized candidates, AutoSND-Q/S. Experiments on 12 real world networks and 3 large real world networks show that AutoSND achieves better search performance and stability and discovers more competitive and structurally interpretable network disman- tling programs. The final candidates form an interpretable structure that uses residual degree as the backbone, adjusts node order with bounded local signals, and restricts the state update range. Code is available at https://github.com/MirrorNew/AutoSND.

</details>


### [85] [Taming the Implicit: Dual-Channel Risk-Aware Reinforcement Fine-Tuning for Continual Multimodal Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03660)
*Yibei Liu,Jiajun Chen,Qianle Zhang,Tangyue Jin,Mengying Zhu,Meng Xi,Yangyang Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAPO (Risk-Aware Policy Optimization) is a dual-channel framework that addresses catastrophic forgetting in continual reinforcement fine-tuning of multimodal large language models by implementing explicit risk governance through policy scaling and data sampling techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) is widely believed to resist catastrophic forgetting, but under pronounced task distributional shifts, forgetting escalates sharply. This stems from implicit reward-variance regularization in RFT being insufficient to suppress uncontrolled optimization risk.

Method: RAPO is a dual-channel framework: 1) Policy channel: Risk-Aware Policy Scaling adaptively calibrates per-sample update magnitude via rollout reliability and Fisher-inspired local predictive sensitivity; 2) Data channel: Risk-Aware Dynamic Bucket Sampling reorganizes training batches through dynamic risk stratification, steering optimization toward informative yet stable samples. It's a plug-and-play strategy requiring no cross-task memory.

Result: On the public MLLM-CL benchmark, RAPO reduces final forgetting by 79.8% relative to its RLOO backbone while retaining new-task competitiveness.

Conclusion: RAPO provides an effective solution for explicit risk governance in continual reinforcement fine-tuning, addressing catastrophic forgetting in multimodal large language models while maintaining task performance, with broad applicability to any RFT algorithm without modification.

Abstract: Reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) is widely believed to inherently resist catastrophic forgetting in continual post-training of multimodal large language models. Under pronounced task distributional shifts, however, forgetting across representative RFT algorithms escalates sharply. This stems from the implicit reward-variance regularization inherent to RFT, which proves incapable of suppressing uncontrolled optimization risk. We propose Risk-Aware Policy Optimization (RAPO), the first dual-channel framework for explicit risk governance in continual RFT. On the policy channel, Risk-Aware Policy Scaling adaptively calibrates per-sample update magnitude via rollout reliability and Fisher-inspired local predictive sensitivity; on the data channel, Risk-Aware Dynamic Bucket Sampling reorganizes training batches through dynamic risk stratification, steering optimization toward informative yet stable samples. As a plug-and-play strategy requiring no cross-task memory, RAPO generalizes to any RFT algorithm without modification. On the public MLLM-CL benchmark, RAPO reduces final forgetting by 79.8% relative to its RLOO backbone while retaining new-task competitiveness.

</details>


### [86] [Shielding for Higher-Order Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03662)
*Filip Cano,Thomas A. Henzinger,Konstantin Kueffner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a method for synthesizing finite-state safety shields that enforce high-order smoothness constraints (like jerk, acceleration limits) by using finite differences and reducing the problem to safety games over history state spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical safety shields only consider binary safety predicates (safe/unsafe states), but many cyber-physical applications like autonomous vehicles require finer-grained constraints involving derivatives of state (speed regulations, acceleration limits, jerk limits). Current approaches are too coarse-grained for these high-order smoothness requirements.

Method: The paper defines differential safety properties using finite differences over discretized state space, characterizes their expressiveness, and reduces shield synthesis to ordinary safety games over a history state space. It provides a synthesis algorithm that stores exactly k past states for properties of order k, and an iterative synthesis procedure for maximally permissive shields operating over hierarchies of derivative constraints.

Result: The authors prove that storing k past states is necessary for properties of order k. They develop an iterative synthesis algorithm that solves constraints in increasing order, using solutions at each iteration to prune unsafe state space regions, making synthesis more efficient in practice.

Conclusion: The paper presents a formal framework for synthesizing safety shields that handle high-order smoothness constraints, filling an important gap in runtime enforcement mechanisms for cyber-physical systems requiring derivative-level safety guarantees.

Abstract: Safety shields are runtime enforcement mechanisms that restrict the actions of a controller to guarantee safety. Classical shields are usually synthesised for state predicates: the current physical state is either safe or unsafe, and the shield disables precisely those actions that can force the system into an unsafe state in the future. In many cyber-physical applications this view is too coarse. A vehicle approaching an obstacle should not only avoid collision, but also respect speed regulations, force limits induced by acceleration, and jerk limits to prevent injuries. From a physical perspective, these requirements are predicated over the derivatives of the state. This paper develops a finite-state safety-game construction for such high-order smoothness constraints. We define differential safety properties using finite differences over a discretised state space, characterise their expressiveness, and reduce shield synthesis to an ordinary safety game over a history state space. We give a synthesis algorithm whose shields store exactly $k$ past states for properties of order $k$ and prove that this memory is necessary. We describe an iterative synthesis procedure for a maximally permissive shield that operates over hierarchies of derivative constraints. The algorithm solves constraints iteratively in increasing order and uses the solution at each iteration to prune the state space for the next constraint. This makes shield synthesis more efficient in practice, as the algorithm refrains from exploring large regions of the state space that are known to be unsafe.

</details>


### [87] [PhyAI: Real-Time Physical AI at the Edge, Scalable Rollouts in the Cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03682)
*Chenghua Wang,Daliang Xu,Dongqi Cai,Duojin Sun,Hao Zhang,Haoze Qian,Huaiyuan Zhang,Jinshuo Cui,Kezhao Zhao,Longxi Gao,Mengwei Xu,Rongjie Yi,Tianyue Zhang,Weikai Xie,Xiyuan Tan,Xuanzhe Liu,Yingying Qin,Yiwen Lu,Yuan Yao,Yuezhi Zu,Yunhan Guo,Ziqi Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhyAI is a unified inference engine for Physical AI policies that works across different deployment settings (cloud, edge, onboard) with significant speed improvements over official implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical AI policies require inference in multiple settings (model evaluation, cloud RL rollout, edge GPU serving, onboard deployment), but these typically use separate inference programs despite sharing checkpoint and action semantics, creating unnecessary complexity and inefficiency.

Method: Built PhyAI, a Physical AI inference engine with a single runtime that keeps architecture-specific logic (conditioning, solver, cache, output) in model adapters while sharing graph execution, kernels, memory management, and parallel services across different hardware setups.

Result: Achieved 1.40x-4.65x speedups over official implementations of various models (pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, MiniCPM-Robot). Reduced latency from 2.46 to 1.18 seconds on eight H20 GPUs for Cosmos3-Nano-Policy-DROID (2.08x speedup). Successfully integrated MiniCPM-Robot on release day via adapter interface.

Conclusion: PhyAI provides a unified inference solution with competitive performance across diverse deployment scenarios, though specialized runtimes can still be faster in some configurations. The paper also introduces the "control-time Roofline" concept to distinguish inference-bound from environment-bound control.

Abstract: Physical AI policies require inference throughout their lifecycle, including model evaluation, cloud reinforcement learning rollout, edge GPU serving, and onboard deployment. Although these settings share the same checkpoint and action semantics, they often rely on separate inference programs. To unify them, we build PhyAI, a Physical AI inference engine with a single runtime that keeps architecture-specific conditioning, solver, cache, and output logic in model adapters while sharing graph execution, kernels, memory management, and parallel services. The same codebase runs vision-language-action (VLA) models and world-action models (WAMs) on single or multiple GPUs across onboard, edge, and cloud deployments. We used the adapter interface to add MiniCPM-Robot on the day of its release. PhyAI achieves 1.40x-4.65x speedups over the official implementations of pi0, pi0.5, GR00T N1.7, and MiniCPM-Robot. On Cosmos3-Nano-Policy-DROID it reduces latency from 2.46 to 1.18 s on eight H20 GPUs (CFG=2, TP=4), a 2.08x speedup. Specialized runtimes remain faster in several configurations, so our goal is one runtime with competitive latency rather than the fastest result in every case. Detailed profiles reveal why different models need different execution policies: on a Hopper-series GPU at batch size one, the pi0.5 action expert accounts for 8.8% of FLOPs but 57.2% of latency; at batch size 32 its share drops to 13.5% and throughput reaches about 100 samples/s. Cosmos3 remains generation-dominated and gains only 14.3% throughput as batch size increases from 1 to 16. We further introduce the control-time Roofline, which distinguishes inference-bound from environment-bound control; the measured pi0.5 points on four LIBERO suites are environment-bound while Cosmos3 stays inference-bound. Code and benchmarks: https://github.com/mingti-org/phyai.

</details>


### [88] [LiveEvalBench: Toward Open-World Evaluation for Web Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03689)
*Yiyao Wang,Zhen Wen,Yinghao Tang,Yixiao Fu,Lin Yuan,Xiaolau Zhang,Jun Zhou,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveEvalBench is an automated evaluation framework that reformulates web-generation as an agentic, adaptive process using collaborative reviewer workflows to assess LLM-generated frontend projects in interactive, implementation-diverse contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks treat web generation as static evaluation, but frontend artifacts are interactive, admit diverse valid implementations, and evolve faster than rigid evaluation pipelines can accommodate. There's a need for a paradigm shift in how we evaluate LLM-generated web projects.

Method: LiveEvalBench uses a collaborative review workflow with three agentic roles: Build Engineer (deployment), Code Engineer (code inspection), and UI Tester (browser-based interaction). It implements an adaptive protocol combining shared rubrics for cross-model comparability with implementation-grounded criteria tailored to each artifact. The framework supports extensible integration of new evaluator roles and assessment dimensions.

Result: Experiments across diverse real-world web-generation scenarios demonstrate that LiveEvalBench aligns closely with human expert judgment and provides fine-grained insights into frontier models' web generation capabilities. The framework successfully addresses the gaps in static evaluation approaches.

Conclusion: LiveEvalBench provides an effective automated framework for evaluating LLM-generated frontend projects that better captures the interactive, diverse, and rapidly evolving nature of web development compared to traditional static benchmarks.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly capable of synthesizing executable frontend projects, yet existing benchmarks still treat web generation as a static evaluation problem. We argue that frontend artifacts demand a different paradigm: they are interactive rather than static, admit diverse yet equally valid implementations, and evolve faster than rigid pipelines can accommodate. To address these gaps, we present LiveEvalBench, an automated framework that reformulates web-generation evaluation as an agentic, adaptive, and extensible process. LiveEvalBench instantiates evaluation as a collaborative review workflow, in which a Build Engineer, a Code Engineer, and a UI Tester collectively gather evidence across the full lifecycle of a frontend project, from deployment and code inspection to browser-based interaction. To handle implementation diversity, an adaptive protocol couples shared rubrics for cross-model comparability with implementation-grounded criteria tailored to each artifact. The framework further supports incremental integration of new evaluator roles and assessment dimensions without pipeline redesign. Experiments across diverse real-world web-generation scenarios show that LiveEvalBench aligns closely with human expert judgment and provides fine-grained insights into frontier models' web generation capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/wyysteelhead/LiveEvalBench

</details>


### [89] [TARL: Transaction-Aware Reliable Ledgers for Executable Memory Management in Long-Term Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03699)
*Han Xiao,Hongjun Xu,Xin Zhang,Yidong Chen,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TARL introduces a 5-action memory update framework to replace binary Write/Hold decisions, improving long-term agent memory by distinguishing between adding, ignoring, revising, rejecting, or deferring information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory systems use binary Write/Hold decisions that cannot properly distinguish between different types of memory updates (adding, ignoring, revising, rejecting, deferring), leading to repeated distortion from single update errors and fundamentally different memory states.

Method: TARL maps statements to five executable actions, identifies affected memory, resolves temporal scope, compares source reliability, and updates accepted, pending, and rejected ledgers. The model is trained by comparing memory states produced by alternative update operations to select the correct one.

Result: TARL improves action prediction and state recovery across multiple evaluation scenarios (in-domain, cross-source, temporal, counterfactual, sequential), reduces memory pollution, preserves conflicting evidence, and limits cumulative corruption.

Conclusion: TARL provides a more nuanced memory update framework that significantly improves long-term agent memory management by replacing binary decisions with five distinct actions, reducing errors and preserving evidence integrity.

Abstract: Persistent memory helps long-term agents retain knowledge, yet a single update error can repeatedly distort future retrieval and reasoning. Most existing systems reduce memory updating to a binary Write/Hold decision, which cannot distinguish whether new information should be added, ignored, used to revise an outdated belief, rejected as unreliable, or deferred for verification. These choices may share the same binary label while producing fundamentally different memory states. We introduce TARL, a memory state update framework that maps each statement to one of five executable actions. TARL identifies the affected memory, resolves its temporal scope, compares source reliability, and updates accepted, pending, and rejected ledgers. It is further trained by comparing the memory states produced by alternative update operations, encouraging the model to select the operation that leads to the correct result. We also introduce TARL-Mem, a benchmark with fine-grained action labels and next-state targets. Across in-domain, cross-source, temporal, counterfactual, and sequential evaluations, TARL improves action prediction and state recovery, reduces memory pollution, preserves conflicting evidence, and limits cumulative corruption. The complete model implementation is provided in the supplementary material.

</details>


### [90] [Less Traffic, Better Outcomes: Competition-Aware Request Dispatch in Real-Time Ad Exchanges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03705)
*Jonaid Shianifar,Blaz Mramor,Fangda Zou,Matthieu C. Martin,Xingsheng Guo,Zhihua Zhu,Rong Zhou,Bichen Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A competition-aware request dispatch framework reduces RTB ad exchange request volume to DSPs by 34.2% while increasing net revenue by 4.6% through intelligent filtering and adaptive thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RTB ad exchanges forward nearly all incoming requests to DSPs, but only a small fraction receive bids. This over-distribution weakens auction outcomes as DSPs throttle participation under compute and budget constraints, reducing effective use of limited bidding capacity.

Method: A competition-aware request dispatch framework uses distributional bid prediction and probabilistic forwarding to decide whether each request should be sent to each DSP. The system adapts per-DSP thresholds over time through lightweight policy optimization to track non-stationary market conditions.

Result: Evaluation through four sequential online experiments on a production platform serving over 20 billion daily requests shows that a full multi-DSP deployment reduces DSP request volume under the policy by 34.2% while increasing net revenue by 4.6% (p<0.001) in a recent 14-day window after initial DSP adaptation. The policy surfaces comparative advantages among DSPs and improves monetized outcomes without increasing overall request volume.

Conclusion: The competition-aware request dispatch framework effectively addresses the over-distribution problem in RTB ad exchanges by intelligently filtering requests sent to DSPs, leading to significant reductions in request volume and improvements in revenue while adapting to non-stationary market conditions.

Abstract: Real-time bidding (RTB) ad exchanges typically forward nearly all incoming requests to demand-side platforms (DSPs), even though only a small fraction receive bids. This over-distribution weakens auction outcomes: DSPs throttle participation under compute and budget constraints, reducing the effective use of limited bidding capacity. We present a competition-aware request dispatch framework that uses distributional bid prediction and probabilistic forwarding to decide whether each request should be sent to each DSP. The system adapts per-DSP thresholds over time through lightweight policy optimization to track non-stationary market conditions. We evaluate the framework through four sequential online experiments on a production platform serving over 20 billion daily requests. A full multi-DSP deployment reduces DSP request volume under the policy by 34.2% while increasing net revenue by 4.6% (p<0.001) in a recent 14-day window after an initial DSP adaptation period. Further analysis highlights strong heterogeneity across traffic segments and reveals that aggregate metrics can be misleading. Segment-level and per-DSP analyses suggest that the policy surfaces comparative advantages among DSPs, improving monetized outcomes without increasing overall request volume.

</details>


### [91] [When Outputs Disperse, Does Epistemic Revision Follow? A Black-Box Coupling Diagnostic for Machine Collectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03722)
*Molood Arman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a diagnostic for LLM collectives that distinguishes between genuine epistemic revision versus mere reformulation of arguments, and finds that different LLM models respond differently to interventions meant to increase diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In collective intelligence research, disagreement typically indicates epistemic diversity that allows groups to revise beliefs. However, in LLM collectives, agents can produce diverse-looking arguments while preserving the same conclusions, breaking this proxy. The paper aims to distinguish between genuine belief revision and premise-preserving reformulation.

Method: The paper operationalizes dispersion-revision coupling using a black-box diagnostic that measures output dispersion through a Coherence Index (CI) and epistemic stance through per-turn annotation. They propose CI with Meta-Predictive Clarity System (MPCS) which inserts a Re-Differentiation Protocol (RDP) when outputs over-converge. They evaluate five-agent collectives from two LLM configurations (gpt-4o-mini and gemini-2.5-flash) across 310 paired episodes per condition.

Result: On gpt-4o-mini, conditional dissent improves false-premise recovery by +17.7 points while static persona diversity harms recovery (-8.1 points). On gemini-2.5-flash, the same intervention yields no gain despite verified dispersion drop. Gemini preserves false premises via intra-framework dissent (94% of post-RDP responses reformulate rather than concede vs 24% on GPT).

Conclusion: Researchers should report per-intervention stance shift and premise-preservation rates alongside accuracy when evaluating LLM collectives, as different models respond differently to diversity interventions, and apparent diversity doesn't guarantee genuine epistemic revision.

Abstract: Collective intelligence research treats disagreement as evidence of epistemic diversity: if agents express different views, the group should retain capacity to revise. In LLM collectives this proxy can break: agents can produce diverse-looking arguments while preserving the same conclusion. We operationalize dispersion-revision coupling: the degree to which an intervention that verifiably increases the dispersion of a collective's outputs in embedding space is accompanied by genuine revision of its epistemic stance rather than premise-preserving reformulation. The diagnostic is black-box: it operates on generated text alone and makes no claims about the internal representations of the generating models. Two channels are measured independently: an output channel, the Coherence Index (CI), verifies that the intervention changed output dispersion; an epistemic channel, per-turn stance annotation, measures whether the collective revised. We propose CI with the Meta-Predictive Clarity System (MPCS), which inserts a Re-Differentiation Protocol (RDP) when outputs over-converge, as a reusable method for estimating this coupling regime. We evaluate five-agent collectives from two configurations (gpt-4o-mini and gemini-2.5-flash; 310 paired episodes per condition). On gpt-4o-mini, conditional dissent improves false-premise recovery by +17.7 points (p<1e-6) while static persona diversity harms recovery (-8.1, p=.007). On gemini-2.5-flash, the same intervention at a comparable budget yields no gain (26.1% vs 27.1%, p=.84) despite a verified dispersion drop; the two treatment effects differ from each other (z=3.79, p<.001). Mechanism tagging shows Gemini preserves the false premise via intra-framework dissent: 94% of tagged post-RDP responses reformulate rather than concede (vs 24% on GPT). We recommend reporting per-intervention stance shift and premise-preservation rate alongside accuracy.

</details>


### [92] [SAT-Edge-Agent: Hardware-in-the-Loop Edge-Agent Orchestration for Onboard Satellite Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03728)
*Longji He,Jeto Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAT-Edge-Agent is a hardware-in-the-loop edge-agent system for onboard satellite intelligence that coordinates AI services to execute vision tasks under resource constraints, achieving 20/20 task completion with mean latencies of 29-61 seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Onboard satellite intelligence requires systems that can translate mission intent into tool calls, manage execution state, and produce machine-consumable results under communication and power limitations of space environments.

Method: Deployed SAT-Edge-Agent on a commercial ARM-based heterogeneous edge SoC using a browser workspace and FastAPI agent to coordinate a local OpenAI-compatible language service with a YOLO-style oriented-object-detection endpoint that returns FAIR1M metadata-backed structured results.

Result: Successfully completed 20/20 attempts for two FAIR1M workloads with mean Full-Agent latencies of 29.353s and 60.937s. Detector execution accounted for only ~2.5-3% of total latency. Mean CPU utilization was ~20.5%. Profiling showed most latency occurs outside detector execution.

Conclusion: The system establishes a reproducible HIL boundary for observable satellite edge-agent orchestration, but does not demonstrate detector accuracy, new geolocation methods, calibrated energy efficiency, or flight readiness.

Abstract: Onboard satellite intelligence requires a task layer that translates mission intent into local tool calls, exposes execution state, and returns machine-consumable artifacts under communication and power constraints. We present SAT-Edge-Agent, a hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) edge-agent system deployed on a commercial off-the-shelf ARM-based heterogeneous edge system-on-chip. A browser workspace and FastAPI agent coordinate a local OpenAI-compatible language service with a project-internal YOLO-style oriented-object-detection endpoint that returns FAIR1M metadata-backed structured results. Two fixed FAIR1M workloads, one single-image and one serial two-image request, were repeated 20 times each and completed 20/20 attempts. Mean Full-Agent latency was 29.353 s and 60.937 s, with empirical P95 values of 31.166 s and 66.882 s. Mean detector time was 861.386 ms and 1510.920 ms, only 2.93% and 2.48% of the corresponding Full-Agent means. Profiling indicates that most visible latency occurs outside detector execution. Mean CPU utilization was 20.761% and 20.482%. A 200-ms NPU-load field averaged 100% for both workloads, but it represents a shared-accelerator software field rather than detector-only occupancy or calibrated utilization. The public evidence package provides sanitized request-level records, redacted JSON, normalized SSE examples, and scripts reproducing the reported statistics. These results establish a reproducible HIL boundary for observable satellite edge-agent orchestration, but do not establish detector accuracy, a new geolocation method, calibrated energy efficiency, or flight readiness.

</details>


### [93] [CARE-Bench: Benchmarking Patient-Facing LLM Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03731)
*Yining Hua,Hongbin Na,Cyrus Ayubcha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARE-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating medical LLMs' ability to provide sequential triage recommendations as a four-label action task, showing current models struggle with action timing and often recommend care prematurely instead of asking for needed clarification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Patient-facing medical LLMs are increasingly being used to answer symptom questions before clinician contact, creating safety concerns about what action users should take next. There's a need to evaluate how well these models can provide appropriate sequential triage recommendations as an action-based task.

Method: Created CARE-Bench, a source-grounded benchmark with 500 cases and 1,059 evaluated patient-disclosure prefixes reconstructed from medical dialogue, consultation, and follow-up-question sources. Evaluated 11 models on 269 held-out rounds using unprompted and minimally prompted open-ended protocols, with a fixed GPT-5.5 mapper to code responses into a four-label action space.

Result: Unprompted models performed poorly (macro-F1: 31.2-50.4). Prompting improved 10 of 11 models (macro-F1: 46.9-63.4), but substantial threshold errors persisted. When the correct action was to ask for more information, only 33.5% of prompted outputs preserved that step, often recommending care prematurely instead.

Conclusion: Patient-facing triage is not a simple prompting problem. The persistence of errors after prompting supports the need for explicit evaluation of action timing before deployment of medical LLMs in patient-facing contexts.

Abstract: Patient-facing medical LLMs and agents increasingly answer symptom questions before clinician contact, where the key safety question is what action the user should take next. We introduce CARE-Bench, a source-grounded benchmark that evaluates sequential patient-facing triage as a four-label per-turn current-action task. CARE-Bench contains 500 cases and 1,059 evaluated patient-disclosure prefixes reconstructed from medical dialogue, consultation, and follow-up-question sources. We evaluate 11 models on 269 held-out rounds under unprompted and minimally prompted open-ended protocols, using a fixed GPT-5.5 mapper to code each response into the four-label action space. Unprompted macro-F1 remains low, ranging from 31.2 to 50.4. Prompting improves 10 of 11 models, with prompted macro-F1 ranging from 46.9 to 63.4, but substantial threshold errors remain. Prompted models often recommend care before needed clarification is obtained; when the correct action was to ask for more information, only 33.5% of prompted outputs preserved the step. The persistence of these errors after prompting suggests that patient-facing triage is not a simple prompting problem and supports explicit evaluation of action timing before deployment.

</details>


### [94] [Failure-Informed Image Self-Augmentation for Multimodal Large Language Model Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03733)
*Chunyang Jiang,Pingping Zhang,Yuzhi Zhao,Wenao Ma,Zhijian Hou,Mengyang Wu,Yiyang Cai,Senkang Hu,Sitong Cheng,Chi-Min Chan,Wei Xue,Yike Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FISA is a self-improvement framework for MLLMs that generates challenging but answer-preserving image augmentations from the model's own failure cases, outperforming generic augmentation methods and improving performance across various VQA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs rely heavily on expensive annotated multimodal data. Self-augmentation offers a cost-effective alternative, but existing methods are text-centric with image augmentation being underexplored and often using generic transformations poorly aligned with model weaknesses.

Method: FISA constructs augmented images from the model's failure cases, generating visually challenging yet answer-preserving complications. It includes self-examination verification and dual fidelity filtering to ensure semantic consistency while avoiding distortion.

Result: Experiments show consistent performance improvements on visual question answering benchmarks in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings. FISA demonstrates compatibility with existing text augmentation, superior data efficiency over generic baselines, and practical filtering effectiveness.

Conclusion: Failure-informed image self-augmentation provides an effective framework for MLLM self-improvement by creating targeted, challenging training data from model failures, overcoming limitations of generic augmentation approaches while maintaining semantic fidelity.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable performance across vision-language tasks, but their progress depends heavily on large-scale, high-quality multimodal data that are costly to annotate. Self-augmentation offers a promising alternative by enabling models to expand their own training data without external supervision. However, existing MLLM self-augmentation methods are largely text-centric, while image augmentation remains underexplored and typically relies on generic or handcrafted transformations that are weakly aligned with the model's actual incapability. We propose Failure-informed Image Self-Augmentation (\textbf{FISA}), a framework for MLLM self-improvement that constructs augmented images from the model's own failure cases. Our method generates visually challenging yet answer-preserving image complications, verifies their utility through self-examination, and applies dual fidelity filtering to avoid semantic distortion. Experiments on visual question answering benchmarks show that the proposed method consistently improves performance across both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings. Further experiments validate the compatibility of FISA with existing textual self-augmentation approaches, the superior data efficiency of the synthesized samples over generic image augmentation baselines, and the practical effectiveness of the proposed filtering strategy.

</details>


### [95] [AgenticECO: An Agentic Framework for ECO on 3D Integrated Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03738)
*Shuo Ren,Yaohui Han,Libo Shen,Zhiqiang Jia,Rongliang Fu,Bei Yu,Tsung-Yi Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgenticECO is an AI-driven workflow for 3D-IC engineering change orders that uses evidence-gated tool agents and a minimal-disturbance router to efficiently fix bond-level defects without full rerouting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With Moore's law slowing, 3D integration faces unique bond-level defects not seen in 2D designs, and post-route ECOs remain manual work. Traditional edit-then-fully-reroute practices make it impossible to attribute signoff numbers to specific edits, and current approaches suffer from router churn entanglement.

Method: AgenticECO combines: 1) evidence-gated tool-using agent workflow for 3D-IC ECO using the open-source TaiWei flow, 2) EcoRoute - a minimal-disturbance ECO-routing layer that drives existing routers without modification, enabling edit attribution, and 3) comprehensive validation through nine matched defect cases, controlled studies, and preregistered visual and diagnostic tests.

Result: AgenticECO cleared 7/9 defect cases vs 2/9 for both full reroute and stock repair approaches, with only 0.66% mean disturbance and no clock nets touched. Cross-backbone rerun cleared all 9 cases. The system demonstrated necessary repair moves under preservation, occupancy-aware landing choices, and acceptance flipping under tight clocks. All results passed routing, extraction, timing, DRC, and equivalence checks.

Conclusion: AgenticECO provides an effective, attributable solution for 3D-IC ECOs that significantly outperforms traditional methods while minimizing disturbance. The evidence-gated agent approach combined with minimal-disturbance routing enables reliable repair attribution and successful defect resolution, representing an important advance for 3D-IC post-route engineering.

Abstract: As Moore's law slows, the industry is turning to three-dimensional integration; yet in merged 3D-IC flows, routed designs expose bond-level defects with no 2D analogue, and post-route engineering change orders (ECO) remain manual, expertise-bound work. Worse, the standard edit-then-fully-reroute practice entangles a repair with router churn, so a signoff number cannot be attributed to the edit that motivated it. We present AgenticECO, an evidence-gated tool-using agent workflow for 3D-IC ECO on the open-source TaiWei flow, paired with EcoRoute, a minimal-disturbance ECO-routing layer that drives the unmodified pinned router so a repair is attributable to its edit. Across nine matched natural defect cases under identical budgets, AgenticECO clears seven versus two for both full reroute and stock repair, at 0.66\% mean disturbance over cleared cases and zero clock nets touched, and a cross-backbone rerun under the same sealed contract clears all nine. Controlled studies show that the repair moves are necessary under preservation, that occupancy-aware choice buys legal landings rather than repair success, and that under tightened clocks minimal disturbance flips accept versus reject. Three preregistered visual studies localize the pixel instrument's edge to contested landing sites, and a preregistered blind diagnostic exactly restores every held-out injected defect, the only arm with zero wrong edits. Every accepted result passes routing, fresh extraction, max/min timing, DRC, and structural-equivalence gates. Code, environment, and per-episode audit artifacts are released as supplementary material.

</details>


### [96] [MissClick: Exploiting Digit-Serialized Coordinates to Attack GUI Grounding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03740)
*Yu Ran,Wentao Zhao,Xin Zhang,Yi Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MissClick: A white-box adversarial attack on GUI visual grounding models that exploits the numerical structure of coordinate outputs, achieving high success rates for both untargeted disruption and targeted hijacking attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GUI visual grounding models generate screen coordinates as sequences of digit tokens, but the security implications of this process have been overlooked. The researchers observed that changing a single digit in the hundreds place can cause large coordinate displacements, motivating attacks that account for numerical place-value structure rather than treating coordinates as ordinary text.

Method: MissClick proposes two goal-specific objectives: MissClick-U maximizes soft-coordinate displacement for untargeted disruption (displacing clicks outside correct regions), while MissClick-T minimizes a place-weighted target-digit loss for targeted hijacking (redirecting clicks to attacker-specified regions). The approach is a simple white-box adversarial attack that exploits the numerical structure of coordinate outputs.

Result: On OS-Atlas and UGround datasets across desktop, web, and mobile platforms: MissClick-U achieved untargeted success rates of 75.07% and 72.93% (improvements of +16.62 and +30.72 percentage points over existing attacks). MissClick-T achieved targeted success rates of 44.86% and 62.67% (improvements of +31.73 and +47.06 percentage points). Soft-coordinate displacement works best for untargeted attacks, while place-weighted target-digit optimization works best for targeted attacks.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that accounting for the numerical and place-value structure of coordinate outputs is crucial for effective adversarial attacks on GUI grounding models. Different attack objectives (untargeted vs targeted) benefit from distinct optimization strategies, revealing fundamental differences in how to best exploit these models' coordinate generation vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Recent GUI visual grounding models generate screen coordinates as sequences of digit tokens that are parsed into numerical values and mapped to executable clicks. The security implications of this coordinate generation process have been largely overlooked. We observe that each coordinate digit is predicted as a categorical token, yet after parsing, changing a hundreds-place digit by one changes the corresponding numerical coordinate component by 100 units, which can induce a large displacement of the executed click. This observation motivates attack objectives that account for the numerical and place-value structure of coordinate outputs rather than treating them as ordinary text. Moreover, untargeted and targeted attacks impose different success conditions--displacing the click outside the correct region versus into an attacker-specified region--and therefore benefit from different objectives. We propose MissClick, a simple and effective white-box adversarial attack with two goal-specific objectives: MissClick-U maximizes soft-coordinate displacement for untargeted disruption, while MissClick-T minimizes a place-weighted target-digit loss for targeted hijacking. Compared with existing attacks against GUI grounding models on OS-Atlas and UGround across desktop, web, and mobile platforms, MissClick-U achieves untargeted success rates of 75.07\% and 72.93\% (+16.62 and +30.72 pp), and MissClick-T achieves targeted success rates of 44.86\% and 62.67\% (+31.73 and +47.06 pp). Attack objective comparison further shows that soft-coordinate displacement yields the highest untargeted attack success rate, whereas place-weighted target-digit optimization yields the highest targeted attack success rate, revealing distinct objective preferences for the two attack goals.

</details>


### [97] [Agents Catching Agents: Shortcut Cascades and Benchmark Gaming in Clinical Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03744)
*Sebastián Andrés Cajas Ordóñez,Agastya Munnangi,Aldo Marzullo,Felipe Ocampo Osorio,Quang Bui,Mohammad Shahin,Armaan Grewal,Emmanuel Paul Kwesiga,Anqi Peter Li,Josephine Nanyonjo,Aaditya Panchal,Arshnoor Bhutani,Nikhil Jaiswal,Milit S. Patel,Maximin Lange,Leo Anthony Celi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Clinical decision support committees using LLM agents are vulnerable to social gaming - when peers assert wrong answers, holdout agents adopt them 38% of the time, but only independent re-queries can detect this manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As clinical decision support moves toward committees of language-model agents deliberating on shared workspaces, there's a need to understand whether such committees can be gamed by shortcuts - cues that benchmarks reward but clinicians would ignore, potentially leading to misleading performance metrics.

Method: The study evaluates committees of LLM agents across seven cohorts on six public datasets spanning text (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, MIMIC-CXR reports), imaging (NIH ChestXray14, MIMIC-CXR-JPG, CheXpert) and tabular ICU records (SUPPORT2). They test how agents respond to peer pressure and pre-screen flags, and evaluate three oversight methods: a gate agent, a judge reading transcripts, and a referee that privately re-queries holdout agents.

Result: Individual Gemini committees resist isolated cues (5-16% flip rate), but social contagion spreads: with two peers asserting wrong answers, holdouts adopt them 38% of the time. Pre-screen system flags also influence agents. A gate oversight agent fails completely (100% false-positive rate), a transcript-reading judge works well on text (100% precision, 93% recall) but fails on imaging, while a referee that privately re-queries holdouts transfers well to imaging (77-88% precision). Social plausibility is key - increasing visual salience doesn't affect contagion, but adding peer voices increases it by 50%.

Conclusion: Clinical decision support committees can be gamed through social plausibility rather than technical shortcuts. Only independent oversight mechanisms that re-query agents can reliably detect such manipulation, as self-reported reasoning doesn't reveal the influence (only 1/10 text and 1/134 imaging drifters mention the rubric they moved toward).

Abstract: Clinical decision support is moving toward committees of language-model agents deliberating on a shared workspace. We ask whether such committees can be gamed by shortcuts, cues a benchmark rewards but a clinician would ignore. Across seven cohorts on six public datasets spanning text (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, MIMIC-CXR reports), imaging (NIH ChestX-ray14, MIMIC-CXR-JPG, CheXpert) and tabular ICU records (SUPPORT2), Gemini committees resist these cues in isolation (flip 5-16%), yet a socially plausible shortcut spreads: when two peers assert the same wrong answer, the holdout under test adopts it in 38% of cases, as does a false "pre-screen" system flag, on both capability tiers. Of three oversight agents, a gate cannot separate adoption from honest agreement (false-positive rate 100%); a same-lineage judge reading only the transcript flags adoption on text (precision 100%, recall 93%) but collapses onto the gate in imaging; a referee that privately re-queries the holdout transfers to imaging (77-88% precision, 13-21% false-positive rate). Tripling a cue's visual salience does not move contagion, whereas a second peer voice raises it by half again. Gaming a hidden rubric is near-silent: only 1/10 text and 1/134 imaging drifters name the rubric they moved toward. What games a committee is social plausibility, and only a referee independent of self-report catches it. Code: https://github.com/criticaldata/benchmaxxing

</details>


### [98] [Risky Business: Measuring The Faithfulness-Safety Tension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03745)
*Dominik Meier,Luca Joshua Francis,Marco Bernhard Kaiser,Terry Ruas,Jan Philip Wahle,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: There's an alignment tension between faithfulness (needed for model monitoring) and robustness (needed to reject unsafe reasoning) in Large Reasoning Models. The paper introduces HazMart dataset and Targeted Reasoning Replacement technique to study this, finding tradeoffs in current models and showing how representation steering can improve safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-Thought reasoning offers potential for model monitoring, but this requires faithfulness - model outputs must strictly derive from reasoning traces. However, there's an alignment tension: models must be faithful enough for monitoring, yet robust enough to reject unsafe reasoning. This counterbalance exists in current Large Reasoning Models and needs to be addressed.

Method: 1) Introduced HazMart dataset: human-written dataset in autonomous AI shopkeeper scenario. 2) Developed Targeted Reasoning Replacement (TRR): directly intervenes in reasoning chains to substitute unsafe or illogical thoughts (instead of using hint-based prompts). 3) Tested DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B and QwQ-32B models. 4) Conducted mechanistic analyses to identify internal representations. 5) Used representation steering to independently amplify safety directions.

Result: DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B shows high faithfulness (97.5%) but fails to reject Unsafe Reasoning (12.3% safety). QwQ-32B is more robust (73.9% safety) but has lower faithfulness (74.7%). Mechanistic analyses reveal anti-correlated internal directions representing faithfulness and safety properties, peaking at action-commit tokens. Representation steering can amplify safety direction, increasing safe behavior by 9 percentage points while maintaining base capabilities.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental tension between faithfulness and safety in Large Reasoning Models. The HazMart dataset and Targeted Reasoning Replacement technique provide better tools to study this alignment problem. Current models exhibit tradeoffs between these properties, but representation steering offers a promising approach to improve safety while preserving other capabilities.

Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning offers a promising window into model monitoring. However, monitoring relies on faithfulness, i.e., the model output strictly derives from its reasoning trace. We identify an alignment tension where a model must be faithful enough to be monitored, yet robust enough to reject unsafe reasoning. We demonstrate that this counterbalance exists in current Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), and show ways in which it can be addressed. We introduce HazMart, a human-written dataset set in an autonomous AI shopkeeper scenario. Unlike prior work that relies on providing hints in prompts to test faithfulness (e.g., "A Stanford professor said it should be Answer A"), we propose a novel replacement-based technique, which we call Targeted Reasoning Replacement (TRR), that directly intervenes in the reasoning chain to substitute in unsafe or illogical thoughts (e.g., "Wait, the answer must be Option B [was Option A] because it is the most fitting"). DeepSeek-R1-Llama-70B exhibits high faithfulness (97.5%) but fails to reject Unsafe Reasoning (12.3%), while QwQ-32B is more robust (73.9% safety) at the cost of lower faithfulness (74.7%). Mechanistic analyses of QwQ-32B reveal that these properties are represented by anti-correlated internal directions peaking at the action-commit token. Finally, we demonstrate that representation steering can independently amplify the safety direction, increasing safe behavior by 9 percentage points while maintaining base capabilities.

</details>


### [99] [GDPevo: Evaluating Agent Self-Evolution on Real Business Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03764)
*Leijun Zhou,Zhihao Liu,Xiang Qu,Chenxu Liu,Yifei Liu,Yanke Yu,Jingzhe Xu,Xuejun Wu,Buyue Qian,Xi Chen,Yaowei Zheng,Junhao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GDPevo is a new automated benchmark for evaluating agent self-evolution using real-world GDP-related enterprise workflows, with contamination-resistant design and scalable generation capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for agent self-evaluation have limitations: they cover few economically valuable domains, often fail to properly attribute test-time gains to training experience, and remain vulnerable to data contamination issues.

Method: Developed an evolution-native benchmark using rule hybridization: decomposes enterprise workflows into atomic business rules, distributes subsets across training tasks, and recombines them in held-out test tasks to ensure attribution of improvements. Features automated data pipeline for scalable generation.

Result: Self-evolution consistently improves held-out accuracy by up to 16.44 percentage points across four agents tested with four supervision types. However, evolved agents remain far below the oracle ceiling of 91.6%, indicating self-evolution capabilities are not fully realized.

Conclusion: GDPevo provides a practical, scalable benchmark for evaluating agent self-evolution in economically relevant domains with robust attribution mechanisms and contamination resistance. Current agents show significant improvement from self-evolution but have substantial room for growth.

Abstract: Agent self-evolution updates an agent's persistent state from prior experience and reuses it to solve related tasks more effectively. Evaluating self-evolution is difficult: existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of economically valuable task domains, do not always design training and test tasks such that test-time gains can be attributed to training experience, and remain vulnerable to data contamination. We present GDPevo, an evolution-native benchmark grounded in GDP-related enterprise workflows, together with the fully automated data pipeline that generates it. Its core mechanism, rule hybridization, decomposes each enterprise workflow into atomic business rules, distributes subsets of these rules across training tasks, and recombines them in held-out test tasks so that test-time gains are attributable.
