GDPevo spans CRM, ERP, finance, healthcare, legal, and data-centric workflows. Its V1 release contains 120 tasks in 12 groups, with five training and five held-out test tasks per group. Full automation enables the pipeline to expand the suite to 240 tasks in 24 groups (V2) within two days, providing a practical response to contamination. Using GDPevo, we evaluate four agents, each comprising a harness and a model, under four supervision types. Self-evolution consistently improves held-out accuracy by up to 16.44 percentage points. But the best evolved agents remain far below the fully informed oracle ceiling of 91.6%, indicating that the self-evolution ability of current agents remains far from fully realized. We publicly release the pipeline, benchmark, and full evaluation results at https://github.com/Prism-Shadow/GDPevo.

</details>


### [100] [Computing Actual Causes for Neural Network Predictions under Structured Causal Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03772)
*Jannick Strobel,Muqsit Azeem,Stefan Leue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel approach to neural network explanation that treats inputs as structured features with dependencies, using Halpern-Pearl actual causes in Boolean Structural Causal Models to provide formal guarantees of completeness and minimality, achieving substantial scalability improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural network explanation methods treat input features as independent, which can yield misleading explanations when inputs have structured dependencies, limiting their trustworthiness and reliability.

Method: The authors formalize explanations as Halpern-Pearl actual causes using Boolean Structural Causal Models to capture input dependencies. They compute HP causes using bound propagation and branch-and-bound techniques while providing formal guarantees of completeness and minimality.

Result: The method substantially outperforms brute-force and ILP baselines in scalability, computing all minimal actual causes on instances with search spaces up to 2.3×10^13 candidate pairs on SCMs with up to 28 nodes within a 180s per-instance budget. In a case study, ignoring input dependencies inflates reported causes by 14.9% spurious ones.

Conclusion: Modeling input dependencies through Structural Causal Models provides more accurate and trustworthy explanations for neural network predictions, addressing limitations of current feature-attribution methods and reducing spurious explanations.

Abstract: Explaining the predictions of neural networks is a central challenge in trustworthy AI. Existing explanation methods, such as those based on feature attribution or minimal sufficient sets, typically treat input features as independent, which can yield misleading explanations when inputs exhibit structured dependencies. We address this by formalizing explanations as Halpern-Pearl (HP) actual causes, modeling input dependencies using Boolean Structural Causal Models (SCMs). We compute HP causes by applying bound propagation and branch-and-bound techniques, while providing formal guarantees of completeness and minimality. Our experiments show that we substantially outperform brute-force and ILP baselines in scalability, and outperform heuristic search as graph size grows, computing all minimal actual causes on instances with search spaces of up to $2.3\times10^{13}$ candidate (cause, contingency) pairs, on SCMs with up to 28 nodes, within a 180s per-instance budget. In a case study, we further show that ignoring input dependencies inflates the number of reported causes, 14.9% of which are spurious under our SCM.

</details>


### [101] [KnowHal: A Knowledge-Driven Benchmark for Comprehensive Multimodal Hallucination Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03782)
*Ruihan Li,Jiyang Tan,Kailin Jiang,Huining Li,Hengyang Lu,Yu Huang,Qian Li,Yuntao Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowHal is a new multimodal hallucination benchmark that extends evaluation beyond entity/attribute/relation hallucinations to include knowledge hallucinations, using paired positive/negative questions across 10 domains to provide more comprehensive assessment of MLLM failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal hallucination benchmarks mainly focus on entity, attribute, and relation hallucinations, while knowledge-related failures are investigated separately. There's a lack of unified evaluation framework that combines all these dimensions, making it difficult to comprehensively assess hallucinations in MLLMs.

Method: Proposes KnowHal benchmark with 1,800 samples across 10 domains/50 categories. Uses semi-automated pipeline: LLM assistance for question generation, CLIP-based filtering, and human verification. Creates paired positive/negative questions over shared images/entities to enable controlled comparisons of perceptual errors, knowledge errors, and false-premise acceptance.

Result: Evaluation of 14 representative MLLMs shows knowledge dimension is consistently most challenging. Most models show substantial performance degradation on negative questions, revealing limited robustness to false premises. The unified framework reveals patterns across hallucination types.

Conclusion: KnowHal addresses an important gap in multimodal hallucination evaluation by unifying four dimensions (entity, attribute, relation, knowledge) with paired question design. It enables more comprehensive assessment and reveals that knowledge hallucinations remain the most challenging problem for MLLMs.

Abstract: Hallucination remains a critical challenge for developing trustworthy Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing benchmarks mainly focus on entity, attribute, and relation hallucinations, knowledge-related failures are often investigated separately, lacking a unified evaluation framework across different hallucination dimensions. To overcome this, we propose \textbf{KnowHal}, a benchmark that explicitly incorporates knowledge hallucination into multimodal hallucination evaluation spanning four dimensions: entity, attribute, relation, and knowledge. KnowHal constructs paired positive and negative questions over shared images and entities, enabling controlled comparisons among perceptual errors, knowledge-related errors, and false-premise acceptance. The benchmark contains 1,800 samples across 10 domains and 50 categories, constructed through a semi-automated pipeline combining LLM assistance, CLIP-based filtering, and human verification. We evaluate 14 representative MLLMs on KnowHal and conduct extensive analyses. Results show that the knowledge dimension consistently presents the greatest challenge for nearly all evaluated models, while most models exhibit substantial performance degradation on negative questions, revealing limited robustness to false premises. By unifying four hallucination dimensions with paired question design, KnowHal addresses an important gap in existing evaluation frameworks and enables a more comprehensive assessment of hallucinations in MLLMs.

</details>


### [102] [Does Forgetting Transfer Across Modalities? A Real-World Benchmark for Cross-Modal Knowledge Unlearning Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03791)
*Chunlin Liu,Junnian Chen,Haitong Jiang,Jianyu Zhao,Yingsen Pang,Jingchen Li,Jiabiao He,Youming Lu,Jinhe Bi,Yuntao Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UNLINK-VL is a benchmark for evaluating cross-modal knowledge unlearning in Vision-Language Models, revealing that text-only unlearning doesn't transfer well to visual contexts, showing the need for cross-modal evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language Models may memorize sensitive, copyrighted, or harmful knowledge, and existing unlearning research focuses too much on individual modalities while insufficiently studying cross-modal transfer of real-world knowledge unlearning.

Method: Created UNLINK-VL benchmark with visually identifiable real-world entities as targets, associated with images and facts from Wikidata. Includes four subsets evaluating direct forgetting, relational forgetting, non-target knowledge preservation, and query robustness. Trained models under text-only and multimodal unlearning settings.

Result: Found pronounced asymmetry: multimodal unlearning remains effective in textual evaluation, but text-only unlearning transfers poorly to visual/cross-modal scenarios. Methods largely preserve general capabilities while revealing that intra-modal evaluation overestimates effectiveness.

Conclusion: Relying solely on intra-modal (especially text-only) evaluation substantially overestimates knowledge unlearning effectiveness in VLMs, demonstrating the critical need for cross-modal unlearning approaches and evaluation benchmarks like UNLINK-VL.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs), like Large Language Models (LLMs), may memorize sensitive, copyrighted, or harmful knowledge from their pretraining corpora. Removing such knowledge is essential for building trustworthy AI systems. However, existing studies primarily focus on forgetting within individual modalities. Although recent work has begun to explore cross-modal consistency in unlearning, the cross-modal transfer of real-world knowledge unlearning remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we introduce UNLINK-VL, a real-world benchmark for cross-modal knowledge unlearning in VLMs. Under a post-hoc unlearning setting in which the original forget and retain corpora are unavailable, UNLINK-VL selects visually identifiable real-world entities as unlearning targets and associates them with corresponding images and one-hop and multi-hop facts derived from Wikidata. The benchmark comprises four complementary subsets that evaluate direct forgetting of target knowledge, the propagation of forgetting through relational knowledge, the preservation of related non-target knowledge, and robustness to semantically equivalent queries. We train models under text-only and multimodal unlearning settings and evaluate forgetting effectiveness and retained utility across textual, visual, and cross-modal scenarios. Extensive experiments reveal a pronounced asymmetry in cross-modal transfer: multimodal unlearning remains effective under textual evaluation, whereas text-only unlearning transfers poorly to visual and cross-modal scenarios. Meanwhile, the evaluated methods largely preserve the models' general capabilities. These findings demonstrate that relying solely on intra-modal evaluation, particularly text-only evaluation, may substantially overestimate the effectiveness of knowledge unlearning in VLMs, underscoring the need for cross-modal unlearning and evaluation.

</details>


### [103] [LatentGuard: Efficient and Inspectable Latent Reasoning for LLM Safeguards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03838)
*Zhinan Liu,Jie Li,Mingyu Kang,Jiayi Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LatentGuard is an efficient and inspectable LLM safeguard framework that uses latent continuous reasoning instead of explicit text generation, reducing token costs while maintaining auditability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reasoning-based guard models are costly to deploy due to generating explicit rationales for every interaction. While latent-reasoning methods reduce token generation by moving reasoning into continuous states, they are underexplored for safety moderation and lack inspection interfaces for deployment.

Method: LatentGuard uses a staged curriculum to progressively compress task-aligned textual rationales into compact latent states, enabling safety verdicts to be predicted directly from continuous representations. An isolated auxiliary decoder generates compact audit artifacts on demand, keeping rationale generation off the standard inference path.

Result: LatentGuard-8B improves mean weighted F1 from 83.95 to 84.91 over GuardReasoner-8B, while reducing critical-path reasoning cost from 268.56 generated rationale tokens to 1.60 latent reasoning tokens. The audit decoder achieves an audit utility score of 85.75.

Conclusion: LatentGuard demonstrates an efficient and inspectable path toward deployable LLM safeguards by combining the benefits of latent continuous reasoning with on-demand auditability, addressing both efficiency and transparency requirements for practical deployment.

Abstract: Reasoning-based guard models improve LLM safeguards, but decoding explicit rationales for every interaction makes them costly to deploy. Although latent-reasoning methods reduce token generation by moving reasoning into continuous states, they remain underexplored for safety moderation and lack an inspection interface for deployment. In this paper, we propose LatentGuard, an efficient and inspectable safeguard framework that brings continuous latent reasoning to guard models. LatentGuard uses a staged curriculum to progressively compress task-aligned textual rationales into compact latent states, enabling safety verdicts to be predicted directly from continuous representations. To preserve inspectability, an isolated auxiliary decoder generates compact audit artifacts on demand, keeping rationale generation off the standard inference path. Experiments show that LatentGuard-8B improves mean weighted F1 from 83.95 to 84.91 over GuardReasoner-8B, while reducing critical-path reasoning cost from 268.56 generated rationale tokens to 1.60 latent reasoning tokens. Its audit decoder achieves an audit utility score of 85.75, demonstrating an efficient and inspectable path toward deployable LLM safeguards.

</details>


### [104] [Oilbird: Training-Free Speculative Decoding with Keys the Verifier Already Computes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03839)
*Tao Jin,Phuong Minh Nguyen,Zhenzhu Yan,Teeradaj Racharak,Naoya Inoue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Training-free speculative decoding with semantic addressing improves draft acceptance by addressing limitations of exact string matching, achieving 4.4x speedup on tool-calling benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free speculative decoding methods use exact string matching to draft tokens from a pool of previous contexts, but this fails on tool-calling traffic where requests repeat with minor variations. Exact matching misses correct drafts that are present in the pool due to addressing limitations rather than coverage problems.

Method: Proposes a semantic draft source that re-keys the same pool using hidden states computed by the verifier at each committed token. This semantic addressing is merged with existing lexical drafters' trees, allowing both exact matching and semantic similarity to guide token drafting.

Result: Oilbird (the proposed method) achieves 4.4x autoregressive decoding speed on API-Bank benchmark, compared to 3.9x for strongest training-free baseline and 2.0x for EAGLE-3. In three published drafters with matched pool and budget, it lifts accepted length by 24-29%. Analysis shows about half of what exact-match drafter misses is present in pool but unreachable by exact matching.

Conclusion: Semantic addressing overcomes limitations of exact string matching in training-free speculative decoding, particularly beneficial for tool-calling workloads where requests have high similarity with minor variations. The approach significantly improves draft acceptance rates and decoding speed without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Training-free speculative decoding drafts by matching an exact suffix of the context against a pool of earlier context. That lookup misses correct drafts already in the pool, most visibly on tool-calling traffic, where a request repeats almost everything but the few values minted for it, and where one rejected token discards the correct continuation behind it. We diagnose the failure position by position across ten benchmarks and find it to be a problem of addressing rather than of coverage: on our densest tool-calling benchmark, about half of what the strongest exact-match drafter misses is present in the pool yet unreachable by exact matching. We therefore propose a second, semantic draft source: the same pool, re-keyed by the hidden state the verifier has already computed at each committed token, together with a merge that lets it ride inside an existing lexical drafter's tree. In three published drafters, at matched pool and budget, it lifts accepted length by 24-29%. Oilbird reaches 4.4x autoregressive decoding speed on API-Bank, against 3.9x for the strongest training-free baseline in our harness and 2.0x for EAGLE-3.

</details>


### [105] [MAFIA: Query-Only Memory Attacks via Probing and Factual Injection against Audited LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03844)
*Jiaming Chen,Yisen Gao,Yanping Li,Zifan Liu,Yumeng Zhang,Jun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAFIA is a memory poisoning attack framework for LLM agents that bypasses auditing through factual injection and strategic placement, achieving high attack success while evading detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing query-only memory attacks fail in realistic settings with large benign memory pools and active input auditing, lacking competitiveness against legitimate memories and ability to pass semantic checks.

Method: Two-stage approach: 1) Strategic placement via memory probing, budget allocation, and scheduling to ensure retrieval competitiveness; 2) Payload design using compact factual cloaks to bypass audits while maintaining malicious intent.

Result: MAFIA achieves up to 90.7% attack success rate while suppressing audit detection from 83.3% to at most 7.4%, demonstrating critical vulnerabilities in agentic memory systems.

Conclusion: MAFIA exposes significant security vulnerabilities in memory-augmented LLM agents, highlighting the need for stronger defenses against sophisticated memory poisoning attacks that can bypass current auditing mechanisms.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM agents rely on rich context for long-horizon reasoning and acting, yet their memory modules expose a persistent attack surface for malicious records, making the study of memory poisoning threats imperative. However, existing query-only attacks often fail to remain effective in two realistic and prevalent settings: large-scale benign memory pools and active input auditing. Consequently, current approaches fall short when facing the dual challenges of high retrieval competitiveness and rigorous semantic checks. To overcome these limitations, we propose MAFIA, a query-only Memory Attack framework via probing and Factual Injection against Audit, tailored to this extended threat model. Specifically, MAFIA introduces: (1) a placement strategy that ensures retrieval-competitive injection via memory probing, budget allocation, and scheduling; and (2) a payload design that bypasses audits using compact factual cloaks, preserving malicious effects while maintaining high semantic similarity. Extensive evaluations reveal that MAFIA achieves up to a 90.7% attack success rate while suppressing audit detection from a peak of 83.3% to at most 7.4%, exposing critical vulnerabilities across agentic memory systems. Code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/JiamingChen1234/MAFIA.

</details>


### [106] [AgentStream: How Well Do Self-Evolving LLM Agents Perform Under Streaming Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00155)
*Dong Yan,Jian Liang,Dapeng Hu,Ran He,Nicholas Jing Yuan,Qi Zhang,Tieniu Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentStream is a framework that evaluates self-evolving LLM agents in realistic streaming scenarios (Isolated, Sequential, Interleaved) rather than isolated single-task settings, revealing that self-evolution reliability varies across scenarios and is gated by model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current research on self-evolving LLM agents predominantly uses independent evaluation, leaving a gap in understanding how these agents behave in realistic streaming settings with diverse and complex task sequences.

Method: Introduces AgentStream framework that organizes agent benchmarks into configurable task streams, instantiating three streaming scenarios (Isolated, Sequential, Interleaved) that progressively vary stream scope and domain composition. Evaluates five self-evolving methods across three frontier foundation models combinatorially.

Result: Self-evolution reliability varies across streaming scenarios; benefit of self-evolution is gated by model capability and shows non-monotonic relationship with model strength; no single method dominates across models and scenarios.

Conclusion: Self-evolving agents should be evaluated under realistic task streams rather than isolated single-task settings. The findings provide concrete guidance for selecting self-evolving methods across different models and streaming scenarios.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can self-evolve by continually improving from their own accumulated experience. However, existing studies predominantly adopt independent evaluation. Consequently, the behavior of self-evolving agents in realistic streaming settings, where agents adapt to diverse and complex task streams, remains poorly understood. To address this gap, we introduce AgentStream, a unified framework that evaluates self-evolving agents spanning diverse evolution components by organizing agentic benchmarks into a configurable task stream and instantiating the \texttt{Isolated}, \texttt{Sequential}, and \texttt{Interleaved} streaming scenarios at test time, which progressively vary the scope and domain composition of the stream. Over these scenarios, we combinatorially evaluate five representative self-evolving methods across three frontier foundation models, disentangling how model capability, method architecture, and streaming scenario jointly shape self-evolution. Our results show that self-evolution reliability varies across streaming scenarios, the benefit of self-evolution is gated by model capability and non-monotonic in model strength, and no single method dominates across models and scenarios. These findings offer concrete guidance for selecting self-evolving methods across models and streaming scenarios. Overall, we advocate that self-evolving agents should be evaluated under realistic task streams rather than isolated single-task settings.

</details>


### [107] [ADMITBench: A Safety-Governed Reference Framework for Evaluating the Admissibility of Industrial LLM Advisories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03866)
*Yash Misra,Javal Vyas,Siddharth Gutta,Mehmet Mercangöz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ADMITBench is a safety-governed evaluation framework for industrial LLM advisories that checks if recommendations are evidence-supported, authority-permitted, and consequence-acceptable according to plant-specific profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for rigorous evaluation of LLM-based industrial advisories at the action level, ensuring safety through explicit checks rather than assuming certification of evaluators, models, or plants.

Method: A versioned, safety-governed evaluation contract framework that implements non-compensatory checks derived from plant profiles to validate recommendation eligibility based on evidence support, authority permission, and consequence acceptability.

Result: ADMITBench Release 0.1.0 provides a public reference implementation for technical and research evaluation, establishing a structured approach to assess industrial LLM advisories with explicit safety checks.

Conclusion: The framework offers a practical approach to evaluating industrial LLM recommendations with safety governance, though it's currently limited to technical evaluation and not authorized for physical execution.

Abstract: This white paper presents ADMITBench, a reference framework for evaluating industrial LLM advisories at the level of the proposed action. The framework implements a versioned, safety-governed evaluation contract that checks whether a recommendation is supported by the available evidence, permitted under the stated authority and procedure, and acceptable under the plant-specific consequence checks encoded in the selected evaluation profile. In this report, \emph{safety-governed} means that eligibility is determined through explicit, non-compensatory checks derived from a versioned plant profile; it does not mean that the evaluator, model, or plant has been safety-certified. Release 0.1.0 is a public reference implementation for technical and research evaluation, not an authorisation for physical execution.

</details>


### [108] [ContinualSkillBench: Can LLM Agents Truly Evolve Their Capabilities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03874)
*Tianyi Guan,Yiding Wang,Haotong Yang,Siyuan Cao,Shirui Liu,Yi Hu,Jiaqi Li,Muhan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current LLM agent frameworks with external skill libraries show limited ability to evolve skills effectively; while sequential execution improves performance, gains vary across models/domains and skills remain fragmented rather than robustly transferable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's uncertainty about whether modern agent frameworks with external skill libraries can effectively evolve their skills and whether these skills actually improve task-solving capabilities, necessitating a systematic evaluation framework.

Method: The authors introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning covering five representative domains, each with 100 interconnected subtasks ordered by increasing difficulty and opportunities for cross-task skill reuse.

Result: Sequential execution generally improves performance but gains vary substantially across models/domains. In-context learning performs comparably to explicit skill maintenance on average, with explicit skills providing selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. Less capable models accumulate larger, more fragmented collections of task-specific skills.

Conclusion: Current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation but struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills, suggesting limitations in skill abstraction and transfer capabilities.

Abstract: Modern agent frameworks equip large language models with external skill libraries to solve complex tasks. However, it remains unclear whether these systems can effectively evolve their skills and whether the resulting skills improve task-solving capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning. It covers five representative domains, each containing 100 interconnected subtasks ordered by increasing difficulty and opportunities for cross-task skill reuse. Our experiments show that sequential execution generally improves performance, but the gains vary substantially across models and domains. Moreover, in-context learning performs comparably to explicit skill maintenance on average, suggesting that much of the improvement arises from adaptation to prior context and feedback rather than reusable skill abstraction alone. Explicit skills nevertheless provide selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. We further find that less capable models tend to accumulate larger, more fragmented collections of task-specific skills. These findings show that current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation, but still struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills.

</details>


### [109] [Intertemporal Preference Steering in Qwen3 via Contrastive Activation Addition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03892)
*Michal Mráz,Justin Shenk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Linear probes can identify temporal-horizon representations in LLMs, enabling bidirectional steering of time-related preferences and improving planning capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding and controlling how AI models handle temporal trade-offs is important for systems that give advice involving delayed costs/benefits, and for addressing safety concerns about long-horizon planning.

Method: Train contrastive linear probes on teacher-forced temporal-choice answers to find short-term vs long-term direction in Qwen3-32B's residual stream, then use contrastive activation-addition steering for evaluation.

Result: Temporal-horizon directions are identifiable with simple linear probes and enable large, bidirectional preference changes; steering strongly shifts indifference thresholds in monetary intertemporal-choice tasks and improves planning capabilities.

Conclusion: Model intertemporal preferences are measurable and steerable, which has implications for AI systems dealing with temporal trade-offs and long-horizon planning safety.

Abstract: We study linear representations of temporal horizon in the large language model Qwen3-32B and use them to change the model's time-related preferences, recommendations, and capabilities. We train contrastive linear probes on teacher-forced temporal-choice answers to find a short-term versus long-term direction in the model's residual stream, and evaluate contrastive activation-addition steering on a held-out binary temporal-choice task, an out-of-distribution monetary intertemporal-choice task, and a TravelPlanner capability benchmark. The central result is that temporal-horizon directions can be identified with simple contrastive linear probes and then used for steering to induce large, bidirectional preference changes. On an out-of-distribution monetary choice task that varies reward size and delay, steering strongly shifts the model's indifference threshold between smaller-sooner and larger-later rewards in both directions. We further show improvements on a planning-related capability metric under moderate temporal steering. These results suggest that model intertemporal preferences are measurable and steerable, which is relevant for AI systems that give advice involving delayed costs and benefits, and for safety questions about long-horizon planning.

</details>


### [110] [When Efficiency Becomes Fragility: Exploiting Dynamic Routing Vulnerabilities in Adaptive UAV Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03902)
*Shaofeng Liang,Runwei Guan,Wenshuo Chen,Jiemin Wu,Bowen Tian,Haozhe Jia,Kaishen Yuan,Songning Lai,Daizong Liu,Yutao Yue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reveals a fundamental security vulnerability in adaptive Transformer trackers - Lipschitz singularities in computational path decisions that enable adversarial attacks manipulating inference topology through imperceptible perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource constraints on UAV platforms have shifted aerial tracking from pure performance to balancing accuracy with efficiency. While adaptive Transformer trackers with dynamic routing architectures address this challenge, the authors discovered a critical structural flaw: mathematical discontinuities at decision boundaries that create exploitable attack surfaces.

Method: The authors formally characterize Lipschitz singularity in adaptive tracking architectures, identifying it as an exploitable attack surface. They propose the Adversarial Path-Inversion (API) framework, which generates imperceptible perturbations to precisely manipulate gating decisions, forcing inference onto altered computational paths.

Result: Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art adaptive trackers show that API achieves superior perturbation stealthiness, more effective attacks, and faster inference speeds compared to other methods.

Conclusion: This work reveals a previously overlooked topological path space attack surface in dynamic tracking networks, providing both a new dimension for security analysis and a theoretical warning for constructing robust adaptive tracking architectures in the future.

Abstract: Resource constraints on UAV platforms have driven a paradigm shift in aerial tracking, from pursuing performance toward balancing accuracy with efficiency. Adaptive Transformer Trackers, which leverage an input-dependent dynamic routing architecture, have emerged as a representative solution to this challenge. However, we reveal that behind this computation-on-demand flexibility hides a critical structural flaw: the Lipschitz singularity of computational path decisions, which has an unbounded local Lipschitz constant at discrete layer-skipping decision boundaries. This mathematical discontinuity renders adaptive tracking networks inherently unstable: tiny input perturbations can be amplified at the gating modules, causing dramatic changes in the inference topology. We formally characterize this singularity in the context of adaptive tracking architectures and, for the first time, identify it as a directly exploitable new attack surface. This insight reveals a previously overlooked and highly vulnerable topological path space attack surface. Based on this, we propose the Adversarial Path-Inversion (API) framework. API generates imperceptible perturbations to precisely manipulate the gating decisions, forcing the inference onto altered computational paths. The severe inconsistency between the original and the inverted paths dismantles the representation capability of the model. Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art adaptive trackers demonstrate that API achieves superior perturbation stealthiness, more effective attack, and faster inference speeds. This work opens a new dimension for the security analysis of dynamic tracking networks and provides a theoretical warning for constructing robust adaptive tracking architectures in the future.

</details>


### [111] [Socially Grounded Agentic AI: Coordinating Plural Perspectives through Social Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03910)
*Matt Ratto,Abhishek Moturu,Daniel Silver*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that pluralistic AI alignment should be framed as a social coordination problem, not just output diversification, and proposes using social theory to design systems that can represent, coordinate, and respond to multiple legitimate perspectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems are deployed across diverse social contexts, alignment can no longer be framed as optimizing a single set of values. Systems must recognize and respond to multiple legitimate perspectives, but current approaches lack an understanding of how values are socially organized, contested, and coordinated in practice.

Method: Drawing on established traditions in sociology, the authors show how perspectives can be understood as structured by roles, shaped through interaction, and distributed across fields of power and expertise. They translate these insights into concrete implications for AI system design, including role-based representations, structured coordination among perspectives, and context-sensitive evaluation.

Result: The paper repositions pluralistic alignment as a problem of socially grounded coordination rather than output diversification. It outlines a design space for systems that engage multiple perspectives in structured and accountable ways, requiring alignment of not just final outputs but also role activations, deliberative traces, aggregation rules, and feedback loops.

Conclusion: Social theory provides essential conceptual and design resources for addressing pluralistic alignment challenges. Future work should focus on implementing and empirically evaluating these approaches in real-world settings to create AI systems that can better navigate diverse social contexts.

Abstract: As AI systems are deployed across increasingly diverse social contexts, alignment can no longer be framed as the optimization of a single, unified set of values. Instead, systems must be able to recognize, represent, and respond to multiple legitimate perspectives. This has led to growing interest in pluralistic alignment, which seeks to move beyond one-size-fits-all models of appropriate behaviour. However, current approaches often lack a clear account of how values are socially organized, contested, and coordinated in practice. In this paper, we argue that social theory provides essential conceptual and design resources for addressing these challenges. Drawing on established traditions in sociology, we show how perspectives can be understood as structured by roles, shaped through interaction, and distributed across fields of power and expertise. We translate these insights into concrete implications for AI system design, including role-based representations, structured coordination among perspectives, and context-sensitive evaluation. For agentic systems, this requires aligning not only final outputs, but also the role activations, deliberative traces, aggregation rules, and feedback loops through which those outputs are produced. Our contribution is to reposition pluralistic alignment as a problem of socially grounded coordination rather than output diversification. We outline a design space for systems that engage multiple perspectives in structured and accountable ways, and we identify directions for future work to implement and empirically evaluate these approaches in real-world settings.

</details>


### [112] [Implementing Causal Perception: Competing SCMs and Situated Fairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03917)
*Jose M. Álvarez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First implementation of causal perception framework showing how competing causal models affect fairness assessments in AI decision systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal perception is a promising probabilistic framework for understanding how agents with different causal models perceive fairness, but it has remained purely theoretical without implementation.

Method: Implemented the causal perception framework of Álvarez and Ruggieri (2025), operationalizing structural and parametrical causal perception. Designed algorithms for computing interventional and counterfactual distributions and proposed distance measures to quantify disagreement. Applied to German Credit dataset.

Result: Causal perception affects accuracy and fairness in multi-expert decision settings; perception verdict is sensitive to distance metric and threshold choices; causal perception changes fairness assessments and threshold-based decisions; bias is situated with respect to agent's SCM.

Conclusion: Competing worldviews in fairness problems cannot be ignored, as bias is situated within agents' structural causal models, demonstrating the importance of considering multiple causal perspectives in fairness assessments.

Abstract: Causal perception occurs when agents with competing Structural Causal Models (SCMs) of the same system infer different probability distributions, including the hypothetical distributions implied by each agent's SCM under the same set of interventions. It shapes how agents reason about the system and how they perceive its fairness. Causal perception is a promising probabilistic framework, but it has remained purely theoretical. This work provides the first implementation of the causal perception framework of Álvarez and Ruggieri (2025). We operationalize structural (agents disagree on the causal graph) and parametrical (agents agree on the causal graph but disagree on its weights) causal perception. We design algorithms for computing interventional and counterfactual distributions and propose suitable distance measures to quantify the disagreement. Using the German Credit dataset, we illustrate how causal perception affects accuracy and fairness in a multi-expert decision setting. We show that the perception verdict is sensitive to the choice of distance metric and threshold. We also show that causal perception changes fairness assessments and threshold-based decisions. Bias proves situated with respect to the agent's SCM, demonstrating that competing worldviews in fairness problems cannot be ignored.

</details>


### [113] [The Transformer Revolution, Part 1: Dynamic Processing through Output- Weight Interconnections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03921)
*Marco Giunti,Fabrizia Giulia Garavaglia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transformers perform dynamic, prompt-dependent computation during inference where the prompt generates transformation parameters that modify token representations, challenging the "stochastic parrot" view of LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the prevailing "stochastic parrot" interpretation that LLMs merely reproduce statistical regularities, and instead demonstrate that Transformers perform meaningful, prompt-dependent computation during inference where the system constructs transformations based on input.

Method: The paper interprets Transformer architecture through the lens of SIDPP (Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing), analyzing how token vectors represent concepts to be transformed, while matrices and vectors represent transforming concepts that can be static (trained) or dynamic (generated from input). The key insight is that Transformers use output-weight interconnections where network outputs determine other networks' weights.

Result: Transformers generate dynamic transformations during inference whose contribution grows with prompt length, potentially equal to or exceeding static processing (strong prompt sensitivity). This dynamic processing emerges from the architectural design where networks' outputs determine other networks' weights.

Conclusion: This SIDPP interpretation has implications for interpretability, predictability, control, and sustainable system design. Since human neural systems have the mechanisms for SIDPP, the authors conjecture human language processing may be a form of SIDPP with similar functional architecture to Transformers.

Abstract: This paper offers a new interpretation of the Transformer during inference. Against the "stochastic parrot" view that large language models merely reproduce statistical regularities learned in training, we argue that Transformers construct and apply prompt-dependent transformations whose parameters are generated during inference. We call this form of computation SIDPP: Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing. The Transformer is interpreted as a system that transforms concepts by means of concepts. Token vectors are the concepts to be transformed; parameterized transformations defined by matrices and vectors are the transforming concepts. These may be static, when fixed through training, or dynamic, when generated from the input sequence. Mechanically, they correspond to groups of simple neural networks. The Transformer's architectural novelty lies in output-weight interconnections, through which the outputs of some networks determine the weights of others, alongside ordinary output-input interconnections. By means of these interconnections, the system constructs transformations from the prompt and uses them to modify token representations. The contribution of dynamic processing grows with prompt length and may equal or exceed that of static processing, a phenomenon we call strong prompt sensitivity. This account bears on interpretability, predictability, control, and the design of smaller, more sustainable systems. Finally, since the human neural system possesses the mechanisms required to implement SIDPP, we argue that a form of SIDPP may, in principle, be neurally realized in the cerebral cortex. We therefore conjecture that human language processing may itself be a form of SIDPP produced by a functional architecture relevantly similar to that of the Transformer.

</details>


### [114] [TACT: Taxonomy-Aligned Post-Training for Pedagogically Adaptive English Tutoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03952)
*Dongjie Yang,Siyan Lin,Leixian Shen,Rui Sheng,Huamin Qu,Zixin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TACT is a human-grounded framework for developing pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors using LLMs, with taxonomies for tutor strategies and student moves, enhanced training data, and a novel optimization approach that outperforms proprietary baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based ESL tutors focus primarily on fluent response generation but lack pedagogical adaptation based on learner behavior and dialogue context. Human-tutoring research provides principles for adaptive support, but they are task-specific and not well integrated into LLM tutor training and evaluation.

Method: Developed two taxonomies: Tutor-Strategy Taxonomy (13 strategies) and Student-Move Taxonomy (characterizing learner behavior). Created TACTCorpus with 260 authentic conversations annotated with 32,379 labels and augmented training data. Post-trained Qwen3.5-4B through supervised fine-tuning followed by taxonomy-aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization to create TACTutor.

Result: TACTutor improved over its backbone by 20.30% on TACTBench (strategy-balanced diagnostic benchmark with 78 authentic contexts) and outperformed all evaluated proprietary baselines. It maintained backbone performance on established educational benchmarks and received highest overall mean rating in blinded study with 50 learners.

Conclusion: TACT provides an effective framework for developing pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors that go beyond fluent response generation to provide context-aware pedagogical support. The open release of data, benchmark, and model weights establishes a foundation for further research in this area.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to provide conversational practice for English-as-a-second-language (ESL) learners. Effective ESL tutoring, however, requires more than fluent response generation: a tutor must select an appropriate pedagogical action based on learner behavior and dialogue context. Human-tutoring research offers principles for adaptive support, but they are often task-specific and remain insufficiently integrated into LLM-based ESL tutor training and evaluation. We present TACT (Taxonomy-Aligned Conversational Tutor), a human-grounded framework for post-training and evaluating pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors. Drawing on established literature, we develop two complementary taxonomies: the Tutor-Strategy Taxonomy with 13 tutor response strategies and the Student-Move Taxonomy characterizing learner behavior by move type and status. Using these taxonomies, we construct TACTCorpus, which enriches 260 authentic teacher-student conversations with 32,379 annotations and quality-controlled augmented training data. We then post-train Qwen3.5-4B through supervised fine-tuning followed by taxonomy-aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization, producing TACTutor and optimizing it for scaffolding quality rather than reference imitation alone. On TACTBench, a strategy-balanced diagnostic benchmark comprising 78 authentic tutoring contexts, TACTutor improves over its backbone by 20.30% and outperforms all evaluated proprietary baselines under the same protocol, while maintaining backbone performance on established external educational benchmarks; in a blinded study with 50 learners, it also receives the highest overall mean rating among the evaluated tutors. We release the data, benchmark, and model weights, providing an open foundation for developing pedagogically adaptive ESL tutors.

</details>


### [115] [A game theory for foundation models shows new paths to rational cooperation through similarity inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03958)
*Alexander Meulemans,Maciej Wołczyk,Marissa A. Weis,Rajai Nasser,Roberta Rocca,Seijin Kobayashi,Guillaume Lajoie,Angelika Steger,Blake Richards,Marcus Hutter,James Manyika,Rif A. Saurous,João Sacramento,Blaise Agüera y Arcas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Foundation model agents consistently cooperate in social dilemmas despite classical game theory predicting mutual defection, due to their "embedded agency" approach where they model themselves as part of the system and use similarity inference during planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents powered by foundation models become more integrated into social and economic systems, we need to understand their collective behavior principles for safety and cooperation. Classical game theory assumes "decoupled agency" where agents treat decision-making as independent, but modern AI agents don't operate this way.

Method: Introduced the "embedded Bayesian agent" theoretical model that shifts from decoupled to embedded agency. These agents model themselves as part of the universe, maintain epistemic uncertainty about their own algorithms, and use similarity inference during planning. Formalized this through "embedded equilibrium," a new solution concept replacing Nash equilibrium.

Result: Foundation model agents engaging in optimal planning consistently converge to stable cooperation in social dilemmas, directly contradicting classical game-theoretic predictions of mutual defection. This occurs because embedded agents treat their own deliberation during planning as evidence: a decision to cooperate predicts a similar decision by a similar partner.

Conclusion: The "embedded equilibrium" provides a foundational game theory for modern AI agent social behavior, showing that when agents model themselves as part of the system and use similarity inference, cooperation emerges naturally in social dilemmas despite classical predictions.

Abstract: As autonomous agents powered by foundation models are increasingly integrated into social and economic systems, understanding the principles governing their collective behavior is essential for ensuring safety and cooperation. Classical game theory, the dominant framework for modeling rational interaction, is built upon the assumption of `decoupled agency,' where agents treat their own decision-making as independent of the environment and other actors. Modern AI agents, however, jointly predict their own future actions alongside external observations. Here, we report a striking finding: when interacting in stylized social dilemmas, foundation model agents engaging in optimal planning consistently converge to stable cooperation, directly contradicting classical game-theoretic predictions of mutual defection. To understand this phenomenon, we introduce the `embedded Bayesian agent,' a theoretical model for foundation model agents. By shifting from decoupled to embedded agency, these agents model themselves as part of the universe they inhabit, maintaining epistemic uncertainty about their own decision-making algorithms. We show that by inferring whether others are behaviorally similar, an embedded agent treats its own deliberation during planning as evidence: a decision to cooperate predicts a similar decision by a similar partner. We formalize this mechanism of similarity inference through the `embedded equilibrium,' a novel solution concept replacing the Nash equilibrium to provide a foundational game theory for the social behavior of modern AI agents.

</details>


### [116] [Interpretable Adaptive Sampling for LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03961)
*Mobina Kashaniyan,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive test-time scaling using fuzzy controller improves LLM reasoning efficiency by dynamically allocating compute based on prompt complexity and model confidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed per-query budgets in test-time scaling pipelines waste compute on easy prompts and are difficult to inspect, as they don't explain why different prompts receive different numbers of samples.

Method: Proposes adaptive test-time scaling with a lightweight fuzzy controller that maps interpretable signals (estimated prompt complexity and model confidence) to per-query sampling budgets, assigning fewer samples to easier/confident prompts and more to harder/uncertain ones.

Result: Across models and datasets, adaptive fuzzy control outperforms several baselines (best-of-N, compute-aware scaling, self-certainty-based) and stays close to full-budget control while reducing average sample count.

Conclusion: Interpretable adaptive sampling is a practical approach for more efficient test-time reasoning in large language models, making compute usage inspectable rather than fixed or opaque.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by generating and aggregating multiple candidate answers, yet many pipelines use fixed per-query budgets that spend the same compute on easy and difficult prompts. These fixed budgets are also difficult to inspect because they do not explain why a given prompt receives a particular number of samples. We propose adaptive} test-time scaling with a lightweight fuzzy controller that maps interpretable signals, including estimated prompt complexity and model confidence, to a per-query sampling budget. The controller assigns fewer samples to easier or more confident prompts and more samples to harder or less certain prompts, making inference-time compute inspectable rather than fixed or opaque. We evaluate under a fair-alignment protocol with matched decoding settings and controlled answer selection, and compare against best-of-$N$, compute-aware scaling, and self-certainty-based baselines on question-answering and mathematical reasoning tasks. Across models and datasets, adaptive fuzzy control improves over several standard baselines and remains close to a selector-matched full-budget control while reducing the average number of samples. These findings suggest that interpretable adaptive sampling is a practical direction for more efficient test-time reasoning in large language models.

</details>


### [117] [Should We Type or Talk to LLM Agents? A Comprehensive Study of Voice and Keyboard Input Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03970)
*Zizhao Hu,Nathan Elijah Segura,Mohammad Rostami,Jesse Thomason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HIVE (Human Input-Variation Engine) evaluates how voice and keyboard input perturbations affect LLM performance, finding voice transcription errors are more harmful than keyboard typos, with token destruction being the key factor causing accuracy drops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different human input channels (typing vs speaking) leave distinct signatures: orthographic noise from keyboards, and transcription disfluencies/restructuring from voice. There's a need to understand how these variations impact LLM performance and robustness.

Method: Developed HIVE - Human Input-Variation Engine, a suite of voice transcription perturbations (using both conventional transcription and AI-backed dictation tools) and QWERTY keyboard perturbations. Used HIVE to evaluate model robustness across various instruction-tuned models.

Result: 1. Voice transcription perturbations lower accuracy across all tested models, with transcription structure rather than fillers causing the harm. 2. Keyboard perturbations cause less damage, requiring many errors before accuracy drops. 3. Token destruction is the key harm factor, not token addition. 4. Channel gap appears only in constructed/deduced answers, not multiple choice. 5. Harm not solely from test-set contamination. 6. Can't be trained away with lightweight adaptation. 7. Thinking budget helps keyboard perturbations but not spoken ones.

Conclusion: LLMs are vulnerable to realistic input perturbations, especially from voice transcription. The core issue is token destruction rather than disfluency. Current methods (training, thinking budgets) can't fully address this vulnerability, highlighting a robustness gap in LLMs for real-world human input variations.

Abstract: Human input reaches language models by typing or speaking, and each channel leaves a distinct signature: orthographic noise for keyboards; for voice, disfluency from conventional transcription and restructuring from AI-backed dictation tools. How do they impact an LLM's performance? In this paper we present HIVE (Human Input-Variation Engine), a suite of voice transcription perturbations and QWERTY keyboard perturbations. We use HIVE to evaluate how robust models are to these perturbations. We present seven findings. (i) Voice transcription perturbations lower accuracy across every instruction-tuned model we test, and it is the structure of the transcription rather than its fillers that carries the cost. (ii) QWERTY keyboard perturbations cost less, and a model absorbs a lot of them before accuracy falls away. (iii) Both trace back to one cause, how many of the question's tokens survive the perturbation: destroying a token is what hurts, while adding new ones alongside it costs little. (iv) The gap between the two channels appears only where the answer must be constructed or deduced; on multiple choice there is none. (v) The harm does not solely come from test-set contamination. (vi) It cannot be trained away with lightweight adaptation. (vii) A thinking budget recovers the keyboard channel almost entirely but leaves the spoken registers untouched, and compressed speech is worse with it.

</details>


### [118] [ReflectRL: Learning from Golden Negative Trajectories via Reflective-to-Direct Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03972)
*Jinhe Bi,Chennan Zhou,Zengjie Jin,Aniri,Shuo Lu,Wenke Huang,Hu Cao,Xun Xiao,Zhihong Zhu,Volker Tresp,Fei Shen,Yunpu Ma,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReflectRL: A lightweight framework that leverages failed expert trajectories (Golden Negative Trajectories) to improve LLM reasoning by learning from flawed reasoning paths through reflection rather than discarding them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory-guided methods discard failed expert trajectories as negative samples, but these Golden Negative Trajectories contain valuable reasoning signals. Reflecting on flawed trajectories can be easier and more effective than solving hard problems from scratch.

Method: ReflectRL uses Golden Negative Trajectories to elicit Reflective Reasoning (analyzing flawed reasoning paths), then applies Reflective-to-Direct Policy Transition to transfer learned reasoning behaviors back to Direct Reasoning. It's a lightweight plug-and-play framework compatible with existing on-policy training methods.

Result: Experiments across 9 benchmarks, 4 LLM backbones, and 4 on-policy training methods show ReflectRL consistently improves reasoning performance with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: Golden Negative Trajectories provide valuable learning signals when treated as flawed trajectories to reflect upon, not just as negative samples. ReflectRL demonstrates that learning from expert failures through reflection is an effective approach for enhancing LLM reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: On-policy training has emerged as a powerful post-training paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models, and is often enhanced by golden trajectories from stronger expert models. However, when the expert fails on harder problems, existing trajectory-guided methods lose their main source of supervision, and these failed trajectories are typically discarded as negative samples. We argue that such failures, which we call Golden Negative Trajectories, can still provide valuable reasoning signals when treated not as demonstrations to imitate, but as flawed trajectories to reflect upon. We identify a Reflection Advantage: for hard problems, reflecting on a flawed trajectory can be easier and more effective than solving the problem directly from scratch. Motivated by this, we propose ReflectRL, a lightweight plug-and-play framework that learns from Golden Negative Trajectories during on-policy training. ReflectRL first uses these trajectories to elicit Reflective Reasoning, then applies Reflective-to-Direct Policy Transition to transfer the acquired reasoning behavior back to Direct Reasoning. Experiments across 9 benchmarks, 4 LLM backbones, and 4 on-policy training methods show that ReflectRL consistently improves reasoning performance with minimal overhead.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [119] [Deep Divide-and-Reduce in Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02628)
*Yusong Deng,Yanjie Li,Weijun Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DDRSR introduces a novel symbolic regression method that overcomes limitations of existing approaches by providing mathematically rigorous expression decomposition without brute-force searches, enabling better discovery of underlying mathematical principles in data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current machine learning approaches to symbolic regression often lack deep understanding of intrinsic mathematical and physical principles, while existing methods like AI Feynman have narrow applicability, rely on brute-force sub-expression searches, and struggle with complex equations.

Method: Deep Divide and Reduce in Symbolic Regression (DDRSR) uses rigorous mathematical deduction and proofs to broaden expression decomposition and reduction applicability, circumventing brute-force substructure searches while ensuring theoretical correctness.

Result: Empirical evaluations show DDRSR provides significant advantages in both expression decomposition and numerical regression tasks compared to existing methods, demonstrating wider versatility and theoretical robustness.

Conclusion: DDRSR represents a theoretically sound alternative to brute-force symbolic regression approaches, though the authors acknowledge applicable scenarios, limitations, and suggest directions for future research to further develop this paradigm.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) is the task of discovering underlying patterns from data and representing them using mathematical expressions. Current machine learning approaches to SR often lack a profound understanding of the intrinsic mathematical and physical principles governing these expressions. While the pioneering AI Feynman method leverages the mathematical properties underlying the data, its expression simplification mechanism suffers from a narrow scope of applicability and is prone to failure on complex equations. Furthermore, its underlying mechanisms rely heavily on brute-force searches for sub-expressions, severely limiting its practical utility. Through rigorous mathematical deduction and proofs, we propose our method, Deep Divide and Reduce in Symbolic Regression (DDRSR). DDRSR fundamentally broadens the applicability of expression decomposition and reduction, circumvents the need for brute-force sub-structure searches, and ensures both wider versatility and strict theoretical correctness. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that these theoretical principles yield significant advantages in both expression decomposition and numerical regression tasks. Finally, we discuss the applicable scenarios and inherent limitations of this paradigm, alongside promising directions for future research.

</details>


### [120] [Multimodal Auto-regressive Transformer Surrogate for Modeling Variable Operations and Quantifying Uncertainty in Geological Carbon Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02629)
*Yifu Han,Louis J. Durlofsky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal transformer surrogate model is developed for geological CO2 storage optimization under uncertainty, combining 3D geomodels, permeability parameters, and control variables to predict key performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve efficiency of geological carbon storage operations by optimizing variable well perforation and injection strategies while accounting for geological uncertainty, requiring a fast and accurate surrogate model to replace computationally expensive flow simulations.

Method: Developed a multimodal auto-regressive transformer surrogate with separate encoders for three input modalities (3D geomodel, relative permeability parameters, control variables) that fuse via self-attention, and a temporal decoder generating predictions auto-regressively through encoder-decoder cross-attention. Trained on 4000 GEOS flow simulations.

Result: The surrogate achieves median saturation MAE of 0.028 and median relative errors of 0.2-5% for other quantities on test data. Successfully captures transition from rate to bottom-hole-pressure control and enables substantial uncertainty reduction when used in hierarchical MCMC data assimilation.

Conclusion: The multimodal transformer surrogate effectively models complex CO2 storage operations under uncertainty, enabling efficient optimization and data assimilation while maintaining accuracy comparable to full-scale simulations.

Abstract: The use of variable well perforation and injection strategies can improve the efficiency of geological carbon storage operations. We develop a new multimodal auto-regressive transformer surrogate to model these operations under geological uncertainty. A modified SEAM CO2 geomodel, which involves a faulted system with three stacked aquifers, is considered. The two injection wells are perforated in stages, from bottom to top, with the stage durations and individual well injection rates treated as control variables. The surrogate model processes three input modalities - the 3D geomodel, scalar parameters characterizing relative permeability functions, and control variables - through separate encoders. These are fused via self-attention in a transformer encoder, and a temporal decoder generates predictions auto-regressively through encoder-decoder cross-attention. The surrogate is trained, using 4000 GEOS flow simulations, to predict saturation and pressure at monitoring locations, total injected and mobile CO2 mass, and saturation footprints. For a new test set, involving randomly sampled geomodels and control variables, the surrogate achieves a median saturation MAE of 0.028 and median relative errors of 0.2-5% for the other quantities of interest. Importantly, it captures the switch from rate to bottom-hole-pressure control. The surrogate model is used within a hierarchical Markov chain Monte Carlo data assimilation procedure for a synthetic true model under three operational strategies. Substantial uncertainty reduction is achieved for key metaparameters, particularly the fault permeabilities. Posterior predictions for saturation footprints and total injected and mobile CO2 mass are also shown to be generally consistent with true model results.

</details>


### [121] [LLMs Can Annotate Attribution Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02632)
*Ameen Patel,Max Zhang,Nathan Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A pipeline for automating the grouping of individual features/neurons into supernodes in circuit tracing, making the manual step faster while maintaining interpretability comparable to human annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Circuit tracing reveals internal computations of language models but requires time-intensive manual grouping of features/neurons into supernodes. This manual step slows down the analysis process and makes circuit tracing less scalable.

Method: The authors present a simple pipeline that automates feature grouping by directly presenting feature descriptions to a language model, which then groups them into supernodes. They use automated interpretability metrics to validate results and demonstrate applications on specific tasks like two-hop Capitals and open-ended exploration of Wikipedia prompt completions.

Result: The automated pipeline produces supernodes as interpretable as human-generated ones. On a two-hop Capitals task, it successfully recovers the intermediate hop supernode in 97 of 100 prompts. In open-ended exploration, the system automatically annotated 1000 attribution graphs and used an LLM judge to identify interesting graphs for human review.

Conclusion: Simple automation can produce meaningful attribution graph annotations, demonstrating that automated circuit tracing is feasible and motivating further work in this direction to make circuit analysis more scalable and efficient.

Abstract: Circuit tracing is an exciting technique for revealing the internal computation of language models, but it requires a time-intensive manual step of grouping individual features or MLP neurons into supernodes. We present a simple pipeline for automating this step: directly presenting feature descriptions to a language model that groups them into supernodes. Using automated interpretability metrics, we confirm that supernodes generated by our pipeline are as interpretable as those generated by human annotators. On a two-hop Capitals task, our pipeline recovers a supernode corresponding to the intermediate hop in 97 of 100 prompts. Finally, we present a simple proof of concept using our pipeline for open-ended exploration, where we automatically annotate 1000 attribution graphs from Wikipedia prompt completions and then use an LLM judge to flag interesting graphs worth human review. We hope this work demonstrates that even simple automation can produce meaningful attribution graph annotations, motivating further work on automated circuit tracing.

</details>


### [122] [GeoID-PINN: Identifiability-Aware Regional Epidemic Inference with Geographic Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02633)
*Weixiong Hua,Fan Bu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoID-PINN is a physics-informed neural network for SIRD epidemiology that incorporates spatial priors to identify regional transmission dependencies, showing improved forecasting accuracy over baselines while revealing challenges in uniquely recovering network structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regional surveillance data contains mixed signals - local transmission, reporting variations, external infections - that are difficult to separate. Current methods struggle to identify the underlying spatial dependence structure between regions, making it hard to distinguish true transmission networks from other factors.

Method: Physics-informed neural network (PINN) for SIRD dynamics with spatial representation via row-stochastic source-composition matrix. This matrix assigns source weights to regions and is regularized toward spatial priors (distance, adjacency, commuting, lead-lag). Validation uses simulation with known truth and retrospective COVID-19 data from 64 Louisiana counties.

Result: In simulation: distance prior gave source-composition error 0.099, worse without regularization (0.159), worst with misspecified prior (0.577). Accurate trajectory fits don't guarantee correct structure recovery. On COVID-19 data: GeoID-PINN reduced MSE from 32,957 to 11,468 and MAE from 70.60 to 57.73 vs autoregressive baseline, though baseline had slightly better NLL. Adjacency prior improved county-level MSE by 6.85% and MAE by 3.1%.

Conclusion: Structured regularization improves forecasting accuracy but doesn't uniquely identify transmission edges. Different priors yield similar performance, suggesting multiple plausible spatial structures can fit data equally well. Results highlight need for prior-sensitivity analysis and caution in interpreting recovered network structures as unique.

Abstract: Regional surveillance data reflect local transmission, reporting, seeding, and external infection pressure, which are difficult to identify separately. We introduce GeoID-PINN, a physics-informed neural network (PINN) for susceptible-infectious-recovered-deceased (SIRD) dynamics. The model represents spatial dependence with a row-stochastic source-composition matrix whose rows assign nonnegative source weights that sum to one. We regularize this matrix toward a spatial prior constructed from distance, adjacency, commuting, or lead-lag information. In a four-region simulation with known truth, a compatible distance prior gives source-composition error 0.099. The error rises to 0.159 without regularization and 0.577 under a strongly misspecified prior, while trajectory fit and transmission-scale estimates remain similar. Accurate trajectories therefore do not guarantee recovery of the regional dependence structure. We also evaluate GeoID-PINN retrospectively using COVID-19 data from 64 Louisiana counties. Relative to an autoregressive negative-binomial baseline, Forecast-Trained Geo-PINN reduces mean squared error (MSE) from 32,957 to 11,468 and mean absolute error (MAE) from 70.60 to 57.73. The baseline has lower negative log likelihood (NLL), 5.158 versus 5.346, indicating better distributional fit but worse point accuracy. In a controlled 15-county comparison, county adjacency reduces MSE by 6.85 percent and MAE by 3.1 percent. Similar performance across plausible priors supports structured regularization but not unique edge recovery. These results require prior-sensitivity and observation-model checks before interpretation.

</details>


### [123] [Verifier-Guided Model Discovery for Physical Dynamical Systems with Pretrained Symbolic Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02662)
*Farbod Faraji,Francesco Belardinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A verifier-guided neural-to-symbolic method transfers pretrained transformers to discover interpretable equations from high-dimensional physical data, successfully applied to Van der Pol oscillators and vortex shedding dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity simulations are expensive, and machine-learning surrogates can be opaque with limited generalizability. While pretrained transformers offer interpretable alternatives by mapping trajectories to equations, transferring them reliably to high-dimensional physical data remains challenging.

Method: Developed a verifier-guided workflow using ODEFormer as symbolic backbone with dynamical and physical-admissibility criteria to select from multi-trajectory candidate equation pool. Applied coordinate reduction and symbolic discovery to vortex shedding at fixed and varying Reynolds numbers.

Result: VG outperformed original ODEFormer workflow on Van der Pol oscillators across held-out initial conditions. Discovered fixed-parameter reduced-order equations for vortex shedding that recover fundamental shedding oscillator and higher harmonics without wake-specific library or prescribed Navier-Stokes structure. Cross-parameter model generalized to withheld regimes.

Conclusion: Established a verifier-guided neural-to-symbolic methodology for interpretable and physically auditable forecasting in natural sciences. Showed reconstruction fidelity alone doesn't determine symbolic discoverability; compatibility between latent dynamics and backbone's pretraining distribution is crucial.

Abstract: Reliable forecasting of nonlinear physical systems underpins scientific discovery and engineering decision-making. Yet high-fidelity simulations are prohibitively costly, and machine-learning surrogates can be opaque and encode assumptions about system dynamics, limiting generalizability. Pretrained transformers mapping synthetic ODE trajectories to equations offer interpretable alternatives, promising transfer without system-specific equation knowledge. Transferring them reliably to high-dimensional physical data, however, remains an open challenge. We develop a verifier-guided (VG) workflow around ODEFormer as a symbolic backbone, using dynamical and physical-admissibility criteria to select from a multi-trajectory candidate equation pool, enabling transfer. On canonical Van der Pol oscillators, VG outperforms the original ODEFormer workflow across held-out initial conditions. We then address vortex shedding, a phenomenon occurring in atmospheric and plasma systems of societal relevance, through coordinate reduction and symbolic discovery at fixed and varying Reynolds numbers. VG discovers fixed-parameter reduced-order equations that recover the fundamental shedding oscillator and higher harmonics without a wake-specific candidate library or prescribed Navier-Stokes structure, while the cross-parameter model generalizes to withheld regimes. Reconstruction fidelity alone did not determine symbolic discoverability, highlighting the importance of compatibility between latent dynamics and the backbone's pretraining distribution. This work establishes a verifier-guided neural-to-symbolic methodology for interpretable and physically auditable forecasting in the natural sciences.

</details>


### [124] [CT-HEG: A Bidirectional, Timestamp-Attributed Event Graph for ICU In-Hospital Mortality Prediction - An Architectural Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02663)
*Mohammad Nasir Uddin,Rahnuma Tabassum Orpita,Asaduzzaman Anik,Eklachur Rahman Bhuiyan,Marjahan Risalat,SM Wali Ullah,Asif Ahamed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CT-HEG schema with CHIRP-Net (heterogeneous GATv2Conv) achieves best ICU mortality prediction performance on irregular EHR data by modeling both temporal irregularity and typed relational structure, with bidirectional connectivity being essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sequence models handle irregular clinical observations but ignore typed relational structure, while existing graph models assume fixed-interval inputs, creating a gap for modeling irregular EHR data with both temporal and relational heterogeneity.

Method: Introduced Continuous-Time Heterogeneous EHR Graph (CT-HEG) schema encoding ICU stays as typed timestamped graphs with three node types and 2D edge attributes. Instantiated as CHIRP-Net, a four-layer heterogeneous GATv2Conv network, evaluated on MIMIC-IV dataset against multiple baselines with ablation studies.

Result: CHIRP-Net achieved 5-seed mean AUROC 0.8449±0.0071; ensemble achieved AUROC 0.8618. Key findings: bidirectional connectivity essential (0.1968 AUROC drop without), time-attentive edge features contributed modest gains, collapsed edge types outperformed full model with fewer parameters, good calibration after temperature scaling.

Conclusion: CT-HEG is effective for irregular EHR data modeling, with bidirectional connectivity being critical for performance. The approach shows promise but requires external validation, temporal evaluation, and fairness audits before claiming robustness. Code is publicly available for further research.

Abstract: Accurate ICU mortality prediction requires modeling irregular clinical observations across heterogeneous entity types. Existing sequence models handle irregular sampling but ignore typed relational structure; existing graph models assume fixed-interval inputs. We introduce the Continuous-Time Heterogeneous EHR Graph (CT-HEG) schema and evaluate which architectural choices drive predictive performance. CT-HEG encodes each ICU stay as a typed, timestamped graph with three node types (visit, vital, lab_event) and 2D edge attributes (t_hours/48, value_norm) encoding timing and value without imputation. We instantiate CT-HEG as CHIRP-Net, a four-layer heterogeneous GATv2Conv network, evaluated on MIMIC-IV v3.1 (31,142 ICU stays, LOS>=48h, 13.4% mortality) with five seeds and bootstrapped confidence intervals, against logistic regression, mTAND, a Transformer, and GRU-D, plus an ablation study. CHIRP-Net achieved 5-seed mean AUROC 0.8449+/-0.0071 (AUPRC 0.4958+/-0.0209); the ensemble achieved AUROC 0.8618 (95% CI: 0.8485-0.8745). Removing reverse edges disconnected observation nodes from the visit readout, cutting AUROC by 0.1968+/-0.0073. Time-attentive edge features contributed 0.0247+/-0.0093 AUROC.
