Collapsing heterogeneous edge types into one relation (7x fewer parameters) outperformed the full model on all seeds. Post-calibration ECE was 0.0307. Temporal and demographic subgroup analyses were explored but not reported here, pending follow-up work. Bidirectional connectivity was necessary for the model to use its inputs at all, and CT-HEG was reasonably well calibrated after validation-fitted temperature scaling. These results support CT-HEG for irregular EHR data, while external validation, a pre-specified temporal evaluation, and a demographic fairness audit remain necessary before any claim of robustness. Code: https://github.com/nasiruddinstudents-ctrl/chirp-net-mimic-iv.

</details>


### [125] [Sphere Retraction Normalizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02668)
*Jie Zhang,Cheng-Fang Su,Yi-Jui Huang,Min-Te Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces p-SpheretNorm, a one-parameter family of angular retractions for spherical residual connections that outperform existing lightweight deep connection methods on nanoGPT, with optimal performance at finite parameter values rather than extremes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Residual connections are standard for training deep networks, but GeoNorm's Riemannian approach using exponential maps is just one member of a broader family of retraction maps. The authors aim to unify Euclidean residual connections and GeoNorm within a single framework and explore whether other retractions might perform better than the exponential map.

Method: The authors show that on a hypersphere, the entire family of retraction maps collapses to a single scalar design choice for converting update magnitude into rotation angle. They instantiate this framework with metric projection retraction (Proj-SpheretNorm) and Cayley retraction (Cay-SpheretNorm), which form a one-parameter family p-SpheretNorm where rotation angle saturates rather than growing unbounded.

Result: On nanoGPT, all three methods (Proj-SpheretNorm, Cay-SpheretNorm, and p-SpheretNorm) outperform existing lightweight deep connection schemes. The best validation loss is achieved at finite p values, indicating that the exponential map (which corresponds to limiting cases) is not the preferred retraction but merely one end of a spectrum.

Conclusion: The work provides a unified framework for spherical residual connections, showing that exponential maps are suboptimal and that a one-parameter family of angular retractions with saturated rotation angles yields better performance, with practical implementations requiring only algebraic operations.

Abstract: Residual connections are the de facto mechanism for training deep neural networks stably. Geodesic Normalization (GeoNorm) recasts them on a Riemannian manifold, orthogonalizing each layer output against the current hidden state and applying the resulting update through the Riemannian exponential map. Every hidden state thus keeps a constant $\ell_{2}$-norm, confining the residual stream to a hypersphere. The exponential map, however, is only one member of a broad family of retraction maps. We show that on the hypersphere this entire family collapses to a single scalar design choice. What distinguishes one retraction from another is only how the magnitude of an update is converted into a rotation angle within the plane spanned by the hidden state and the update. This view places Euclidean residual connections and GeoNorm in one framework. Instantiating it with the metric projection retraction and the Cayley retraction yields Proj-SpheretNorm and Cay-SpheretNorm, which are exactly norm-preserving yet require only algebraic operations. Both prove to be members of a one-parameter family of angular retractions, $p$-SpheretNorm, whose rotation angle saturates rather than growing without bound. The two methods above are recovered exactly at $p = 1$ and $p = 2$, while the identity map and GeoNorm arise only as limits at either end. On nanoGPT, all three methods outperform existing lightweight deep connection schemes, and the best validation loss is attained at finite $p$, indicating that the exponential map is not the preferred retraction for spherical residual streams but merely one end of a spectrum.

</details>


### [126] [Learning Molecular Representations from Cellular Phenotypes with Structure Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02688)
*Xuan Lin,Jingyu Sheng,Tengfei Ma,Li Sun,Dapeng Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhenMol is a structure-preserving multimodal learning framework that integrates cellular phenotype information with molecular representations while maintaining chemical structural organization, outperforming existing methods on molecular property prediction, retrieval, and clinical outcome prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal representation learning methods for phenotypic drug discovery often optimize cross-modal alignment without considering intrinsic chemical space organization, leading to distorted molecular representations and loss of structural information that is crucial for understanding molecular properties and relationships.

Method: PhenMol uses a disentanglement approach that separates molecular and cellular representations into shared and private components. It employs a dedicated molecular branch to preserve chemical structures while enabling phenotype-guided alignment. This structure-preserving design maintains molecular neighborhood organization while integrating cellular phenotype information.

Result: Experiments on ~30,400 molecule-cell morphology pairs showed PhenMol improves molecular property prediction across 270 bioactivity tasks, enhances molecule-phenotype retrieval, and improves clinical trial outcome prediction. Structural analysis using ECFP4 fingerprints demonstrated that PhenMol better preserves molecular neighborhoods and reduces embedding distortion compared to existing multimodal alignment methods.

Conclusion: PhenMol highlights the importance of structure-aware constraints in multimodal molecular representation learning and provides an effective approach for integrating cellular phenotypes with chemical knowledge, advancing phenotypic drug discovery by maintaining crucial structural information while leveraging phenotype data.

Abstract: Phenotypic drug discovery enables the discovery of functional relationships between molecular structures and cellular responses. However, existing multimodal representation learning methods often optimize cross-modal alignment without considering the intrinsic organization of chemical space, resulting in distorted molecular representations and loss of structural information. We propose \textbf{PhenMol}, a structure-preserving framework for phenotype-aware molecular representation learning. PhenMol disentangles molecular and cellular representations into shared and private components, enabling phenotype-guided alignment while preserving chemical structures through a dedicated molecular branch. This design integrates cellular phenotype information without disrupting molecular neighborhood organization. Experiments on approximately $3.04 \times 10^{4}$ molecule--cell morphology pairs demonstrate that PhenMol improves molecular property prediction across 270 bioactivity tasks, molecule--phenotype retrieval, and clinical trial outcome prediction. Moreover, ECFP4-based structural analysis shows that PhenMol better preserves molecular neighborhoods and reduces embedding distortion compared with existing multimodal alignment methods. These results highlight the importance of structure-aware constraints in multimodal molecular representation learning and provide an effective approach for integrating cellular phenotypes with chemical knowledge for drug discovery.

</details>


### [127] [GLOBE: Trajectory-Aligned Gradient Matching with Structured SparseOptimization for Coreset Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02690)
*Hetian Liu,Jin Cui,Mengcheng Shi,Yanbin Hu,Xinyue Long,Boran Zhao,Pengju Pen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GLOBE is a coreset selection method that uses gradient trajectories across multiple training checkpoints with multi-order matching and structured sparsity to achieve better data-efficient learning for on-device training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device training of deep neural networks is constrained by computational and memory costs of large datasets. Existing gradient-based coreset selection methods have limitations: they rely on single-model snapshot gradients, use greedy/pursuit-based selection that can't capture evolving optimization dynamics, and struggle with strongly correlated samples.

Method: GLOBE formulates sample selection as a globally optimized sparse weighting problem using gradient trajectories across multiple training checkpoints. It introduces multi-order matching that aligns first-order mean and projected uncentered second-order moments of gradient trajectories. The method combines Group LASSO, Elastic Net regularization, and nonnegative budget constraints to induce group- and sample-level sparsity while stabilizing correlated trajectory weights. Class-balanced Top-K selection ensures category coverage under limited budgets.

Result: Experiments across six benchmarks and five evaluation architectures show GLOBE consistently outperforms existing coreset selection methods in downstream test accuracy, particularly at low retention ratios (compact subsets).

Conclusion: The combination of dynamic gradient information (via trajectories), multi-order distribution matching, and structured sparsity is effective for data-efficient learning in on-device training scenarios, offering superior performance compared to existing coreset selection approaches.

Abstract: On-device training of deep neural networks is fundamentally constrained by the computational and memory costs of large-scale datasets. Coreset selection offers a practical solution by retaining only a compact subset of real training samples. However, existing gradient-based methods commonly rely on gradients computed at a single model snapshot and employ greedy or pursuit-based selection procedures, limiting their ability to capture evolving optimization dynamics and handle strongly correlated samples. We propose GLOBE (Gradient Local-Balanced Extraction), a trajectory-aligned coreset selection framework that formulates sample selection as a globally optimized sparse weighting problem. GLOBE represents each sample by a gradient trajectory constructed across multiple training checkpoints, thereby capturing its influence throughout different stages of optimization. To preserve the training behavior of the full dataset, we introduce a multi-order matching objective that jointly aligns the first-order mean and projected uncentered second-order moments of gradient trajectories. GLOBE further combines Group LASSO, Elastic Net regularization, and nonnegative budget constraints to induce group- and sample-level sparsity while stabilizing the weights of correlated trajectories. Finally, class-balanced Top-K selection maintains adequate category coverage under limited sampling budgets. Experiments across six benchmarks and five evaluation architectures demonstrate that GLOBE consistently outperforms existing coreset selection methods in downstream test accuracy, particularly at low retention ratios. These results highlight the effectiveness of combining dynamic gradient information, multi-order distribution matching, and structured sparsity for data-efficient learning.

</details>


### [128] [Output-Aware Rotation for INT2 KV-Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02691)
*Vincent-Daniel Yun,Woosang Lim,Minsoo Cheong,Sunwoo Lee,Murali Annavaram,Sai Praneeth Karimireddy,Sungjoo Yoo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OptR is an output-aware rotation method for ultra-low-bit INT2 quantization of KV cache that minimizes post-attention-output error through per-head orthogonal corrections and attention-equivalent key reparameterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The KV cache has become a major memory and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context LLM inference, making ultra-low-bit quantization crucial. Existing INT2 methods optimize cache statistics or proxy errors before complete attention readout, but the model is ultimately affected by errors propagated through attention and output projection W_O.

Method: OptR decomposes post-W_O attention-output error into key- and value-induced terms, learns per-head orthogonal corrections through full INT2 quantization and attention path, and applies attention-equivalent key reparameterization to reduce channel-wise offsets without changing softmax distribution.

Result: Across three models and five reasoning and coding benchmarks, OptR consistently improves both QuaRot and OSCAR baselines, strengthens long-context retrieval performance, while preserving the paged KV-cache format with negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: OptR provides an effective output-aware approach to KV cache quantization that better aligns with the actual error propagation path in attention mechanisms, achieving superior performance in long-context scenarios while maintaining practical deployment considerations.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache has become a major memory and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context large language model inference, making ultra-low-bit quantization increasingly important. However, existing rotation-based INT2 methods optimize cache statistics or proxy errors before the complete attention readout, even though the model is ultimately affected by the error propagated through attention and the output projection $W_O$. To address this mismatch, we propose \textit{OptR}, an output-aware rotation method that minimizes post-$W_O$ attention-output error. OptR decomposes the post-$W_O$ attention-output error into key- and value-induced terms and learns per-head orthogonal corrections through the full INT2 quantization and attention path. OptR further applies an attention-equivalent key reparameterization to reduce large channel-wise offsets without changing the softmax distribution. Across three models and five reasoning and coding benchmarks, OptR consistently improves both QuaRot and OSCAR and strengthens long-context retrieval, while preserving the paged KV-cache format with negligible inference overhead.

</details>


### [129] [PatTree: a novel approach for automated creation of multimodal, graph-based patient representations for medical classification tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02692)
*Julia Gehrmann,Lars Quakulinski,Hamza Naseem,Oya Beyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PatTree is a graph-based patient representation that enables automated structuring of heterogeneous clinical data for AI classification tasks without requiring data standardization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical real-world data is heterogeneous with missing values, multiple time points, diverse modalities, and inconsistent formats, making AI applications challenging. Current data harmonization approaches are resource-intensive and error-prone, limiting scalability of holistic AI decision support.

Method: PatTree: a graph-based, holistic patient representation derived from real-world clinical data through automated structuring of multimodal data. It enables early-stage data integration without pre-standardized inputs by representing heterogeneous data in a unified knowledge graph that preserves semantic relationships across modalities.

Result: Using ADNI-1 cohort (n=763), classification on PatTree achieved state-of-the-art performance: 98.5% balanced accuracy and 0.987 F1 score in three-class classification distinguishing Alzheimer's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and cognitively normal individuals on held-out test set.

Conclusion: Assumption-free, automated structuring of multimodal medical data can serve as a scalable foundation for clinical AI pipelines, bypassing tedious data preparation and standardization while achieving high classification performance.

Abstract: Access to holistic, multimodal data improves the performance of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in medical classification tasks compared to utilizing single modalities or data sources. However, the inherent heterogeneity and complexity of clinical real-world data pose significant challenges to structured data analysis and AI application. This heterogeneity includes missing values, multiple time points, diverse modalities, and inconsistent formats and semantics. Data harmonization prior to data integration tackles this challenge but remains resource-intensive and error-prone, limiting the scalability and reproducibility of holistic, AI-driven decision support on clinical real-world data. We therefore propose PatTree, a graph-based, holistic representation of patients that can be derived from real-world clinical data through the automated structuring of multimodal clinical data. PatTree enables early-stage data integration without relying on pre-standardized inputs. While representing heterogeneous clinical data within a unified knowledge graph, PatTree preserves the semantic relationships between data elements across modalities and data sources, facilitating interoperability and machine-interpretable data access. Using a subset of the ADNI-1 cohort (n = 763), we demonstrate that classification of patients is directly feasible on PatTree reaching state-of-the-art classification performance. In the three-class classification task distinguishing Alzheimer's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and cognitively normal individuals, we achieve a balanced accuracy of 98.5% and an F$_1$ score of 0.987 on the held-out test set. Our results show that assumption-free, automated structuring of multimodal medical data can serve as a scalable foundation for clinical AI pipelines bypassing tedious data preparation and standardization.

</details>


### [130] [Measuring Explainer Stability via Attribution Separability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02697)
*Eddie Conti,Álvaro Parafita,Axel Brando*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A distribution-based framework to evaluate the stability of attribution methods, enabling assessment of feature ranking reliability and comparison of attribution method robustness across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most attribution methods produce variable attribution scores due to stochastic components, creating uncertainty about the reliability of feature importance rankings.

Method: Proposed a distribution-based framework that captures attribution score stability, allowing assessment of separability in ranked attribution vectors and identification of the largest reliable ranking index.

Result: The framework enables evaluation of explainer stability, provides a complementary criterion for assessing attribution method stability, and allows comparison of AMs based on ranking robustness across datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed approach offers a systematic way to assess the stability of attribution methods, addressing their stochastic nature and providing valuable insights into feature ranking reliability.

Abstract: Attribution methods (AMs) assign an importance score to each feature and are widely adopted to explain black-box models. However, most methods can produce variable attribution scores due to stochastic components in their definition. In this paper, we propose a distribution-based framework to capture the stability of attribution scores. In particular, our approach allows to understand the degree of separability in the ranked attribution vector and obtain the largest index for which a feature ranking remains reliable. We further extend this framework to compare AMs based on the robustness of their rankings across a dataset. Through experiments, we demonstrate how to apply our method to evaluate explainer stability. Overall, our approach provides a complementary criterion for evaluating the stability of AMs.

</details>


### [131] [NANQ: Noise-Floor-Aware Mixed-Precision Non-Uniform Quantization for Analog Compute-in-Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02700)
*Yizhe Chen,Wenshuai Yao,Saiya Wang,Yuannuo Feng,Wenbo Qi,Kechao Tang,Ngai Wong,Wenyong Zhou,Wang Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NANQ is a noise-aware mixed-precision non-uniform quantization framework for analog CIM that accounts for hardware noise profiles, improving accuracy over existing methods that ignore noise floors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CIM-oriented quantization methods primarily minimize ideal quantization error while ignoring hardware noise floors (device variation and read noise), leading to inefficient precision allocation and degraded performance for low-bit quantized models on analog compute-in-memory systems.

Method: Proposes NANQ framework that: 1) models magnitude-dependent weight noise from measured eFlash CIM array responses, 2) converts noise profile into adaptive quantization density (finer resolution in low-noise regions, avoiding ineffective precision in noise-dominated regions), 3) assigns layer-wise bit-widths by identifying each layer's precision saturation point under hardware noise using a unified threshold.

Result: On-chip experiments on eFlash CIM SoC show: 8.05 percentage point accuracy improvement for vision models and 54.7% PPL reduction for language models under 2-bit weight-magnitude quantization over PowerQuant baseline. Mixed-precision NANQ achieves most gains with only 3.2-3.8 equivalent bits.

Conclusion: NANQ effectively addresses hardware noise in analog CIM systems through noise-aware quantization, demonstrating significant improvements in accuracy while maintaining low-bit operations, making it a practical solution for energy-efficient neural network inference on analog CIM hardware.

Abstract: Analog compute-in-memory (CIM) enables energy-efficient neural network inference, but device variation and read noise can severely degrade low-bit quantized models. Existing CIM-oriented quantization methods mainly minimize ideal quantization error, ignoring the hardware noise floor and thus causing inefficient precision allocation. We propose NANQ, a noise-aware mixed-precision non-uniform quantization framework for analog CIM. NANQ models magnitude-dependent weight noise from measured responses of an eFlash CIM array and converts the noise profile into an adaptive quantization density, assigning finer resolution to low-noise regions while avoiding ineffective precision in noise-dominated regions. It further assigns layer-wise bit-widths by identifying each layer's precision saturation point under hardware noise using a unified threshold. On-chip experiments on an eFlash CIM SoC show that, under 2-bit weight-magnitude quantization, NANQ improves vision-model accuracy by 8.05 percentage points and reduces language-model PPL by 54.7% on average over PowerQuant. Mixed-precision NANQ captures most of the gains obtainable from additional quantization resources with only 3.2-3.8 equivalent bits.

</details>


### [132] [Can Training Logs Make Model Comparisons More Precise?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02705)
*Wei-Jung Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training logs from model runs can improve precision when comparing performance differences between models, but only with careful covariate selection to avoid introducing noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When comparing stochastically trained models, we need to estimate performance differences and their uncertainty from repeated runs. The paper explores whether training logs from these runs can make such comparisons more precise.

Method: Uses arm-specific covariate adjustment: each model is adjusted only with statistics from its own runs, while the raw mean difference remains the reported effect. Conducted a vision study spanning three architectures and three datasets, using simple adjustments based on early training logs.

Result: Simple adjustments based on early training logs often reduce uncertainty in model comparisons. However, broad searches through training logs for the most correlated statistic often add more noise than they remove, even when useful statistics exist in hindsight.

Conclusion: Training logs are useful for more precise model comparisons, but only when adjustment avoids large selection noise from covariate selection.

Abstract: Comparing stochastically trained models requires estimating both a performance difference and its uncertainty from repeated runs. We study whether training logs from those same runs can make such comparisons more precise. Because training-log covariates are produced during training rather than measured before it, we use arm-specific covariate adjustment: each model is adjusted only with statistics from its own runs, and the raw mean difference remains the reported effect. In a vision study spanning three architectures and three datasets, simple adjustments based on early training logs often reduce uncertainty in model comparisons. The main limitation is covariate selection. Broadly searching the log pool for the most correlated statistic often adds more noise than it removes, even when useful statistics exist in hindsight. Training logs therefore appear useful for more precise model comparisons, but only when the adjustment avoids large selection noise.

</details>


### [133] [Designing a Good Virtual Node: Addressable and Cardinality-Preserving Global Memory for Message Passing Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02709)
*Félix Marcoccia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an addressable and cardinality-preserving virtual memory system for message-passing neural networks that improves global communication without using self-attention, maintaining multiplicity information through novel cross-attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard virtual node approaches in message-passing neural networks compress the entire graph into a single homogeneous state that's broadcast identically to all nodes, creating a finite-capacity bottleneck. The authors want to improve global communication without resorting to self-attention mechanisms.

Method: Proposes two key innovations: 1) Factorizing global memory into independently writable and readable states using addressable cross-attention slots, and 2) Adding private key/value anchors to each slot query to preserve multiplicity information (preventing softmax attention's invariance to uniform replication). The approach achieves O(nMd) arithmetic cost and can implement 1-WL refinement on bounded color domains.

Result: The method is validated through experiments on multiplicity-aware Two-Radius tasks, motif counting, and constrained link-set prediction. The addressable and cardinality-preserving virtual memory successfully preserves multiplicity information while maintaining computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed virtual memory system effectively addresses the capacity bottleneck in traditional virtual node approaches by enabling independent memory access while preserving crucial multiplicity information, offering a viable alternative to self-attention for global graph communication in neural networks.

Abstract: Virtual nodes give message-passing neural networks a simple global communication route, but the standard node--VN--node pipeline compresses the graph into one homogeneous state and broadcasts it identically to every node. Building on the Two-Radius analysis of Mishayev et al., we ask how auxiliary virtual memory can relieve this finite-capacity bottleneck without self-attention. We identify two requirements. First, the global memory should be factorized into independently writable and readable states: this can be achieved using addressable cross-attention slots. Second, addressability alone does not preserve multiplicity, because softmax attention is invariant to uniform replication. Inserting each slot query as a private key/value anchor recovers the discarded normalization mass and yields, on bounded color domains, an injective multiset representation able to implement a 1-WL refinement. Experiments on multiplicity-aware Two-Radius, motif counting, and constrained link-set prediction support this addressable and cardinality-preserving virtual memory at (O(nMd)) arithmetic cost.

</details>


### [134] [Neural Networks with Local Converging Inputs for Efficient Options Pricing Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02778)
*Harris Cobb,Wenbo Hao,Yingjie Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NNLCI uses neural networks with minimal high-fidelity training data to locally correct numerical solutions for multi-asset option pricing, achieving 4-12x RMSE reduction on test sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to improve efficiency of existing numerical methods for pricing multi-asset options in real-time trading and risk management, especially for high-dimensional problems with high computational requirements.

Method: Neural Networks with Local Converging Inputs (NNLCI) use a neural network to locally correct solutions from a coarse mesh and a refined mesh, requiring only minimal high-fidelity training data with the most concise input format.

Result: NNLCI reduces the root-mean-square error (RMSE) of refined-mesh numerical solutions by approximately 4-12 times on test sets for various options (cash-or-nothing options in 1D, 2D, 3D, and single-asset barrier options under Heston model), even with training on only small parameter subsets.

Conclusion: NNLCI significantly reduces computational requirements for high-dimensional option pricing problems, offering low training costs and strong generalization ability, making it suitable for real-time trading and risk management applications.

Abstract: We present a novel application of Neural Networks with Local Converging Inputs (NNLCI) to improve the efficiency of existing numerical methods for pricing multi-asset options. The most concise input format for NNLCI has been introduced, offering substantial convenience and efficiency. NNLCI uses a neural network to locally correct solutions from a coarse mesh and a refined mesh (relative to the coarse one), requiring only a minimal amount of high-fidelity training data. We demonstrate this approach on cash-or-nothing options under the Black-Scholes equation in one, two, and three spatial dimensions, and on single-asset down-and-out barrier call options under the Heston stochastic-volatility model (whose pricing PDE is two-dimensional in the spot price $S$ and the instantaneous variance $v$). In each case, NNLCI reduces the root-mean-square error (RMSE) of the refined-mesh numerical solution by a factor of approximately 4-12 on test sets, even when the neural network is trained on only a small subset of parameter combinations. These results demonstrate that NNLCI significantly reduces computational requirements for high-dimensional problems in real-time options trading and risk management, offering low training costs and strong generalization ability.

</details>


### [135] [Evaluation Blindness: How Silent Measurement Failures Corrupt AI Systems from Training to Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02786)
*Priyanka Bajaj*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces "evaluation blindness" - when AI systems fail silently because measurement functions produce healthy-looking readings while the system is actually failing, with no signals indicating the problem. The concept applies to both training and deployment stages, with real-world case studies showing 53% of verifiable failures are silent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems can fail silently, with failures propagating through training loops, evaluation pipelines, and production monitoring without detection until downstream harm occurs. Current literature treats training-time and deployment-time failures separately, but both suffer from similar measurement blindness problems that prevent detection of system failures.

Method: The paper provides a formal detectability predicate unifying training and deployment stages, conducts four training-time case studies of concrete breakdowns (including a real implementation bug in TRL), and develops a six-class taxonomy of production failures validated against 50 real-world incidents from court documents and regulatory filings. A failure budget framework ties acceptable failure rates to use-case risk class.

Result: The analysis found that 53% of verifiable public failures were silent. The six-class taxonomy includes an Operational category that is 100% silent by structural definition. Real case studies demonstrate concrete breakdowns like corrupted gradients appearing as normal loss decreases. The research reveals that measurement infrastructure is fundamentally flawed for detecting certain classes of AI failures.

Conclusion: Measurement infrastructure is a correctness concern across the full AI lifecycle, not just at evaluation time. The concept of "evaluation blindness" demonstrates that current monitoring approaches are insufficient for detecting silent failures, requiring fundamental rethinking of how AI systems are measured and validated throughout their lifecycle.

Abstract: AI systems can fail silently. The failure propagates through training loops, evaluation pipelines, and production monitoring stacks until downstream harm makes it visible. This paper introduces evaluation blindness: a measurement function M exhibits evaluation blindness with respect to failure class F when it produces readings indistinguishable from a healthy state while the system is actually failing, with no auxiliary signal flagging the gap.
  The problem surfaces at two lifecycle stages the literature has treated separately. At training time, reward models are gamed, importance-sampling corrections are silently miscalculated, and benchmark contamination inflates fine-tuning evaluations, all while loss curves look healthy and gradient updates proceed normally. At deployment time, monitoring fails to catch six classes of production failure, including an Operational category that is 100% silent by structural definition.
  We provide a formal detectability predicate unifying both stages. Four training-time case studies trace concrete breakdowns, including a real implementation bug in TRL PR #6594 where gradients are corrupted as loss decreases normally. A six-class taxonomy validated against 50 real-world incidents from court documents and regulatory filings finds that 53% of verifiable public failures were silent. A failure budget framework ties acceptable failure rates to use-case risk class.
  The implication is direct: measurement infrastructure is a correctness concern across the full AI lifecycle, not just at evaluation time. Data, code, and taxonomy schema are at https://github.com/priyanka25aug/llm-failure-taxonomy.

</details>


### [136] [Topological Simplification in Predictive Coding Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02816)
*Adam Shaw,Jiayu Li,Michael Sperling,Michael Kim,Alvin Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Study uses persistent homology to analyze topological changes in predictive coding networks (PCNs) across layers, finding connections between model size, topological simplification, reconstruction error, and differences from MLPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how topological features of learned representations change across layers in neuro-inspired predictive coding networks, examining compression-reconstruction tradeoffs and comparing to standard MLPs.

Method: Train PCNs on synthetic and MNIST datasets, apply quantitative layer-wise persistent homology analysis to measure topological feature changes, correlate topological simplification with model size and reconstruction error, compare with matched MLPs via seed-level bootstrap.

Result: Smaller PCNs collapse connected components earlier than larger models (Spearman ρ ∈ [0.72, 0.79]), strong negative correlation (ρ = -0.58) between simplification depth and reconstruction error, PCNs consistently simplify 3.6 layers later than MLPs on average.

Conclusion: Persistent homology provides useful quantitative lens on compression-reconstruction tradeoff in PCNs; both model capacity and bidirectional dynamics shape when this tradeoff resolves across layers.

Abstract: We study the topology of learned representations in predictive coding networks (PCNs), a neuro-inspired bidirectional architecture, using a quantitative layer-wise persistent homology analysis. We train well-performing PCNs on a synthetic classification dataset ($\geq 99.9\%$ test accuracy) and on MNIST ($\geq 95\%$ test accuracy), and measure how topological features change across layers for different architectures and activation functions. We find that smaller PCNs collapse connected components across layers earlier than larger models (Spearman $\unicode{x1D70C} \in [0.72, 0.79]$ across activations), with model size measured as the sum of hidden-layer widths. We also observe a strong negative correlation ($\unicode{x1D70C} = -0.58$) between the depth at which simplification occurs and reconstruction error; i.e., architectures that simplify later reconstruct better. Finally, a seed-level bootstrap comparison across architectures and activations shows that PCNs consistently collapse connected components later than matched MLPs, with an average difference of $3.6$ layers. These results suggest that persistent homology offers a useful quantitative lens on the compression--reconstruction tradeoff in PCNs, and that both model capacity and the recurrent, bidirectional dynamics of predictive coding inference shape when this tradeoff is resolved across layers.

</details>


### [137] [Wiring Beats Blending: What Transfers Between Transformer Sizes -- and What Doesn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02829)
*Ravi Satya Durga Prasad Yenugula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large pretrained models can be converted to smaller versions via optimal initialization (compensation + rescaling) rather than direct parameter copying, enabling faster training to given quality levels than from-scratch training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practice trains every model size from scratch, but this is inefficient. The paper investigates whether pretrained large models can be converted into smaller siblings instead, saving compute and time.

Method: Characterizes Pythia model family conversion (1.4B->410M) through representation/parameter alignment analysis. Identifies that dense weight projection fails due to architectural structure breakage. Proposes two independent levers: least-squares compensation (for optimal zero-shot function) and variance-preserving rescale (for training dynamics). Tests in matched-budget continued pre-training experiments.

Result: Conversion value lives in initialization, not parameter transfer. At low budgets (30M tokens), compensation beats strongest subcloning variants. Both compensation and from-scratch initialization beat direct from-scratch training (by up to 18x at low budget). Method has boundaries - stacking both levers over-corrects at ~5x scale differences due to ill-conditioning.

Conclusion: Pretrained large models can be effectively converted to smaller siblings through optimal initialization strategies (compensation + rescaling), enabling token-efficient training that reaches target quality faster than from-scratch training, with dimension-aware regularization needed for large scale differences.

Abstract: Model families train every size from scratch. Can a pretrained large model be converted into a smaller sibling? We characterize the 1.4B->410M conversion in the Pythia family end-to-end: (i) representations align strongly across sizes (ridge R^2=0.84) while parameters align weakly; (ii) dense weight projection is functionally destructive -- provably not an assembly artifact -- because basis mixing breaks rotary, per-head, GELU, and LayerNorm structure; (iii) after the best-fit linear operator, weight residuals are statistically indistinguishable from noise under shuffle controls; (iv) conversion value therefore lives in initialization. In matched-budget continued pre-training we decompose conversion into two independent levers -- least-squares compensation (function: best zero-shot) and variance-preserving rescale (dynamics: best endpoints). Compensation is a token-efficient, low-budget win rather than a universal one: at 30M tokens it beats the strongest subcloning variant on both a width-reduced pair (84.0 +/- 1.8 vs. 89.7 +/- 3.7, 3/3 seeds) and a held-out depth-reduced pair (109.3 vs. 117.9, 3/3 seeds), reaching a given quality with fewer tokens; at a 33x larger budget the two converge to parity (40.0 vs. 40.0), both far ahead of from-scratch, which transfer initialization always beats -- by up to 18x at low budget, the margin narrowing at convergence and at the largest scale. We further map the method's boundary: at ~5x the donor scale (6.9B->1.4B) stacking both levers over-corrects, which we trace to ill-conditioning of the compensation solve at large width, pointing to dimension-aware regularization as the fix. Code, checkpoints, and the frozen evaluation corpus are released.

</details>


### [138] [NOMADD: Numerical Optimization of Models Adapting to Data Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02845)
*Swapn Shah,Keith Burghardt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-hoc algorithm that reduces concept drift for tabular models by extrapolating parameter evolution from historical training periods, achieving competitive performance to state-of-the-art with minimal training time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data drift and concept drift degrade tabular model performance over time, but real-time mitigation is challenging due to lack of immediate labeled data or impractical re-training. Existing drift reduction tools are typically bespoke to neural networks and require model retraining adaptations.

Method: Fits base model separately on each labeled training period, measures how parameters evolve against a single anchor model pooled over all periods, compresses those changes with low-rank factorization, and extrapolates each latent factor forward with damped, regularized forecasting.

Result: On the 18-dataset Drift-Resilient TabPFN benchmark, extrapolation improves every base family it's applied to, achieving performance competitive with state-of-the-art Drift-Resilient TabPFN with just seconds of training. The method works with various models from trees to neural networks to tabular foundation models.

Conclusion: This post-hoc drift reduction tool offers a practical alternative to computationally expensive methods, being model-agnostic, fast to train, and competitive with state-of-the-art approaches. The paper also explores extending this methodology to other data modalities.

Abstract: Tabular model performance degrades when feature distributions change over time or the relationship between features and outcome variables change over time, known as data drift and concept drift, respectively. These issues are challenging to mitigate in real time because labeled data may not be immediately available, or re-training a model could be impractical. While tools exist to reduce drift, they are typically bespoke to neural network architectures and adapt how models are trained. In this paper, we offer an alternative post-hoc method to reduce concept drift, which is applicable to a variety of models, from trees to neural networks to tabular foundation models. This new tool is especially useful when constraints, such as high model accuracy, bounded inference time, or model size requires users to choose between different models for their specific use-cases. Our algorithm fits the base model separately on each labeled training period, measures how its parameters evolve against a single anchor model pooled over all of those periods, compresses those changes with a low-rank factorization, and extrapolates each latent factor forward with a damped, regularized forecast. On the 18-dataset Drift-Resilient TabPFN benchmark, evaluated under that benchmark's own protocol and metric, the extrapolation improves every base family it is applied to, and achieves performance competitive with the state-of-the-art Drift-Resilient TabPFN with seconds of training. In contrast, Drift-Resilient TabPFN requires pre-training on millions of synthetic datasets over approximately 1,300 GPU-hours, and is orders of magnitude slower in inference (depending on the model). In the discussion, we explore the promise and challenges of extending this tool to other modalities.

</details>


### [139] [Adaptive Sampling for Automated Post-Disaster Rapid Damage Assessment via Level-Set Cost-Aware Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02868)
*Boyang Xu,Mostafa Reisi Gahrooei,Mohammad Ilbeigi,Hao Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cost-aware Bayesian optimization framework with level-set estimation for autonomous UAVs to efficiently assess post-disaster damage by dynamically guiding data collection to most informative regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional post-disaster damage assessment methods are static, labor-intensive, expensive, and struggle to adapt to dynamic conditions, creating a need for rapid, reliable, and cost-effective assessment for emergency response.

Method: Combines cost-aware Bayesian optimization with level-set estimation to continuously guide autonomous data collectors (like UAVs) toward most informative regions, dynamically updating damage estimates across geographic zones while minimizing operational costs.

Result: Validated with controlled synthetic toy study showing agent's ability to efficiently trace damage boundaries, recover underlying damage map, and rapidly reduce predictive uncertainty. Further evaluation using high-fidelity R2D disaster data shows algorithm provides accurate, timely damage estimates for emergency response.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables cost-effective, adaptive post-disaster damage assessment by systematically reducing uncertainty while minimizing operational costs, supporting faster and more informative emergency response.

Abstract: Natural disasters frequently inflict severe damage to the built environment, which demands a rapid, reliable, and cost-effective damage assessment for emergency response. However, traditional methods for post-disaster damage assessment often rely on static, labor-intensive data collection strategies that can be prohibitively expensive and struggle to adapt to dynamic post-disaster conditions. In this study, we propose a cost-aware Bayesian optimization framework combined with level-set estimation that continuously guides autonomous data collectors, e.g., an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV), toward the most informative regions. By dynamically updating damage estimates across different geographic zones, our approach systematically reduces uncertainty while minimizing operational costs. The proposed framework is first validated using a controlled synthetic toy study, demonstrating the agent's ability to efficiently trace damage boundaries, recover the underlying damage map, and rapidly reduce predictive uncertainty. Furthermore, the approach is evaluated using high-fidelity disaster data generated by the Regional Resilience Determination (R2D) software. The results of the algorithm provide accurate and timely damage estimates that support informative and fast emergency response.

</details>


### [140] [Contrast-invariant deep ptychography neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02869)
*Albert Vong,Steven Henke,Oliver Hoidn,Hanna Ruth,Junjing Deng,Apurva Mehta,David Shapiro,Alexander Hexemer,Nicholas Schwarz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Ptychography neural networks improve scaling generalization using factorization to separate object texture from measurement scaling, enabling consistent reconstructions across varying illumination conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ptychography neural networks suffer from scaling inconsistencies when generalizing out of distribution, limiting their real-world viability. The scaling mismatch between training and testing conditions reduces practical applicability.

Method: 1) Factorization strategy that decouples learned object texture from measurement scaling, enabling a single network to produce measurement-consistent reconstructions. 2) Predicting objects in real and imaginary units instead of amplitude/phase representation. 3) Synthetic object sampling strategy minimizing phase distribution mismatch between synthetic training data and experimental targets.

Result: Up to 5x reduction in Fourier error over previous PtychoPINN-torch baseline across 5 experimental datasets spanning multiple beamlines and facilities.

Conclusion: The proposed factorization approach and improved training strategies significantly enhance ptychography neural network generalization, making them more viable for real-world applications by addressing scaling inconsistencies across varying experimental conditions.

Abstract: Ptychography neural networks suffer from scaling inconsistencies when generalizing out of distribution, limiting their real world viability. We address this scaling mismatch using a factorization strategy which decouples the learned object texture from measurement scaling, enabling a single trained network to produce measurement-consistent reconstructions across varying illumination conditions. This requires predicting the learned object in real and imaginary units instead of the canonical amplitude and phase representation. We additionally introduce a synthetic object sampling strategy that minimizes phase distribution mismatch between synthetic training data and experimental targets. These improvements yield up to a 5x reduction in Fourier error over the previous PtychoPINN-torch baseline across 5 experimental datasets spanning multiple beamlines and facilities.

</details>


### [141] [Maglev: Sliding Recurrent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02870)
*Bo Liu,Qiang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Coupled Recurrent Transformer (CRT) uses two models - a prefiller with full attention and a decoder with sliding-window attention - trained with memory consistency loss to achieve better performance while maintaining parallel training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to address limitations of existing attention mechanisms in Transformers. Sliding-window attention has length constraints and may lose global context, while full attention is computationally expensive. The authors want to develop an architecture that maintains parallelizability during training while improving over sliding-window baselines.

Method: CRT consists of two coupled models: 1) Prefiller (Q) uses full attention (or interleaved full/sliding-window) to produce memory targets, 2) Decoder (P) uses only sliding-window attention with recurrent K/V injection to produce decoder memories. They're trained with a memory consistency loss that aligns the memories from both models, enabling inference to use just the efficient decoder (P) while benefiting from the prefiller's global context.

Result: CRT improves validation loss and downstream pretraining benchmarks over both sliding-window and latent recurrent transformer baselines. Parameter sharing between P and Q reduces memory usage while preserving most performance gains.

Conclusion: CRT provides an effective recurrent Transformer architecture that generalizes sliding-window attention while remaining parallelizable, achieving better performance than existing approaches through coupled model training with memory consistency objectives.

Abstract: We introduce \ours{}, a recurrent Transformer architecture with fixed-size memory that generalizes sliding-window attention while remaining parallelizable during training. \ours{} consists of two coupled models: a prefiller $Q$, which leverages full attention\footnote{In practice, we use interleaved full and sliding-window attention for $Q$, as this yields stronger performance. The essential requirement is that $Q$ be more expressive than $P$, with access to the full history.} to produce memory targets $m'_t$, and a decoder $P$, which uses only sliding-window attention and recurrent K/V injection to produce decoder memories $m_t$ for next-token prediction. We train \ours{} with a memory consistency loss that aligns $m_t$ with $m'_t$, allowing inference to use $P$ alone. Empirically, \ours{} improves validation loss and downstream pretraining benchmarks over sliding-window and latent recurrent transformer baselines. Moreover, sharing parameters between $P$ and $Q$ reduces parameter memory while preserving most of the gains.

</details>


### [142] [GoT-CD: Graph-of-Thoughts Causal Discovery and the Fragility of Post-hoc Path-Specific Fairness Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02877)
*Nitish Nagesh,Elahe Khatibi,Thomas Dean Hughes,Mahdi Bagheri,Pratik Gajane,Amir M. Rahmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GoT-CD is a causal discovery method using Graph-of-Thoughts reasoning with complete edge set units that achieves competitive structural accuracy, but structural fidelity alone doesn't guarantee faithful fairness audits—path-specific effects can persist even when discovery misses key pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal discovery is used in clinical settings for mechanism reasoning and fairness audits, but current methods evaluate on aggregate structural metrics that weight all edges equally, ignoring whether specific pathways needed for fairness audits survive discovery or how audits behave when pathways are missing.

Method: GoT-CD uses Graph-of-Thoughts reasoning where the reasoning unit is a complete candidate edge set: multiple graphs are generated in parallel, scored by a deterministic validity function, and merged under a hard union constraint forbidding invented edges, with greedy projection enforcing DAG structure before commitment.

Result: GoT-CD returns valid DAGs on all five reported benchmarks, achieves best DAG-valid F1 score among LLM methods on Asia, Alzheimer's, and COVID-Respiratory datasets, but shows that 5 of 8 discovered graphs on Alzheimer's benchmark miss unfair paths, reporting null overall effects while mediated effects persist.

Conclusion: Structural fidelity in causal discovery doesn't guarantee faithful fairness audits; path-specific effects can persist even when discovery misses pathways, necessitating downstream path-specific fairness analysis alongside structural discovery.

Abstract: Causal discovery recovers directed structure from observational data and is increasingly used in clinical settings to support mechanism reasoning and fairness audits of predictive models. Path-specific counterfactual fairness asks whether a protected attribute influences an outcome through illegitimate pathways, but these estimands are defined relative to a supplied causal graph and therefore inherit whatever errors the discovery step introduces. Discovery methods are routinely scored on aggregate structural metrics that weight all edges equally, and no established evaluation asks whether the specific pathway an audit depends on survives discovery---or what the audit reports when that pathway is missing. Here we show that full-graph Graph-of-Thoughts reasoning yields acyclic discovered graphs that are structurally competitive with large language model (LLM) baselines, yet that structural fidelity alone does not guarantee fairness-faithful audits. We introduce GoT-CD, in which the reasoning unit is a complete candidate edge set: multiple graphs are generated in parallel, scored by a deterministic validity function, and merged under a hard union constraint that forbids invented edges, with greedy projection enforcing a DAG before commitment. GoT-CD returns a valid DAG on all five reported benchmarks and achieves the best DAG-valid F1 score among LLM methods on Asia, Alzheimer's, and COVID-Respiratory datasets. On an Alzheimer's benchmark with known unfair path, a post-hoc path-specific audit shows that five of eight discovered graphs recover no path from the sensitive attribute to the outcome and therefore report a null overall effect while mediated effects persist, necessitating downstream path-specific fairness analysis along with structural discovery.

</details>


### [143] [Population-Robust Feature Selection via Generalized Welfare Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02887)
*Ruiqi Lyu,Alistair Turcan,Bryan Wilder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PopFS is a feature selection method that learns one shared feature set deployable across multiple populations while allowing each population to train its own model, balancing overall performance with worst-population protection through tunable welfare objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard feature selection methods optimize for one large population, while existing robust approaches learn one shared model for every population. There's a need for methods that can handle heterogeneous populations with a single deployable feature set (like limited questionnaires or sensor sets) while protecting populations that benefit least.

Method: PopFS uses a tunable welfare objective to balance overall predictive benefit against protecting least-benefiting populations. It employs a two-stage approach: 1) multitask sparse learning to reduce candidate feature pool, then 2) direct search over hard feature sets by ranking promising additions/swaps and fully refitting only a shortlist.

Result: Across eight population splits from six prediction tasks (five tabular/public-health datasets), PopFS consistently achieves strong average and worst-population performance while scaling to thousands of features. A COVID-19 nowcasting study across 43 states showed that adjusting welfare objectives improved least-served states with little impact on average performance.

Conclusion: PopFS provides an effective, scalable method for robust feature selection across heterogeneous populations, enabling practitioners to trade off average performance against fairness concerns while maintaining interpretability and practical deployability.

Abstract: Choosing which features to collect is a deployment decision: the same limited questionnaire, test panel, or sensor set may need to serve several heterogeneous populations. Standard feature-selection methods typically optimize for one large population, while existing robust approaches tend to learn one shared model for every population. We introduce PopFS, a method for learning one shared, deployable feature set that is robust to population differences while letting each pop- ulation train its own model. PopFS uses a tunable welfare objective that lets practitioners balance overall predictive ben- efit against stronger protection of the populations that benefit least. To make this objective practical at scale, PopFS first uses multitask sparse learning to reduce the candidate pool, then searches directly over hard feature sets by ranking promising additions and swaps and fully refitting only a shortlist. Across eight population splits from six prediction tasks drawn from five tabular and public-health datasets, PopFS consistently achieves strong average and worst-population performance while scaling to thousands of candidate features. A 43-state COVID-19 nowcasting study further shows that changing the welfare objective can improve the least-served states with lit- tle change in average performance and yields an interpretable change in the selected symptom signals. Our code is available at https://github.com/Rachel-Lyu/PopFS.

</details>


### [144] [Robust Counterfactual Policy Optimisation via Nondeterministic Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02893)
*Jessica Lally,Milad Kazemi,Nicola Paoletti,David Watson,Sander Beckers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework for optimizing counterfactual policies in stochastic MDPs by properly separating latent confounding from irreducible stochasticity, validated on a sepsis treatment simulator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing counterfactual inference approaches for sequential decision-making assume deterministic causal models, but Markov Decision Processes are inherently stochastic. There's a need to address this mismatch and properly handle both latent confounding and irreducible stochasticity in counterfactual policy optimization.

Method: The authors formalize counterfactual policy optimization under probabilistic nondeterministic causal models that separate latent confounding from irreducible stochasticity. They propose a practical optimization problem for identifying robust counterfactual policies using a sensitivity analysis framework.

Result: The approach is validated on a sepsis treatment simulator where diabetes status acts as a hidden global confounder, demonstrating the framework's effectiveness in handling both stochastic environments and latent confounding variables.

Conclusion: The paper presents a novel approach to counterfactual policy optimization that properly accounts for both stochasticity in MDPs and latent confounding, offering a practical framework with applications to real-world problems like medical treatment optimization where hidden confounders exist.

Abstract: Counterfactual inference approaches for sequential decision-making typically assume deterministic causal models, where all randomness stems from latent variables. However, Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) are inherently stochastic. We address this by formalising counterfactual policy optimisation under probabilistic nondeterministic causal models, which properly separates latent confounding from irreducible stochasticity, and here propose a first practical optimisation problem for identifying robust counterfactual policies under a sensitivity analysis framework. We validate our approach on a sepsis treatment simulator, where diabetes status acts as a hidden global confounder.

</details>


### [145] [AnchorKV: Anchor-Residual KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02901)
*Malik Khalaf,Yara Shamshoum,Nitzan Hodos,Yuval Sieradzki,Assaf Schuster*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AnchorKV compresses LLM KV cache 20x without token eviction by using anchors for representation and selective refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV cache is memory bottleneck in long-context LLM inference; existing methods have tradeoffs: eviction degrades performance when discarded tokens become important, while quantization offers limited compression.

Method: AnchorKV uses a small set of anchors stored exactly, represents other tokens through their most similar anchor, and selectively refines those approximations that most affect model output.

Result: Achieves 20x compression while preserving accuracy across models and datasets, retaining 99% of full-cache score at 70B scale.

Conclusion: AnchorKV enables efficient long-context LLM inference by compressing KV cache significantly without token discard, maintaining performance at a fraction of the cost.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache is the primary memory bottleneck in long-context LLM inference. Existing approaches attack it from opposite ends: eviction methods permanently discard tokens, degrading performance whenever a discarded token later proves essential, while quantization methods retain all tokens at low precision but offer limited compression. We propose AnchorKV, a compression scheme that shrinks the cache by $20\times$ without discarding a single token. AnchorKV represents the cache using a small set of anchors stored exactly, expresses every other token through its most similar anchor, and refines only those whose approximation most affects the model's output. AnchorKV consistently preserves accuracy across models and datasets, retaining 99% of the full-cache score at the 70B scale, while keeping the entire context at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [146] [Bayesian Data Reweighting Improves Multimodal Retrieval for Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02907)
*Jingchen Sun,Shaobo Han,Ruiyi Zhang,Naresh Kumar Devulapally,Ming Liu,Yitao Long,Vishnu Suresh Lokhande,Changyou Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Bayesian Data Reweighting, a method to improve multimodal retrievers by adaptively downweighting likely false negatives during contrastive training, leading to better performance on knowledge-based VQA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive training methods for multimodal retrievers treat all unmatched query-document pairs as equally informative negatives, which is problematic because many unmatched documents may still be semantically relevant or partially useful (false negatives).

Method: Bayesian Data Reweighting - a probabilistic framework that models query-document importance as latent variables and adaptively infers posterior weights to downweight likely false negatives. Uses closed-form posterior updates under conjugate priors and stochastic EM optimization.

Result: The method consistently improves retrieval accuracy across three different retrievers and seven knowledge-based visual question answering (VQA) benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed Bayesian Data Reweighting approach effectively addresses the false negative problem in multimodal retriever training, leading to more accurate retrieval performance for knowledge-based VQA tasks by adaptively weighting training examples based on their likelihood of being true negatives.

Abstract: Multimodal retrievers are essential for knowledge-based visual question answering, where they retrieve external evidence for image-question pairs. However, existing contrastive training methods typically treat all unmatched query-document pairs as equally informative negatives, which is problematic because many unmatched documents may still be semantically relevant or partially useful. We propose Bayesian Data Reweighting, a probabilistic framework that models query-document importance as latent variables and adaptively infers posterior weights to downweight likely false negatives. With closed-form posterior updates under conjugate priors and stochastic EM optimization, our method consistently improves retrieval accuracy across three retrievers and seven knowledge-based VQA benchmarks.

</details>


### [147] [Forecasting Revenue with its Customer-Base Drivers: When and Why Coordination Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02911)
*Kyeongbin Kim,Daniel McCarthy,Dokyun Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CBMT (Customer-Based Multi-task Transformer) jointly forecasts customer-base primitives to improve revenue forecasting accuracy compared to existing benchmarks and direct revenue forecasting approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aggregate revenue forecasts lack decomposition into acquisition, repeat purchasing, and spending per order components, limiting their usefulness for detailed business planning and customer-based valuations.

Method: Developed CBMT using weekly transaction panels for 966 companies across 25 industries; learns shared structure, retains separate primitive forecasts, and aligns their combination with downstream revenue.

Result: CBMT reduces mean total-sales error by 30% vs strongest customer-base benchmark, beats direct Transformer forecasting by 2.65% (though not statistically significant), and outperforms single-task forecasts for 74.3% of firms. Performs better for firms with strongly co-moving primitives.

Conclusion: Coordinated customer-base forecasts support revenue planning but warrant greater caution when customer-base dynamics are highly volatile, and calibration-period routing rules don't improve average accuracy over always deploying CBMT.

Abstract: Revenue forecasts guide acquisition budgets, demand planning, and customer-based valuations, yet an aggregate forecast does not show whether change reflects acquisition, repeat purchasing, spending per order, or offsetting movements. Using weekly transaction panels for 966 companies in 25 industries, the authors develop the Customer-Based Multi-task Transformer (CBMT), which learns shared structure, retains separate primitive forecasts, and aligns their combination with downstream revenue. CBMT's mean total-sales error is 30% below the strongest representative established customer-base benchmark. It is also 2.65% below a Transformer that forecasts total sales directly, although the paired difference is not statistically significant (p=.222), and it beats separately estimated single-task forecasts for 74.3% of firms. CBMT's source MAE is lower in 23 of 24 benchmark-by-outcome comparisons, with the remaining difference not statistically distinguishable from zero. Firms whose primitives co-move more strongly are more likely to benefit from joint forecasting; selected-family scenario-3 comparisons are consistent with gains from shared representation and revenue alignment but remain diagnostic rather than causal. Accuracy deteriorates for all models when customer-base dynamics are highly volatile, and CBMT's advantage narrows there. Calibration-period routing rules do not improve average accuracy over always deploying CBMT. The results show how coordinated customer-base forecasts support revenue planning and when they warrant greater caution.

</details>


### [148] [When Should Graph Attention Be Sparse? Learning a Per-Edge Tsallis Index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02938)
*Kleyton da Costa,Bernardo Modenesi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LTGA (Learnable Tsallis Graph Attention) replaces GAT's fixed softmax with a learnable Tsallis entropy-based attention mechanism that can adapt its shape (heavy-tailed to compact-support) to different graph types through a tunable q parameter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard graph attention uses softmax normalization, which assumes maximum entropy under Shannon statistics. However, different graph types (homophilic vs heterophilic) require different attention shapes, and one fixed normalization cannot optimally serve both types of graphs.

Method: LTGA uses Tsallis entropy with a learnable index q that can be learned jointly with model weights. It operates at four granularities (global scalar to per-edge index) and starts from the GAT baseline using bounded reparameterization. The method allows attention to interpolate between heavy-tailed (q<1), softmax (q=1), and compact-support (q>1) distributions.

Result: LTGA-Edge achieves best average rank (2.75) across eight benchmarks, though statistical tests don't show significant superiority (p=0.199). Learning q doesn't beat searching it: validation-tuned grid (61.4%), tuned α-entmax (62.2%), and capacity-matched control (62.0%) vs LTGA-Edge (61.7%). However, learned q enables single-run training instead of grid search and produces interpretable attention: q values prune 42% of attention coefficients to exactly zero, selectively removing wrong edges (restoring them costs 7.1 points vs random pruning costs 13.0 points more).

Conclusion: While LTGA's performance improvement isn't statistically significant compared to parameter search alternatives, it offers practical advantages: eliminates grid search requirements and provides interpretable attention mechanisms that selectively prune irrelevant edges. The method demonstrates that adapting attention shape to graph characteristics is valuable, particularly through the automatic sparsification effect when q deviates from 1.

Abstract: Graph attention normalizes neighborhood scores with softmax, the maximum-entropy choice under Shannon statistics. But homophilic and heterophilic graphs want different attention shapes, and one fixed normalization cannot serve both. We propose \textbf{LTGA} (\textbf{L}earnable \textbf{T}sallis \textbf{G}raph \textbf{A}ttention), a graph attention layer whose Tsallis entropic index $q$ is learned jointly with the weights, interpolating continuously between heavy-tailed ($q\!<\!1$), softmax ($q\!=\!1$) and compact-support ($q\!>\!1$) attention at four granularities from a global scalar to a per-edge index, under a bounded reparameterization that starts every model at the GAT baseline. Across eight benchmarks at ten seeds, LTGA-Edge takes the best average rank ($2.75$), but the omnibus test does not reject ($p\!=\!0.199$) and learning $q$ does not beat searching it: a validation-tuned frozen grid reaches $61.4\%$, tuned $α$-entmax $62.2\%$ and a capacity-matched $q\!\equiv\!1$ control $62.0\%$, against $61.7\%$ for LTGA-Edge. What the learned index buys is one run instead of a grid, and an interpretable mechanism: where $q$ leaves $1$, it prunes $42\%$ of attention coefficients to exactly zero, and those edges are selectively the wrong ones, restoring them costs $7.1$ points, while random pruning at the same rate costs $13.0$ more. Project page: https://kleyt0n.github.io/ltga

</details>


### [149] [Federated generative event models for tokenized electronic health records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02939)
*Michael C. Burkhart,Luke Solo,Inhyeok Lee,S'Khaja Charles,Zewei "Whiskey" Liao,Kaveri Chhikara,Dema Therese,Wan-Ting Liao,Catherine A. Gao,William F. Parker,Brett K. Beaulieu-Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning of EHR foundation models across multiple health systems is technically feasible and preserves most centralized performance, but transportability remains a challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electronic health record (EHR) foundation models face limitations due to institutionally siloed data and substantial performance degradation when transferred across different healthcare sites, highlighting the need for solutions that can leverage multi-institutional data while addressing data privacy concerns.

Method: Researchers evaluated federated training of tokenized generative event models (GEMs) across 122,251 ICU hospitalizations from three independent health systems, using harmonized Common Longitudinal ICU Data Format. They compared within-site, cross-site, centralized, and federated training (FedAvg and FedAvgM) configurations on 12 clinical prediction tasks, assessing transportability via cross-site penalties.

Result: GEMs achieved the highest mean within-site and cross-site ROC-AUC with substantially better transportability than conventional supervised models (LightGBM). Federated learning approached centralized GEM performance within 5-10 communication rounds, but centralized multi-site training provided only modest improvements over local training. Multi-site models were most beneficial when local data was limited, with diminishing returns as institutional data accumulated.

Conclusion: Federated GEM training is technically feasible and preserves most centralized performance, but the key challenge remains learning truly transportable representations that can effectively translate heterogeneous multi-site data into reliable target-site benefits.

Abstract: Electronic health record foundation models are limited by institutionally siloed data and substantial performance degradation under cross-site transfer. We evaluated federated training of tokenized generative event models (GEMs) across 122,251 intensive care hospitalizations from three independent health systems harmonized to the Common Longitudinal ICU Data Format. Models were assessed on 12 post-24-hour clinical prediction tasks using within-site, cross-site, centralized, and federated training configurations. GEMs achieved the highest mean within-site and cross-site ROC-AUC and were substantially more transportable than conventional supervised models: their average cross-site penalties were 0.025 ROC-AUC and 0.027 PR-AUC, compared with 0.079 and 0.089 for LightGBM. Federated Learning (FedAvg and FedAvgM) approached the performance of centralized GEM training, with most gains obtained within 5-10 communication rounds. However, centralized multi-site training provided only modest improvements over complete local training. Multi-site models were most useful when local training data were limited, with their advantage narrowing as institutional data accumulated. These findings show that federated GEM training is technically feasible and preserves most centralized performance, but that the main open challenge is learning transportable representations to translate larger, but heterogeneous data from multiple health systems into a reliable target-site benefit.

</details>


### [150] [Sedentary Behavior Classification for Wearable Sensors with a CNN-BiLSTM Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02946)
*Yuliang Chen,Weiwei Shi,Jingjing Zou,Rong Zablocki,Animesh Kumar,Jordan A. Carlson,Sheri J. Hartman,Mikael Anne Greenwood-Hickman,Paul R. Hibbing,Marta Jankowska,Jay Yang,Arun Kumar,Loki Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep learning model trained on hip accelerometer data can transfer to wrist-worn accelerometers for sitting detection, with finetuning needed due to sensor placement differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate detection of sedentary behavior is crucial for health studies, but posture-based classification with wrist-worn sensors remains challenging due to higher signal variability compared to hip-worn sensors.

Method: Used CHAP (CNN-BiLSTM model) originally developed for hip accelerometers, evaluated its zero-shot performance on wrist data, and adapted it through finetuning with varying amounts of labeled wrist data from the iWatch dataset with ground-truth posture labels from wearable cameras.

Result: The hip-trained model performed strongly on hip data without retraining, but accuracy dropped on wrist data due to sensor placement shift. Finetuning CHAP provided consistent advantages over transformer models trained from scratch.

Conclusion: Hip-based pretraining provides a useful starting point for wrist deployment of sedentary behavior detection models, but wrist-specific adaptation is necessary to handle the higher signal variability at the wrist location.

Abstract: Accurate detection of sedentary behavior is important for studying health risks related to prolonged sitting, but posture-based classification remains challenging with wearable sensors, especially at the wrist. We study whether a deep learning model trained on hip-worn accelerometer data can transfer to wrist-worn accelerometer data for sitting versus non-sitting classification. We use CHAP, a CNN-BiLSTM model originally developed for hip accelerometers, and evaluate its zero-shot performance on wrist data as well as its adaptation through finetuning with varying amounts of labeled wrist data. Experiments are conducted on the iWatch dataset with ground-truth posture labels derived from wearable cameras. The hip-trained model performs strongly on hip data without retraining, but accuracy drops on wrist data due to sensor placement shift. Finetuning CHAP provides consistent advantages over transformer models trained from scratch. These findings suggest that hip-based pretraining provides a useful starting point for wrist deployment, while highlighting the need for wrist-specific adaptation to handle higher signal variability.

</details>


### [151] [ATFlash: Per-RoPE-Wavelength Attention Windows for Compute/Memory-Efficient LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02947)
*Shun-ichiro Hayashi,Daichi Mukunoki,Tetsuya Hoshino,Takahiro Katagiri*

Main category: cs.LG
