TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel pruning method for RoPE-based attention called per-RoPE-wavelength distance window, which prunes query-key inner-product terms beyond wavelength-proportional distances while maintaining all keys reachable through low-frequency pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RoPE attention decomposes into frequency pairs where each pair's wavelength limits position discrimination range. Current methods like dynamic-sparse attention have limitations, and there's a need for efficient attention mechanisms that maintain long-context performance while reducing computational cost.

Method: Proposes per-RoPE-wavelength distance window that prunes query-key inner-product terms beyond wavelength-proportional distances. Unlike sliding windows, every key remains reachable via low-frequency pairs. The method has input-independent reduction rate with closed-form logarithmic scaling in sequence length, implemented as a slice of query-key contraction axis.

Result: Achieves 37-48% pruning of query-key inner-product terms within native context lengths on Qwen2.5-0.5B and Llama-3.2-3B. Maintains 96-98% top-1 match rate and 10^-3-nat level mean output-distribution KL on LongBench-v2. Speedups up to 1.29× at 128K context on RTX PRO 6000 and 1.31× at 1M-token context on Qwen2.5-7B-1M with 57% pruning.

Conclusion: The per-RoPE-wavelength distance window provides an effective frequency-level pruning method that preserves long-context performance while achieving significant computational speedups. It's orthogonal to existing token-level methods and can be combined with them for additional efficiency gains.

Abstract: The attention score with rotary position embeddings (RoPE) decomposes exactly into a sum over its 2D-rotation frequency pairs, and each pair's wavelength limits how far it can discriminate position. Aligned with this structure, we propose the per-RoPE-wavelength distance window: it prunes the query--key inner-product terms beyond a wavelength-proportional distance. Unlike a sliding window, every key remains reachable, at least through the low-frequency pairs. The reduction rate is input-independent, with a closed form logarithmic in the sequence length $N$, in contrast to dynamic-sparse methods like MInference. Such token-level selection is orthogonal to our frequency-level pruning. The window can therefore be applied on top of those methods. On Qwen2.5-0.5B and Llama-3.2-3B, the window prunes 37--48\% of the query--key inner-product terms within each model's native context length. Relative to full attention, the top-1 match rate stays at 96--98\% and the mean output-distribution KL at the $10^{-3}$-nat level on LongBench-v2 contexts. We examine absolute scores on long-context benchmarks such as RULER, OpenAI-MRCR, LongCodeQA, and $\infty$Bench: they are broadly preserved. We implement the window as a slice of the query--key contraction axis, leaving the online-softmax recurrences untouched, and port it with minimal diffs into the released FlashAttention-4 prefill and FlashInfer decode. On RTX PRO 6000 with Llama, both ports outpace stock with gains growing with context length, up to $1.29\times$ at 128K. End to end on Qwen2.5-7B-1M, with 57\% of the inner-product terms pruned, the speedup reaches $1.31\times$ at a 1M-token context.

</details>


### [152] [Rubrics as Privileged Information for Open-Ended Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02948)
*Deepika Bablani,Ajay Gupta,Wanming Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Soft rubric privileged information outperforms both reference completion distillation and rubric-as-reward RL for open-ended generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) shows promise in verifiable domains like math with hard privileged information, but needs extension to open-ended generation where rubrics provide soft privileged information that guides preferences while admitting many valid responses.

Method: Extend OPSD to open-ended generation using soft rubric privileged information as dense supervision signal for distillation, comparing against rubric-as-reward RL and reference completion privileged information distillation across Qwen and Llama model families.

Result: Rubric privileged information outperforms rubric-as-reward RL by up to +0.10 absolute score on HealthBench, and beats reference-PI distillation by +0.034 to +0.079 across three models. On ResearchQA, soft rubric PI achieves 66.6% vs. 64.2% for reference-PI and 57.6% for RaR RL.

Conclusion: Soft rubric privileged information provides richer training signal than hard reference completion PI for open-ended generation tasks, offering better supervision by capturing preference structure across valid responses rather than over-constraining to single references.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD), where a single model acts as both student and teacher with different contexts, has shown promise in verifiable domains like math, where hard privileged information (PI) in the form of ground-truth answers structurally constrains valid continuations. We extend OPSD to open-ended generation using soft PI in the form of rubrics that guide preferences but admit many valid responses. Rubrics have served as scalar rewards for reinforcement learning (RL); we show that they provide substantially richer signal as dense PI for distillation, and contrary to intuition, soft rubric PI provides a larger and more effective training signal on student roll-outs than hard reference completion PI in this regime. A reference completion is one point in a set of valid responses, so distilling towards it over-constrains the student, while rubrics specify the preference structure shared across the set of valid responses. We show the effectiveness of using rubrics as PI for open-ended generation across Qwen and Llama model families and show that it outperforms rubric-as-reward (RaR) RL using HealthBench, a benchmark that grades open-ended health responses against physician-created rubrics, providing dense token-level supervision for open-ended tasks; RuPI beats RaR RL by up to +0.10 absolute score and, under matched recipe and KL direction, beats reference-PI by +0.034 to +0.079 absolute score across three models. We further show that these findings generalize to training on the RubricHub Science corpus and evaluating on ResearchQA: soft rubric PI outperforms both reference-PI distillation and RaR RL (66.6% vs. 64.2% and 57.6%).

</details>


### [153] [Schedule-Informed Temporal Fusion Forecasting of Hourly Airport Security-Checkpoint Throughput](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02950)
*Yinxiao Zhang,Sen Wang,Yi Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework transforms flight schedules into checkpoint throughput forecasts using Poisson-distributed seat capacity and Temporal Fusion Transformers, achieving 9.33% error for 6-hour forecasts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Security checkpoint staffing requires forecasts of passenger screening demand, but flight schedules only provide departure times, not passenger arrival times at checkpoints.

Method: Distribute domestic/international seat capacity across pre-departure hours using truncated Poisson kernels, then use Temporal Fusion Transformers to combine schedule-derived signals with historical throughput, scheduled activity, and temporal variables.

Result: Achieved 9.33% weighted MAPE for 6-hour forecasts (vs. 12.16% for RNN and 11.37% for LSTM), with lowest errors during peak periods. For recursive updates across 24-96 hour horizons, errors remained 10.60-11.04%.

Conclusion: The framework supports advance staffing and multiday checkpoint planning by transforming flight schedules into screening-load signals without passenger-flight matching, but forecasts should be interpreted with local capacity/queue information.

Abstract: Checkpoint staffing requires accurate forecasts of when screening demand will occur, yet flight schedules record departure times rather than passenger arrival times at security checkpoints. This study develops a framework that converts known flight schedules into temporally aligned signals for forecasting hourly checkpoint throughput. Using 2023-2024 Transportation Security Administration throughput data and Cirium Diio flight schedules for Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport, domestic and international seat capacity was distributed across pre-departure hours using truncated Poisson kernels. A Temporal Fusion Transformer then combined these schedule-derived arrival-intensity signals with historical throughput, scheduled activity, and temporal variables. Models were trained chronologically, with July-December 2024 reserved for testing, and evaluated against recurrent neural network and long short-term memory models across five random seeds. For direct six-hour forecasts, the proposed model achieved a weighted mean absolute percentage error of 9.33%, compared with 12.16% for the recurrent neural network and 11.37% for long short-term memory, while also producing the lowest errors during peak periods. With six-hour recursive updates, errors remained between 10.60% and 11.04% across 24-96 hour horizons, although longer horizons contained fewer valid forecast origins. By transforming scheduled departures into interpretable pre-departure screening-load signals without requiring passenger-flight matching, the framework supports advance staffing, lane-opening, and multiday checkpoint planning. Because observed throughput reflects realized processing rather than unconstrained arrivals, the forecasts should be interpreted together with local staffing, capacity, queue, and wait-time information.

</details>


### [154] [SP3O: Reinforcement Learning from Segment Preferences without Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02951)
*Evan Assmus,Qining Zhang,Lei Ying*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SP3O is a reward-model-free, critic-free, gradient-based preference-based RL algorithm that uses segment-level preferences instead of trajectory-level feedback, improving performance in long-horizon tasks while reducing human evaluation effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current preference-based RL methods have limitations: reward-model-free approaches are restricted to bandits/deterministic MDPs (DPO, P3O) or use slower gradient-free optimization, while most methods require trajectory-level feedback which is burdensome for human evaluators on long trajectories.

Method: SP3O (Segment Pairwise Proximal Policy Optimization) uses segment-level preference feedback to construct policy value difference estimators via off-policy importance sampling, then computes policy gradient using a PPO-type loss function without requiring reward models or critics.

Result: The algorithm shows improved performance compared to other PbRL/RLHF methods in robotic control and LLM finetuning settings, particularly excelling in long-horizon tasks. The paper also analyzes the tradeoff in segment length selection.

Conclusion: SP3O provides an efficient, gradient-based, reward-model-free solution for preference-based RL that leverages segment-level preferences to reduce human evaluation burden while maintaining strong performance, especially for long-horizon problems.

Abstract: Preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) for general stochastic MDPs often requires training a reward model. Existing reward-model-free methods are either restricted to bandits or deterministic MDPs, such as DPO or P3O, or use zeroth-order, gradient-free optimization, which in general exhibits a slower convergence rate than gradient-based algorithms. Furthermore, existing reward-model-free preference-based RL algorithms almost exclusively use trajectory-level feedback, which can require significant effort from a human evaluator when trajectories are long. On the other hand, segments are much shorter, so they are easier to compare and evaluate. In this paper, we introduce a novel reward-model-free, critic-free, and gradient-based PbRL algorithm compatible with segment preferences named Segment Pairwise Proximal Policy Optimization (SP3O). SP3O utilizes segment-level preference feedback to construct an accurate policy value difference estimator via off-policy importance sampling, and then uses the estimator to compute the policy gradient via a PPO-type loss function. We provide a theoretical basis for the algorithm and analyze the tradeoff in choosing the segment length. We also evaluate it experimentally against other PbRL/RLHF algorithms in robotic control and LLM finetuning settings to show its improved performance, especially in long-horizon tasks.

</details>


### [155] [Inverted Detection and Control in Steering Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02957)
*Max Torop,Aria Masoomi,Jennifer Dy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Some steering vectors that appear discriminative for concepts actually promote opposite behavior when used for control, requiring sign flips to work properly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges a key assumption in steering vectors (SVs) - that they are linearly discriminative and that shifting along positive/negative directions promotes/suppresses concepts. The authors identify cases where highly discriminative SVs aligned with positive representations actually promote opposite behavior.

Method: The authors identify inverted-steering vectors (ISVs) that show an inverted detection-control phenomenon. They provide geometric characterization of ISVs' effects and propose an approach to distinguish ISVs without requiring generation or response scoring. They use targeted sign flips to improve a detection-based steering pipeline via Inference Time Intervention (ITI).

Result: The approach improves results in 27/30 experiments, with improvements ranging from +0.9% to +138%. Evaluations were conducted on Gemma 3 12B, Qwen 2.5 14B, and Olmo 3 7B across 5 concepts.

Conclusion: The discovery of inverted-steering vectors reveals limitations in current steering vector assumptions and provides a practical solution through sign flips to improve concept control in LLMs, significantly enhancing steering pipeline performance.

Abstract: Steering vectors (SVs) are widely used to influence the expression of concepts (e.g., truthfulness) in large language model outputs. A key assumption underpinning SVs is that they are linearly discriminative with respect to the concept: representations of texts that exhibit the concept are more aligned with the SV than those that do not, motivating shifts along the positive or negative SV direction to respectively promote or suppress the concept. In this work, we identify an inverted detection-control phenomenon in which some highly discriminative SVs that are aligned with positive representations can consistently promote the opposite behavior. We refer to such vectors as inverted-steering vectors (ISVs). We provide a geometric characterization of ISVs' effects, finding that steering along these directions systematically pushes representations in discriminative downstream heads as if the concept were absent, even prior to decoding. Motivated by this analysis, we propose an approach for distinguishing ISVs without requiring generation or associated response scoring. This enables targeted sign flips, which we use to improve a foundational detection-based steering pipeline via Inference Time Intervention (ITI). Our approach improves results in 27/30 experiments, ranging from +0.9% to +138%. We evaluate our findings on Gemma 3 12B, Qwen 2.5 14B, and Olmo 3 7B across 5 concepts.

</details>


### [156] [Scaling an Autoregressive Transformer for Single-Cell Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02961)
*Aleksandr Sharipov,Yusif Mukhtarov,Igor Molybog*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A self-supervised transformer model for generating realistic single-cell gene expression vectors, with scaling law analysis showing compute-optimal performance for a single-cell foundation model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a foundation model for single-cell gene expression data that can generate additional realistic gene expression vectors for a given cell type, addressing the need for more diverse and representative single-cell data.

Method: Causal transformer paired with a learned quantized VAE tokenizer trained with cross-entropy loss. Evaluated by generating gene expression vectors conditioned on held-out data and comparing distributions to ground truth. Scaling properties studied by varying model parameters and training data size.

Result: First jointly-fit two-exponent scaling law and compute-optimal frontier for a single-cell foundation model, demonstrating effective generation of biologically faithful gene expression vectors.

Conclusion: The approach provides a scalable foundation model for single-cell gene expression that can generate realistic data and could be fine-tuned for applications like perturbation response prediction.

Abstract: We study a self-supervised generation task for single-cell gene expression vectors: given a set of vectors from a cell type, we aim to generate additional gene expression vectors of that cell type. For this task we characterize both the biological fidelity of the generated gene expression vectors and the scaling behavior of the pretraining loss. The model is a causal transformer paired with a learned quantized VAE tokenizer, trained with a cross-entropy loss. To evaluate the model, we condition it on held-out gene expression vectors of a cell type and generate vectors of gene expression, comparing the resulting distribution over gene expression vectors to the ground truth distribution of that cell type. We study the scaling properties of the proposed architecture by varying the number of trained parameters and the amount of training data. To our knowledge, we find the first jointly-fit two-exponent scaling law and compute-optimal frontier for a single-cell foundation model. Finally, we discuss how this pretrained model could be finetuned for perturbation response prediction.

</details>


### [157] [A Physics-Informed Hybrid Neural Operator for Transient Magnetization Prediction in Power Magnetics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02965)
*Yachao Zhu,Qiujie Huang,Sinan Li,Yang Li,Gang Lei,Jianguo Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PI-HNO is a compact neural model that predicts transient magnetization responses for magnetic components under complex operating conditions using B-H energy consistency regularization, achieving accurate predictions with minimal parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic components in modern power converters operate under challenging conditions (non-sinusoidal waveforms, fast transitions, minor loops, DC bias, temperature variations) where traditional steady-state core-loss formulas and single-valued material curves fail to capture transient magnetization responses accurately.

Method: Proposes Physics-Informed Hybrid Neural Operator (PI-HNO) - a compact material-specific neural model with B-H energy consistency regularization. Combines a local recurrent branch for boundary-state representation and rate-dependent response evolution with a Preisach-inspired global branch for waveform-level hysteresis context extraction. Takes measured B(t)-H(t) history, input B(t) series, and operating-condition info to predict H(t) series and reconstructed B-H trajectory.

Result: Evaluation on MagNetX transient database with 14 ferrite materials shows PI-HNO achieves mean B(t)-H(t) energy consistency error of 1.92% and 95th percentile error of 7.60% using only 4777 trainable parameters per model. Demonstrates compact trade-off between sequence accuracy and energy consistency, with ablation studies confirming contributions of local, global, and energy-aware components.

Conclusion: PI-HNO successfully addresses the limitations of traditional methods for transient magnetization prediction in complex operating conditions through a compact, physics-informed neural approach that maintains B-H energy consistency while achieving accurate predictions with minimal parameters, enabling better modeling of magnetic components in modern power converters.

Abstract: Magnetic components in high-frequency, high-power-density converters are increasingly driven by non-sinusoidal flux-density waveforms with fast transitions, minor-loop operation, dc bias, and temperature variation. Under these conditions, steady-state core-loss formulas and single-valued material curves cannot fully capture transient magnetization responses. This work proposes the Physics-Informed Hybrid Neural Operator (PI-HNO), a compact material-specific neural model with B-H energy-consistency regularization for core-loss-oriented transient magnetization prediction. Given the measured B(t)-H(t) history, the input B(t) series over the prediction interval and operating-condition information, PI-HNO predicts the H(t) series and the corresponding reconstructed B-H trajectory. The model integrates a local recurrent branch for boundary-state representation and rate-dependent response evolution with a Preisach-inspired global branch that extracts waveform-level hysteresis context. Evaluation on the MagNetX transient database using material-specific models for 14 ferrite materials demonstrates that PI-HNO achieves a compact trade-off between sequence accuracy and B(t)-H(t) energy consistency, with the mean and 95th percentile B(t)-H(t) energy consistency errors of 1.92% and 7.60%, respectively, using only 4777 trainable parameters per model. Ablation studies further demonstrate that the local, global, and energy-aware regularized components provide distinct contributions to transient magnetization prediction.

</details>


### [158] [Temporal Leakage in LLM Backtesting: Measurement, Validation, and Adjusted Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02985)
*Zeyu Zhang,Bradly C. Stadie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Backtest contamination checks comparing pre/post-cutoff scores are uninformative; recency effects mimic leakage, requiring external references for accurate measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard contamination checks in LLM backtests are insufficient because they fail to distinguish between genuine model skill and temporal recency effects that mimic data leakage, potentially leading to incorrect conclusions about model performance.

Method: Two measurement approaches: 1) using known cutoff dates to identify leakage at temporal boundaries, and 2) employing matched clean control samples to identify leakage globally and compute leakage-adjusted scores. Also analyzes where leakage concentrates (surprising outcomes with extensive training coverage) and validates estimators using twin models with planted leakage.

Result: Standard contamination check fails on flagship models for questions they cannot have memorized. Recency effects structurally mimic leakage. Validation shows estimators recover injected leakage and return null on clean questions. Deployment on frontier models detects one cutoff-localized signature and clears five models whose apparent advantages were recency alone.

Conclusion: Passive backtests cannot separate recency effects from genuine skill without external reference. Measurement requires information outside the backtest. Backtests need one defensible reference rather than being discarded entirely.

Abstract: The standard check for contamination in LLM backtests is simple: compare scores before and after the training cutoff. We show this check is uninformative. Four flagship models fail it on questions they cannot have memorized: every scored question resolved after their cutoffs. The reason is structural. Models legitimately know more about times near their cutoff, so recency mimics leakage, and we prove no passive backtest can separate the two from genuine skill. Measurement, not just detection, requires information from outside the backtest. We supply it in two forms. A known cutoff identifies leakage at the boundary; a matched clean control identifies it globally and yields a leakage-adjusted score. We also derive where leakage hides: it concentrates on outcomes that surprised the crowd and were well covered in training, and partial memorization is disproportionately rewarded. We validate the estimators against ground truth by planting leakage in twin models, where they recover the injected dose and return null on clean questions. Deployed on frontier models, they detect one cutoff-localized signature and, at the audit's power floor, clear five models whose apparent advantages were recency alone. Backtests need not be discarded; they need one defensible reference.

</details>


### [159] [AcceptMoE: Commitment-Weighted Self-Sizing Verifier Expert Sets for Efficient MoE Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02989)
*Shuang Liang,Hao,Chen,Zhiwen Mo,Qianzhou Wang,Guoyu Li,Lingxiao Ma,Wayne Luk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AcceptMoE is an expert selection method for speculative decoding of Mixture-of-Experts models that dynamically adjusts expert eligibility during verification, reducing computational costs and expert traffic while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional speculative decoding for MoE models activates the union of experts from all tree nodes during parallel verification, which is inefficient because only a small subset reaches accepted output. This leads to unnecessary computational costs, especially under offloading scenarios where expert weight transfer adds significant overhead.

Method: AcceptMoE combines target-router scores with offline-estimated commitment probabilities to automatically adjust the number of eligible experts for each verification block, eliminating the need for user-specified expert budgets. Under offloading, it conditions expert eligibility on cache residency rather than predicting routes and prefetching expert weights.

Result: Across 12 model-task pairs spanning three MoE targets and four benchmarks, AcceptMoE achieves only 0.27 percentage points lower accuracy than EAGLE-3 speculative decoding with natural routing. It reaches 1.29x throughput with all experts in GPU memory and 2.06x throughput under physical expert offloading, while reducing host-to-device traffic by 73.6-77.1%.

Conclusion: AcceptMoE effectively reduces computational and communication costs for MoE speculative decoding while maintaining near-baseline accuracy, making it particularly valuable for offloading scenarios where expert traffic is a bottleneck.

Abstract: Speculative decoding verifies a tree of draft tokens in one target-model forward pass. For a mixture-of-experts (MoE) target, however, parallel verification can activate the union of the experts selected by all tree nodes, even though only a small subset of those nodes reaches the accepted output. Token count, activated-expert union size, and expert-weight traffic are therefore distinct cost measures: reducing the token workload need not shrink the expert union proportionally, and under offloading, transfer traffic also depends on cache residency. We introduce AcceptMoE, a verifier-side expert selector that combines target-router scores with offline-estimated commitment probabilities and automatically adjusts the number of eligible experts for each verification block, eliminating the need for a user-specified expert budget. Under offloading, AcceptMoE conditions expert eligibility on cache residency instead of predicting natural routes and prefetching the corresponding expert weights. Although constraining target-expert eligibility changes the model distribution, across 12 model-task pairs spanning three MoE targets and four benchmarks, AcceptMoE's mean accuracy is 0.27 percentage points lower than that of EAGLE-3 speculative decoding with natural routing. Served with SGLang at batch size one, it reaches 1.290 times the throughput of this baseline with all expert weights in GPU memory, and 2.06 times under physical expert offloading, while reducing host-to-device traffic by 73.6 percent to 77.1 percent.

</details>


### [160] [Joint Affine Spectral Shaping: Coupling Weight and Bias Updates Beyond Weight-Only Muon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02991)
*Gongyue Zhang,Honghai Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Joint Regularized Inverse (JRI) optimization applies spectral shaping to both weights and biases jointly, outperforming weight-only or separate bias optimizers in BERT-mini training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current matrix spectral optimizers only reshape weight-update spectra while treating vector-valued biases separately, leaving uncertainty about whether this separation is neutral or could be improved by joint optimization.

Method: Formulates each affine layer as a joint momentum matrix A=[M_W, αm_b] and applies a capped regularized-inverse spectral map to the complete matrix. Tested on four-layer BERT-mini trained from scratch on IMDb with five-seed ablation comparing exact-SVD Muon, weight-only inverse shaping, affine-probe inverse shaping, and the proposed JRI approach.

Result: JRI raises selected test accuracy from 84.903±0.242% to 85.738±0.180% and lowers test loss from 0.3479 to 0.3291, with all five seeds improving. It reduces bias-update norm from 0.02095 to 0.00301, lowers boundary-function share from 86.58% to 78.97%, and changes cosine between weight-induced boundary motion and explicit bias from +0.030 to -0.137. 22-seed replication confirms results (85.743±0.203%).

Conclusion: Joint affine spectral allocation is a small but consistent extension to weight-only spectral optimization that improves performance by jointly optimizing both weights and biases through spectral shaping rather than treating them separately.

Abstract: Matrix spectral optimizers reshape weight-update spectra but usually delegate vector-valued biases to a separate optimizer. We study whether this separation is neutral. We formulate each affine layer as a joint momentum matrix $A=[M_W,αm_b]$ and apply a capped regularized-inverse spectral map to the complete matrix, producing both the weight and physical bias updates. A strict five-seed ablation on a four-layer BERT-mini trained from scratch on IMDb compares exact-SVD Muon, weight-only inverse shaping, affine-probe inverse shaping, and the proposed joint regularized inverse (JRI). Weight-only inverse shaping raises validation-loss-selected test accuracy from $84.903\pm0.242\%$ to $85.562\pm0.308\%$ and lowers selected test loss from $0.3479$ to $0.3345$. Allowing bias to alter the joint SVD while retaining an independent Adam bias update does not improve over weight-only inverse shaping. Using the transformed bias jointly raises selected test accuracy to $85.738\pm0.180\%$ and lowers test loss to $0.3291$, with all five seeds improving relative to the probe baseline. During the peak-performance window, JRI preserves the eligible weight-update norm while reducing the bias-update norm from $0.02095$ to $0.00301$, lowers boundary-function share from $86.58\%$ to $78.97\%$, and changes the cosine between weight-induced boundary motion and explicit bias from $+0.030$ to $-0.137$. An independent 22-seed replication yields $85.743\pm0.203\%$ selected test accuracy. These results identify joint affine spectral allocation as a small but consistent extension to weight-only spectral optimization.

</details>


### [161] [A Graph Signal Processing Perspective on Numerical Sequence Representations in LLM In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03015)
*Jiajun Bao,Zihao Qi,Toni J. B. Liu,Gurbir Arora,Raphaël Sarfati,Nicolas Boullé,Christopher J. Earls*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs organize numerical information in their internal representations based on input complexity, with simpler inputs showing global connectivity and smooth signals, while complex inputs show localized connectivity and broad-spectrum signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work has studied LLM numerical inference through output-level evaluations (prediction error), there's limited understanding of how numerical information is organized within LLM internal representations.

Method: Adopting a graph signal processing perspective where attention induces weighted graphs over tokens and token hidden states define signals on nodes. Using quantitative graph-spectral diagnostics and qualitative token-graph visualizations to analyze internal organization.

Result: Representations become more clearly differentiated by input dynamical complexity as context length increases. Simpler inputs produce attention graphs with stronger global connectivity and smoother, spectrally concentrated hidden states, while complex inputs produce more localized graphs with broader spectral support and greater high-frequency energy.

Conclusion: There are systematic, context-dependent internal signatures associated with numerical in-context learning that are conserved across different LLM model families.

Abstract: Pretrained large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated in-context learning (ICL) capabilities for numerical inference over sequences serialized as text. Prior work has identified and characterized this form of numerical inference primarily through output-level evaluations such as prediction error. However, how numerical information is organized within LLM representations remains much less understood. To study this internal organization, we adopt a graph signal processing perspective in which attention induces a weighted graph over tokens, while token hidden states define signals on its nodes. Quantitative graph-spectral diagnostics and qualitative token-graph visualizations reveal that representations become more clearly differentiated by input dynamical complexity as context length increases. Simpler inputs produce attention-induced token graphs with stronger global connectivity and smoother, spectrally concentrated hidden-state signals, whereas more complex inputs produce more localized graphs and hidden-state signals with broader spectral support and greater high-frequency energy. Together, these findings point to systematic, context-dependent internal signatures associated with numerical ICL that are conserved across model families.

</details>


### [162] [Paired Recipient-based Evaluation of Survival Prediction for Deceased Donor Kidney Transplants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03017)
*Misaki Matsuura,Mohammadreza Nemati,Dulat Bekbolsynov,Stanislaw Stepkowski,Kevin S. Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Machine learning models for kidney transplant survival prediction achieve ~60% accuracy in paired recipient evaluation, revealing limitations of C-index and offering more clinically relevant metrics for donor-recipient matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve post-transplant outcomes by using machine learning to predict kidney graft survival for better donor-recipient matching, addressing the need for clinically relevant evaluation metrics beyond traditional C-index.

Method: Proposed novel paired recipient-based evaluation framework using deceased donor kidney transplant data from SRTR. Compared graft outcomes between two recipients from the same donor to assess counterfactual benefits. Tested five survival prediction models ranging from linear to deep learning architectures.

Result: All five models achieved approximately 60% paired recipient-based accuracy. Translated this accuracy into interpretable post-transplant years gained. Demonstrated limitations of C-index and showed that paired recipient-based metric is more clinically relevant and better reflects real-world allocation settings.

Conclusion: The paired recipient-based evaluation framework provides a more meaningful metric for assessing kidney transplant prediction models than traditional C-index, offering clinically interpretable measures of potential benefit for donor-recipient matching decisions.

Abstract: There has been significant interest in using machine learning algorithms to predict kidney transplant outcomes, such as the number of years until a graft inevitably fails. These prediction algorithms could possibly be used for pre-transplant donor-recipient matching to identify more compatible donors and recipients and thus improve post-transplant outcomes. In this study, we explore the use of survival prediction models trained on deceased donor kidney transplant data from the Scientific Registry of Transplant Recipients (SRTR). We propose a novel paired recipient-based evaluation framework that compares graft outcomes between two recipients who received kidneys from the same deceased donor, allowing us to evaluate the counterfactual benefit of changing the recipient for a certain donor. We find that five different survival prediction models, ranging in complexity from linear to deep learning-based models, all result in ~60% paired recipient-based accuracy. We further translate this accuracy into an interpretable quantity of post-transplant years gained. We also highlight major limitations of the commonly used concordance index (C-index) metric for evaluating survival prediction accuracy in this setting and demonstrate that our proposed paired recipient-based accuracy metric is more clinically relevant and better reflects real-world allocation settings.

</details>


### [163] [PLAN: Parallel Liquid-Inspired Approximation Network for Efficient Representation Learning in Flexible Job Shop Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03041)
*Dhivya Dharshini Kannan,Wei Zhang,Jieyi Bi,Yingpeng Du,Tianjun Wei,Jie Zhang,Zuming Liu,Anupam Trivedi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PLAN: A lightweight parallel liquid-inspired network that improves FJSP scheduling performance while reducing parameters and inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current DRL approaches for FJSP use heavy attention-based architectures that have excessive parameters and high inference latency at larger scales, creating a need for more efficient alternatives.

Method: Proposes PLAN, a lightweight framework that reformulates continuous liquid-state dynamics into discretized parallelizable formulation, decouples state evolution from context aggregation, and serves as plug-and-play backbone for various FJSP variants.

Result: Improves makespan by 1.2-2.3% across different FJSP benchmarks (with up to 10.2% improvement in one setting), reduces inference latency by 13.2-31.7%, and uses only 22-47% of baseline parameters.

Conclusion: PLAN provides a parameter-efficient, fast-inferencing alternative to heavy attention-based models for FJSP, with superior performance across multiple variants while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches for flexible job shop scheduling (FJSP) heavily rely on attention-centric architectures to achieve state-of-the-art performance. However, these models suffer from excessive parameter counts and prohibitive inference latency as problem scales expand. While liquid neural networks (LNNs) offer a parameter-efficient alternative for modeling adaptive state evolution, their inherently sequential dynamics bottleneck computational efficiency. To resolve this trade-off, we propose PLAN (Parallel Liquid-inspired Approximation Network), a lightweight representation learning framework that reformulates continuous liquid-state dynamics into a discretized and parallelizable formulation. PLAN structurally decouples state evolution from context aggregation, where liquid-inspired updates handle the primary evolving state representation, and a lightweight context aggregation module provides complementary global context. Furthermore, PLAN acts as a versatile, plug-and-play backbone that generalizes to complex FJSP variants, pairing with a compact stochastic module for stochastic FJSP and replacing heavy heterogeneous graph transformers in multi-faceted dynamic FJSP. Extensive evaluations across deterministic, stochastic, and multi-faceted dynamic FJSP benchmarks show that PLAN reduces the average makespan by 1.2%, 1.4%, and 2.3%, respectively, compared with the corresponding state-of-the-art baselines, with the improvement reaching 10.2% in one benchmark setting. PLAN also reduces average inference latency by 13.2%, 31.7%, and 26.9%, respectively, with a maximum reduction of 69.2% on the largest instances, while using only 22$-$47% of the baseline parameters.

</details>


### [164] [Exploiting Separability in Multi-Scale Grey-Box Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03045)
*Joshua E. Hammond,Tyler A. Soderstrom,Brian A. Korgel,Michael Baldea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bilevel Bayesian Optimization separates variables into black-box (expensive) and white-box (explicit equations) components, using outer BO for black-box variables and inner global optimization for white-box variables, achieving better performance with exact constraint satisfaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world optimization problems have mixed variable types: expensive black-box functions and explicit white-box equations. Traditional methods treat everything as black-box or use approximations, leading to inefficiencies and constraint violations.

Method: The proposed method uses a bilevel formulation: 1) Outer Bayesian Optimization (BO) with Gaussian process surrogate for expensive black-box variables, 2) Inner global optimization subproblem that solves explicit white-box equations exactly. White-box constraints are satisfied exactly when the inner optimizer converges to feasible points, avoiding penalty functions or approximations.

Result: On 13 benchmark problems, bilevel BO achieves lower regret with fewer iterations and less wall clock time compared to alternative methods. The advantage is robust to initialization set size, exploration parameters, and inner-solver choice.

Conclusion: Bilevel BO effectively exploits the separability of grey-box optimization problems, providing exact constraint satisfaction without approximations while improving computational efficiency and solution quality.

Abstract: We consider grey-box optimization problems where the decision variables naturally partition into black-box variables (as arguments to an expensive black-box function) and white-box variables, governed by a set of explicit, closed-form equations that also depend on the output of the black-box function. We exploit this separability through a bilevel reformulation: an outer Bayesian optimization (BO) to optimize the scalar objective as a function of black-box variables alone, while an inner problem solves the white-box subproblem via global optimization. The Gaussian process surrogate used in BO is therefore defined rather than and white-box constraints are satisfied exactly whenever the inner optimizer converges to a feasible point---without penalty functions, chance constraints, or moment approximations. On a suite of 13 benchmark problems, bilevel BO achieves lower regret, with fewer iterations and wall clock time. This advantage is robust to initialization set size, exploration parameters, and inner-solver choice.

</details>


### [165] [Revisiting TD Target Aggregation under Uncertainty in Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03069)
*Lipeng Zu,Xiaonan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SADQ is a modified DQN that uses one-step model rollouts to guide next-state action selection, reducing overestimation bias caused by noisy Q-value maximization while maintaining DQN's framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard DQNs suffer from estimation noise sensitivity in the maximization operator, which deterministically favors the largest Q-value estimate regardless of its reliability, leading to amplified errors through bootstrapping and overestimation bias.

Method: SADQ introduces a simple modification to Q-learning: using one-step rollout predictions from a learned dynamics model to guide the comparison among candidate next-state actions during TD target formation. This adds structure to the aggregation step without changing the underlying learning framework.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows SADQ reduces bootstrap-induced overestimation pointwise. Empirically, it consistently improves training stability across classical control tasks, real-world vector-based environments, and Atari benchmarks compared to strong DQN variants.

Conclusion: SADQ successfully regularizes DQN's aggregation step using model-based rollouts, attenuating unreliable maxima while preserving standard convergence properties under diminishing model error, offering a practical improvement to deep Q-learning stability.

Abstract: Deep Q-Networks (DQNs) learn value functions through bootstrapped temporal-difference updates, where future returns are approximated using a greedy maximization over next-state action values. While effective, this aggregation rule is inherently sensitive to estimation noise: when Q-values are uncertain, the maximization operator deterministically favors the largest estimate, regardless of its reliability, leading to amplified errors through bootstrapping. In this work, we propose the \textbf{S}uccessor Rollout \textbf{A}ggregation \textbf{D}eep \textbf{Q}-Network (SADQ), a simple modification to Q-learning that regularizes how the TD target is formed. SADQ uses one-step rollout predictions from a learned dynamics model to guide the comparison among candidate next-state actions, introducing additional structure into the aggregation step without altering the underlying learning framework. The resulting mixed Bellman update attenuates unreliable maxima while preserving the standard fixed point under diminishing model error. We provide theoretical analysis showing that SADQ reduces bootstrap-induced overestimation in a pointwise manner. Empirically, SADQ consistently improves training stability across classical control tasks, real-world vector-based environments, and Atari benchmarks when compared to strong DQN variants.

</details>


### [166] [SynEnergy: Anomaly Semantic-Guided Diffusion for Synthetic Energy Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03087)
*Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ravikumar Gelli,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SynEnergy is a two-stage diffusion framework that generates synthetic energy consumption data while preserving anomalous events, outperforming existing methods in anomaly preservation and downstream task quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained energy consumption data is crucial for grid applications but restricted by privacy concerns, creating need for synthetic data generation. Existing methods smooth out or underrepresent important anomalous events caused by extreme weather, infrastructure failures, and behavioral shifts, which are sparse, localized, and shaped by complex dependencies.

Method: Proposes SynEnergy, a two-stage diffusion-based framework: 1) Heterogeneous Graph-based Anomaly Semantic Learning (HG-ASL) extracts region-specific anomaly semantics by modeling spatial and attribute dependencies across urban regions from sparse residual structures; 2) Anomaly Semantic-guided Diffusion (AS-Diff) injects learned anomaly semantics into the denoising process to generate realistic consumption sequences while preserving anomalous patterns.

Result: Evaluation on four real-world energy consumption datasets against 11 baselines shows SynEnergy improves anomaly preservation fidelity by average 12.21% and downstream quality by 2.96%, while maintaining competitive overall generation fidelity.

Conclusion: SynEnergy effectively addresses the challenge of preserving anomalous events in synthetic energy data generation through its two-stage diffusion approach that models heterogeneous dependencies and injects anomaly semantics, enabling realistic generation of rare but important consumption patterns for grid applications.

Abstract: Fine-grained energy consumption data are essential for applications such as demand forecasting, demand response planning, and grid reliability assessment. However, access to such data is often restricted by privacy concerns and data-sharing constraints, motivating growing interest in synthetic energy data generation. Although existing methods can reproduce overall consumption distributions and recurring temporal patterns, they often smooth out or underrepresent anomalous events caused by extreme weather, infrastructure failures, and behavioral shifts. Preserving these events is challenging because they are sparse, localized in time and space, and shaped by heterogeneous dependencies across geographical proximity and regional attributes. To address these challenges, we propose SynEnergy, a two-stage diffusion-based framework for anomaly-preserving energy consumption data generation. The first stage, Heterogeneous Graph-based Anomaly Semantic Learning (HG-ASL), extracts region-specific anomaly semantics from sparse residual structures by jointly modeling spatial and attribute dependencies across urban regions. The second stage, Anomaly Semantic-guided Diffusion (AS-Diff), injects the learned anomaly semantics into the denoising process to generate realistic consumption sequences while preserving anomalous patterns. This design enables controllable generation for individual regions and scales naturally to city-wide settings. We evaluate SynEnergy on four real-world energy consumption datasets against 11 general-purpose and energy-specific generation baselines. Experimental results show that SynEnergy improves anomaly preservation fidelity by an average of 12.21% and downstream quality by 2.96%, while maintaining competitive overall generation fidelity compared to baselines.

</details>


### [167] [SMOPD: Multi-Reward Reinforcement Learning via Specialize-and-Merge Online Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03092)
*Wen Wang,Jiahua Bao,Tu Yongsiqi,Yihao Liu,Haotian Zhou,Haoxuan Ma,Mengyu Zhou,Wenkui Fan,Junwei He,Xiaoxi Jiang,Guanjun Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SMOPD is a two-stage training method that improves multi-reward RL by first training specialized teachers for each reward signal, then merging them via online policy distillation to handle rewards with different granularities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GDPO method normalizes each reward dimension but still struggles with rewards of different granularities (dense vs sparse). Sparse rewards provide insufficient optimization signals, preventing effective learning of corresponding capabilities.

Method: Two-stage approach: Stage1 trains multiple reward-specialized teachers using reward-priority configurations; Stage2 uses online policy distillation to merge these specialized capabilities into a single student policy while maintaining balanced optimization.

Result: SMOPD outperforms GDPO across 1.5B, 3B, and 7B model backbones on both complementary rewards (tool-calling accuracy and format) and conflicting rewards (helpful and harmless) settings.

Conclusion: SMOPD effectively addresses the granularity imbalance problem in multi-reward RL by separating reward specialization and merging stages, enabling better optimization of sparse rewards without sacrificing fine-grained reward learning.

Abstract: We aim to improve model performance in multi-reward reinforcement learning training process. Existing Group reward-Decoupled Normalization Policy Optimization (GDPO) has mitigated the issue of reward signals masking one another during direct scalarization by normalizing each reward dimension separately before aggregation. However, our experiments show that GDPO still struggles to balance reward signals with different granularities. Specifically, in some particular training tasks, the model may receive a dense reward that assigns fine-grained scores ranging from 0.1 to 1.0, together with a sparse reward that provides only binary feedback of either 0 or 1. In such cases, we find that the sparse reward may provide an insufficient optimization signal, preventing its corresponding capability from being effectively reinforced. Therefore, how can we strengthen the optimization signal from the sparse reward without sacrificing the capability already learned from the fine-grained reward? To overcome this limitation, we propose Specialize-and-Merge Online Policy Distillation (SMOPD), a two-stage training method for multi-reward optimization. Stage1-Specialize: SMOPD first employs reward-priority configurations to train multiple reward-specialized teachers, allowing each reward to be learned under conditions where its signal can effectively drive optimization. Stage2-Merge: SMOPD then utilizes online policy distillation to combine the reward-specialized capabilities of these teachers into a single student policy, while maintaining balanced task-level optimization. To validate our method, we conduct experiments on two multi-reward settings: complementary rewards(tool-calling accuracy and format) and conflicting rewards (helpful and harmless rewards). Based on above settings, SMOPD outperforms GDPO across 1.5B, 3B and 7B backbones.

</details>


### [168] [Convex-Hull-Neighborhood Smooth Dual Generalization: Controlling Local Correction Propagation in Offline RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03108)
*Yi Yang,Zhennan Chen,Mingfeng Lv,Hanlei Li,Zhengsen Ruan,Lvqing Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CSDG is a novel offline RL method that decomposes Bellman backups into in-sample value targets plus convex hull neighborhood corrections, enabling safe generalization to nearby OOD actions without separate regularization mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing offline RL methods struggle to balance generalization to nearby out-of-distribution (OOD) actions with control of bootstrap amplification errors, often using separate mechanisms for OOD region control and target influence regularization.

Method: CSDG expresses Bellman backups as in-sample value targets plus CHN-local corrections. It smooths in-sample-oriented and OOD-oriented candidates at different perturbation radii, uses mixture coefficient λ to scale correction contributions, maintains recursive discount γ, and approximates quantities with asymmetric bounded noise and expectile regression.

Result: Theoretical analysis provides exact one-step correction identity, time-varying iterate bound, and fixed-point bound dependent on branch discrepancy. Experiments on Gym-MuJoCo and AntMaze show strong aggregate performance and stable value estimation without exact support classification or additional pessimistic OOD penalty.

Conclusion: CSDG provides a unified framework that makes generalization contributions explicit while separating them from in-sample reference paths, enabling safe use of nearby OOD actions in offline RL with both theoretical guarantees and practical effectiveness.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning (offline RL) can benefit from nearby out-of-distribution (OOD) actions, but estimation errors at these actions may be amplified by bootstrapping. Existing regularization and local-generalization methods control either the admissible OOD region or the influence of generalized targets, often through separate mechanisms. We propose Convex Hull Neighborhood Smooth Dual Generalization (CSDG), which expresses the Bellman backup as an in-sample value target plus a CHN-local correction. This formulation makes the generalized contribution explicit and separates it from the in-sample reference path. The correction is obtained by smoothing in-sample-oriented and OOD-oriented candidates sampled at different perturbation radii. A mixture coefficient lambda scales its contribution to each backup, while the recursive discount remains gamma. Under boundedness and fixed perturbation kernels, we derive an exact one-step correction identity, a time-varying iterate bound, and a fixed-point bound that depends only on the branch discrepancy at the fixed point. We further characterize the implicit policies induced by the idealized operators and give a conditional non-degradation criterion. The practical algorithm approximates these quantities using asymmetric bounded noise and expectile regression, without exact support classification or an additional pessimistic OOD penalty. Experiments on Gym-MuJoCo and AntMaze show strong aggregate performance and stable value estimation. Code is available at: https://github.com/YOUNG-fnxm/CSDG

</details>


### [169] [Double Descent in Gradient Boosting Decision Trees via Split-Candidate Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03111)
*Ryuichi Kanoh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Double descent phenomenon in gradient boosting decision trees can be induced by scaling split-candidate budgets, with test error peaking at intermediate capacity before decreasing further.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While double descent is well-studied for neural networks via explicit capacity parameters like width, there's no analogous single-axis capacity parameter for gradient boosting decision trees (GBDTs) to systematically study their double descent behavior.

Method: Propose using number of split candidates as a capacity parameter for GBDTs. Develop an empirical tree-kernel diagnostic to analyze how candidate-induced paths group training examples. Test predictions across multiple GBDT implementations (XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost) with random-forest controls.

Result: Found test-error peaks at intermediate split-candidate budgets across all GBDT implementations, consistent with double descent. Predictions validated: deeper trees reach the regime with fewer candidates, larger datasets require finer grids, and label noise amplifies the peak. Random forests improve monotonically under same intervention.

Conclusion: Split-candidate scaling serves as an effective single-axis capacity intervention for studying GBDTs. The observed double descent arises from interaction between candidate-induced geometry and boosting dynamics, distinguishing GBDTs from random forests which don't show this phenomenon.

Abstract: Double descent is commonly studied by scaling an explicit capacity parameter, such as neural-network width. For gradient boosting decision trees (GBDTs), however, an analogous single-axis capacity parameter has not been established. We propose the number of split candidates as an operational capacity parameter for GBDTs. Holding other training controls fixed, increasing the split-candidate budget refines the feature-quantization grid and expands the dictionary of root-to-leaf paths from which boosting selects its updates. To analyze this expansion, we construct an empirical tree-kernel diagnostic that summarizes how candidate-induced paths group the training examples. A regime in which the empirical kernel rank grows toward the sample size and very small positive eigenvalues emerge exposes noise-sensitive directions; in this regime, test error peaks before decreasing again at larger split-candidate budgets. This perspective predicts that deeper trees should reach the regime with fewer split candidates, larger training sets should require finer grids, and label noise should make the peak more pronounced. Experiments support these predictions and show test-error peaks at intermediate split-candidate budgets across XGBoost, LightGBM, and CatBoost, whereas a random-forest control improves monotonically under the same split-candidate sweep. Taken together, our analysis and experiments support split-candidate scaling as a single-axis capacity intervention for studying GBDTs and suggest that the observed double descent arises from an interaction between candidate-induced geometry and boosting dynamics.

</details>


### [170] [Simulation-free and finite-time diffusion model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03117)
*Kentaro Kaba,Masayuki Ohzeki,Yuki Sughiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed framework designs diffusion processes with tractable conditional marginals, enabling both simulation-free training and finite-time generation simultaneously, connecting score matching and flow matching methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion models face a trade-off between simulation-free training and finite-time generation. Existing approaches typically choose reference processes that sacrifice one for the other.

Method: Framework designs reference diffusion processes by first prescribing tractable time-dependent conditional distributions, then constructing reference processes that realize these distributions as marginals. This reveals that score matching emerges naturally through process reversal.

Result: Shows that conditional flow matching arises as the small-noise limit of the proposed framework, providing a unified perspective connecting different training approaches for generative models.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables designing diffusion processes that achieve both simulation-free training and finite-time generation simultaneously, providing insights into the relationship between score matching and flow matching methods.

Abstract: The performance of generative diffusion models is determined by the choice of the reference diffusion process connecting the empirical and prior distributions. Conventional approaches typically trade off simulation-free training against finite-time generation. We propose a framework for designing the reference process that achieves both simultaneously. The key idea is to prescribe tractable time-dependent conditional distributions and then construct the reference process realizing them as its marginals. This framework reveals that score matching is not fundamental to diffusion-model training but instead emerges naturally through reversal of the reference process. We further show that conditional flow matching arises as the small-noise limit of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [171] [Trajectory-Guided Forget-Recover Network for Continual LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03123)
*Zezheng Wu,Xinghe Cheng,Qinggang Zhang,Haoran Luo,Jiapu Wang,Qing Yang,Jingwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TFR-Net is a machine unlearning method that addresses challenges in continual unlearning by tracking channel-level risk, separating persistent vs transient target-related channels, and recovering model capacity by reactivating dormant channels with low forget risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world continual unlearning scenarios, two key challenges exist: (1) unlearning interventions may redistribute target-related computation across remaining pathways, allowing previously forgotten knowledge to re-emerge, and (2) repeated unlearning interventions progressively reduce model capacity needed to preserve retained utility.

Method: TFR-Net tracks channel-level risk across unlearning requests, separates persistent target-related channels from transient hotspots and suppresses only the persistent ones. It also recovers model capacity by reactivating dormant channels that make strong contributions to retained utility and show low current/historical forget risk, with recovery accepted only when retained-utility degradation remains within predefined tolerance.

Result: Experiments on four datasets show TFR-Net consistently achieves a more favorable trade-off between unlearning effectiveness and retained utility compared to representative baselines.

Conclusion: TFR-Net effectively addresses the challenges of continual machine unlearning by intelligently managing channel-level forgetting and capacity recovery, maintaining a better balance between unlearning effectiveness and preserved utility across multiple unlearning requests.

Abstract: Machine unlearning aims to eliminate the influence of sensitive data on a model. In the real world, unlearning requests arrive continually, which gives rise to two challenges. First, an unlearning intervention may redistribute target-related computation across remaining pathways, allowing previously forgotten knowledge to re-emerge. Second, repeated unlearning interventions may progressively reduce the model capacity needed to preserve retained utility. To address these challenges, we propose the Trajectory-guided Forget-Recover Network (TFR-Net). TFR-Net tracks channel-level risk across requests. It separates persistent target-related channels from transient hotspots and suppresses only the persistent ones. TFR-Net also recovers model capacity by reactivating dormant channels. These channels make strong contributions to retained utility and show low current and historical forget risk. The recovery is accepted only when retained-utility degradation remains within a predefined tolerance. Experiments on four datasets show that TFR-Net consistently achieves a more favorable trade-off between unlearning effectiveness and retained utility than representative baselines.

</details>


### [172] [Lightweight Chunk Selection for Mobile Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03148)
*Sicong Chang,Yidan Shen,Wen Yu,Jiefu Chen,Xin Fu,Renjie Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight RAG chunk selection method that uses LLM query intent, MoE routing signals, and chunk embeddings to align with evidence prototypes for efficient mobile deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG improves factual grounding but is computationally expensive on mobile/edge devices due to retrieved context. Existing context reduction methods require additional LLMs or compressors that are too costly for strict mobile budgets. The top-ranked retrieved chunk is not always the most evidence-supporting, as retrieval similarity doesn't guarantee evidential sufficiency.

Method: Proposes a lightweight selector combining three feature sources: question hidden states (LLM query intent), MoE routing-derived expert signals (generator's internal structure), and retrieved chunk embeddings (candidate evidence geometry). A compact MLP maps features to an evidence prototype in chunk embedding space, selecting the candidate most aligned via cosine similarity. Includes optional task-aware feature selection to reduce input dimensions for stricter budgets.

Result: The proposed selector consistently improves rank-1 evidence selection over mobile-applicable baselines by an average of 2.5%. Evaluation uses semantic chunk-correctness labels based on evidence sufficiency rather than answer-string containment.

Conclusion: Using LLM-side query representations and MoE routing information aligned with retrieval-side candidate embeddings is an effective and parameter-efficient strategy for mobile-applicable RAG chunk selection, offering computational efficiency without sacrificing evidence quality.

Abstract: RAG improves the factual grounding of LLM by incorporating external knowledge, but deploying RAG on mobile and edge devices remains challenging because retrieved context increases computation and memory. A direct way to reduce this cost is to retain only one retrieved chunk before generation, but the top-ranked retrieved chunk is not always the most evidence-supporting one, since retrieval similarity does not necessarily imply evidential sufficiency. Existing context-reduction methods can improve context quality, but often require additional LLMs or compressors that are costly under a strict mobile budget. In this paper, we study lightweight RAG chunk selection as an evidence-alignment problem. Our selector combines three complementary feature sources: question hidden states that represent LLM-side query intent, MoE routing-derived expert signals that capture the generator's internal routing structure, and retrieved chunk embeddings that preserve candidate-side evidence geometry. A compact multilayer perceptron maps these features to an evidence prototype in the chunk embedding space, and the candidate most aligned with this prototype is selected by cosine similarity. For stricter deployment budgets, we further introduce an optional task-aware feature selection strategy to reduce the selector input dimension. To support supervised evaluation, we construct semantic chunk-correctness labels based on evidence sufficiency rather than answer-string containment. Experiments show that the proposed selector consistently improves rank-1 evidence selection over mobile-applicable baselines by an average of 2.5%. These results suggest that using LLM-side query representations and MoE routing information and aligning them with retrieval-side candidate embedding is an effective and parameter-efficient strategy for mobile-applicable RAG chunk selection.

</details>


### [173] [On the Implicit Flatness Bias of Sharpness-Aware Minimization: A Linear Stability Analysis with Quantitative Hyperparameter Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03197)
*Jiaxin Deng,Junbiao Pang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAM's implicit bias toward flat minima is quantitatively explained through linear stability analysis, revealing that stable SAM minima must satisfy Hessian eigenvalue bounds that depend on batch size, learning rate, and perturbation radius.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While SAM improves generalization by seeking parameters robust to local perturbations, the quantitative mechanism underlying its implicit bias toward flat minima remains unclear. The perturbation radius ρ is typically treated as an isolated tuning parameter, despite defining the neighborhood where SAM measures sharpness.

Method: Analyze mini-batch SAM near interpolating minima through linear stability. Under local linearization and gradient-noise alignment assumptions, derive theoretical bounds relating Hessian eigenvalues to hyperparameters. Validate with controlled study of 900 models on CIFAR-100 using ResNet-18 and VGG-19. Implement Taylor-Locality Controlled SAM (TLC-SAM) that adjusts ρ based on Taylor-approximation error.

Result: Proved that linearly stable minima satisfy λ_max ≤ ∛[bΓ/(2ρη²)], quantitatively characterizing SAM's implicit flatness bias. Shows that smaller batch size, larger learning rate, or larger radius restricts SAM to flatter minima. Empirical validation confirms increasing ρ consistently reduces largest Hessian eigenvalue across batch-size and learning-rate settings. TLC-SAM further reduces top Hessian eigenvalue relative to fixed-radius SAM.

Conclusion: The analysis provides quantitative hyperparameter bounds and a stability–locality perspective for analyzing and designing SAM variants, revealing the necessary trade-off between ρ being large enough to promote flatness yet local enough to preserve approximation and stable training.

Abstract: Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) improves generalization by seeking parameters whose loss is robust to local adversarial perturbations, but the quantitative mechanism underlying its implicit bias toward flat minima remains unclear. In particular, the perturbation radius $ρ$ is typically treated as an isolated tuning parameter, despite defining the neighborhood in which SAM measures sharpness. We analyze mini-batch SAM near an interpolating minimum through linear stability. Under local linearization and gradient-noise alignment assumptions, we prove that every linearly stable minimum satisfies $λ_{\max}\leq\sqrt[3]{bΓ/(2ρη^2)}$, where $λ_{\max}$ is the largest Hessian eigenvalue, $b$ is the batch size, $η$ is the learning rate, and $Γ$ bounds the gradient norm. The bound quantitatively characterizes SAM's implicit flatness bias: holding the other quantities fixed, a smaller batch size, a larger learning rate, or a larger radius restricts linearly stable SAM to flatter minima. It also exposes a necessary trade-off: $ρ$ should be large enough to promote flatness, yet remain local enough to preserve the approximation and stable training. We validate this prediction in a controlled study of 900 models on CIFAR-100 with ResNet-18 and VGG-19, where increasing $ρ$ is consistently associated with a smaller largest Hessian eigenvalue across batch-size and learning-rate settings. Finally, we instantiate the analysis in Taylor-Locality Controlled SAM (TLC-SAM), which adjusts $ρ$ using the observed Taylor-approximation error and further reduces the top Hessian eigenvalue relative to fixed-radius SAM. Our results provide quantitative hyperparameter bounds and a stability--locality perspective for analyzing and designing SAM variants.

</details>


### [174] [Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Self-Distilled Reward Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03223)
*Ranxu Zhang,Guinan Chen,Chenshaodong,Jinghao Lin,Xiaozhou Xu,Sunzhe,Yanyong Zhang,Chao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADRS introduces a framework for generating token-level credit in agentic reinforcement learning by distilling privileged skill knowledge into return-aligned rewards, improving performance on long-horizon language tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic RL with LLMs suffers from sparse trajectory-level rewards that don't credit intermediate decisions, while existing methods fail to properly calibrate teacher scores, relate teacher confidence to returns, and integrate signals into native RL credit construction.

Method: ADRS constructs token-level credit by: 1) centering/normalizing privileged token scores within each step, 2) modulating scores with Teacher Value Advantage gate based on confidence-return association, and 3) integrating gated signal into native RL credit construction while keeping rollouts skill-free.

Result: Experiments across three interactive benchmarks show ADRS consistently improves performance on long-horizon tasks, with gains persisting across RL backbones, reduced-data settings, unseen tasks, and extended training.

Conclusion: ADRS effectively addresses sparse reward issues in agentic RL by distilling privileged skill knowledge into return-aligned token-level credit, enabling better credit assignment for intermediate decisions while maintaining skill-free rollouts.

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning enables LLM agents to learn through interaction, but sparse trajectory-level rewards reveal success without identifying which intermediate decisions deserve credit. Training-only privileged skills can provide denser supervision by allowing the same frozen policy snapshot to rescore fixed tokens from skill-free trajectories while conditioned on task-matched procedural skills. Existing methods, however, do not jointly calibrate teacher scores across interaction steps, relate teacher confidence to realized returns, and integrate the resulting signal into native reward-to-advantage construction. We introduce Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Self-Distilled Reward Shaping (ADRS), a framework for constructing return-associated token-level credit for multi-turn language agents. ADRS centers and normalizes privileged token scores within each step, modulates them with a return-associated Teacher Value Advantage (TVA) gate based on within-group confidence--return association, and incorporates the gated token signal into native RL credit construction. Together, these components determine what the teacher prefers, when that preference is return-relevant, and how it enters the native reinforcement-learning credit path, while keeping rollouts and inference skill-free. Finally, experiments across three interactive benchmarks show that ADRS consistently improves performance on long-horizon tasks, with gains persisting across RL backbones, reduced-data settings, unseen tasks, and extended training. For anonymous review, our code is available at the following the link: https://github.com/gitrxh/ADRS-arxiv

</details>


### [175] [SAKI: Score-Aware Low-Rank Key Indexing for Long-Context KV Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03228)
*Lin Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAKI is a training-free KV cache compression method that directly preserves attention scores rather than reconstructing keys, using covariance-weighted SVD for optimal rank reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing low rank KV cache methods focus on preserving model weights or key variance, but neither directly targets the attention scores used during inference. There's a need for methods that minimize attention score distortion for better recall performance.

Method: Derived expected attention score distortion from rank r key compression, showing it yields a covariance-weighted low rank objective. Under margin condition, controlling this distortion improves top k recall. Optimal solution has closed-form asymmetric factorization from SVD of covariance-weighted query key operator, leading to SAKI - a training-free KV cache index.

Result: SAKI outperforms key PCA across multiple models (LLaMA 3.1 8B, Qwen 2.5 7B, Mistral 7B v0.1, Llama 3.2 3B) at every tested rank. At rank 32, reduces 13-30% of PCA's remaining top 64 recall error, improving from 0.748 to 0.799 on LLaMA 3.1 8B and from 0.786 to 0.850 on Qwen 2.5 7B. Improves 68-89% of attention heads per model, with largest gains in deeper layers. Predicted MSE reductions match empirical with Pearson correlation of 0.997.

Conclusion: SAKI demonstrates that directly optimizing attention scores through covariance-weighted low rank approximation outperforms traditional key reconstruction methods, providing a theoretically grounded and effective approach to KV cache compression with significant empirical improvements.

Abstract: Existing low rank KV cache methods preserve either model weights or key variance, neither of which directly reflects the attention scores used during inference. We derive the expected attention score distortion caused by rank r key compression and show that it yields a covariance weighted low rank objective. Under a margin condition, controlling this distortion also improves top k recall. The optimal rank r solution has a closed form asymmetric factorization obtained from the SVD of the covariance weighted query key operator. This motivates SAKI, a training free KV cache index that directly preserves attention scores rather than key reconstruction quality.
  Across LLaMA 3.1 8B, Qwen 2.5 7B, Mistral 7B v0.1, and Llama 3.2 3B, SAKI outperforms key PCA at every tested rank. At rank 32, it removes 13 to 30 percent of PCA's remaining top 64 recall error, including improvements from 0.748 to 0.799 on LLaMA 3.1 8B and from 0.786 to 0.850 on Qwen 2.5 7B. It improves 68 to 89 percent of attention heads per model, with the largest gains in deeper layers. Predicted score MSE reductions closely match empirical measurements, with a Pearson correlation of 0.997, while ablation studies confirm that the gains arise from optimizing the attention score objective rather than covariance weighting alone. Analysis of the scoring operator further explains why weight only, invariant subspace, and key reconstruction methods can be suboptimal.

</details>


### [176] [FinVerse: Financial Time-Series Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03259)
*Jaehoon Lee,Jun Seo,Seunghan Lee,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Minjae Kim,Sungdong Yoo,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FinVerse is a finance-specific time-series forecasting benchmark with domain-aware evaluation metrics designed to assess models based on their real-world decision-making usefulness rather than generic error metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
