Motivation: Existing time-series benchmarks evaluate heterogeneous series with uniform error-based metrics, which don't necessarily reflect real-world decision quality. Strong performance on generic metrics doesn't guarantee useful financial forecasts for practical applications like stock trading.

Method: Created FinVerse benchmark containing 116,897 financial time series with 171.1M observations, selecting 60,232 series as evaluation targets based on economic relevance. Defined 11 metric families (78 metrics) and assigned appropriate evaluation metrics to each series based on underlying economic meaning, rather than using uniform metrics across all series.

Result: Analysis of 43 public time-series foundation models revealed that strong performance under generic forecasting criteria doesn't necessarily translate to useful financial forecasts, highlighting the disconnect between conventional evaluation and real-world decision needs.

Conclusion: Domain-aware benchmarks like FinVerse are needed to evaluate forecasting models under objectives closer to real-world decision making, as generic benchmarks fail to capture the practical utility of forecasts in specific domains like finance.

Abstract: As time-series foundation models have emerged, the need for benchmarks that can evaluate their forecasting ability in meaningful ways has become increasingly important. Existing time-series forecasting benchmarks provide useful standardized comparisons, but they often evaluate heterogeneous series with uniform error-based metrics. Strong performance under such metrics does not necessarily imply that a model's forecasts will support the best real-world decisions across domains. For example, in stock forecasting, correctly predicting whether a price will rise or fall can be more directly relevant to realized returns than minimizing point-wise forecast error alone. To this end, we introduce FinVerse, a finance-domain time-series forecasting benchmark that takes a first step toward more realistic evaluation. The released FinVerse data artifact contains 116,897 financial time series with 171.1M observations, of which 60,232 series with 17.4M observations are selected as evaluated targets based on their economic relevance to financial decisions. Unlike generic forecasting benchmarks that primarily emphasize uniform point-forecast or probabilistic accuracy, FinVerse defines 11 metric families comprising 78 evaluation metrics and assigns the most appropriate evaluation metrics to each individual time series based on its underlying economic meaning. Our analysis of 43 public time-series forecasting foundation models shows that strong performance under generic forecasting criteria does not necessarily translate into useful financial forecasts. This finding highlights the need for domain-aware benchmarks that evaluate models under objectives closer to real-world decision making.

</details>


### [177] [ED-DiT: Physics-Guided Diffusion Pretraining for Transferable Molecular Representations from Electron Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03260)
*Liang Shuang,Haocheng Wang,Jiayi Song,Shuquan Ye,Ben Fei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ED-DiT: A physics-guided Diffusion Transformer for self-supervised pretraining on electron-density point clouds that learns transferable representations for various electronic-structure-related tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electron density provides rich 3D molecular electronic structure information but remains underexplored for self-supervised pretraining. The paper aims to investigate whether electron-density fields can enable transfer learning across diverse electronic-structure tasks, addressing the need for reusable molecular representations especially in data-limited scenarios.

Method: Proposes ED-DiT, a physics-guided Diffusion Transformer that performs self-supervised pretraining on electron-density point clouds. The method reconstructs corrupted and partially masked log-density fields across diffusion noise levels. A key innovation is the electron-number consistency constraint that preserves total electronic mass during pretraining.

Result: Extensive experiments on six EDBench tasks show ED-DiT consistently outperforms same architecture trained from scratch, especially with limited supervision. Achieves significant improvements: reduces RMSE from 2.2474 to 1.3753 for molecule-conditioned density prediction; with only 10% labels, improves orbital energy prediction RMSE from 0.0293 to 0.0138; surpasses available baselines across multiple tasks.

Conclusion: Physics-guided electron-density pretraining effectively learns transferable molecular representations that generalize across diverse electronic-structure tasks. The approach demonstrates strong performance gains particularly in data-limited scenarios, establishing the value of electron-density-based self-supervised learning for molecular representation learning.

Abstract: Pretraining has shown strong potential for learning transferable representations, yet it remains underexplored for electron-density-based molecular learning. Electron density provides a continuous three-dimensional description of molecular electronic structure, capturing both local spatial patterns and global physical quantities. This raises a key question: can electron-density fields be used for self-supervised pretraining to learn a shared representation that transfers across diverse electronic-structure-related tasks? We propose ED-DiT, a physics-guided Diffusion Transformer for self-supervised pretraining on electron-density point clouds. ED-DiT learns reusable representations by reconstructing corrupted and partially masked log-density fields across diffusion noise levels. An electron-number consistency constraint is further introduced to preserve the total electronic mass. The pretrained encoder can be adapted to property prediction, open-/closed-shell classification, molecule-electron-density retrieval, and molecule-conditioned electron-density prediction. Experiments on six EDBench tasks show that ED-DiT consistently outperforms the same architecture trained from scratch, especially under limited supervision. For molecule-conditioned electron-density prediction, it reduces RMSE from 2.2474 to 1.3753 and surpasses the available baseline. With only 10% labels, it improves orbital energy prediction RMSE from 0.0293 to 0.0138. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of physics-guided electron-density pretraining for learning transferable molecular representations.

</details>


### [178] [The Ignition Is Real, and It Lives at the Readout: Latent composition, difficulty-clocked ignition, and the interface-constituted commit in a recurrent-depth reasoner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03263)
*Simon Lam-Muir*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The study confirms that "compositional ignition" in latent-reasoning models represents real computation rather than artifacts or data inheritance, with the effect manifesting at the readout layer rather than hidden states, though some previous claims about hidden-state behavior were not fully confirmed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the reported "compositional ignition" phenomenon in latent-reasoning models represents genuine computational processes, instrument artifacts, or simply patterns inherited from verbal training data, as this distinction is crucial for understanding how these models perform reasoning.

Method: Recreated a published 30M-parameter recurrent-depth reasoner from scratch using the same recipe and seed, monitored its development process, certified fidelity through a pre-registered whole-signature gate, and measured resolution simultaneously in both vocabulary readout and hidden state channels to isolate the source of the ignition effect.

Result: The ignition is real and occurs at the readout layer: arrival time increases systematically with problem depth, resolution is sharp and persistent, and the signature reproduces across different training trajectories. Decision margin jumps 5.8-8.0 logits in one iteration. Hidden state direction changes were observed but fell slightly below statistical thresholds in decoder coordinates, and previous claims about velocity troughs were withdrawn as coordinate-dependent artifacts.

Conclusion: Compositional ignition represents genuine computational emergence in these models, primarily manifesting at the readout layer with lawful depth-dependent timing. While some geometric changes occur in hidden states, the core phenomenon is real computation rather than artifacts or data inheritance, though several previously reported effects were not confirmed under rigorous controlled analysis.

Abstract: We test whether the "compositional ignition" reported in latent-reasoning models is real computation, an instrument artifact, or inherited from verbal training data. We grow an independent realization of a published 30M-parameter recurrent-depth reasoner from scratch (same recipe and seed), film its development, certify fidelity through a pre-registered whole-signature gate, and measure resolution in two channels at once: the vocabulary readout and the hidden state. The ignition is real and lives at the readout: arrival time rises lawfully with problem depth, resolution is sharp and holds, and the signature reproduces across two same-seed realizations with divergent training trajectories. At commitment the decision margin jumps 5.8-8.0 logits in one iteration, exceeding the 90th percentile of near-threshold non-event steps in 96% of cases; the signed margin's zero-crossing there is definitional and carries no evidential weight, so the evidence is that conditioned magnitude. The hidden-state direction snaps in raw geometry, meeting its pre-registered criterion (in the decoder's LayerNorm coordinates it attenuates just below our bar, so the composite decoder-coordinate claim is not confirmed), and then freezes in both (descriptively so in decoder coordinates; angular steps 52.9 to 1.2 degrees over eight iterations), while subsequent displacement is predominantly radial (0.961 of squared-norm) and readout-null to a measured bound (radial logit effect <=5.7e-6). An earlier velocity-trough claim is withdrawn: pre-registered normalization controls showed it coordinate-dependent. Intermediates were never recoverable through the tied readout (relay 0.00). All criteria were frozen before their data; the predictions ledger, including this paper's own withdrawn headline, ships in the companion repository.

</details>


### [179] [Noise-Aware Shrinkage for Differentially Private Zeroth-Order Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03277)
*Lele Zheng,Weifeng Kong,Xinyi Zhang,Ke Cheng,Tao Zhang,Yulong Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE is a noise-aware shrinkage method for differentially private zeroth-order optimization that adaptively attenuates noise-dominated updates based on signal quality estimation, improving model utility without additional privacy costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DP-ZO methods use fixed-scale update reconstruction, ignoring that signal strength varies during training. This causes noise-dominated updates to receive excessive weight, degrading model utility.

Method: Proposes SAGE: estimates signal quality by subtracting known Gaussian noise variance from observed second moment, stabilizes estimate through temporal tracking, compares with warm-up reference to derive bounded shrinkage factor. Pure post-processing requiring no additional privacy budget or queries.

Result: Experiments on RoBERTa-large, OPT-1.3B, and OPT-6.7B show SAGE outperforms existing baselines in most settings under same privacy budgets while maintaining forward-only memory efficiency of DP-ZO.

Conclusion: SAGE effectively addresses noise-dominated update issues in DP-ZO, improving utility without additional privacy costs, making it a practical enhancement for private fine-tuning of large language models.

Abstract: Differentially private zeroth-order optimization (DP-ZO) enables memory-efficient private fine-tuning of large language models using only forward evaluations. Existing aggregation-based DP-ZO methods reconstruct model updates at a fixed scale, ignoring that the strength of useful signals varies throughout training. Consequently, noise-dominated updates may receive excessive weight and degrade model utility. To address this issue, we propose SAGE, a noise-aware shrinkage method that adaptively attenuates privatized estimates according to their estimated signal quality. SAGE subtracts the known Gaussian noise variance from the observed second moment to estimate the underlying signal energy, stabilizes this estimate through temporal tracking, and compares its current signal-to-noise level with a warm-up reference to derive a bounded shrinkage factor. As pure post-processing, SAGE requires neither additional privacy budget nor model queries and introduces only constant additional state. Our theoretical analysis shows that shrinkage reduces the quadratic update-risk term faster than the linear descent term, preserving useful descent while limiting the influence of noise-dominated updates. Experiments on RoBERTa-large, OPT-1.3B, and OPT-6.7B demonstrate that SAGE outperforms existing baselines in most settings under the same privacy budgets while preserving the forward-only memory efficiency of DP-ZO.

</details>


### [180] [The Tell-Tale Trace: Detecting Reasoning Failures in LLMs Using Chain-of-Thought Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03291)
*Shashwat Sourav,Aishwarya Balwani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that analyzing the structural dynamics of chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning—rather than just semantic correctness of individual steps—can diagnose and correct LLM reasoning failures, particularly premature verification collapse on Boolean satisfiability tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CoT evaluation methods focus on semantic correctness of individual reasoning steps, overlooking distributed failures across the reasoning trajectory. Verbalized CoTs also may not faithfully reflect internal model reasoning, creating a need for analysis methods that don't assume semantic faithfulness.

Method: The authors study various LLMs on verifiable Boolean satisfiability (SAT/UNSAT) tasks with variable complexity near each model's capability frontier. They tag CoT sentences by reasoning function and analyze reasoning dynamics, identifying premature verification collapse patterns in incorrect traces.

Result: On SAT problems: incorrect traces enter clause checking earlier, repeat similar operations, and finalize sooner. On UNSAT problems: models move presumptuously toward incorrect SAT conclusions, checking candidate assignments rather than deriving contradictions. A targeted proof-search prompt intervention raised Llama3-70B accuracy from 13.3% to 85%, correcting 84.6% of errors.

Conclusion: Capability failures manifest as distributed, task-dependent changes in visible reasoning structure. CoT dynamics analysis (agnostic to whether verbalized traces reflect internal computations) can effectively diagnose and correct reasoning failures in LLMs.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning improves large language model (LLM) performance while also providing an observable interface to the model's reasoning process. Existing approaches that leverage verbalized CoTs to monitor reasoning correctness, however, largely evaluate the semantic correctness or consistency of individual intermediate steps, rather than how the reasoning process evolves across the trace. As a result, failures distributed across the reasoning trajectory, rather than those localized to a single incorrect step, remain comparatively underexplored. Furthermore, verbalized CoTs need not faithfully reflect the model's internal reasoning, motivating analyses that do not treat individual statements as literal accounts of internal computation. In this work, we therefore ask whether the dynamics of visible CoT can be leveraged to systematically distinguish successful from failed reasoning without assuming such semantic faithfulness. We study a range of LLMs on verifiable Boolean satisfiability tasks with variable complexity, enabling controlled comparisons near each model's capability frontier. Tagging CoT sentences by reasoning function reveals premature verification collapse on SAT problems: incorrect traces enter clause checking earlier, repeat similar operations, and finalize sooner. On UNSAT problems, models presumptuously move towards incorrect SAT conclusions, checking candidate assignments rather than deriving contradictions across constructed cases. Subsequently, a targeted proof-search prompt intervention raises Llama3-70B accuracy from 13.3% to 85%, correcting 84.6% of these errors. These results show that capability failures can manifest as distributed, task-dependent changes in the structure of visible reasoning, and that CoT dynamics agnostic to whether the verbalized trace reflects the model's internal computations can help diagnose and correct failures.

</details>


### [181] [Provably Learning Multi-Head Attention with Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03294)
*Sunyeop Kim,Insung Kim,Jian Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient algorithm for learning multi-head attention parameters from black-box access using exact value queries without subspace assumptions, achieving exact recovery with polynomial query complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work requires pairwise orthogonal subspace assumptions and known subspace bases for multi-head attention learning. This paper aims to learn multi-head attention parameters without these restrictive assumptions, from only black-box input-output access.

Method: Uses rational function interpolation to separate canonical heads by varying token copies, then matches heads across different queries by adding selected token vectors. Recovers canonical representation by merging heads with same W_h and summing corresponding v_h.

Result: Achieves exact recovery of canonical attention parameters up to permutation with probability one using 4Hd²-2H+1 value queries (or 4H₀d²-2H₀+1 with upper bound) of length up to 2H+1. Extends to one-layer Transformers with bias-free ReLU feed-forward networks.

Conclusion: Presents first efficient algorithm for learning multi-head attention without subspace assumptions, with exact recovery guarantee and polynomial query complexity, extending to Transformer architectures.

Abstract: We study the problem of learning multi-head softmax attention from black-box input-output access. The learner may query arbitrary real-valued token sequences and observe only the scalar output at the final token. Recent work gives an algorithm using $O(d^2)$ value queries to recover the single-head parameters $(W,v)$. For multiple heads, the same work establishes identifiability under the assumption that the heads occupy pairwise orthogonal subspaces. Applying the single-head recovery algorithm separately to the heads additionally requires bases for these subspaces to be known. We recover a canonical representation by merging heads with the same $W_h$, summing their corresponding $v_h$, and discarding a merged head when this sum is zero, without these subspace assumptions. By varying the number of copies of a token, our algorithm obtains samples of a rational function whose interpolation separates the canonical heads. Additional queries formed by adding selected token vectors then match the same head across different queries. When the oracle outputs and all subsequent computations are exact, the learner chooses its query vectors at random and recovers the canonical pairs $\{(W_h,v_h):h\in[H]\}$ up to permutation with probability one. When $H$ is known, it uses exactly $4Hd^2-2H+1$ value queries of maximum length $2H+1$. If only a known upper bound $H_0$ is available, the algorithm uses $4H_0d^2-2H_0+1$ value queries of maximum length $2H_0+1$. For approximate oracle outputs, we give conditions under which the parameter error is at most a model- and query-dependent constant multiple of the output error. Finally, we extend our result to a one-layer Transformer with multi-head attention followed by a bias-free ReLU feed-forward network. Under additional conditions, we recover a functionally equivalent Transformer without relying on a separate algorithm for learning the feed-forward network.

</details>


### [182] [Any-OPD: Heterogeneous On-Policy Distillation for Flow-Matching Models via Representation-Space Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03316)
*Siming Fu,Zheming Fu,Ruizhe He,Hualiang Wang,Jie Huang,Xiaoxiao Ma,Mingchen Zhong,Weihu Huang,Xiaoxuan He,Haojun Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Any-OPD is the first framework for on-policy distillation between arbitrary latent flow-matching generators that speak different "languages" (different VAEs, architectures, timestep grids), treating the teacher as a black-box sampler and using a model-agnostic vision representation for comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy distillation assumes identical VAE latents, matching architectures, and common timestep grids between teacher and student. This fails when deploying a student from a different model family than the strongest available teacher, as teacher latents can't serve as targets in foreign coordinate systems, per-pixel losses degenerate, and timestep indices lose meaning across mismatched schedules.

Method: Treats teacher as black-box sampler and connects models at exactly one point: a frozen, model-agnostic vision representation where independently decoded outputs are compared. Recovers trajectory correspondence by matching continuous noise levels instead of step indices, with an anchoring phase where teacher samples are re-encoded through student's own VAE to ensure on-policy gradient measures sample quality rather than domain mismatch.

Result: Distilling 12B FLUX.1-dev into 2.5B SD3.5-Medium lifts student's PickScore from 0.846 to 0.884 and HPSv3 from 9.12 to 10.97, rivaling teacher at a fifth of its size, where direct latent regression fails to train at all.

Conclusion: Any-OPD successfully enables cross-family distillation where standard methods fail, making teacher correction possible even when models use incompatible latents, architectures, and timestep grids by using vision-space comparison and noise-level matching.

Abstract: On-policy distillation, in which a teacher corrects samples that the student itself generates, presupposes that the two models speak the same language: identical VAE latents, matching architectures, and a common timestep grid. We ask what happens when none of this holds, as when the strongest teacher available and the student one wishes to deploy come from different model families, and find that the standard recipes have no answer: teacher latents cannot serve as targets in a foreign coordinate system, per-pixel losses against a teacher that stochastically re-draws local detail degenerate into blur or divergence, and timestep indices lose their meaning across mismatched schedules. We present Any-OPD, to our knowledge the first framework for on-policy distillation between arbitrary pairs of latent flow-matching generators. Any-OPD treats the teacher purely as a black-box sampler and connects the two models at exactly one point: a frozen, model-agnostic vision representation in which their independently decoded outputs are compared, sidestepping every assumption about latents, features, or architecture. Trajectory correspondence is recovered by matching continuous noise levels instead of step indices, and a brief anchoring phase, in which teacher samples are re-encoded through the student's own VAE, ensures the on-policy gradient measures sample quality rather than domain mismatch. Distilling the 12B FLUX.1-dev into the 2.5B SD3.5-Medium, Any-OPD lifts the student's PickScore from 0.846 to 0.884 and HPSv3 from 9.12 to 10.97, rivaling the teacher at a fifth of its size, where direct latent regression fails to train at all.

</details>


### [183] [AS-FedBridge: Pseudo-Spike Bridge Distillation for Heterogeneous ANN-SNN Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03324)
*Shengyang Li,Yiting Dong,Liuyang Song,Ximing Wang,Luyuan Xie,Cong Li,Qingni Shen,Zhaofei Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AS-FedBridge is a federated learning framework that bridges the representational gap between ANN and SNN clients using a lightweight Bridge with Pseudo-Spike Interface, enabling efficient mixed ANN-SNN federated learning with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning on edge devices needs energy-efficient SNNs, but mixed ANN-SNN training faces representational misalignment due to semantic gaps between continuous ANN activations and discrete SNN spikes.

Method: Proposed AS-FedBridge framework with a lightweight Bridge equipped with Pseudo-Spike Interface that projects continuous ANN signals into spike-compatible space for ANN-SNN alignment.

Result: Showed positive correlation between ANN-SNN alignment and FL performance; achieved advanced accuracy across four datasets while mitigating heterogeneity challenges; enabled controllable performance-resource trade-off with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: AS-FedBridge establishes a practical foundation for mixed ANN-SNN federated learning by effectively bridging the representational gap while maintaining efficiency and robustness against heterogeneity.

Abstract: Federated learning enables collaborative model training across distributed edge devices while strictly preserving data privacy. To facilitate practical deployment on resource-constrained edge devices, Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) have emerged as a promising alternative to traditional Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) due to their sparse computing mechanisms and high energy efficiency. However, jointly training ANNs and SNNs exposes a challenge of representational misalignment, which is intrinsically caused by differences in information representation, specifically the semantic gap between continuous real-valued activations in ANNs and discrete spatio-temporal spikes in SNNs. To overcome this barrier, we propose AS-FedBridge, a novel federated learning framework tailored for mixed ANN-SNN clients. AS-FedBridge features a lightweight Bridge equipped with a Pseudo-Spike Interface, which effectively projects continuous signals into a spike-compatible space to facilitate ANN-SNN alignment. Given the absence of existing mixed ANN-SNN federated frameworks, we establish a comprehensive benchmark to evaluate against multiple advanced heterogeneous FL methods. Our empirical analysis demonstrates a positive correlation between the degree of ANN-SNN alignment and the collaborative FL performance. Across four datasets, AS-FedBridge consistently demonstrates advanced accuracy while mitigating extreme scale, architecture, and client heterogeneity challenge. Furthermore, our framework enables a highly controllable trade-off between model performance and resource efficiency. AS-FedBridge accomplishes these robust performance gains while introducing only marginal computational overhead, establishing a robust and practical foundation for mixed ANN-SNN federated learning systems.

</details>


### [184] [Tight Worst-Case Bounds for the Smallest Eigenvalue of ReLU NTK Gram Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03368)
*Zhao Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes tight dimension-free bounds on the smallest eigenvalue of the continuous ReLU derivative Gram matrix for unit vectors, showing it scales as Ω(Δ_±/√log n) and O(Δ_±/√log n).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the spectral properties of ReLU derivative Gram matrices, which are important in deep learning theory (e.g., neural tangent kernel analysis) and kernel methods. The authors want to characterize how the minimum eigenvalue depends on the geometric separation of input vectors.

Method: The authors study the continuous ReLU derivative Gram matrix H defined by averaging pairwise gated inner products over a Gaussian direction. They prove a universal dimension-free lower bound using mathematical analysis techniques, and construct worst-case examples to show the bound is tight. They work with projective separation Δ_± which measures minimum distance between vectors or their negatives.

Result: The main result is the tight bound λ_min(H) = Θ(Δ_±/√log n) up to universal constants, establishing both a lower bound of Ω(Δ_±/√log n) and a matching upper bound via worst-case constructions. This shows the scaling with projective separation and log of n is optimal.

Conclusion: The paper provides a complete characterization of the minimum eigenvalue of ReLU derivative Gram matrices, showing it scales with projective separation divided by √log n. This has implications for optimization in deep learning and kernel methods, indicating how input geometry affects optimization landscape properties.

Abstract: For $n$ unit vectors $x_1,\ldots,x_n \in \mathbb{R}^d$, we study the continuous ReLU derivative Gram matrix $H$, whose entries are obtained by averaging pairwise gated inner products over a standard Gaussian direction. Writing $ Δ_\pm := \min_{i \neq j} \min\{ \|x_i-x_j\|_2, \|x_i+x_j\|_2 \} $ for their projective separation, we prove the universal dimension-free lower bound $ λ_{\min}(H) = Ω( Δ_\pm/\sqrt{\log n} ) $. Conversely, we construct worst-case families satisfying the matching upper bound $ λ_{\min}(H) = O( Δ_\pm/\sqrt{\log n} ) $, showing that this rate is tight up to universal constants.

</details>


### [185] [Benign interpolation and Occam's razor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03386)
*Tom F. Sterkenburg,Daniel A. Herrmann,Jan-Willem Romeijn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A philosophical critique of how contemporary machine learning explanations for "benign interpolation" use the concept of "simplicity" as an explanatory placeholder without proper theoretical foundations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to clarify debates about why deep learning models generalize well even when perfectly fitting training data (benign interpolation), which contradicts classical statistical learning theory. It examines how new explanations appeal to simplicity preferences but lack proper theoretical justification.

Method: Philosophical analysis comparing classical statistical learning theory with contemporary machine learning explanations. The method involves conceptual clarification of what "simplicity" means in both contexts and examining the logical structure of arguments connecting simplicity to generalization.

Result: Identifies an explanatory gap: classical theory provides theorems connecting model class simplicity to generalization, while contemporary explanations merely label individual model properties as "simplicity" without proving connections to generalization. Shows that "simplicity" serves as a rhetorical placeholder rather than a theoretically justified principle.

Conclusion: The contemporary appeal to simplicity in explaining benign interpolation is philosophically problematic - it uses the name "simplicity" to do the work that theorems used to do, making unsubstantiated assumptions appear as applications of a familiar methodological principle, thus creating an explanatory gap that needs addressing.

Abstract: Contemporary deep learning methods generalize well even when they fit their training data perfectly, a phenomenon known as benign interpolation. This phenomenon cannot be accounted for by classical statistical learning theory and has prompted a range of attempted new explanations in the statistics and machine learning literature. A common feature of these new proposals is an appeal to a simplicity preference among interpolating models, often presented as a form of Occam's razor. We clarify this debate for a philosophical audience and argue that this new appeal to simplicity creates an explanatory gap. The classical theory offers theorems which connect the simplicity of model classes to good generalization, thus underwriting methodological simplicity norms. The new accounts instead appeal to properties of individual models, which they interpret as a kind of simplicity. Lacking a provable connection to generalization, it is the name "simplicity" that does the work a theorem used to do, making a substantive and unargued assumption look like the application of a familiar methodological principle.

</details>


### [186] [TimeRLM: Recursive Language Models Enable Precise Anomaly Localization in Long-Context Time-Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03391)
*Nicolas Zumarraga,Lorenzo Steno,Ning Wang,Max Rosenblattl,Thomas Kaar,Maxwell A. Xu,Kevin O'Sullivan,Markus Kreft,Elgar Fleisch,Paul Schmiedmayer,Patrick Langer,Robert Jakob*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TimeRLM applies recursive language model architecture to time-series anomaly detection, outperforming traditional TSLMs on long-context localization tasks through code-based interaction with external time-series data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise anomaly localization in long-context time series is challenging across critical monitoring applications. Traditional Time-Series Language Models (TSLMs) suffer performance degradation at long contexts, similar to issues seen in other domains like text and vision.

Method: TimeRLM uses a Recursive Language Model (RLM) formulation for time-series that keeps context external to the LLM. It sequentially manipulates time-series signals using code and vision capabilities, allowing the model to query and interact with data through code generation.

Result: TimeRLM outperforms all evaluated TSLMs and baselines on 4 out of 5 AnomalyXL-Localize tasks, achieving 0.682 IoU on localization vs. 0.329 max for baselines. Post-training with RL further improves performance while reducing required interaction turns by two-thirds. The model maintains or improves performance on real-world ECG, sleep, and software data despite being trained only on synthetic data.

Conclusion: Recursive interaction with time-series is an effective approach for long-horizon retrieval tasks. TimeRLM demonstrates that keeping context external and using code-based querying significantly improves anomaly localization performance, and RL post-training enhances efficiency and generalization to real-world data.

Abstract: Precise anomaly localization over long-context time series is a crucial task in monitoring applications across clinical care, industrial operations, financial services, and logistics, where brief evidence may hide inside long spans of high-frequency data. Time-Series Language Models (TSLMs) are able to ingest time series data and verbalize findings on anomalies in natural language; however, recent benchmarks report a decrease in retrieval performance at long contexts, mirroring failure modes in text, vision, and audio. In the text domain, Recursive Language Models (RLMs) can recover much of this lost performance by keeping context external to the large language model (LLM), allowing the model to query it through code. We present TimeRLM, an RLM formulation for time-series that sequentially manipulates the signal using code and vision capabilities. We further introduce AnomalyXL, a synthetic long-context anomaly localization benchmark with programmatically injected anomalies that require precise retrieval. We implement five different task categories and two variants: AnomalyXL-MCQ and AnomalyXL-Localize. TimeRLM outperforms every evaluated TSLM and single-pass baseline on four of the five AnomalyXL-Localize tasks, reaching 0.682 IoU on localization and 0.745 on classify-with-evidence, versus at most 0.329 and 0.072 across all baselines. We post-train TimeRLM using reinforcement learning. The resulting model further improves performance and requires approximately one-third as many agent interaction turns as its untrained base model to produce a final answer. On unseen real-world ECG, sleep and software observability recordings, the post-trained TimeRLM retains or improves performance, surpassing TSLMs despite being trained exclusively on synthetic data. Our findings suggest recursive interaction with time-series is an effective approach for long-horizon retrieval.

</details>


### [187] [Shorter Reasoning, Earlier Answers? An Evaluation of Reasoning Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03401)
*Francesca Carlon,Vincent Ginis,Andres Algaba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Paper evaluates how shortening reasoning traces affects LLM accuracy on complex reasoning tasks, finding that early stopping or concise prompts can improve performance under tight token limits, but high-effort reasoning often achieves higher final accuracy if allowed to finish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long reasoning traces in LLMs increase computational cost and latency. While prompts and settings can shorten reasoning, it's unclear whether shorter traces simply stop earlier or actually improve efficiency while maintaining accuracy.

Method: Compared paired runs on same questions at matched reasoning horizons using 198 GPQA Diamond and 500 MMLU-Pro questions. Tested Qwen3-14B with numeric/concision prompts announcing token limits, and gpt-oss-20b/-120b with trained effort settings.

Result: Qwen prompts shortened traces by 12-17% with mixed accuracy changes. Concise instructions raised MMLU-Pro accuracy by 3.8pp at 512 tokens. GPT-OSS low/medium-effort reasoning showed 14.5-26.3pp higher accuracy than matched-horizon high-effort. Early stopping helps under tight deadlines but high effort finishes stronger.

Conclusion: Tight deadlines favor lower effort or concise instructions, but high-effort reasoning can recover higher final accuracy if allowed to finish. Evaluations should report multiple metrics: correct completion before deadline, stopped-run answers, unfinished-run differences, and correct answer probability.

Abstract: Large language models often reason at length before answering, increasing cost and latency. Prompts and trained settings can shorten this reasoning, but a shorter trace may only show that the model stopped sooner. Here, we evaluate paired runs of the same question at matched reasoning horizons across 198 GPQA Diamond and 500 MMLU-Pro questions. We test a numeric/concision prompt that announces a token limit for Qwen3-14B and the trained effort settings of gpt-oss-20b and -120b. The Qwen prompt shortens reasoning traces by 12-17%, while accuracy changes at matched token limits are small and mixed. A concise/early-answer instruction raises MMLU-Pro accuracy by 3.8 percentage points at 512 tokens, including +2.7 points when both runs are unfinished. Its gain at 2,048 tokens is uncertain. For gpt-oss, candidate-logit answers from completed low- and medium-effort reasoning are 14.5-26.3 points more accurate than matched-horizon high-effort answers. Most of the 512-token advantage comes from lower effort finishing earlier, while differences among unfinished runs are smaller and mixed. Wrong early answers often concentrate probability on the chosen option, so earlier stopping does not uniformly improve probability quality. In these tests, a tight deadline can favor lower effort or a concise instruction, whereas allowing high effort to finish can recover higher final accuracy. Evaluations should report correct completion before a deadline, the answer obtained when a run is stopped, differences among unfinished runs, and probability assigned to the correct answer separately.

</details>


### [188] [Stop Replacing Noise with Noise: Two-Source Reliability Assessment for Label Correction and Sample Reweighting in Label-Noise Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03432)
*Wenxiao Fan,Kan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE decouples reliability assessment of observed labels and pseudo-targets in noisy-label learning, using separate scores for each source to avoid the complementarity problem in existing refurbishment methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current refurbishment-based noisy-label learning methods use a single cleanliness score that creates a hidden coupling: reducing trust in the observed label automatically increases trust in the pseudo target. This is problematic because pseudo targets learned from corrupted supervision may reproduce the noise they're meant to correct.

Method: TRACE (Two-Source Reliability Assessment framework for Label Correction and Sample Reweighting) assesses observed labels using three criteria: loss fit, shallow relation stability, and prediction agreement. It separately assesses pseudo targets using model confidence. These source-specific scores independently control target correction and supervision strength without assuming complementary reliability.

Result: TRACE improves representative refurbishment baselines across both synthetic and real-world noisy benchmarks, yielding more reliable pseudo supervision.

Conclusion: Decoupling reliability assessment of observed labels and pseudo-targets through separate scoring mechanisms addresses the fundamental complementarity problem in noisy-label learning, leading to more robust performance when handling corrupted supervision.

Abstract: Refurbishment-based noisy-label learning mixes an observed label with a model-derived pseudo target, typically using one sample-wise cleanliness score to control both branches. This creates a hidden coupling: reducing trust in the observed label automatically increases trust in the pseudo target. We show that this complementarity can replace one unreliable signal with another because a pseudo target learned from corrupted supervision may reproduce the noise it is meant to correct. Our representation diagnostics provide a consistent account of this mismatch: noisy supervision redirects deeper layers more strongly, whereas shallower relations remain comparatively stable and provide information beyond the loss posterior. We therefore propose TRACE, a Two-Source Reliability Assessment framework for Label Correction and Sample Reweighting. TRACE assesses the observed label using loss fit, shallow relation stability, and prediction agreement, while separately assessing the pseudo target using model confidence. Its source-specific scores control target correction and supervision strength without assuming complementary reliability. Across synthetic and real-world noisy benchmarks, TRACE improves representative refurbishment baselines and yields more reliable pseudo supervision.

</details>


### [189] [Approximate Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03447)
*Yuannuo Feng,Zegang Peng,Yuxin Xie,Yubing Ye,Yizhe Chen,Wenshuai Yao,Wenyong Zhou,Wang Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ASD improves speculative decoding by allowing budgeted acceptance of mismatched draft tokens to reuse more target-greedy suffix, boosting throughput 3-15% without training or new models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard greedy verification in speculative decoding stops at the first mismatched draft token, discarding potentially reusable target-scored suffix tokens that remain greedy under the realized prefix.

Method: ASD replaces binary first-mismatch truncation with budgeted longest-prefix selection using: 1) local target-logit regret gate, 2) per-block exception cap, and 3) persistent request-level regret budget to accept selected mismatches.

Result: ASD improves fixed-workload throughput by 3.05%-15.26% over strict verification, with 7.78% average gain across seven Qwen3-14B + DSpark-14B tasks. On DeepSeek-V4-Flash with DSpark, it increases verifier-side acceptance by ~10%-16% on GSM8K and MATH-500.

Conclusion: ASD is a training-free verifier that significantly improves speculative decoding efficiency by intelligently accepting mismatches within budget constraints, reducing to standard verification when budget is zero, requiring no model changes or fine-tuning.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by verifying a draft block with a target model in parallel. Under standard greedy verification, decoding stops at the first draft token that differs from the target argmax, discarding the remaining target-scored suffix. Although accepting such a mismatch changes the decoding trajectory, it can make a contiguous suffix reusable when its tokens remain target-greedy under the realized prefix. In this paper, we introduce \textbf{Approximate Speculative Decoding (ASD)}, a training-free verifier that replaces binary first-mismatch truncation with budgeted longest-prefix selection. ASD accepts selected mismatches subject to a local target-logit regret gate, a per-block exception cap, and a persistent request-level regret budget, then reuses the contiguous target-greedy suffix without additional approximate decisions or target-model forward passes. ASD requires neither a new draft model nor fine-tuning, and exactly reduces to standard greedy verification when the budget is zero. Experiments show that ASD improves fixed-workload throughput by $3.05\%$--$15.26\%$ over matched strict verification and averages a $7.78\%$ gain across seven Qwen3-14B + DSpark-14B tasks. On DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B) with DSpark it also raises verifier-side acceptance by roughly $10\%$--$16\%$ on GSM8K and MATH-500 in an FP4-to-FP8 compatibility setting. The source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/Kissmetothemoon/ASD

</details>


### [190] [Beyond the Gegenbauer Paradigm: q-Orthogonal Kernels for Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03482)
*Álvaro Sánchez-Paniagua Ríos,Juan P. Llerena,Alberto Lastra,Nuria Torrado,Edmundo J. Huertas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel family of q-Hermite orthogonal polynomial kernels for SVMs that achieve competitive performance while offering mathematical interpretability, numerical stability, and potential for quantum computing applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SVMs heavily depend on kernel function choice, with classical kernels like RBF being popular but orthogonal polynomial kernels offering mathematically interpretable alternatives that can incorporate structured prior knowledge. There's a need for kernels that combine mathematical elegance with practical performance.

Method: Extends orthogonal polynomial kernel paradigm by introducing a novel family based on discrete q-Hermite I polynomials, which generalize classical Hermite polynomials through a deformation parameter q. Formally defines the q-Hermite kernel and establishes its validity under Mercer's theorem, leveraging the kernel's inherent boundedness properties that prevent annihilation and explosion effects without explicit scaling mechanisms.

Result: Extensive experiments across 20 benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive performance compared to both classical kernels and other orthogonal polynomial kernels, with advantages in numerical stability and computational simplicity. The kernel shows practical effectiveness while maintaining mathematical interpretability.

Conclusion: q-orthogonal polynomials constitute a promising direction for kernel design, bridging mathematical elegance with practical machine learning applications, and provide conceptual/algorithmic resources that may be further extended to emerging quantum computing paradigms. The work offers open-source implementation for reproducibility.

Abstract: The performance of Support Vector Machines (SVMs) critically depends on the kernel function choice, which enables implicit mapping of data into high-dimensional feature spaces. While classical kernels like Radial Basis Function (RBF) remain popular, orthogonal polynomial kernels offer mathematically interpretable alternatives that can incorporate structured prior knowledge. This work extends the orthogonal polynomial kernel paradigm by introducing a novel family based on discrete $q$-Hermite I polynomials, a class of $q$-orthogonal polynomials that generalize classical Hermite polynomials through a deformation parameter $q$. We formally define the q-Hermite kernel and establish its validity under Mercer's theorem. The kernel's inherent boundedness properties naturally prevent annihilation and explosion effects without requiring explicit scaling mechanisms. Extensive experiments across 20 benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed kernel achieves competitive performance compared to both classical kernels and other orthogonal polynomial kernels, while offering advantages in numerical stability and computational simplicity. Our results confirm that $q$-orthogonal polynomials constitute a promising direction for kernel design, bridging mathematical elegance with practical machine learning applications, that provides conceptual and algorithmic resources that may be further extended to emerging quantum computing paradigms. To facilitate full reproducibility, we provide the complete implementation and experimental pipeline in an open-access GitHub repository at https://github.com/Kokechacho/SVMs-QSVMs.

</details>


### [191] [FedCARE: A Multi-Objective Personalised Federated Learning Framework for Smart Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03498)
*Rojalini Tripathy,Padmalochan Bera,Shreya Ghosh,Rajkumar Buyya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCARE is a multi-objective personalized FL framework that addresses healthcare-specific challenges of heterogeneous clinical objectives and partially overlapping feature spaces through a two-stage approach combining Pareto-driven global optimization with client-specific fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world healthcare FL faces challenges beyond non-IID data, including heterogeneous clinical objectives (different hospitals optimizing distinct metrics like mortality risk, readmission reduction, etc.) and partially overlapping feature spaces where institutions have private clinical features they cannot share. Existing FL methods don't adequately handle both multi-objective optimization and personalization simultaneously.

Method: Two-stage training: (1) Learns a shared global backbone from common clinical features using Pareto-driven multi-objective federated optimization; (2) Each client independently fine-tunes the shared backbone using private features and local clinical objectives for institution-specific personalization without additional communication overhead.

Result: FedCARE consistently outperforms standard FL (FedAvg), multi-objective FL, and personalized FL baselines on real-world healthcare datasets MIMIC-III and Diabetes 130-US Hospitals, achieving up to 12.5% AUROC improvement and 32.0% MAE reduction over FedAvg.

Conclusion: FedCARE effectively addresses the dual challenges of multi-objective optimization and personalization in healthcare FL through its innovative two-stage approach, demonstrating superior performance on real-world clinical datasets while respecting data privacy and institutional specificity requirements.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across distributed healthcare institutions without centralising sensitive patient data. However, real-world healthcare federations are often characterised not only by non-IID data, but also by heterogeneous clinical objectives and partially overlapping feature spaces. Different hospitals may optimise distinct and potentially conflicting objectives, such as mortality risk prediction, readmission reduction, or length-of-stay estimation, while also retaining institution-specific clinical features that cannot be shared with other participants. Existing personalised FL methods mainly address statistical heterogeneity, whereas multi-objective FL approaches typically learn a shared global model without explicit client-level adaptation. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{FedCARE}, a multi-objective personalised FL framework for smart healthcare services. FedCARE follows a two-stage training strategy. First, it learns a shared global backbone from common clinical features using Pareto-driven multi-objective federated optimisation. Second, each client independently fine-tunes the shared backbone using its private features and local clinical objectives, enabling institution-specific personalisation without additional communication overhead. We implement FedCARE in a cloud-based client-server federated deployment on the Melbourne Research Cloud and evaluate it on two real-world healthcare datasets, MIMIC-III and Diabetes 130-US Hospitals. Experimental results show that FedCARE consistently outperforms standard FL, multi-objective FL, and personalised FL baselines, achieving up to 12.5% AUROC improvement and 32.0% MAE reduction over FedAvg.

</details>


### [192] [Robust General Utility for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03562)
*Zixuan Liu,Fangzheng Wu,Brian Summa,Zizhan Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces robust general-utility RL, a minimax framework that trains policies against potential utility misspecification during deployment, extending classic RL to handle uncertainty in utility functions while providing unified theoretical guarantees and algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reinforcement learning with general utility assumes fixed, correctly specified utility functions during training, but in practice, deployment utilities can differ from training ones, creating a robustness gap that existing methods don't address.

Method: The authors propose a minimax learning framework that trains policies to be robust against utility misspecification within a prescribed uncertainty set. They develop two provably convergent stochastic algorithms: for concave utilities, a projected stochastic gradient descent-ascent method; for nonconcave utilities, a stochastic prox-extragradient algorithm.

Result: The framework strictly generalizes standard general-utility RL and unifies existing RL frameworks like reward-robust RL and constrained RL. Theoretical convergence guarantees are established: stationarity guarantees for concave utilities and first-order stationarity guarantees for nonconcave utilities. Experiments on LLM safety alignment and exploration maximization show convergence behavior consistent with theory.

Conclusion: The proposed robust general-utility RL framework effectively addresses the practical problem of utility misspecification between training and deployment, providing both theoretical guarantees and practical algorithms while unifying multiple existing RL paradigms through a single framework.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) with general utility extends classic RL by optimizing an arbitrary utility functional of the policy-induced occupancy measure, thereby enabling a broader range of applications. However, previous work on general utility RL typically assumes the evaluation utility is fixed and correctly specified. In practice, the utility used at deployment can deviate from the training one, creating a robustness gap that prior work does not address. Motivated by this, we propose robust general-utility RL, a minimax learning framework that trains policies against utility misspecification within a prescribed uncertainty set. Our framework strictly generalizes standard general-utility RL while also providing a unified view of many existing RL frameworks, including reward-robust RL and constrained RL, through appropriate choices of the utility uncertainty set. We further develop provably convergent stochastic algorithms for two regimes. For concave utilities, we develop a projected stochastic gradient descent-ascent method and establish stationarity guarantees. For the more challenging nonconcave regime, we propose a stochastic prox-extragradient algorithm that mitigates ill-posed behavior induced by nonconcavity, with convergence guarantees to approximate first-order stationarity. Experiments on LLM safety alignment and exploration maximization tasks further corroborate the convergence behavior consistent with our theory.

</details>


### [193] [Pin Once, Swap Light: Subspace-Aligned Centroid-Residual Training for Efficient Ultra-LoRA Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03579)
*Xiang Li,Pengcheng Wang,Huazheng Wang,Saurabh Bagchi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SALT proposes a hierarchical LoRA fine-tuning framework that uses domain centroids with ultra-low-rank task residuals to achieve high task performance while significantly improving serving efficiency through reduced memory footprint and PCIe transfer overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-tenant LoRA serving systems face a critical trade-off: high-rank adapters provide better task performance but have large GPU VRAM footprint and PCIe swapping overhead that limit scalability, while ultra-low-rank adapters (r≤2) reduce memory/overhead but suffer from performance degradation.

Method: Three-phase hierarchical framework: 1) Provider jointly trains high-capacity domain centroids on public domain data using an alignment regularizer to cohere task subspaces; 2) Users fine-tune ultra-low-rank task residual adapters on private data atop frozen centroids; 3) Inference: centroid pinned in GPU VRAM, task residuals dynamically swapped on demand.

Result: Across various LLM scales, SALT recovers high-rank accuracy using r≤2 residuals, achieving up to 18.5% absolute accuracy gains over state-of-the-art compression baselines and reducing per-adapter memory by up to 16x. Integrated into vLLM, improves serving throughput by up to 51% under PCIe pressure and 28% under GPU VRAM constraints for Llama-3.2-3B.

Conclusion: SALT successfully resolves the serving efficiency vs. task performance dilemma by decoupling domain knowledge (centroids) from task-specific information (residuals), enabling efficient multi-tenant LoRA serving with minimal performance degradation.

Abstract: Modern multi-tenant Low-Rank Adapters (LoRAs) serving systems concurrently host tens to hundreds of LoRA adapters. Though powerful, this introduces a critical system dilemma between serving efficiency and task performance: higher-rank adapters generally achieve better downstream task performance, but their GPU VRAM footprint and Host-to-Device PCIe swapping overhead severely constrain scalability. Conversely, ultra-low-rank adapters ($r \le 2$) minimize both VRAM footprint and PCIe transfer overhead, but suffer from downstream task performance degradation. To solve this problem, we propose Subspace-Aligned LoRA Training (SALT), a serving efficiency-aware hierarchical fine-tuning framework. Our solution operates in three phases. First, a provider jointly trains high-capacity domain centroids on public data within the domain using a novel alignment regularizer that coheres in-domain task subspaces into a unified basis. Next, users fine-tune ultra-low-rank task residual adapters on private data atop those frozen centroids. Finally, during inference, the provider pins the centroid in GPU VRAM and dynamically swaps in each user's task residual on demand. Across LLMs of varying scales, SALT recovers high-rank accuracy using $r \le 2$ residuals, achieving up to 18.5% absolute accuracy gains over state-of-the-art compression baselines and reducing per-adapter memory by up to 16x. When integrated into vLLM, SALT improves serving throughput by up to 51% under PCIe bandwidth pressure and 28% under GPU VRAM constraints for Llama-3.2-3B.

</details>


### [194] [Design-Time Optimization of Deep Neural Networks for Intermittent Learning on Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03589)
*Jakob Schubert,Maximilian Kasper,Maximilian Linke,Benedict Herzog,Mark Deutel,Axel Plinge,Dominik Seuss,Christopher Mutschler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method for designing DNNs for intermittent, energy-autonomous learning on MCUs using hardware-aware energy prediction with multi-objective optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile applications with energy-harvesting (e.g., solar-powered) face interruptions in AI learning when energy runs out, making on-device learning unreliable on MCUs.

Method: Combines hardware-aware energy prediction model with multi-objective optimization to estimate per-layer energy consumption for both inference and training, including intermittent checkpointing overhead, based on DNN compute and memory features.

Result: Energy predictor achieves 16.6% weighted absolute percentage error, enabling reliable architecture selection under intermittency constraints; validated with autoencoders for anomaly detection on Cortex-M4 MCU.

Conclusion: Bridges gap between multi-objective optimization, automated DNN design, deployment on energy-harvesting systems, and intermittent learning, enabling truly autonomous AI at the edge.

Abstract: We present a method for designing deep neural networks (DNNs) for intermittent, energy-autonomous, on-device learning on microcontroller units (MCUs). In mobile applications where the energy can run out, e.g., when solar-powered, executing artificial intelligence (AI) faces a technical issue as learning can be interrupted at any time. Our approach combines a hardware-aware energy prediction model with multi-objective optimization (MOO), enabling offline DNN optimization at the design stage without repeated deployment and online testing on the target MCU. Our proposed energy predictor estimates per-layer energy consumption for both DNN inference and training, including the intermittent checkpointing overhead, based on implementation-specific compute and memory features extracted from the DNN model. We validate our approach using autoencoders for anomaly detection on a Cortex-M4 MCU, where our predictor achieves a weighted absolute percentage error of 16.6%, which is sufficient for reliable architecture selection under intermittency constraints. As a result, this work bridges the gap between MOO, automated DNN design, deployment on energy-harvesting systems, and intermittent learning, truly enabling autonomous AI at the edge.

</details>


### [195] [A Theory of Conditional Collapse under Low-Rank Weight-Space Ablations: I. The Single-Block Theory and Synthetic Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03620)
*Abdallah Khemais*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates when activation patching and weight-space ablation agree on causal responsibility, showing they can disagree fundamentally in idealized models and small transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when activation patching and weight-space ablation - two different causal attribution methods that operate on different objects (forward pass vs parameters) - actually agree on which components are causally responsible for model behaviors.

Method: Studies an idealized model with additive conditional computation through residual streams, proves three exact mathematical results about deletion/patching/ablation, validates with small transformers trained on synthetic conditional tasks, and tests against a real pretrained model in a companion paper.

Result: 1) Exact conditions for deletion symmetry; 2) Patching moves readout by contrast while ablation moves by absolute level - neither bounds the other; 3) First-order interaction formula for attention head compositions; 4) Strong correlation (-0.83 Spearman) between idealized model predictions and measured interactions in transformers; 5) Results extend beyond single residual blocks.

Conclusion: Activation patching and weight-space ablation can fundamentally disagree about causal responsibility due to different mathematical properties (contrast vs absolute level), and the paper provides theoretical foundations and empirical validation showing when and why these methods diverge.

Abstract: Activation patching and weight-space ablation both claim a component is causally responsible for a behavior, yet they act on different objects: one forward pass versus the parameters behind every forward pass. We ask when they agree.
  We study an idealized model where a conditional computation is carried additively through a residual stream, $F(x)=F_0(x)+\sum_iα_i(x)v_i$, read out by a linear functional, and prove three exact results. First, deleting a subset of carriers collapses a matched input pair onto the same unconditional output \emph{if and only if} the removal is symmetric on the pair and leaves no outside contrast; the error is deterministic, and we give its exact form even when the two conditions hold only approximately. Second, patching a carrier moves the readout by its donor-receiver \emph{contrast}, while ablating it moves the readout by its \emph{absolute level}; neither bounds the other, and we construct pairs where every single-carrier patch flips the decision while no single-carrier ablation does. Third, for an attention head composed with its own layer's normalization and MLP, we derive an exact first-order interaction formula with a provably second-order remainder, vanishing identically when only the MLP is ablated but not, in general, when a head is.
  Small transformers trained on a synthetic conditional task illustrate all three predictions: across thirty-nine ablation configurations the measured interaction is strongly rank-correlated with the idealized model's predictive accuracy (Spearman $-0.83$), and a second task and architecture reproduces the same pattern, including a further polarity reversal.
  The single-block interaction result extends past one residual block, and the synthetic validation is tested against a real pretrained model, in a companion paper that takes this theory further along both axes.

</details>


### [196] [ConformalShift: Targeted Event Reordering Against Adaptive ECG Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03628)
*Arash Vashagh,Yasmin Vashagh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConformalShift is a novel timing-based attack that manipulates event ordering to suppress ventricular heartbeat detection in adaptive conformal prediction systems without modifying actual data, classifier scores, or labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive conformal prediction helps recover clinically important heartbeat classes missed by classifiers, but its decisions are sensitive to event order due to delayed feedback. There's a need to understand security vulnerabilities when the system relies on timing of authentic information.

Method: ConformalShift is a bounded event-reordering attack that searches for feasible permutations of authentic preceding events to lower the ventricular threshold before evaluating a selected target event. It doesn't modify ECG waveforms, labels, classifier scores, or event multiset, only manipulates timing.

Result: On MIT-BIH confirmation records, the attack suppressed 66.7% of eligible targets for Extra Trees and 60.0% for HistGradientBoosting (vs 4.4% and 12.0% random baselines). Transferred configurations outperformed random scheduling on INCART, while reduced displacement budget weakened attack effectiveness.

Conclusion: Adaptive healthcare monitoring systems can be compromised through manipulation of authentic information timing alone, even when all data contents, labels, and classifier outputs remain unchanged, revealing a novel security vulnerability.

Abstract: Adaptive conformal prediction can recover clinically important heartbeat classes missed by a point classifier, but delayed feedback makes its decisions sensitive to event order. We introduce ConformalShift, a bounded event-reordering attack that suppresses the ventricular class for rescued events without modifying ECG waveforms, labels, classifier scores, or the event multiset. ConformalShift searches for feasible permutations of authentic preceding events that lower the ventricular threshold before a selected target is evaluated. On disjoint MIT--BIH confirmation records, the attack suppressed 66.7% of eligible targets for Extra Trees and 60.0% for HistGradientBoosting, compared with random-schedule rates of 4.4% and 12.0%, respectively. Transferred configurations also outperformed random scheduling on INCART, while reducing the displacement budget weakened the attack on both datasets. These results show that adaptive monitors in healthcare can be compromised through the timing of authentic information, even when waveforms, labels, classifier outputs, and event contents remain unchanged.

</details>


### [197] [POEM: Phase-Aware $\mathrm{SO}(2)$ Feature Rotation for Time Series Forecasting Under Periodicity Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03630)
*Jiawen Zhu,Shuhan Liu,Shengxuan Li,Qiming Shi,Di Weng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: POEM is a phase-aware forecasting framework using SO(2) rotation to address periodicity drift in time series by learning phase-correction coordinates and extrapolating them with Directional Phase Increment Attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning methods for time series forecasting model sequences on fixed time grids and struggle with periodicity drift (varying cycle timing and phase over time), limiting their ability to handle phase-related variations.

Method: Proposes POEM framework that uses SO(2) rotation group to learn phase-correction coordinates and applies invertible SO(2)-based rotation to paired latent features to reduce phase variability. Uses Directional Phase Increment Attention (DPIA) to retrieve historical phase increments from similar temporal contexts and integrate them into future phase corrections.

Result: POEM achieves competitive performance in experiments, and qualitative visualizations show that the learned phase-aware transformation makes latent trajectories more regular.

Conclusion: The POEM framework effectively addresses periodicity drift in time series forecasting by incorporating phase-aware transformations through SO(2) rotation and phase correction extrapolation, leading to more regular latent representations and competitive forecasting performance.

Abstract: Deep learning has advanced time series forecasting, but periodicity drift, in which cycle timing and phase vary over time, remains a challenging problem. Existing methods predominantly model these sequences on fixed time grids, suffering from a limited ability to accommodate phase-related variation. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{POEM}, a phase-aware forecasting framework based on latent feature rotation using the special orthogonal group in two dimensions, denoted by $\mathrm{SO}(2)$. POEM aims to reduce the phase-related variability by learning a phase-correction coordinate and applying an invertible $\mathrm{SO}(2)$-based rotation to paired latent features. To extrapolate this correction coordinate, Directional Phase Increment Attention (DPIA) retrieves historical phase increments from similar temporal contexts and integrates them into future phase corrections. Experiments demonstrate that POEM achieves competitive performance, while qualitative visualizations suggest that the learned phase-aware transformation makes latent trajectories more regular.

</details>


### [198] [CausalOPD: First-Wrong-Step Supervision for Distilling Causal Chain Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03673)
*Jian Zhang,Bingyi Wang,Yizhi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CausalOPD: A curriculum online process distillation framework that uses causal reasoning and short-horizon RL to correct step-dependent errors in critical reasoning tasks, outperforming standard distillation methods and proprietary models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many critical reasoning tasks require step-dependent causal chains where early errors propagate and correct conclusions can mask invalid reasoning. While LLMs perform well, privacy, latency, and controllability needs motivate distillation into locally deployable models. Standard trajectory imitation fails to correct process errors on the student's own rollout distribution.

Method: CausalOPD framework with: 1) Knowledge-augmented teacher providing trajectories grounded in domain-specific causal rules, entity relations, and constraints; 2) Student generating on-policy trajectories; 3) Teacher identifying the first wrong step (earliest constraint-violating transition); 4) Short-horizon RL repairing from verified prefix; 5) Causal-stage curriculum advancing from evidence-level to mechanism-level to conclusion-level errors.

Result: Across three domains: improves average path correctness by 23.4 percentage points over sequence-level online process distillation; reduces right-label-wrong-reasoning rate from 15.7% to 4.4%; domain-specific 8B students surpass evaluated proprietary references in path correctness across all domains.

Conclusion: CausalOPD effectively addresses step-dependent reasoning errors through localized failure repair guided by causal constraints and curriculum learning, enabling high-quality distillation for critical reasoning tasks while maintaining local deployability.

Abstract: Many critical reasoning tasks, including clinical diagnosis, legal judgment, and industrial fault diagnosis, require step-dependent causal chains in which early errors propagate and correct conclusions can mask invalid reasoning. Although large language models perform well on such tasks, privacy, latency, and controllability motivate distillation into locally deployable models. Standard trajectory imitation does not correct process errors on the student's own rollout distribution. We propose CausalOPD, a curriculum online process distillation framework. A knowledge-augmented teacher first provides trajectories grounded in domain-specific causal rules, entity relations, and structural constraints. The student then generates on-policy trajectories, and the teacher identifies the first wrong step, defined as the earliest transition that verifiably violates available constraints. Starting from the verified prefix, short-horizon reinforcement learning repairs this localized failure. A causal-stage curriculum advances from evidence-level to mechanism-level and conclusion-level errors, following their propagation order. Across three domains, CausalOPD improves average path correctness by 23.4 percentage points over sequence-level online process distillation and reduces the right-label-wrong-reasoning rate from 15.7% to 4.4%. The domain-specific 8B students also surpass both evaluated proprietary references in path correctness across all domains.

</details>


### [199] [DiagLoop: A Counterfactual Data Flywheel with Stage-Localized Reinforcement for Diagnostic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03674)
*Jian Zhang,Bingyi Wang,Yizhi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiagLoop: A counterfactual data flywheel system that generates training supervision from codified physical/clinical relations to improve diagnostic reasoning without case-level expert annotations, achieving significant accuracy gains over conventional baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal diagnostic models need to explain conclusions from evidence for effective repairs/treatments, but face challenges: scarce serious cases, lack of reasoning paths in records, and poor data transfer across configurations making local deployment difficult.

Method: DiagLoop uses a counterfactual data flywheel with: 1) codified physical relations/clinical guidelines authored per mechanism family, 2) training-only teacher proposing counterfactual worlds by varying causes/contexts/observations, 3) independent hybrid checker admitting only valid worlds, 4) student reasoning through symptom abstraction, causal-chain construction, root-cause attribution, 5) stage-specific failure identification, 6) bounded repair for nonterminal failures, 7) weakness-profile guided data generation, and 8) stage-localized RL with replay/preservation to reduce forgetting.

Result: 8B parameter model improves strict path correctness over strongest conventional baseline by 11.6 points across 8 industrial systems and 5.5 points across 10 disease categories. Gains over deranged-routing control are 3.9 and 2.3 points respectively. Model exceeds proprietary references in both domains, even when they receive few-shot examples or specification in context.

Conclusion: DiagLoop demonstrates that counterfactual data flywheels can effectively train diagnostic models using synthesized scenarios without case-level expert reasoning annotations, overcoming data scarcity and transfer limitations while maintaining separate admission/attribution/reward/regeneration checks to ensure validity.

Abstract: Causal diagnostic models must explain how conclusions follow from evidence because diagnoses guide repairs and treatments. Yet serious cases are scarce, records rarely contain reasoning paths, and data transfer poorly across configurations, complicating local deployment. We present DiagLoop, a counterfactual data flywheel that converts codified physical relations or clinical guidelines, authored once per mechanism family, into training supervision beyond recorded cases. A training-only teacher proposes counterfactual worlds by varying causes, contexts, and observations, while an independent hybrid checker admits only valid worlds. The student reasons through symptom abstraction, causal-chain construction, and root-cause attribution. Stage-specific criteria identify its earliest failure. For nonterminal failures, a bounded repair probes downstream competence, and the resulting weakness profile guides subsequent data generation. Stage-localized reinforcement learning updates only the model-generated continuation, while replay and preservation reduce forgetting. The same criteria govern admission, attribution, reward, and regeneration through checks separate from the proposer. Using only synthesized scenarios and no case-level expert reasoning annotations, the resulting 8B model improves strict path correctness over the strongest conventional baseline. Gains are 11.6 points across eight industrial systems and 5.5 points across ten disease categories. Gains over a deranged-routing control are 3.9 and 2.3 points, respectively. The model also exceeds the evaluated proprietary references in both domains, even when they receive few-shot examples or the specification in context.

</details>


### [200] [LAEF: A Lead-Agnostic ECG Foundation Model Towards Point-of-Care Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03690)
*Edoardo Coppola,Stefano Fiorini,Pietro Liò,Mattia Savardi,Alberto Signoroni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LAEF is a compact 7M-parameter ECG foundation model that can natively process any subset of ECG leads without architectural modifications, outperforming larger 12-lead models in point-of-care settings with 1-2 leads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ECG foundation models are limited to fixed 12-lead inputs, which degrades performance for point-of-care devices (smartwatches, handheld ECG recorders) that only capture 1-2 leads, creating a gap between clinical and consumer-grade ECG analysis.

Method: LAEF represents ECGs as variable-size spatiotemporal graphs with physiologically motivated intra- and inter-lead connectivity, processed by a Graph Attention Network. It's pre-trained on 9.2M 12-lead ECGs using masked node modeling with stochastic lead sampling to learn representations robust to lead configuration.
