Result: LAEF matches specialized 12-lead baselines that are 12× larger when using full leads. With 1-2 leads (point-of-care settings), it outperforms zero-padded alternatives on 17/18 datasets with one lead and 14/18 with two leads, achieving +3.2 average AUROC gain. Representation analysis confirms architectural lead-agnosticism, and lead-importance studies show stable performance across different single leads while recovering clinical lead-condition associations.

Conclusion: LAEF successfully bridges the gap between clinical 12-lead systems and consumer point-of-care devices by providing a single, compact model that works robustly across any lead configuration, enabling better ECG analysis for wearable and handheld devices while maintaining clinical-grade performance.

Abstract: Point-of-care cardiac devices such as smartwatches and handheld ECG recorders typically capture 1--2 leads, yet existing ECG foundation models are architecturally constrained to fixed 12-lead inputs, degrading or failing under these reduced configurations. We introduce LAEF (Lead-Agnostic ECG Foundation), a 7M-parameter ECG foundation model that can natively process any lead subset without zero-padding or architectural modification. LAEF represents ECGs as variable-size spatiotemporal graphs with physiologically motivated intra- and inter-lead connectivity, processed by a Graph Attention Network that scales naturally with active lead count.Pre-trained on 9.2M 12-lead ECGs via masked node modelling with stochastic lead sampling, LAEF learns representations robust to lead configuration. Across 18 downstream datasets, LAEF is on par with specialized 12-lead baselines over 12$\times$ larger at full lead availability. Under direct point-of-care-oriented diagnostics (1--2 leads), it outperforms all zero-padded alternatives on 17 out of 18 datasets with with a single randomly sampled lead and on 14 out of 18 with 2 leads, with an average AUROC gain of +3.2 points. Representation analysis links this advantage to architectural lead-agnosticism, and a lead-importance study across 164 cardiovascular conditions shows population-level performance is stable across single standard input leads while still recovering established clinically lead-condition associations.

</details>


### [201] [Learning and Clustering on Temporal Graphs: Principles, Primitives, and Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03696)
*Nelson Aloysio Reis de Almeida Passos,Emanuele Carlini,Salvatore Trani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph neural networks vs traditional clustering methods on temporal graphs: algorithmic methods excel without strong node attributes; neural methods win when structure, time, and attributes align; GPU acceleration enables scalable community detection for graph pooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's tension between graph neural networks and established clustering algorithms for temporal graph learning, especially regarding efficiency and accuracy trade-offs. The authors aim to bridge graph learning and community detection through shared spectral foundations.

Method: Three-perspective framework: 1) Principles - connecting graph learning and community detection via spectral foundations and detectability thresholds in stochastic block models; 2) Primitives - making spectral clustering and multislice modularity optimization tractable through GPU-accelerated temporal backends; 3) Pooling - viewing community detection as theory-grounded coarse-graining operator for temporal graphs.

Result: Algorithmic clustering methods remain better when node attributes are absent or weak, with scalability being the main limitation rather than accuracy. Neural models perform best when structural, temporal, and attribute signals align. GPU acceleration enables scalable temporal clustering.

Conclusion: Traditional clustering methods are appropriate for attribute-weak scenarios, while neural networks excel with aligned signals. GPU-accelerated primitives offer a path toward theory-grounded temporal graph pooling, raising key questions about when community-based coarse-graining preserves essential dynamics for downstream learning tasks.

Abstract: This work focuses on the problem of learning on temporal graphs, with particular emphasis on the task of clustering: obtaining coarse-grained representations by aggregating information from nodes, edges, and temporal dynamics - a task related to pooling in machine learning on graphs, or community detection in network science. Although graph neural networks reach state-of-the-art performance across many downstream graph tasks, their advantage over established descriptive and inferential clustering algorithms is far less settled, especially under demands of efficiency and recovery accuracy. We frame this tension through three linked perspectives: principles, connecting graph learning and community detection through shared spectral foundations and detectability thresholds in stochastic block model regimes; primitives, making spectral clustering and multislice modularity optimization tractable through GPU-accelerated temporal backends; and pooling, viewing principled community detection as a theory-grounded coarse-graining operator for temporal graphs. Our results indicate that algorithmic methods remain the appropriate tool where attributes are absent or weak - scalability rather than accuracy being the binding obstacle - while neural models are most compelling when structural, temporal, and attribute signals align. By making temporal clustering scalable, GPU-accelerated primitives suggest a route toward theory-grounded pooling, while raising a central question: when does community-based coarse-graining preserve the dynamics needed for downstream learning tasks?

</details>


### [202] [To Describe or Construct Statistical Learning Models Using the Category-theoretical Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03706)
*Congwei Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides an overview of statistical learning models with a category-theoretic perspective, aimed at attracting researchers from mathematics and other fields to statistical learning research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Statistical learning is a mainstream field in ML/AI with many real-world applications, but there's a need to bridge it with more theoretical mathematical perspectives like category theory to attract researchers from diverse fields.

Method: The paper summarizes classical statistical learning models and algorithms, then provides a category-theoretic perspective for understanding these models, making the field more accessible to mathematically-oriented researchers.

Result: A comprehensive overview of statistical learning from both practical and theoretical perspectives, creating a bridge between applied statistical learning and abstract category theory.

Conclusion: By presenting statistical learning through category theory, the paper aims to lower entry barriers and foster interdisciplinary collaboration, potentially leading to new theoretical insights and applications.

Abstract: Statistical learning is a fascinating field that has long been the mainstream of machine learning/artificial intelligence. A large number of results have been produced which can be widely applied to real-world problems. It also leads to many research topics and also stimulates new research. This report summarizes some classical statistical learning models and well-known algorithms, especially for amateurs, and provides a category-theoretic perspective on understanding statistical learning models. The aim is to attract researchers from other fields, including basic mathematics, to participate in the research related to statistical learning.

</details>


### [203] [Amortized Interventional Forecasting for Multivariate CIR Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03715)
*Andreas Sauter,Sumit Sourabh,Drona Kandhai,Erman Acar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CIR-ACTIVA: An amortized causal inference framework for multivariate CIR processes that estimates distributional causal effects across multiple time horizons without retraining per scenario, validated on synthetic CDS spread data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CIR models capture only correlated co-movement between financial time series (like CDS spreads), not causal influence. They cannot answer what-if questions about system responses when one series is externally shocked, which is crucial for stress testing and understanding interventional effects.

Method: Two main contributions: 1) An amortized model for distributional causal effect estimation that treats trajectories as time-stamped observations and predicts calibrated multi-horizon shock responses without per-scenario retraining. 2) A causal multivariate CIR data-generating process that provides paired observational and interventional ground truth unavailable in real markets. The framework is instantiated and calibrated on CDS spreads.

Result: CIR-ACTIVA demonstrates superior performance on synthetic ground truth in both causal selectivity in joint distribution and horizon-resolved calibration. It maintains selectivity even when interventional law varies over time, with particularly strong gains at short horizons. The framework can answer what-if queries about coupled spread systems that observational forecasters cannot handle.

Conclusion: CIR-ACTIVA enables a new class of causal what-if queries on financial systems like CDS stress testing, going beyond observational forecasting by capturing interventional causal effects. The synthetic validation approach provides a robust framework for causal inference in financial time series where experimental interventions are impossible.

Abstract: Mean-reverting dynamics are pervasive in finance, and the Cox--Ingersoll--Ross (CIR) process is a standard model for the time series they produce, from short rates to credit default swap (CDS) spreads. Yet CIR models capture only \emph{correlated} co-movement, not \emph{causal} influence between series, so they cannot answer the system's response when one series is externally shocked, which observational conditionals confound with historical co-movement. We make two contributions. First, an amortized model for distributional causal effect estimation that frames trajectories as time-stamped observations and predicts the calibrated multi-horizon shock response without retraining per scenario. Second, a causal multivariate CIR data-generating process that supplies the paired observational and interventional ground truth that real markets cannot. We instantiate and calibrate the framework on CDS spreads as a testbed. CIR-ACTIVA's validity is established on synthetic ground truth, independent of how well the simulator matches reality, while practical grounding is assessed by backtesting the generated traces against real CDS data. Against observational and amortized causal-inference baselines, CIR-ACTIVA leads on both causal selectivity in the joint distribution and horizon-resolved calibration, retaining its selectivity once the interventional law varies over the horizon, with gains concentrating at short horizons. This opens up a class of what-if queries on coupled spread systems, CDS stress testing among them, that observational forecasters cannot answer.

</details>


### [204] [UNVaMP: Neural Knowledge Tracing with Variational Regularization of Latent Knowledge Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03811)
*Carson J. Cook,Ahmed J. Zerouali,Anthony Schmidt,Reginald Ziedzor,Paul Lin,Luke G. Eglington*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UNVaMP is a knowledge tracing architecture that combines neural networks with interpretable measurement to track student learning, offering both high predictive performance and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop a knowledge tracing method that not only accurately predicts student responses but also provides interpretable insights into student learning trajectories, addressing the trade-off between predictive performance and interpretability in educational systems.

Method: UNVaMP integrates observed student-item interactions with internal memory to produce evolving latent representations of student knowledge. It offers two configurations: a pure neural model (UNVaMP-MLP) and a hybrid model with an interpretable measurement function (UNVaMP-MIRT using 1PL MIRT). The architecture provides mechanisms for controlling trajectory smoothness, quantifying uncertainty, and supporting heterogeneous input features.

Result: UNVaMP-MLP achieved the strongest predictive performance on three out of four datasets, while UNVaMP-MIRT (hybrid) lagged only slightly behind, showing modest predictive cost for interpretability. The model successfully quantifies uncertainty, controls volatility, and incorporates auxiliary inputs that induce structured changes in predictive behavior. Simulation studies show well-behaved knowledge state estimation under controlled conditions.

Conclusion: UNVaMP bridges the gap between predictive performance and interpretability in knowledge tracing, offering both practical utility for real-world education systems and the ability to recover underlying structure from student-item interactions.

Abstract: We introduce the Unified Neural Variational Measurement of Proficiency (UNVaMP) architecture, a knowledge tracing method that integrates observed student-item interactions with internal memory to produce evolving latent representations of student knowledge. These representations support accurate predictions of future responses while enabling explicit control over the smoothness of estimated learning trajectories. UNVaMP can be configured as either a purely neural model or a hybrid model that predicts responses through an interpretable measurement function over the latent space. We show that a pure neural configuration (UNVaMP-MLP) achieves the strongest predictive performance among compared models on three out of four datasets. Meanwhile, a hybrid configuration (UNVaMP-MIRT, using a 1PL MIRT measurement function) lags only slightly behind UNVaMP-MLP, indicating that the predictive cost of interpretability is modest.
  Beyond predictive accuracy, UNVaMP provides the following: a principled mechanism for controlling volatility when estimating student latent variables, quantification of uncertainty over student knowledge state estimates, and flexible input specification that supports heterogeneous student-item interaction features. In addition, the hybrid UNVaMP-MIRT configuration generates interpretable moment-in-time student knowledge state estimates. Using an experimental dataset, we show that auxiliary inputs induce structured changes in the predictive behavior of UNVaMP-MIRT, consistent with sensitivity to underlying structure beyond response correctness. Furthermore, through a simulation study, we show that UNVaMP yields well-behaved knowledge state estimates under controlled measurement conditions. In total, these results indicate that UNVaMP is both useful for real-world education systems and capable of recovering underlying structure from student-item interactions.

</details>


### [205] [Resume Means Resume: A Machine-Checked Conformance Contract for Checkpoint, Interrupt, and Resume Semantics in Workflow Persistence Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03836)
*Sajjad Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes execution state persistence in agent workflow frameworks, finding none provide reliable resume contracts. It introduces RESUME CONTRACT with 6 properties, identifies framework failures via TLA+ modeling, and proposes REMIT as a verified solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent workflow frameworks that persist execution state for crash recovery lack clear contracts about what "resume" means for already-executed effects, leading to inconsistent and unreliable behavior across different frameworks.

Method: 1) Define RESUME CONTRACT with 6 key properties for persistence APIs. 2) Use TLA+ model to check reference semantics exhaustively. 3) Test frameworks with a 39-cell fault matrix. 4) Develop deterministic LLM-free harness to measure framework behavior. 5) Propose REMIT, a Verus-verified reference sequencer.

Result: All tested frameworks (LangGraph 1.2.9, CrewAI 1.15.2, pydantic-graph 1.x) have different conformance profiles and exhibit critical failures: LangGraph re-executes work after crashes, CrewAI re-executes completed methods, pydantic-graph can't resume after mid-node crashes. Consume-once property fails under concurrent delivery. REMIT repairs fork and validity issues.

Conclusion: Current agent workflow frameworks lack proper resume contracts leading to unreliable crash recovery. The RESUME CONTRACT provides a formal foundation, and REMIT demonstrates that verified implementation can achieve correct recovery semantics across distributed environments.

Abstract: A framework that persists execution state so a run can be interrupted, survive a crash, and continue must decide what a resume means for effects that already fired. Five widely deployed agent workflow frameworks answer differently, none exposes a machine-checkable contract, and behavior violates even the fragments they state. The RESUME CONTRACT states six properties over the persistence API (prefix continuation, effect exactly-once, fork determinism, checkpoint validity, consume-once, recovery determinism), plus fork-intent and liveness obligations. A TLA+ model checks a reference semantics exhaustively, unchanged at scaled bounds (7.4 million states); a 39-cell fault matrix yields the separating models independence requires, and consume-once splits, its consumption clause independent of all six others. A deterministic, LLM-free harness measures them at pinned releases. LangGraph 1.2.9 durably records a second resume value and never consults it, persists schema-invalid state silently, and re-executes durably recorded work after a real SIGKILL: exactly-once across interrupts, at-least-once across crashes, on one API. CrewAI 1.15.2 re-executes completed effect-bearing methods against its written claim; pydantic-graph 1.x cannot resume after a mid-node crash; no two probed frameworks share a conformance profile. Consume-once holds sequentially and fails under concurrent delivery: k processes resuming one parked interrupt fire the gated effect k times, saturation 1.0 in 36 of 40 cells, and the failure crosses hosts. REMIT, a reference sequencer whose Verus-verified recovery core is line-identical to the shipped executable, repairs the fork and validity cells. The cross-process cell is repaired at the read path, and that repair ships: an opt-in gate claims consumption in the shared store, serving one racer and refusing the rest before any node executes.

</details>


### [206] [FedCritic-MIMO: Communication-Efficient Serverless Federated Critic Learning for Massive-MIMO Resource Control in Open and Disaggregated 6G RANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03852)
*Amin Farajzadeh,Melike Erol-Kantarci*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCritic-MIMO enables communication-efficient, serverless federated multi-agent reinforcement learning for 6G RAN controllers through peer-to-peer sparse critic exchange without centralized training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for AI-native resource control across independent cell-level controllers in disaggregated 6G RANs, where controllers must jointly manage complex resource allocation tasks (user scheduling, power allocation, beamforming, interference, QoS) with limited inter-controller signaling and no centralized trainer.

Method: Proposes FedCritic-MIMO: each base station retains local actor and personalized critic, exchanges only compatible shared critic parameters peer-to-peer via interference-aware graph. Uses wireless-aware event triggering, adaptive layer-wise top-k sparse critic exchange with error feedback, and balanced interference-aware fusion.

Result: In reuse-1 multi-cell massive-MIMO simulations, achieves best performance-communication tradeoff: highest throughput, improved user-rate distribution, increased QoS satisfaction, lowest interference cost per bit, and 76% reduction in critic-communication overhead compared to uncompressed distributed critic exchange.

Conclusion: Serverless exchange of compatible shared critic parameters can effectively coordinate RAN controllers without centralized trajectory collection or parameter-server aggregation, enabling practical AI-native resource control in disaggregated 6G networks.

Abstract: This paper proposes FedCritic-MIMO, a communication-efficient serverless federated multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for AI-native resource control across independently deployable cell-level controllers in open and disaggregated 6G RANs. Controllers share no trainer, retain local actors and personalized critic components, and exchange only compatible shared critic parameters. FedCritic-MIMO targets reuse-$1$ multi-cell massive-MIMO OFDMA deployments, where RAN controllers jointly manage user scheduling, per-stream power allocation, beamforming, interference, and long-term QoS with limited inter-controller signaling. Each base station locally executes its actor without centralized training or actor federation, while critic knowledge is exchanged peer-to-peer over an interference-aware graph. It enables this collaboration through wireless-aware event triggering, adaptive layer-wise top-$k$ sparse critic exchange with error feedback, and balanced interference-aware fusion. We establish conditional finite-time stationarity and consensus guarantees for the balanced, compressed peer-to-peer critic recursion under a fixed-policy, frozen-target critic-regression model. In strongly interference-coupled reuse-$1$ simulations, FedCritic-MIMO achieves the best performance-communication tradeoff among heuristic, independent-learning, centralized-training, and communication-ablation baselines. It achieves the highest held-out throughput, improves user-rate distribution and mean SINR, increases QoS satisfaction, and attains the lowest interference cost per delivered bit among learning baselines. It reduces critic-communication overhead by $76\%$ relative to uncompressed distributed critic exchange. These results demonstrate that serverless exchange of compatible shared critic parameters can coordinate RAN controllers without centralized trajectory collection or parameter-server aggregation.

</details>


### [207] [Quantization Effects on Biomedical LLM Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03854)
*Anton Rasmussen,Hong Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decoder LM classification performance depends heavily on implementation choices like prompt templates and scoring rules, which can reverse calibration rankings and cause accuracy differences comparable to model-level effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When using decoder language models as classifiers, implementation choices (prompt template, verbalizer, scoring rule) are rarely treated as experimental variables despite potentially dominating apparent calibration and performance metrics.

Method: Controlled evaluation of three Mistral-7B variants (Base, BioMistral, Instruct) on PubMed RCT sentence classification using four answer-text prompt templates and three precision levels (FP16, INT8, INT4) with different scoring protocols (summed vs mean token log-likelihood).

Result: Probability extraction protocol dominates apparent calibration (reverses rankings between models), prompt templates cause accuracy differences of 7-24 percentage points (comparable to model effects), INT8 quantization has minimal effects on specialized models but larger on base model, INT4 effects are heterogeneous but not catastrophic, and temperature scaling only helps under specific scoring rules.

Conclusion: Prompt template design and scoring normalization are first-order experimental decisions when evaluating decoder language model calibration, not just minor implementation details, and should be systematically varied in evaluations.

Abstract: When decoder language models are used as classifiers, predicted class probabilities depend on implementation choices, including the prompt template, verbalizer (label-to-token mapping), and scoring rule, that are rarely treated as experimental variables. We present a controlled evaluation of three Mistral-7B variants (Base, BioMistral, and Instruct) on PubMed RCT sentence classification (n=2000) under FP16, INT8, and INT4 precision using four answer-text prompt templates. Our primary finding is that the probability extraction protocol dominates apparent calibration. Switching from summed to mean token log-likelihood scoring reverses the calibration ranking between models: BioMistral average expected calibration error increases from 0.097 to 0.289, whereas Instruct decreases from 0.237 to 0.096, while accuracy changes by less than 1 percentage point for the specialized models but 4-6 percentage points for the base model. Prompt template choice produces accuracy differences of 7-24 percentage points, comparable to or larger than model-level effects. On one template, BioMistral outperforms Instruct although the overall mean favors Instruct by only 1.3 percentage points. For BioMistral and Instruct, INT8 quantization changes accuracy and F1 by only 1-2 percentage points relative to FP16, whereas the base model shows larger INT8 effects on some templates (up to +4.2 percentage points). INT4 produces heterogeneous but non-catastrophic effects. Temperature scaling reduces expected calibration error under summed scoring for both models but only for that scoring rule. A fine-tuned PubMedBERT reference achieves 82.7% accuracy but uses about 176000 labeled training examples, precluding direct comparison. These results demonstrate that prompt template design and scoring normalization are first-order experimental decisions when evaluating decoder language model calibration.

</details>


### [208] [Bi-semantic Chemical Embedder for Joint Representation Learning of SMILES and Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03855)
*David Ming Segura,Jeremy Goumaz,Joshua W. Sin,Bojana Ranković,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CheMatE is a chemistry-oriented embedding model that unifies molecular structure (SMILES) and scientific language understanding in a single representation space, preventing overfitting to chemical syntax while maintaining semantic capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer models have been successfully applied to chemistry using text-based molecular representations like SMILES, but domain-adaptive pre-training often causes catastrophic forgetting of foundational semantic capabilities as models overfit to chemical syntax.

Method: Built on ModernBERT backbone with two-stage training: (1) continued masked language modeling on large-scale SMILES-annotated scientific documents (FineWeb + ChemPile, ~22B tokens), (2) Matryoshka contrastive learning via Multiple Negative Ranking Loss using algorithmically generated SMILES-text pairs.

Result: CheMatE achieves competitive performance across both molecular property prediction and scientific language understanding tasks, demonstrating robust and transferable representations that unify structural and contextual signals.

Conclusion: CheMatE effectively prevents catastrophic forgetting by unifying structural and contextual signals within a single text-based framework, yielding representations that work well for both specialized chemistry tasks and general-purpose language understanding.

Abstract: Transformer models have revolutionized natural language processing (NLP), and text-based molecular representations like SMILES have successfully extended these architectures to chemistry. However, domain-adaptive pre-training often causes models to overfit to chemical syntax, catastrophically forgetting their foundational semantic capabilities. To address this challenge, we introduce CheMatE, a chemistry-oriented embedding model that jointly captures molecular structure and domain-specific natural language within the same representation space. Built on a ModernBERT backbone, CheMatE learns bi-semantic representations through a two-stage training procedure: continued masked language modeling (MLM) followed by a Matryoshka contrastive learning stage via Multiple Negative Ranking Loss (MNRL). First, we train the model using MLM on a novel, large-scale corpus of SMILES-annotated, long-context scientific documents that were constructed and curated from FineWeb and ChemPile (comprising 10.4B and 11.5B tokens, respectively). Subsequently, the model undergoes contrastive learning using a synthetic dataset of SMILES-text pairs algorithmically derived from our original training corpus. This design exposes the model to SMILES-enriched scientific literature, enabling bi-semantic understanding. We evaluate CheMatE across a range of downstream tasks covering molecular property prediction and scientific language understanding. Our results demonstrate that coupling our custom-curated datasets with this sequential training strategy yields robust, highly transferable representations. By effectively unifying structural and contextual signals within a single text-based framework, CheMatE achieves competitive performance across both specialized chemistry models and general-purpose language model baselines.

</details>


### [209] [CRS-Triage: Confidence- and Reliability-Aware Selective Triage under Incomplete Clinical Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03862)
*Guan Qiang,Yushen Chen,Tianlong Liu,David Rotenberg,Ethan H. Kim,Fang Fang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRS-Triage is a confidence-aware ML system for emergency triage that selectively defers uncertain cases, handles incomplete/unreliable EHR data by evaluating modality reliability and consistency, and penalizes under-triage to avoid missing high-acuity patients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergency triage requires fast, reliable decisions but EHR data (structured data and clinical text) are often incomplete, unreliable, and inconsistent. Existing ML models for triage prediction typically require complete and reliable EHR data, which limits their practical application in real clinical settings with data quality issues.

Method: Proposes CRS-Triage (confidence- and reliability-aware selective triage) that: 1) Separately evaluates reliability of structured data and clinical text, 2) Jointly considers consistency between the two modalities to estimate confidence scores for predictions, 3) Uses confidence scores with a threshold to selectively defer uncertain cases, 4) Penalizes under-triage errors (missing high-acuity patients) to prefer over-triage (assigning slightly higher acuity) rather than under-triage.

Result: Experiments on MIMIC-IV-ED dataset show CRS-Triage achieves strong predictive performance, provides better risk-coverage trade-off (balancing model confidence and coverage), and remains reliable even when EHR data are incomplete, degraded, or inconsistent across modalities.

Conclusion: CRS-Triage effectively addresses the challenge of unreliable/incomplete EHR data in emergency triage by incorporating confidence estimation, selective prediction, and under-triage penalization, making ML-based triage more practical and safe for real clinical applications.

Abstract: Emergency triage requires reliable decisions within a short time period. However, the available electronic health record (EHR) data, including structured data and clinical text, are often incomplete, unreliable, and inconsistent. This makes machine learning (ML)-based triage prediction more challenging, as existing ML models typically rely on complete and reliable EHR data to accurately predict patients' acuity levels. To address this, we propose confidence- and reliability-aware selective triage (CRS-Triage) to predict patients' acuity levels with a confidence score. By comparing the confidence score with a predefined threshold, CRS-Triage can selectively determine whether the model should make the decision or defer the case. Specifically, CRS-Triage separately evaluates the reliability of structured data and clinical text and then jointly considers the consistency between the two modalities to estimate the confidence of each prediction. Moreover, to reduce the risk of missing high-acuity patients, namely under-triage, CRS-Triage prefers to assign patients slightly higher acuity levels, namely over-triage, by penalizing under-triage errors. Experiments on the MIMIC-IV-ED dataset show that CRS-Triage achieves strong predictive performance. It also provides a better risk-coverage trade-off and remains reliable when the available EHR data are incomplete, degraded, or inconsistent across modalities.

</details>


### [210] [GENESIS: Towards Explainable Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03868)
*Abhinav Thorat,Ravi Kumar Kolla,Vishak K Bhat,Harsh Vardhan Singh Chauhan,Niranjan Pedanekar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GENESIS is an explainable causal discovery framework that achieves 100% decision traceability by decomposing graph construction into interpretable decision points supported by statistical evidence, Markov Blanket consistency, or explicit domain reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two fundamental challenges in causal discovery: (1) statistical methods lack power to resolve structural ambiguities in low-sample regimes, and (2) existing LLM-assisted hybrid approaches fail to explain individual edge decisions, which is critical in real-world applications where no ground-truth DAG exists and every structural decision must be independently justified.

Method: GENESIS decomposes graph construction into interpretable decision points. It first identifies and scores three-node structural motifs (chains, forks, colliders) to establish transparent structural priors, then progressively refines the graph by integrating these priors with observational evidence, invoking domain knowledge only when statistical evidence is insufficient.

Result: GENESIS achieves 100% decision traceability across all settings. It consistently outperforms purely statistical causal discovery methods on most benchmark datasets across all sample regimes in terms of Structural Hamming Distance, while achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLM-assisted approaches.

Conclusion: The paper establishes explainability as a first-class objective in causal discovery through the GENESIS framework, which provides auditable evidence for every edge decision while maintaining competitive performance with existing approaches.

Abstract: Causal Discovery (CD) from observational data faces two fundamental challenges. First, purely statistical methods often lack the power to resolve structural ambiguities in low-sample regimes. Second, although LLM-assisted hybrid approaches improve structure recovery through semantic reasoning, the influence of that reasoning on individual edge decisions remains largely opaque. Consequently, existing hybrid methods fail to satisfy a fundamental requirement: explaining why a particular edge is included or excluded in the learned directed acyclic graph (DAG). This is critical in real-world applications, where no ground-truth DAG exists and every structural decision must be independently justified. We formalize this requirement as decision traceability, requiring every inferred edge to be supported by auditable statistical evidence, Markov Blanket consistency, or explicit domain reasoning. We propose GENESIS, an explainable hybrid CD framework that decomposes graph construction into interpretable decision points. GENESIS first identifies and scores three-node structural motifs, including chains, forks, and colliders, to establish transparent structural priors, then progressively refines the graph by integrating these priors with observational evidence, invoking domain knowledge only when statistical evidence is insufficient. By design, every edge decision is resolved through an auditable source of evidence. Experiments show that GENESIS achieves 100% decision traceability across all settings, establishing explainability as a first-class objective in causal discovery. Despite this additional requirement, GENESIS consistently outperforms purely statistical CD methods on the majority of benchmark datasets across all sample regimes in terms of Structural Hamming Distance (SHD), while achieving performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLM-assisted approaches.

</details>


### [211] [Enhancing VLM Reward Models Through Structure-Aware Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03875)
*Pyrros Koussios,Chenhao Li,Xin Chen,Andreas Krause*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAFT is a self-supervised method that fine-tunes foundation VLMs online to denoise and improve their reward signals for RL, using structural priors via LoRA adapters without ground-truth supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM-based reward functions in RL are often noisy and unreliable, limiting their direct utility. Manual reward engineering is a bottleneck, and while foundation VLMs offer promise by using text-observation similarity, they produce imperfect reward signals that need refinement.

Method: SAFT (Structure-Aware Fine-Tuning) uses self-supervised online fine-tuning of VLMs without ground-truth supervision. It leverages intrinsic structural priors to regularize the VLM's latent space via LoRA adapters, refining imperfect reward signals by incorporating task structure as inductive bias.

Result: SAFT consistently denoises the reward landscape, yields faster policy convergence, and substantially improves alignment (measured by EPIC distance) relative to base models. The method works across a spectrum of base model capabilities, suggesting failures often stem from structural brittleness rather than semantic misunderstanding.

Conclusion: SAFT offers a scalable path for stabilizing text-conditioned RL by replacing extensive human preference annotation with structural inductive biases. It underscores the broader value of incorporating task structure as a general inductive bias for improving VLM-based reward models.

Abstract: Designing effective reward functions remains a major bottleneck in Reinforcement Learning (RL). Recent work uses large foundation Vision-Language Models (VLMs) as reward models, computing text-observation similarity to bypass manual reward engineering. Although promising, these rewards are often noisy and unreliable, limiting their direct utility during deployment. We present Structure-Aware Fine-Tuning (SAFT), a simple, self-supervised method that refines these imperfect reward signals online without access to ground-truth supervision. SAFT leverages intrinsic structural priors to regularize the VLM's latent space via LoRA adapters. We rigorously evaluate SAFT across a spectrum of base model capabilities to demonstrate its versatility. Our results show that SAFT consistently denoises the reward landscape, yielding faster policy convergence and substantially improved alignment (EPIC distance) relative to the underlying base model, suggesting that failures can often be attributed to structural brittleness rather than semantic misunderstanding. By replacing extensive human preference annotation with structural inductive biases inherent to the task, SAFT offers a scalable path for stabilizing text-conditioned RL and underscores the broader value of incorporating task structure as a general inductive bias.

</details>


### [212] [Operationally Feasible Synthetic Power-Grid Scenarios via Learning the AC-Operable Joint Distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03878)
*Chenhan Xiao,Xinyu He,Haoran Li,Hanghang Tong,Yang Weng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hierarchical diffusion-based framework that generates AC-operable synthetic power grids by learning the joint distribution of topology, branch parameters, and load profiles while incorporating AC power-flow constraints directly into the generation process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing synthetic grid generation methods often produce scenarios with low AC feasibility and robustness, limiting their practical utility for power-system studies. While recent approaches have incorporated engineering knowledge through post-generation validation or optimization, they still struggle with operational feasibility.

Method: A feasibility-aware distribution-learning framework using hierarchical diffusion models. The framework decomposes generation into three stages: 1) topology and bus-attribute generation, 2) branch-parameter generation conditioned on structure, and 3) load-profile generation conditioned on both structure and electrical characteristics. AC power-flow convergence and operational constraints are integrated into the diffusion-based distribution learning rather than enforced after generation.

Result: Experiments on benchmark systems show the framework significantly improves operational feasibility and contingency robustness while maintaining strong statistical fidelity. It eliminates the need for optimization-based post-processing that traditional methods require.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully generates synthetic power grids that are AC-operable and robust to contingencies by embedding operational constraints directly into the distribution learning process, offering a practical solution for creating realistic synthetic grid scenarios for power-system applications.

Abstract: Synthetic power-grid scenarios are essential for planning, resilience assessment, contingency analysis, and data-driven power-system applications. Recent synthetic grid generation methods have improved structural realism and operational feasibility by incorporating engineering knowledge through post-generation validation, optimization, or physics-aware generation. However, generated scenarios may still exhibit low AC feasibility and robustness, limiting their practical value for downstream power-system studies. This paper proposes a feasibility-aware distribution-learning framework that learns the AC-operable joint distribution of network topology, branch electrical parameters, and time-varying load profiles. Instead of enforcing feasibility after generation, the proposed framework incorporates AC power-flow convergence and operational constraints into hierarchical diffusion-based distribution learning. This enables the generator itself to produce operationally feasible grid scenarios through efficient diffusion sampling. The hierarchical architecture decomposes the high-dimensional generation task into three engineering-motivated stages: topology and bus-attribute generation, branch-parameter generation conditioned on the generated structure, and load-profile generation conditioned on both network structure and electrical characteristics. Experiments on benchmark systems demonstrate that the proposed framework significantly improves operational feasibility and contingency robustness while maintaining strong statistical fidelity and eliminating optimization-based post-processing.

</details>


### [213] [Omega-S: A Functional Resilience Index for LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03887)
*Alberto Acedo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Omega-S is a simple 3-line regularization penalty computed from weight matrices alone that prevents catastrophic forgetting during LLM fine-tuning without needing previous-task data, Fisher matrix, or old weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning large language models on new data causes catastrophic forgetting - degradation of previously learned capabilities. Existing regularization methods require storing old weights, computing Fisher matrices, or using previous-task data.

Method: Omega-S is a drop-in penalty computed from the weight matrix alone, needing no previous-task data, Fisher matrix, or stored old weights. It's based on Tr(A^3) but effectively reduces to a penalty on the variance of node degrees (row magnitude in square modules, directional alignment in non-square ones).

Result: On Llama-3-8B with LoRA, fine-tuned from code to prose: Omega-S beats no regularization (9/10 seeds), tuned weight decay (10/10 seeds), and tuned EWC (8/10 seeds). Retention ratio improved from 62.9% to 84.1%. Pass@1 improved from 0.173 to 0.238 on HumanEval.

Conclusion: Omega-S effectively prevents catastrophic forgetting during LLM fine-tuning with minimal computational overhead (under 4% cost increase) and no requirement for previous-task data. The paper also quantifies high variability in retention ratios across identical configurations.

Abstract: Fine-tuning a large language model on new data degrades what it previously learned. We present Omega-S, a drop-in penalty computed from the weight matrix alone: it needs no previous-task data, no Fisher matrix and no stored copy of the old weights. It is three lines in an existing training loop and adds under 4% to the cost of a step.
  Retention. On Llama-3-8B with LoRA, fine-tuned from code to prose and measured by HumanEval over ten seeds, Omega-S retains more of the original capability than no regularisation on 9 of 10 seeds (0.173 -> 0.238 absolute pass@1; sign test one-sided p=0.011, Wilcoxon p=0.006), as a retention ratio, 62.9% -> 84.1%. It also beats tuned weight decay on 10 of 10 seeds (p=0.002) and tuned EWC on 8 of 10 (p=0.014), every arm re-measured in the same session.
  Mechanism, measured rather than asserted. Omega-S is topological by construction, its objective built from Tr(A^3), but we measured which of its four factors actually moves and three do not: their elasticity with respect to the weights is at or below 1e-4, against 9e-3 for the degree-variance term. As implemented, the composite reduces to a penalty on the variance of node degrees, which means row magnitude in square modules and directional alignment in non-square ones. We report this because a method whose name promises one thing and whose gradient does another should say so. We also enumerate the open design choices, including a contrast-preserving construction that does what it was designed to do and makes retention worse on all ten seeds.
  Repeating an identical configuration, same seed and same hardware, gives a standard deviation of 0.104 in retention ratio. We have not found this quantified for low-rank fine-tuning of language models, and it bounds every seed-paired comparison in this literature, ours included.
  Code, per-seed results and the full record of negative results are available.

</details>


### [214] [Cross-Model KV Cache Transfer in LLM Families: A Closed-Form Linear Mapping for Prefill Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03893)
*Taekyung Heo,Rasoul Shafipour,Ritchie Zhao,Maximilian Golub,Mohammad Mahdi Kamani,Ritika Borkar,Makesh Tarun Chandran,Pantea Zardoshti,Bita Darvish Rouhani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method for transferring KV cache between different-sized models in the same family to skip prefill computation, using linear or nonlinear mapping to reuse source model's KV cache in target models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production deployments often swap between different-sized models for cost-quality cascading, mid-conversation switching, and routing. Each swap forces the receiver model to recompute prefill from scratch, which is computationally expensive.

Method: Proposes cross-model KV cache transfer with a closed-form ridge mapper that: 1) selects top-k most predictive source layers for each target layer, 2) strips RoPE from keys before mapping for position-free reusable fits, 3) fits ridge regression on a small calibration set (500 FineWeb-Edu sequences). Also explores nonlinear MLP mapping for difficult cases.

Result: Across six model pairs in three families, the linear mapper retains 73-98% of receiver's standalone-prefill accuracy on four pairs, with two degrading sharply. Nonlinear MLP recovers up to +37 percentage points HellaSwag retention on failures. The mapper runs 2.7-25x faster than re-prefill and remains stable across multi-turn handoff.

Conclusion: Cross-model KV cache transfer is practical and effective, significantly reducing computational overhead when switching between models in production deployments while maintaining most of the accuracy benefits.

Abstract: Production deployments often swap between different-sized models in a family for cost-quality cascading, mid-conversation switching, and routing, and each swap forces the receiver to repay the prefill from scratch. We propose cross-model KV cache transfer, where the receiver reuses the source's KV cache, skipping prefill. We find that cross-model KV has substantial linear structure across matched-KV pairs, where source and target share KV head count and per-head dimension. On Qwen3 14B->32B, one source layer explains 56% of variance in the target's keys and 32% in values, rising to 79% and 65% with multiple source layers. Building on this, we design a closed-form ridge mapper that operates per head and proceeds in three steps. First, for each target layer we select the top-k most predictive source layers and concatenate their KV as input. Second, we strip RoPE from the keys before mapping, so the fit is position-free and reusable across context lengths. Third, we fit ridge regression on a small calibration set of 500 FineWeb-Edu sequences of 1,024 tokens each. Surprisingly, across six pairs in three families, this linear mapper retains 73-98% of the receiver's standalone-prefill accuracy on four pairs, while two degrade sharply. A nonlinear MLP recovers up to +37 pp HellaSwag retention on the failures. The mapper runs 2.7-25x faster than re-prefill and remains stable across multi-turn handoff, making cross-model KV cache transfer practical.

</details>


### [215] [Sparse Weight Decomposition for Efficient Circuit Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03913)
*Chuanhao Yan,Xuhan Huang,Yawen Duan,Zhenfei Yin,Hang Zhao,Bryan Dai,Jie Fu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SWD reparameterizes pretrained weight matrices into sparse factors to enable circuit extraction without additional training, achieving better fidelity with less data and computational cost than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing circuit extraction methods require learning auxiliary sparse representations or training sparse models, which incurs substantial additional computation and creates a fidelity gap between the analyzed representation and the original pretrained model.

Method: Proposes Sparse Weight Decomposition (SWD) which factorizes each weight matrix into two sparse factors with shared intermediate coordinates that serve as individually addressable circuit units, supporting the same scoring, selection, and ablation workflow without separate network training.

Result: SWD matches held-out fidelity of strong baselines using <1% of data, achieves same circuit sufficiency/necessity targets with fewer active edges/units across GPT-2, Qwen2.5, and Qwen3.5-27B, remains effective for full-model replacement after fine-tuning, and has zero-data variant for broader use.

Conclusion: SWD provides an efficient, high-fidelity approach for circuit extraction from pretrained transformers that avoids training separate replacement networks, enabling more accessible and effective mechanistic interpretability analysis.

Abstract: Dense pretrained transformers do not naturally expose interpretable units for circuit extraction. Existing approaches obtain such units by learning auxiliary sparse representations or training sparse models, incurring substantial additional computation while potentially introducing a fidelity gap between the representation being analyzed and the original pretrained model. We propose Sparse Weight Decomposition (SWD), which reparameterizes pretrained linear projections by factorizing each weight matrix into two sparse factors whose shared intermediate coordinates serve as individually addressable circuit units. Without training a separate replacement network, this parametric representation supports the same scoring, selection, and ablation circuit extraction workflow used for methods that learn sparse features. Across single-matrix replacements, SWD matches the held-out fidelity achieved by Transcoder and other strong baselines while using less than 1% of the data that those baselines use to train their replacements. For matched replacement fidelity, SWD reaches the same circuit sufficiency and necessity targets with fewer active read/write edges and selected units across tasks on GPT-2, Qwen2.5, and Qwen3.5-27B. We further show that SWD remains effective for full-model replacement of all attention and MLP weight matrices after fine-tuning the nonzero factor values. Finally, SWD also features a zero-data variant, allowing broader use of mechanistic interpretability analysis (e.g., per-step analysis).

</details>


### [216] [Trajectory inference via Acceleration Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03916)
*Bartolo Dazzini,Giovanni Conforti,Alain Durmus,Aram-Alexandre Pooladian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new algorithm called Acceleration Matching (AM) for trajectory inference that avoids expensive preprocessing and simulation-based training by lifting to phase space and regressing onto conditional acceleration fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory inference faces computational challenges with existing algorithms that rely on expensive preprocessing subroutines to enforce smoothness or simulation-based training objectives.

Method: Lift the interpolation problem to phase space and regress onto an explicit conditional acceleration field that induces random, smooth trajectories agreeing with prescribed marginals.

Result: AM is competitive with or superior to existing algorithms on several benchmark problems, requires only positional data, avoids trajectory simulation during training, and has no expensive preprocessing.

Conclusion: Acceleration Matching provides an efficient alternative to existing trajectory inference methods by overcoming computational limitations through phase space lifting and acceleration field regression.

Abstract: Trajectory inference is a fundamental problem in many scientific domains: given a collection of unpaired snapshots of observations at discrete time points, the goal is to generate smooth trajectories that best resemble and interpolate the data. Existing algorithms exhibit computational challenges: they either rely on preprocessing subroutines to enforce smoothness or on simulation-based training objectives, both of which can be expensive. In order to overcome these limitations, we propose a new algorithm called Acceleration Matching (\texttt{AM}). Our approach consists of lifting the original interpolation problem to phase space and then regressing onto an explicit conditional acceleration field that induces random, smooth trajectories that agree with the prescribed marginals. Importantly, our resulting training algorithm only requires positional data, avoids trajectory simulation during training, and is devoid of expensive preprocessing. We provide ample numerical evidence suggesting that \texttt{AM} is competitive with or superior to existing algorithms on several benchmark problems from the existing literature.

</details>


### [217] [PRISM: Powerful Time Series to Image (TS2I) Representations for Multivariate Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03926)
*Mateusz Smendowski,Kamil Faber,Piotr Nawrocki,Nathalie Japkowicz,Roberto Corizzo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM introduces a systematic framework for converting multivariate time series into multi-channel images for anomaly detection, achieving competitive performance with time-domain methods and identifying channelization as a critical design factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series anomaly detection (TSAD) performance is sensitive to representation choices, especially for multivariate data. While time series to image conversion works for forecasting/classification, it's unclear how to best map multivariate series to multi-channel images and whether vision backbones can match time-domain TSAD baselines.

Method: PRISM is a plug-and-play meta-workflow for systematic construction and evaluation of image-based representations for multivariate TSAD. It introduces MSM, a novel statistics-based channelization scheme, and leverages ImageNet-pretrained vision encoders.

Result: PRISM configurations compete with 24 time-domain baselines, achieving best VUS-PR on 10/14 datasets with 41% average improvement over best competing methods on those datasets. MSM provides 11-27% gains over PCA-based alternatives. ImageNet-pretrained encoders transfer effectively, with frozen encoders retaining 92% of fine-tuned performance while training 1.8x faster.

Conclusion: Image-based representations via PRISM are competitive for TSAD, with channelization identified as a critical design dimension. Pretrained vision models transfer effectively to time series tasks, offering training efficiency benefits while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) underpins applications in predictive maintenance, finance, and cloud computing, however performance remains sensitive to representation choices, especially in multivariate settings. While transforming time series into images has shown success in forecasting and classification, it remains unclear how multivariate, high-dimensional series should be mapped to multi-channel images and whether vision backbones can match time-domain baselines in TSAD. We introduce PRISM, a plug-and-play meta-workflow enabling systematic construction and evaluation of image-based representations for multivariate TSAD. Our evaluation spanning over 7,000 experiments shows that well-designed PRISM configurations are competitive with 24 time-domain baselines, achieving the best VUS-PR on 10 of 14 datasets, with an average improvement of 41% over the best competing method on those datasets. Further, we identify channelization - how the channel dimension of multi-channel images is constructed - as a critical and previously understudied design dimension, and introduce MSM, a novel statistics-based scheme achieving 11-27% gains over PCA-based alternatives. Finally, ImageNet-pretrained encoders transfer effectively to TSAD, with frozen encoders retaining 92% of fine-tuned performance while training 1.8 times faster. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Smendowski/PRISM.

</details>


### [218] [A Physics-Flavored Transformer Network for Parametrizing Contraction Dynamics of Engineered Skeletal Muscle Tissues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03927)
*Mattias Luber,Timo Betz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-flavored neural network (PFNN) automates kinetic phenotyping of engineered skeletal muscle tissues by integrating physical models with deep learning, enabling scalable extraction of meaningful parameters from force-time data through hybrid training on synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current functional characterization of engineered skeletal muscle tissues (ESMs) relies on simplistic metrics like peak force, discarding critical kinetic information. Existing mechanistic models are too mathematically complex for scalable application, limiting widespread adaptation in the field.

Method: Developed a Physics-Flavored Neural Network (PFNN) that integrates a stretched-exponential physical model into a CNN-Transformer architecture. Used hybrid training: first trained on synthetic data to develop "physical intuition," then underwent unsupervised self-alignment on unlabeled real-world measurements to address biological data scarcity.

Result: The approach achieves high-fidelity parameterization across diverse contractile phenotypes and cell lines, including Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy models. The scalable, self-improving pipeline bridges the gap between idealized biophysics and noisy in vitro data.

Conclusion: The physics-flavored neural network provides a robust tool for high-throughput biophysical research by automating kinetic phenotyping of engineered skeletal muscle tissues, overcoming limitations of both simplistic metrics and overly complex mechanistic models.

Abstract: Engineered Skeletal Muscle Tissues (ESMs) have become a key structure for biomedical disease modeling and pharmacological screening, yet their functional characterization often relies on simplistic metrics like peak force, discarding critical kinetic information. This is partially due to the high level of mathematical complexity which mechanistic models introduce to capture these dynamics. Hence, exactly the complexity prevents scalable application and widespread adaptation in the field. Here we present a Physics-Flavored Neural Network (PFNN) that automates the kinetic phenotyping of ESMs. Our architecture integrates a stretched-exponential physical model into a CNN-Transformer, enabling the extraction of physically meaningful parameters directly from force-time profiles. To address the scarcity of labeled biological data, we employ a hybrid training paradigm: the model develops a "physical intuition" on synthetic data before undergoing unsupervised self-alignment on unlabeled real-world measurements. Our results demonstrate that this physics-flavored approach achieves high-fidelity parameterization across diverse contractile phenotypes and cell lines, including Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy models. Our scalable, self-improving pipeline bridges the gap between idealized biophysics and noisy \emph{in vitro} data, providing a robust tool for high-throughput biophysical research.

</details>


### [219] [Latent Reward Registers for Diffusion Preference Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03929)
*Yuanshen Guan,Zipeng Feng,Zhiwei Xiong,Peiqin Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent Reward Registers enable preference alignment in diffusion models by estimating terminal rewards from intermediate latents, using register tokens with DiT. This enables efficient training via Reward-Gradient On-Policy Distillation and inference-time steering via Reward-Guided Sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aligning diffusion models with human preferences typically relies on sparse terminal rewards evaluated only on final samples, creating a severe temporal credit assignment problem across the multi-step denoising process. This makes it challenging to determine which intermediate steps contribute to good final outcomes.

Method: Proposes Latent Reward Registers - learnable, position-free register tokens prepended to the input sequence of a frozen Diffusion Transformer (DiT) that estimate terminal preferences directly from intermediate noisy latents. This provides a dense, differentiable reward signal throughout denoising. Enables two strategies: 1) Reward-Gradient On-Policy Distillation (RG-OPD) for training via reward-guided updates along trajectories without expensive rollouts, and 2) Reward-Guided Sampling (RGS) for inference-time steering via magnitude-matched reward gradients.

Result: At high noise levels (μ=0.8), registers achieve highest pairwise accuracy among latent reward models. RG-OPD outperforms online RL baselines while reducing GPU hours by 33x. RGS establishes new SOTA among training-free methods, enhancing both alignment and perceptual metrics.

Conclusion: Latent Reward Registers effectively solve the credit assignment problem in diffusion model alignment by providing dense reward signals from intermediate states. The approach enables both efficient training and inference-time guidance without modifying the generator's core parameters, achieving superior performance with significant computational efficiency gains.

Abstract: Aligning diffusion models with human preferences usually relies on a sparse terminal reward evaluated on the final generated samples, presenting a severe temporal credit-assignment challenge across the multi-step denoising process. We propose Latent Reward Registers, a mechanism that estimates terminal preference directly from intermediate noisy latents by prepending learnable, position-free register tokens to the input sequence of a frozen Diffusion Transformer (DiT). This independent readout mechanism extracts latent reward evidence without altering the generator's hidden states or velocity field. The resulting dense, differentiable reward signal throughout the full denoising process facilitates two alignment strategies. For training, Reward-Gradient On-Policy Distillation (RG-OPD) distills reward-guided updates along on-policy trajectories, bypassing the computationally expensive rollouts of standard policy gradients. For inference, Reward-Guided Sampling (RGS) steers trajectories via magnitude-matched reward gradients without parameter updates. Empirically, at high noise levels (u = 0.8), the registers reach the highest pairwise accuracy among the evaluated latent reward models. Furthermore, RG-OPD outperforms online reinforcement learning baselines while reducing GPU hours by up to 33x, and RGS establishes a new state-of-the-art among training-free methods, strictly enhancing both alignment and perceptual metrics. Code and weights are available at https://github.com/Guanys-dar/latent-reward-register

</details>


### [220] [Muon Meets Mamba: Spectral Optimization for State Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03941)
*Arslan Battalov,Karim Kramin,Alexander Markotenko,Sofia Sinitsina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon optimizer shows token efficiency benefits when applied only to output projections in Mamba-2 models, not input projections, with advantages persisting beyond compute-optimal training points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon is a recently proposed optimizer with strong evidence mainly from Transformer models, but its behavior on state-space models like Mamba-2 is largely unknown. The paper aims to systematically evaluate Muon's effectiveness on Mamba-2 models compared to AdamW.

Method: Controlled comparison between Muon and AdamW on Mamba-2 130M model, varying which weight groups are trained with Muon (output projection only, input projection only, or both). Experiments conducted on two corpora with two token budgets, and training extended well past compute-optimal points.

Result: Muon benefits are localized: training only the output projection with Muon outperforms training the input projection or both. The advantage is primarily token efficiency, holding across different corpora and token budgets, and persisting beyond compute-optimal training points. Conditioning improvements don't explain the gains - Muon lowers condition number of whichever projection it trains, but the better-conditioned input projection doesn't provide benefits.

Conclusion: Muon optimizer provides token efficiency advantages specifically when applied to output projections in Mamba-2 models, not input projections. This localized benefit persists beyond typical training durations and is not explained by conditioning improvements, suggesting more complex optimization dynamics at play.

Abstract: Muon is a recent optimizer that orthogonalizes the update to each weight matrix with a Newton-Schulz iteration, which performs steepest descent under the spectral norm. Almost all the evidence for it comes from Transformer models, and its behavior on state-space models is largely unreported. We compare Muon with AdamW on Mamba-2 130M under a controlled protocol that varies only which weight groups are trained with Muon. The benefit is localized. Muon on the output projection alone beats Muon on the input projection or on both. The advantage is mainly one of token efficiency. It holds on two corpora and two token budgets, and persists when training continues well past the compute-optimal point. Conditioning does not explain the gain. Muon lowers the condition number of whichever projection it trains, but the better-conditioned input projection is not the one that helps.

</details>


### [221] [Assessment of Conditional Diffusion Model for Synthetic Histopathology Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03990)
*Seyed Kahaki,Shijie Li,Weijie Chen,Nicholas Petrick*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using domain-specific evaluation metrics based on foundation models pretrained on pathology data, rather than ImageNet-based metrics, to better assess synthetic histopathology image quality and its impact on downstream tasks like nuclei segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation methods for synthetic histopathology images may not fully assess data quality for medical applications, as conventional metrics like FID and IS rely on ImageNet-pretrained models that may not capture domain-specific features relevant to pathology.

Method: 1) Proposed modified FID and IS using foundation models pretrained on digital pathology datasets; 2) Used precision-recall metrics for additional quality assessment; 3) Generated synthetic datasets with varied quality using conditional denoising diffusion models with two-step training on four benchmark datasets; 4) Measured correlation between synthetic data quality metrics and nuclei segmentation performance using AJI+ and Dice coefficient.

Result: Pathology-specific metrics showed improved discriminative power: modified IS had higher correlation with downstream task performance (r=0.6096 with AJI+, p=0.0122) vs original IS (r=0.0708, p=0.7944). Diversity of generated training data had stronger positive correlation with segmentation performance than visual fidelity of individual images.

Conclusion: Domain-specific evaluation metrics using pathology-pretrained foundation models provide better assessment of synthetic histopathology image quality than conventional ImageNet-based metrics. Focusing on increasing variety of generated data may be more beneficial than individual image fidelity for improving downstream task performance.

Abstract: Synthetic histopathology image generation has emerged as an approach that may address data scarcity in computational pathology, yet current evaluation methodologies may not fully assess synthetic data quality for medical applications. This work investigates and addresses limitations in existing evaluation metrics, investigating an approach for assessing synthetic histopathology image quality through domain-specific metrics and downstream task validation. We show that conventional synthetic data evaluation metrics such as Frechet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Score (IS) may have limitations when applied to histopathology images due to their reliance on ImageNet-pretrained feature extractors. To address these limitations, we propose for consideration modified FID and IS approaches utilizing foundation models pretrained on digital pathology datasets, supplemented by precision-recall based metrics as part of an additional quality assessment. Using conditional denoising diffusion models trained on four benchmark datasets, with a two-step training approach, we generated synthetic datasets with systematically varied quality characteristics. We also measured the correlation between the synthetic data quality metrics with downstream nuclei segmentation performance using common metrics including the aggregated Jaccard index (AJI+) and the Dice coefficient. The study results suggest that pathology-specific metrics may provide improved discriminative power. Specifically, the modified Inception Score indicates higher correlation with downstream task performance (r=0.6096 with AJI+, p=0.0122), compared to the original IS (r=0.0708, p=0.7944). Our observations indicate that increasing the variety of generated training data has a higher positive correlation with segmentation model performance than improving the visual fidelity of individual generated images.

</details>


### [222] [Test-Time Scaling in Reasoning LLMs: Inference Regimes, Evaluation, and Reproducibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04001)
*Mohsen Hariri,Weicong Chen,Nahal Shahini,Vikash Singh,Kai Ye,Amirhossein Samandar,Debargha Ganguly,Sreehari Sankar,Yanyan Zhang,Shouren Wang,Jerry Peng,Biyao Zhang,Michael Hinczewski,Vipin Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a systematic framework for analyzing test-time scaling in LLMs, distinguishing different inference algorithms, establishing evaluation principles, and specifying reproducibility requirements for reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current literature treats diverse test-time scaling algorithms (single trajectory, voting, verification, search) as interchangeable under a single "compute budget," making results difficult to compare across studies due to different statistical structures, compute accounting, and failure modes.

Method: Develops a three-axis framework: 1) Formalizes test-time scaling as budgeted inference over autoregressive prefix trees, distinguishing sequential, leaf-level, and prefix-level scaling regimes; 2) Treats the entire inference system as the evaluated object with evaluation principles for end-to-end performance and diagnostics; 3) Specifies reproducibility requirements distinguishing exact replay from distributional reproducibility.

Result: The framework enables systematic comparison of test-time scaling methods, introduces evaluation profiles that recover common metrics, prescribes protocol-matched compute reporting, organizes the reasoning ecosystem by model-side and interface mechanisms, and releases over 2 billion reasoning traces with rich verification signals.

Conclusion: Test-time scaling algorithms need systematic characterization and reporting standards to enable meaningful comparisons across studies, and the proposed framework provides the necessary principles for reproducible evaluation of reasoning capabilities in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models can solve substantially harder reasoning problems with more inference-time compute. The term "test-time scaling," however, now covers diverse inference algorithms that extend deliberation along a single trajectory, sample completed candidates and aggregate them through voting or verification, or search over unfinished partial states. These algorithms differ in their statistical structure, compute accounting, and failure modes. Treating these procedures as interchangeable under a single scalar "budget," or reporting accuracy without the inference protocol that produced it, makes results difficult to compare across studies. We develop a systematic account of test-time scaling along three axes. First, we formalize test-time scaling as budgeted inference over the implicit prefix tree of an autoregressive model and distinguish three structural regimes: single-trajectory sequential scaling, leaf-level scaling with terminal reduction, and prefix-level scaling. Second, we treat the evaluated object as the entire inference system and develop evaluation principles that separate end-to-end system performance from candidate-bank diagnostics. We introduce an evaluation profile whose coordinates and simple functionals recover or bound common repeated-sampling metrics, and prescribe protocol-matched reporting of compute and uncertainty. Third, we specify reproducibility requirements for inference protocols, distinguishing exact replay from distributional reproducibility and identifying the artifacts needed to support each. We also organize the open-weight reasoning ecosystem by model-side and interface mechanisms, apply these principles to broad-knowledge, symbolic-reasoning, and competition-mathematics benchmarks, and assemble over 2 billion full reasoning traces for release with progressively richer verifier and token-level signals.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [223] [Everyone Conforms, No One Believes: Pluralistic Ignorance in LLM Agent Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02758)
*Yashwanth YS*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLM agents robustly exhibit pluralistic ignorance—publicly conforming to norms they privately oppose—revealing systematic bias in social simulations and limited ability for norm change through dissent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM-based multi-agent simulations capture pluralistic ignorance, a crucial social phenomenon where people publicly conform to norms they privately reject, believing others support them. This drives norm persistence, social movements, and political revolutions but hasn't been tested in LLM social simulations.

Method: Created a benchmark of 100 scenarios across 10 domains and 5 authority levels grounded in human pluralistic ignorance literature. Evaluated 8 models from 6 organizations. Tested public conformity rates despite private opposition. Introduced "norm entrepreneur" experiments where one agent publicly dissents to test cascade potential. Conducted prompt component ablation across all models to isolate drivers of conformity.

Result: LLM agents show robust pluralistic ignorance: 64-94% publicly conform despite private opposition. Conformity is domain-sensitive (highest in workplace/social relationships) and model-dependent but uncorrelated with model capability. Norm entrepreneur interventions fail in 7 of 8 models (<26% cascade success), with one model showing zero cascades. GPT-4o is outlier at 48% cascade success. Ablation shows conformity emerges even without false-consensus framing or fit-in goals (52-92% in minimal condition).

Conclusion: Model selection is an unacknowledged degree of freedom that fundamentally shapes simulation outcomes. LLM simulations systematically overestimate social norm stability and miss fragile tipping-point dynamics of real-world norm change. Pluralistic ignorance emerges robustly in LLM agents, suggesting inherent conformity biases in current models.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems are increasingly used to simulate social dynamics, from opinion formation to collective decision-making. These simulations can reproduce certain social phenomena, but it is unknown whether they capture pluralistic ignorance, a state where a majority privately rejects a norm yet publicly conforms, each believing they are alone in dissenting. This phenomenon drives norm persistence, social movements, and political revolutions. We show that pluralistic ignorance emerges robustly in LLM agent populations. We construct a benchmark of 100 scenarios across 10 domains and 5 authority levels, grounded in the human pluralistic ignorance literature, and evaluate 8 models from 6 organizations. Agents publicly conform at rates of 64 to 94% despite privately opposing the norm. Conformity is domain-sensitive (workplace and social relationship scenarios produce near-universal compliance) and highly model-dependent, though uncorrelated with capability. We test whether a single "norm entrepreneur" can break the false consensus by publicly dissenting. For 7 of 8 models, cascades succeed less than 26% of the time, with one model showing zero cascades across all scenarios. GPT-4o is a notable outlier at 48%, revealing qualitatively distinct dynamics across model families.
