A prompt component ablation across all 8 models establishes that conformity is emergent rather than instruction-driven: removing both the false-consensus framing and fit-in goal reduces conformity but does not eliminate it (52 to 92% in the minimal condition). Our findings identify model selection as an unacknowledged degree of freedom that fundamentally shapes simulation outcomes. More broadly, the near-absence of cascades suggests LLM simulations may systematically overestimate the stability of social norms, missing the fragile tipping-point dynamics that drive real-world norm change in human societies.

</details>


### [224] [Stateful Governance for Concurrent Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02764)
*Yuxiang Peng,Xiaodi Wu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Provenact is a runtime architecture for stateful governance in concurrent AI agent systems that prevents stale authorizations by ensuring committed effects are authorized against up-to-date policy states through policy-state serializability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are increasingly executing consequential operations (refunds, inventory reservations, financial transfers) that require governance over effects, not just model outputs. Existing safeguards make authorization decisions based on request-time information, which becomes stale as policy-relevant state (budgets, inventory, approval status, risk signals) changes before effects occur, leading to stale authorizations.

Method: The paper introduces policy-state serializability as a correctness condition requiring committed effects to be explainable as authorized against policy state immediately before they occur. It presents Provenact, a runtime architecture that keeps policies as reviewable programs while coordinating state and effects to preserve policy decisions. A PostgreSQL-backed prototype was implemented and evaluated.

Result: Provenact prevents stale authorizations missed by baselines that pass policy state as ordinary request context, preserves delayed approvals while unrelated work proceeds, keeps policy evolution mostly in policy text rather than trusted provider code, and avoids policy violations in an LLM-free procurement workflow where baselines produce stale authorizations over shared budgets and inventory.

Conclusion: Provenact demonstrates a viable approach for integrating stateful governance boundaries into agent frameworks and provider-backed domains where agents act on shared resources, addressing the fundamental challenge of stale authorizations in concurrent agentic systems.

Abstract: AI agents are moving from advisory interfaces into systems that execute consequential operations: issuing refunds, reserving scarce inventory, provisioning cloud resources, and initiating financial transfers. These workflows require governance over effects, not only over model outputs. Existing safeguards often decide whether an action is allowed from the information available when the action is requested. For stateful policies, that request-time view may be incomplete: budgets, inventory, approval status, and risk signals can change before the effect occurs, making an earlier authorization or approval stale.
  This paper studies stateful governance for concurrent agentic systems. We identify stale authorization as the core failure mode and define policy-state serializability, a correctness condition requiring committed effects to be explainable as authorized against the policy state immediately before they occur. We present Provenact, a runtime architecture that keeps policies as reviewable programs while coordinating the state and effects needed to preserve their decisions. In experiments with a PostgreSQL-backed prototype of Provenact, the system prevents stale authorizations missed by baselines that pass policy state as ordinary request context, preserves delayed approvals while unrelated work proceeds, keeps policy evolution mostly in policy text rather than trusted provider code, and avoids policy violations in a scripted, LLM-free procurement workflow where agent-governance baselines produce stale authorizations over shared budgets and inventory. More broadly, Provenact suggests a path for integrating stateful governance boundaries into agent frameworks and provider-backed domains where agents act on shared resources.

</details>


### [225] [Emergence of Biased Consensus in Multi-Agent LLM Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02827)
*Maya Okawa*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM debates exhibit collective bias emergence driven by noise and conformity, with phase transitions predicted by physics-inspired models and suppressed by agent heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM debates show strong performance but their safety and fairness risks remain poorly understood. Interaction can amplify single LLM biases, raising concerns for real-world deployment. There's a need to understand the emergence of collective (often biased) norms in these debates.

Method: 1) Identify emergence of collective bias in multi-agent LLM debates
2) Show noise (e.g., LLM sampling temperature) as a key driver
3) Propose analytical framework using physics-inspired theoretical models of social dynamics
4) Predict phase transition to collective bias when conformity surpasses critical threshold
5) Test theoretical predictions through controlled experiments
6) Study effect of agent heterogeneity
7) Apply insights to realistic decision-making tasks

Result: - Observed finite-size crossover consistent with underlying phase transition
- Agent heterogeneity suppresses emergence by smoothing (rounding) the transition
- Insights generalize to realistic decision-making tasks (investment decisions, LLM-as-a-judge evaluation)
- Collective bias emerges when conformity exceeds critical threshold given LLMs' initial bias and debate noise

Conclusion: Multi-agent LLM debates can develop collective biases through social dynamics similar to phase transitions. Noise and conformity are key drivers, while agent heterogeneity helps suppress bias emergence. These findings highlight important safety concerns for deploying multi-agent LLM systems and provide analytical tools for understanding and mitigating bias amplification.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM debates achieve strong performance on decision-making tasks as well as problem-solving benchmarks, yet their safety and fairness risks remain poorly understood. Notably, interaction can amplify the biases of single LLMs, raising concerns for real-world deployment. We identify the emergence of collective (often biased) norms in multi-agent LLM debates and show that noise (e.g., LLM sampling temperature) is a key driver. To explain this, we propose an analytical framework drawing on physics-inspired theoretical models of social dynamics. We predict a phase transition to collective bias when conformity surpasses a critical threshold given the LLMs' initial bias and debate noise. We test the theoretical predictions through controlled experiments and observe a finite-size crossover consistent with an underlying phase transition. We further find that agent heterogeneity suppresses emergence by smoothing (rounding) this transition. Finally, we show that these insights generalize to realistic decision-making tasks, including investment decisions and LLM-as-a-judge evaluation.

</details>


### [226] [SABRE: A Multi-Agent Approach for Selecting Out-of-Distribution Detectors Under a Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02959)
*Mary Wisell,Salimeh Sekeh*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: SABRE is an adaptive system that dynamically selects the most reliable OOD detector for each domain at inference time, preventing performance inversion where fixed detectors fail across different domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current post-hoc OOD detection assumes detectors chosen on benchmarks remain reliable when deployed, but this fails across domains - detectors can invert performance (scoring in-distribution as anomalous) and the best detector varies by domain.

Method: Three language-model agents operate under budget: Selector chooses detectors from a library, Reporter consolidates evidence, Analyst calibrates reliability on small labeled sample. Four new multimodal density detectors included. System infers operating regime from data without prior knowledge.

Result: SABRE tracks the strongest detector in each domain, recovers reliable detection where conventional detectors invert, converges to sound detectors. Component analysis shows agents complementary: Reporter's feedback yields gains, Analyst's calibration prevents inversion.

Conclusion: No fixed detector can be trusted across domains - reliability must be established at deployment. SABRE shows this can be done automatically through per-regime selection at inference.

Abstract: Post-hoc out-of-distribution (OOD) detection for vision-language models assumes that a detector chosen on a benchmark stays reliable once deployed. We show this fails across domains: on a single frozen encoder, a detector that leads in one domain can invert in another, scoring in-distribution inputs as more anomalous than genuine outliers, and the best detector changes from domain to domain, so no fixed choice is reliable throughout. We introduce SABRE (Selective Agentic Budgeted Reliability Ensemble,) which replaces this fixed choice with per-regime selection at inference. Three language-model agents reason over a library of post-hoc detectors under a bounded query budget: a Selector chooses which detector to consult next, a Reporter consolidates the evidence for each input, and an Analyst calibrates detector reliability on a small labeled sample held out from the deployment domain and disjoint from the test data, weighting selection and aggregation without ever observing a scored input's label. The library includes four multimodal density detectors we propose. Inferring the operating regime from data, SABRE tracks the strongest detector in each domain without prior knowledge of it, recovering reliable detection where a conventional detector inverts and converging to that detector where it is sound. A component analysis shows the agents are complementary: the Reporter's feedback yields consistent gains, and the Analyst's calibration is decisive against inversion, ruling out unreliable detectors so that aggregation no longer cancels the sound ones. Since no fixed rule can be trusted across domains, reliability must be established at deployment rather than assumed from a benchmark, and SABRE shows this can be done automatically.

</details>


### [227] [When Truth Is Distributed: Misinformation Derails Collective Fact Recovery in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03421)
*Chenfei Yan,Zeyang Yue,Feifei Zhao,Erliang Lin,Lu Jia,Haibo Tong,Mingyang Lyu,Chengyi Sun,Yi Zeng*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLM-based multi-agent systems are vulnerable to deception, where a single false testimony spreads rapidly through the system, severely degrading truth recovery from 72.5% to 14.2% in collaborative reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM-based multi-agent systems show promise for collaborative reasoning, current evaluations focus only on final outcomes, leaving reliability and information propagation dynamics unclear, especially how local errors might amplify into collective risks through communication.

Method: Researchers developed Hi-Agreement, a controlled evaluation framework that pairs all-honest collaboration with controlled deception scenarios. They used 120 five-agent object-movement environments where partial observations determine unique endpoints, evaluating 3 homogeneous LLM-based multi-agent systems. The framework analyzes aggregation through multi-stage voting, testimony adoption, and evidence-root lineage propagation.

Result: Aggregate truth recovery dropped dramatically from 72.50% to 14.17% when deception was introduced. Single false testimony was adopted more readily than truthful testimony, propagated to higher orders, and persisted through honest agents even after the deceiver exited. Observers without first-hand evidence could suppress incorrect consensus but didn't improve truth recovery.

Conclusion: Distributed fact recovery in LLM-based multi-agent systems is fragile due to a mechanism where false evidence gains collective influence through adoption and continued propagation by other agents after entering communication, highlighting systemic vulnerabilities in collaborative reasoning.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems promise effective collaborative reasoning, but communication may amplify local errors into collective risks. Existing evaluations emphasize final outcomes, leaving the reliability and propagation dynamics of distributed information aggregation unclear. We introduce Hi-Agreement, a controlled evaluation framework that strictly pairs all-honest collaboration with controlled deception by a key evidence holder and analyzes the aggregation process through multi-stage voting, testimony adoption, and evidence-root lineage propagation. Using 120 five-agent object-movement environments where partial observations jointly determine a unique endpoint, we evaluate 3 homogeneous LLM-based multi-agent systems. Across these paired conditions, aggregate truth recovery falls from 72.50% to 14.17%, with significant declines for every system. Process tracing and exit ablations show that a single false testimony is adopted more readily than truthful testimony, propagates to higher orders, and persists through honest agents after the deceiver exits. Observers without first-hand evidence suppress incorrect consensus but do not improve truth recovery. Together, these findings reveal both the fragility of distributed fact recovery and its underlying mechanism: false evidence gains collective influence through its adoption and continued propagation by other agents after entering communication.

</details>


### [228] [Group Perspective Matters: Regulating Debate Relationships Can Mitigate Blind Conformity in Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03648)
*Hao Wu,Shoucheng Song,Chang Yao,Haoyu Wang,Huaiyu Wan,Youfang Lin,Kai Lv*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: DEAR is a novel framework that dynamically regulates debate relationships among LLMs in multi-agent debates using reinforcement learning to reduce blind conformity and improve reasoning performance while being more token-efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Multi-Agent Debate (MAD) approaches suffer from blind conformity, where LLMs tend to agree with each other even when wrong. Traditional individual evaluation methods (confidence, perplexity) fail to reflect reasoning correctness and may worsen conformity. The paper aims to address this by shifting focus from individual evaluation to group interaction dynamics.

Method: DEAR defines "Debate Relationships" and regulates them through three stages: 1) "What" - perceiving group consultation tendency and uncertainty using quantified consensus and divergence as group evidence; 2) "Who" - introducing a Selection RL-Agent to dynamically select reference peers; 3) "How" - adopting a Behavior RL-Agent to adaptively adjust generation behaviors. The framework uses multi-agent reinforcement learning to jointly optimize these RL agents as a sequential decision-making process.

Result: Extensive experiments show DEAR achieves superior performance compared to existing approaches while significantly reducing token consumption, indicating both effectiveness and efficiency improvements.

Conclusion: Regulating debate relationships from a group perspective effectively mitigates blind conformity in multi-agent LLM debates. The DEAR framework successfully addresses limitations of individual evaluation methods through dynamic relationship regulation, leading to better reasoning performance with improved token efficiency.

Abstract: Multi-Agent Debate (MAD) improves the reasoning performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) through multi-round interaction. However, LLMs in MAD are highly susceptible to blind conformity. Existing individual evaluation methods, typically based on confidence or perplexity, fail to reflect the correctness of reasoning and may even exacerbate blind conformity. To address this, we shift the perspective from individual evaluation to group interaction. We define mutual referencing among LLMs as \textbf{Debate Relationships} and recognize that regulating these relationships is the key to mitigating blind conformity. In this paper, we propose a novel framework for \textbf{D}ynamically r\textbf{E}gulating deb\textbf{A}te \textbf{R}elationships (DEAR) from the group perspective. At first, DEAR quantifies consensus and divergence as \textit{group evidence} to capture the debate state. Then, DEAR operates through three stages: 1) What: perceiving group consultation tendency and uncertainty; 2) Who: introducing a Selection RL-Agent to dynamically select reference peers; and 3) How: adopting a Behavior RL-Agent to adaptively adjust generation behaviors. Notably, we formulate the execution of the two RL-Agents as a sequential decision-making process, jointly optimizing via multi-agent reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DEAR achieves superior performance while significantly reducing token consumption.

</details>


### [229] [An Actionable Diagnosis of Multilingual, Multi-Agent Planning Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03735)
*Vikas Pahuja,Jonathan Brokman,Omer Hofman,Tamir Nizri,Daniel Vishna,Seraphina Goldfarb-Tarrant,Kelly Marchisio,Hisashi Kojima,Roman Vainshtein*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: TART improves multilingual multi-agent systems by making planning failures explicit to planners, achieving 5.6 percentage point accuracy gains across 11 languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual multi-agent systems degrade significantly beyond English, but prior work rarely identifies how task-critical information is lost when user requests are converted into executable plans. The authors aim to understand why these systems fail and develop mitigation strategies.

Method: 1) Study the planner as request-to-action interface; 2) Derive taxonomy of planning-grounding failures from real-world task executions; 3) Introduce TART (Taxonomy-Guided Actionable Representation) that makes taxonomy's key aspects explicit to planner and downstream sub-agents.

Result: 1) LLM-based analysis shows planning failures constitute increasing share of unsuccessful executions as language-resource availability declines; 2) TART consistently improves performance across multiple languages, three LLM backbones, two datasets, and two agentic configurations; 3) On multilingual GAIA, raises state-of-the-art system's accuracy by 5.6 percentage points averaged across 11 languages.

Conclusion: Identifying and making planning failures explicit through taxonomy-guided representations (TART) effectively mitigates multilingual performance degradation in multi-agent systems, with strongest benefits for low-resource languages.

Abstract: Multilingual multi-agent systems exhibit substantial degradation beyond English, yet prior work rarely identifies how task-critical information is lost when user requests are converted into executable plans. We study the planner in a multi-agent system as the request-to-action interface and derive an actionable taxonomy of planning-grounding failures from failed real-world task executions. LLM-based analysis shows that these failures constitute an increasing share of unsuccessful executions as language-resource availability declines, with the strongest effects in low-resource languages. To test whether the taxonomy supports mitigation, we introduce TART, Taxonomy-Guided Actionable Representation, that makes the taxonomy's key aspects explicit to the planner and downstream sub-agents. Across multiple languages, three LLM backbones, two datasets, and two agentic configurations, TART consistently improves performance. On multilingual GAIA, it raises a state-of-the-art system's accuracy by 5.6 percentage points averaged across eleven languages spanning low- to high-resource settings.

</details>


### [230] [History Matters: Meta-policy Delegation with Heterogeneous Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03833)
*Ziqing Lu,Avinash Reddy Mudireddy,Sarra Alqahtani,Weiyu Xu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper proposes MARL-based approaches for task delegation in heterogeneous multi-agent systems, introducing history-dependent policies and novel monetary mechanisms to optimize collaboration under resource constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents become increasingly important in decision-making systems, there's a need for effective collaboration in heterogeneous multi-agent systems where agents have different capabilities and operating costs. The paper addresses how to enable agents to delegate tasks efficiently under resource-constrained scenarios.

Method: The authors first develop a multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) based delegation training that enables sequential delegation decisions while minimizing total execution cost. They then extend this to MARL with prescribed delegation topologies. Additionally, they introduce two new frameworks: 1) history-dependent policies that include interaction history and previous joint actions, even in fully observable environments; 2) novel multi-dimensional monetary mechanisms to facilitate collaboration and delegation.

Result: The paper presents novel frameworks for improving coordination in multi-agent systems through history-dependent policies that go beyond conventional MARL methods limited to current state dependencies. The monetary mechanism provides an innovative approach to facilitate delegation and collaboration in heterogeneous MAS settings.

Conclusion: This research advances multi-agent collaboration by addressing key challenges in heterogeneous systems through MARL-based delegation approaches, history-aware policies that improve coordination beyond current-state-only methods, and economic-inspired mechanisms for efficient resource allocation and task delegation in constrained environments.

Abstract: AI agents are expected to play an increasingly important role in future decision-making systems. In this paper, we consider collaborative systems composed of heterogeneous multi-agent systems (MAS), where their members have different capabilities and operating costs. We study how agents can delegate tasks to one another so that certain research tasks can be completed effectively under resource-constrained scenarios. We first develop a multi-agent reinforcement learning-based (MARL) delegation training that enables agents to make sequential delegation decisions while minimizing the total execution cost. We then extend this approach to MARL with prescribed delegation topologies. Furthermore, we introduce two new frameworks for collaboration and delegation in multi-agent systems. The first framework proposes that an agent's policy depends not only on the current state of the underlying Markov decision process but also on the interaction history, including previous joint actions. This history-dependent formulation can improve coordination even in fully observable environments, where conventional MARL methods typically restrict policies to depend only on the current state. The second framework proposes a novel, potentially multi-dimensional monetary mechanism to facilitate the collaboration and delegation for MAS.

</details>
